formulation of the Schrödinger bridge (SB) problem. While optimal transport focuses on static couplings between distributions, the dynamic SB lifts the problem to the level of
stochastic processes
. Instead of directly transporting mass, we seek the most likely evolution of a stochastic system that transforms an initial distribution into a target distribution over time. In this section, we formalize this dynamic viewpoint and develop the stochastic calculus tools, including path measures, Itô processes, and change-of-measure techniques, necessary to analyze and solve the dynamic SB problem.

2.1
Dynamic Optimal Transport Problem

Just like how the static Schrödinger bridge problem could be traced back to the Monge-Kantorovich optimal mass transport problems, we begin our discussion of the dynamic Schrödinger bridge problem with
Bernamou-Brenier (dynamic) optimal transport problem

(
benamou2000computational
)
. The
key idea
that differentiates the
dynamic
from the
static
problems is the intuition behind transporting mass with a
continuous flow over a time interval
rather than static couplings between marginals.

Concretely, given an initial distribution
\pi_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at time
t=0
and a target distribution
\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
at time
t=T
, the dynamic formulation aims to determine the continuous-time evolution of a marginal probability density
p_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
that evolves mass from
\pi_{0}\to\pi_{T}
over
t\in[0,T]
.

Recall that (
Kantorovich’s OMT Problem
) aims to minimize the cost of transporting states
\bm{x}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}
to
\bm{x}_{T}\sim\pi_{T}
defined as
c(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
. A natural cost function is the Euclidean distance
c(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T}):=\|\bm{x}_{T}-\bm{x}_{0}\|^{2}
, which is the
straight-line distance
between
\bm{x}_{0}
and
\bm{x}_{T}
. To reformulate this into a
dynamic
transport problem, we can consider transporting the state continuous state
\bm{x}
over the straight line which travels a total distance of
\|\bm{x}_{T}-\bm{x}_{0}\|^{2}
. Concretely, the quadratic transport cost from
\bm{x}_{0}\to\bm{x}_{T}
is equivalent to the integrating energy-minimizing velocity field
\bm{v}_{t}(\bm{x}):=\frac{d}{dt}\bm{x}_{t}
over
t\in[0,T]
defined as:

\displaystyle\|\bm{x}_{T}-\bm{x}_{0}\|^{2}=\inf_{\bm{x}_{t}:\bm{x}_{0}\to\bm{x}_{T}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{v}_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}dt

(2.1)

Leveraging this identity, we can reframe the static OT problem as minimizing the
total kinetic energy
of transporting particles from the distribution
\pi_{0}
, where particles at location
\bm{x}
move with velocity
\bm{v}_{t}(\bm{x})
, weighted by the probability mass of the particle
p_{t}(\bm{x})
(Figure
3
). Jointly minimizing the velocity and marginal probability path
(\bm{v}_{t},p_{t})
yields the
dynamic OT problem
.

Figure 3:
Comparison Between Static and Dynamic Optimal Transport.
Illustration of the relationship between the static and dynamic formulations of optimal transport between two marginal distributions
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
. The static formulation minimizes the transport cost directly between endpoints via the quadratic cost
\|\bm{x}_{T}-\bm{x}_{0}\|^{2}
, while the dynamic (Benamou–Brenier) formulation instead seeks a time-dependent velocity field
\bm{v}_{t}(\bm{x})
that continuously transports mass from
\pi_{0}
to
\pi_{T}
while minimizing the kinetic energy
\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{v}_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}dt
. The white trajectory represents a particle path under the optimal flow, illustrating how dynamic OT realizes the same optimal coupling as static OT through continuous mass evolution.

Definition 2.1

(Dynamic Optimal Transport (OT) Problem)
.

Given two marginal constraints
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the
dynamic optimal transport (OT) problem
aims to find the optimal probability flow
p^{\star}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and velocity field
\bm{v}^{\star}_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
t\in[0,T]
that minimizes:

\displaystyle\inf_{(p_{t},\bm{v}_{t})}\left\{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\bm{v}_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt\right\}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}+\nabla\cdot(p_{t}\bm{v}_{t})=0\\
p_{0}=\pi_{0},\hskip 9.24994ptp_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(Dynamic OT Problem)

whre the continuity equation
\partial_{t}p_{t}+\nabla\cdot(p_{t}\bm{v}_{t})=0
enforces conservation of probability mass over the continuous flow.

The solution to (
Dynamic OT Problem
) is a time-interpolation of the
optimal transport map

M^{\star}
defined as:

\displaystyle M^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x}_{0})=(1-t)\bm{x}_{0}+tM^{\star}(\bm{x}_{0}),\quad\bm{x}_{0}\sim\pi_{0},\quad t\in[0,T]

(Optimal Transport Map)

which yields the optimal marginal density
p^{\star}_{t}
as the pushforward of
\pi_{0}
via the transport map at time
t\in[0,T]
denoted
p^{\star}_{t}=(M_{t}^{\star})_{\#}\pi_{0}
.

This Bernamou-Brenier (dynamic) formulation characterizes optimal transport minimization of kinetic energy over
straight
,
deterministic
flows satisfying the continuity equation. However, most real-world systems do not naturally evolve in straight lines, but rather stochastic paths that traverse non-linear manifolds. This naturally leads us to the
dynamic Schrödinger bridge problem
, which considers the optimal transport problem where the underlying dynamics are
stochastic
rather than deterministic.

2.2
Dynamic Schrödinger Bridge Problem

While the dynamic optimal transport (OT) formulation characterizes the most efficient deterministic flow that transports probability mass between two distributions, many real-world systems evolve under intrinsic stochasticity. This motivates a
stochastic generalization of dynamic OT
, leading to the
dynamic Schrödinger bridge (SB) problem
. Instead of searching over deterministic velocity fields, the SB formulation asks:
Among all stochastic evolutions that transform an initial distribution into a target distribution over a time horizon
[0,T]
, which one is
most likely
relative to a given reference dynamics?

To answer this, the dynamic SB problem introduces a
reference path measure

\mathbb{Q}
which describes the baseline stochastic dynamics of a system. The dynamic Schrödinger bridge problem then selects, among all processes
matching prescribed initial and terminal marginals
, the one that deviates minimally from the reference process in relative entropy.

Definition 2.2

(Dynamic Schrödinger Bridge Problem)
.

Let
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be probability measures on state space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
be a
reference path measure
, where
\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is the space of probability paths over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

The
dynamic Schrödinger bridge (SB) problem
seeks a new path measure
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with initial and terminal marginals matching
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
which minimizes the relative entropy with respect to
\mathbb{Q}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}=\underset{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))}{\arg\min}\big\{\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q}):p_{0}=\pi_{0},p_{T}=\pi_{T}\big\}

(Dynamic SB Problem)

where
\text{KL}(\cdot\|\cdot)
denotes the KL divergence on path space given by:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}\right]

(2.2)

This perspective reveals the Schrödinger bridge as an entropy-regularized analogue of the (
Dynamic OT Problem
). In the limit of vanishing noise, the problem recovers classical optimal transport, while for positive noise, it admits a rich stochastic structure. In particular, when the reference dynamics is Brownian motion, the optimal path measure inherits a Markov structure and can be characterized through a time-dependent drift correction of the reference process. In this section, we will break down these ideas, starting with path measures and Itô processes, then deriving the path-space KL divergence, and finally exploring the dynamic SB problem through the lens of the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman and Fokker-Planck system or equations and the Hopf-Cole transform.

2.3
Path Measures and Itô Processes

Before diving deeper into Schrödinger Bridge theory, let’s first establish the foundations of
path measures and Itô processes
that will help us solve the SB problem. First, we define a
stochastic process
as a random variable
\bm{X}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that evolves over a time horizon
t\in[0,T]
, denoted
\bm{X}_{0:T}:=(\bm{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
. The distribution of many such random variables follows a time-dependent marginal probability distribution, denoted
p_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Figure 4:
Brownian Motion and Controlled Itô Processes.
Illustration of one-dimensional stochastic trajectories generated by two stochastic differential equations (SDEs) starting from
\bm{X}_{0}=0
over the time interval
t\in[0,1]
.
Left:
Sample paths of pure Brownian motion of the form
d\bm{X}_{t}=\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
, where increments are Gaussian with variance proportional to the timestep
\bm{B}_{t+\Delta t}=\bm{B}_{t}+\sqrt{\Delta t}\bm{z}
with
\bm{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bm{I}_{d})
.
Right:
Sample paths of a controlled Itô process of the form
d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t))+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
, where we set
\bm{f}\equiv 0
and
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t):=\frac{2-\bm{x}}{T-t+\epsilon}
which pulls the process to
\bm{X}_{T}=2
.

Specifically, we want to consider only stochastic processes
\bm{X}_{0:T}
that
depend
only on the
past
and
present
states of the system, which are formally said to be
adapted to the filtration

(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
. The filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is simply a sequence of events
\mathcal{F}_{t}
that is
ordered
such that all events in
\mathcal{F}_{s}
for
s\leq t
are in
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{F}_{s}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{t}
for
s\leq t
. This definition allows us to define a
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted process
which depends only on the past and present events.

Definition 2.3

(
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-Adapted Process)
.

A stocastic process
\bm{X}_{0:T}
is said to be
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted if the random variable
\bm{X}_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable for all
t\in[0,T]
, where:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t}:=\sigma(\bm{X}_{\tau}:0\leq\tau\leq t)

(2.3)

which is the sigma-algebra
10
10

10

This is not to be confused with the
\sigma
notation for the diffusion coefficient. We will only use
\sigma
to denote sigma-algebra sparingly.
generated by the history of the process up to time
t
or all the information available from the process up to time
t
. Equivalently, this means that for all measurable sets
S\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
over the state space, the process
\bm{X}_{0:T}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\{\bm{X}_{t}\in S\}\in\mathcal{F}_{t}

(2.4)

which is a formal way of stating that all values
\bm{X}_{t}\in S
can be determined using only information up to time
t
.

A specific stochastic process that we will see often is
Brownian motion
, or
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted Wiener process
, which begins at the origin and evolves via independent Gaussian steps with variance that is proportional to the time increment.

Definition 2.4

(Brownian Motion)
.

Brownian motion
(\bm{B}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a type of stochastic process that starts at
\bm{B}_{0}=\bm{0}
and evolves via
independent Gaussian increments
defined as

\displaystyle\bm{B}_{t+\Delta t}=\bm{B}_{t}+\sqrt{\Delta t}\bm{z},\quad\bm{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})

(2.5)

where
\bm{z}
is sampled independently from a unit isotropic Gaussian with zero-mean across all time steps. This means that each increment is Gaussian
\bm{B}_{t+\Delta t}-\bm{B}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\Delta t\bm{I}_{d})
and independent of
\mathcal{F}_{t}
.

Now that we have defined the notion of stochastic processes and Brownian motion, we can define an
Itô process
which is the class of stochastic processes that are the foundation of most generative modeling frameworks, from diffusion to flow matching to Schrödinger bridge matching.

Definition 2.5

(Itô Process)
.

An Itô Process is a stochastic process
(\bm{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
whose state
\bm{X}_{t}
can be written as

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{X}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)ds+\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(2.6)

which can be equivalently defined as the solution of an
stochastic differential equation
(SDE) of the form

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(2.7)

where
\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is known as the
drift
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the
diffusion
coefficient matrix. In most applications, the diffusion coefficient is a constant or time-dependent scalar
\sigma_{t}:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
11
11

11

While we introduce the theory of path measures with the general covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}_{t}
, we simplify to the scalar time-dependent diffusion coefficient
\sigma_{t}
in the remainder of this guide as it is most commonly used throughout the literature.
, which simplifies the SDE into
d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
.

Next we consider stochastic dynamics where there exists some external influences that perturb the drift of the process via some control drift. These
controlled Itô processes
can be modeled with a controlled stochastic differential equation (SDE), where the time-dependent control velocity
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
enters the drift in the directions spanned by the diffusion coefficient
\sigma_{t}
.

Definition 2.6

(Controlled Itô Process)
.

A controlled Itô process is obtained by introducing a control
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
12
12

12

In the remainder of this guide, we omit explicitly writing the domain and codomain (
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
) when it is clear from context.
that modifies the drift in the directions spanned by the diffusion coefficient, which yields the
controlled stochastic differential equation
(SDE) of the form:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\left(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u},t)+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u},t)\right)dt+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Controlled SDE)

where
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the reference drift and
\bm{\Sigma}_{t}:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is the diffusion coefficient. Equivalently, the controlled process can be written as:

\displaystyle\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{X}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{\tau}^{u},\tau)+\bm{\Sigma}_{\tau}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{\tau}^{u},\tau)\right)d\tau+\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{\tau}d\bm{B}_{\tau}

(2.8)

Observe that the control is scaled by the diffusion coefficient
\bm{\Sigma}_{t}\bm{u}
rather than added independently. This parameterization of the controlled SDE guarantees that the controlled process remains
absolutely continuous
with respect to the reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
defined using the diffusion term
\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
. This property ensures that the relative entropy used in the dynamic SB objective takes a tractable form, which we will show explicitly in Section
2.5
. Intuitively, scaling with the diffusion coefficient ensures that the control drift
only steers the process in directions within the space supported by the stochastic noise
, whereas adding the control drift independently without scaling could push the process in directions where the noise has no support.

Having developed an understanding of uncontrolled and controlled Itô processes, we now derive a
key property
of Itô processes, which states that applying some function to the state produces another Itô process over the transformed coordinates. This property is captured via
Itô’s formula
.

Theorem 2.7

(Itô’s Formula in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
)
.

Consider an Itô process
(\bm{X}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d})_{t\in[0,T]}
and a scalar function
\phi(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
that twice continuously differentiable
\phi\in C^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T])
13
13

13

functions in the Hessian are continuous and produces a square integrable random variable
\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)\in L_{2}
(i.e.,
\mathbb{E}[|\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)|^{2}]<\infty
. Then, the transformed random variable
\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)
is also an Itô process that evolves via the SDE given by

\displaystyle d\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)=\partial_{t}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)d\bm{X}_{t}+\frac{1}{2}d\bm{X}_{t}^{\top}(\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t))d\bm{X}_{t}

(2.9)

Substituting
d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
, we rewrite the formula as:

\displaystyle d\bm{Y}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\partial_{t}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{\top}\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right)\bigg]dt+\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Itô’s Formula)

which means that the space of Itô processes is
closed under twice-differentiable functions
. If
\phi(\bm{x}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a scalar function dependent only on
\bm{x}
, then the partial derivative
\partial_{t}\phi
disappears, and
\phi(\bm{X}_{t})
follows the SDE:

\displaystyle d\phi(\bm{X}_{t})=\left[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{X}_{t})\right]dt+\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t})^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(2.10)

Proof.
In this proof, we will break down the steps from (
2.9
) to (
Itô’s Formula
). After substituting
d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}_{t}dt+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
where we denote
\bm{f}_{t}\equiv\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)
for simplicity, we get:

\displaystyle d\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)=\partial_{t}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}(\bm{f}_{t}dt+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t})+\frac{1}{2}(\bm{f}_{t}dt+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t})^{\top}(\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t))(\bm{f}_{t}dt+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t})

(2.11)

Itô’s calculus defines
dtdt=\bm{B}_{t}=0
and
(dt)^{2}=0
, so we can simplify the second term to:

\displaystyle d\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)=\partial_{t}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}(\bm{f}_{t}dt+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t})+\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t})^{\top}(\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t))(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t})}_{(\bigstar)}

(2.12)

Now, we will simplify the (
\bigstar
) term in the equation. Recall that
\bm{\Sigma}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
d\bm{B}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
by decomposing it into component form. Substituting the matrix-vector product in component form
(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t})_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{ik}d\bm{B}_{t}^{k}
into (
\bigstar
), we have:

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}})^{\top}(\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t))({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\left[\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{ik}d\bm{B}_{t}^{k}}\right)(\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t))_{ij}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{j\ell}d\bm{B}_{t}^{\ell}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k,\ell}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{ik}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{j\ell}(\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t))_{ij}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{d\bm{B}_{t}^{k}d\bm{B}_{t}^{\ell}}_{\delta_{k\ell dt}}}=\sum_{i,j,k}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{ik}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{jk}}_{(\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\Sigma}^{\top})_{ij}}}(\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t))_{ij}dt

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\sum_{i,j}(\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t))_{ij}(\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\Sigma}^{\top})_{ij}}_{\text{Tr}(\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\Sigma}^{\top}\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t))}}dt=\text{Tr}(\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\Sigma}^{\top}\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t))dt

(2.13)

Substituting the simplified (
\bigstar
) back into (
2.12
), we get the final form of the SDE for the transformed Itô process:

\displaystyle d\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}(\bm{f}_{t}dt+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t})+\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{\top}\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right)dt

\displaystyle=\bigg[\partial_{t}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{\top}\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right)\bigg]dt+\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(2.14)

which is Itô’s formula for an arbitrary twice-continuous function
\phi(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}\in C^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T])
and SDE
d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
.

When
\phi
doesn’t depend on
t
and is only a function of the state
\phi(\bm{x}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have
\partial_{t}\phi(\bm{x})=0
. Additionally, when the diffusion is an isotropic Gaussian
\bm{\Sigma}_{t}=\sigma_{t}\bm{I}_{d}
, then the trace term reduces to:

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{\top}}\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right)=\text{Tr}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}^{2}\bm{I}_{d}\bm{I}_{d}^{\top}}\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right)={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma^{2}_{t}}\text{Tr}\left(\nabla^{2}\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right)={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}^{2}}\Delta\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)

(2.15)

With these simplifications, the
Itô formula
becomes:

\displaystyle d\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)

\displaystyle=\bigg[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{X}_{t})\bigg]dt+\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Simplified Itô Process)

which is the
key ingredient
to several derivations and proofs that we will study in this guide.
\square

Since the Schrödinger bridge problem studies
controlled stochastic processes
, we establish the following Corollary which is a direct consequence of our proof for (
Itô’s Formula
), but simply replaces the drift of the uncontrolled SDE
\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)
with the drift of the controlled SDE
(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t))
from Definition
2.6
.

Corollary 2.8

(Itô Formula for Controlled SDEs)
.

Consider a controlled stochastic process
(\bm{X}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d})_{t\in[0,T]}
generated by the controlled SDE:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Controlled SDE)

where
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
is the control drift and
\sigma_{t}
is the scalar diffusion coefficient. For any scalar function
\phi(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}\in C^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T])
14
14

14

where
C^{1,2}
denotes that the function is continuously differentiable with respect to
t
and twice continuously differentiable with respect to
\bm{x}
, the transformed stochastic process defined by
\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)
follows the SDE:

\displaystyle d\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)=\left[\partial_{t}\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+(\bm{f}+\sigma\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\right]dt+\nabla\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Controlled Itô Formula)

If
\phi(\bm{x}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a scalar function dependent only on
\bm{x}
, then the partial derivative
\partial_{t}\phi
disappears, and
\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})
follows the SDE:

\displaystyle d\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})=\left[(\bm{f}+\sigma\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})\right]dt+\nabla\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(2.16)

The controlled Itô formula shows that, for any sufficiently smooth test function, the drift term of the transformed process is determined by a
linear differential operator
acting on that function. This operator is called the
infinitesimal generator of the SDE

\mathcal{A}_{t}
, which describes the instantaneous rate of change of the expected value of
\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)
at time
t
, explicitly written as:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}_{t}\phi)(\bm{x},t)=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]-\phi(\bm{x},t)}{\Delta t}\right]

(Infinitesmal Generator)

which plays a central role in describing the local evolution of observables.

Definition 2.9

(Infinitesimal Generator of Itô Process)
.

For any test function
\phi(\bm{x},t)\in C^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T])
, the
infinitesimal generator of the uncontrolled SDE
is the operator
\mathcal{A}_{t}
defined as:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}_{t}\phi)(\bm{x},t):=\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{x},t)+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{x},t)

(Uncontrolled Generator)

and the
infinitesimal generator of the controlled SDE
is the operator
\mathcal{A}_{t}^{u}
defined as:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}^{u}_{t}\phi)(\bm{x},t):=(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{x},t)+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{x},t)

(Controlled Generator)

Equivalently, the controlled generator can be written in terms of the uncontrolled generator by expanding the divergence as:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}^{u}_{t}\phi)(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{x},t)}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\mathcal{A}_{t}\phi)(\bm{x},t)}+\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{x},t)

(2.17)

This definition of the infinitesimal generator can be used to rewrite Itô’s formula for uncontrolled and controlled SDEs as:

\displaystyle d\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{A}_{t})\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle d\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{A}^{u}_{t})\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Controlled Itô Formula with
\mathcal{A}^{u}_{t}
)

Using the infinitesimal generator, we can define a specific family of stochastic processes, called
martingales
.

Definition 2.10

(Martingales)
.

A
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted stochastic process
\bm{Y}_{0:T}:=(\bm{Y}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is called a
martingale
if it is integrable for all
t\in[0,T]
(i.e.,
\mathbb{E}[|\bm{Y}_{t}|]<\infty
) and satisfies the
martingale property
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bm{Y}_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]=\bm{Y}_{s}\implies\mathbb{E}[\bm{Y}_{t}]=\mathbb{E}[\bm{Y}_{s}],\quad\forall 0\leq s\leq t

(Martingale Property)

which means that the expectation is
constant over time
. Furthermore, we have that if
\bm{Y}_{t}:=\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)
is the martingale process generated by the function
\phi(\bm{x},t)\in C^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T])
, the uncontrolled generator vanishes, such that:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}_{t}\phi)(\bm{X}_{t},t)=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)=0

(2.18)

Given the definition of Itô processes as stochastic differential equations with drift and diffusion terms, we observe that there are infinitely many possible paths under the same SDE due to the non-deterministic diffusion term, which injects randomness into each trajectory; however, certain paths are more likely than others under a given drift.

Sampling an infinite number of paths under a specific SDE forms a
probability distribution in the path space
, known as a
path measure

\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, which given an Itô process
\bm{X}_{0:T}\in C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
returns the probability that the process was generated under that SDE. Specifically, we will consider the general space of
controlled path measures
with a control drift
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
, where
\bm{u}\equiv 0
yields the
reference path measure

\mathbb{Q}
.

Definition 2.11

(Controlled Path Measure)
.

A
controlled path measure

\mathbb{P^{u}}\in C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a probability measure on the path space
C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
induced by a stochastic differential equation (SDE) with control
\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
defined in (
Controlled SDE
). For any measurable set of trajectories
S\subseteq C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\mathbb{P}^{u}(S)
is the probability that the trajectories in the set are generated under the controlled SDE:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{u}(S)=\text{Pr}\left(\bm{X}_{0:T}\in S\text{ under the controlled SDE of }\mathbb{P}^{u}\right)

(2.19)

Throughout this guide, we will use a slight abuse of notation and denote the probability of a stochastic path under
\mathbb{P}^{u}
as
\mathbb{P}^{u}(\bm{X}_{0:T})
.

Next, we will define how the marginal densities evolve over the continuous time interval via the
Fokker-Planck equation
, which provides an alternative representation of path measures as deterministic
partial differential equations
instead of non-deterministic SDEs.

2.4
Fokker-Planck and Feynman-Kac Equations

Defining a path measure with an SDE only captures the evolution of single particles along the trajectories supported by the path measure, but tells us nothing about the evolution of the distribution generated by many particles under a path measure. The
Fokker-Planck equation
allows us to define the behavior of the
probability distribution
of particles
p_{t}
at each time point
t\in[0,T]
, such that individual states simulated by the SDE in (
2.7
) are samples
\bm{X}_{t}\sim p_{t}
.

Theorem 2.12

(Fokker-Planck Equation)
.

Let
\bm{X}_{0:T}
be a stochastic process governed by the SDE:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(2.20)

where
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
is the drift and
\sigma_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
is the scalar diffusion coefficient. Then, the marginal density
p_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of the particles generated by the SDE evolves via the
Fokker-Planck equation
defined as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x}))+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})

(Fokker-Planck Equation)

where
\bm{X}_{t}\sim p_{t}
. Equivalently,
p_{t}
is probability law of
\bm{X}_{t}
, denoted
\text{Law}(\bm{X}_{t})=p_{t}
.

Intuition.
Before we derive the Fokker-Planck equation explicitly, let us first consider the problem of characterizing the distribution over particles generated from an SDE without direct access to the density
p_{t}
. From Itô’s formula (Theorem
2.7
), we can define the evolution of samples from the SDE via twice-differentiable functions over the state space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

With the capability to study the random variable with arbitrary smooth functions, known as
test functions

\phi(\bm{x}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we can extract different pieces of information about the distribution
p_{t}
by taking the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t})]
. For instance, the expectation of the identity function
\phi(\bm{x})=\bm{x}
measures how the mean of the distribution evolves over time, and the squared function
\phi(\bm{x})=\bm{x}^{2}
measures how the variance evolves over time. Unlike the test function used to derive Itô’s formula, we don’t require it to depend on time, since we use it only to probe the spatial structure of the density at each time. Intuitively, by analyzing the evolution of
any
arbitrary smooth functions on
\bm{X}_{t}
, we can derive the evolution of the density
p_{t}
.

Derivation.
Consider a test function
\phi(\bm{x})\in C_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
that is twice differentiable and has compact support such that the boundary terms vanish in integration by parts. By Itô’s formula in (
Simplified Itô Process
), the random variable
\phi(\bm{X}_{t})
is an Itô process that evolves via the SDE:

\displaystyle d\phi(\bm{X}_{t})=\bigg[\underbrace{\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t})}_{\text{drift contribution}}+\underbrace{\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{X}_{t})}_{\text{diffusion contribution}}\bigg]dt+\underbrace{\sigma_{t}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t})^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}}_{\text{stochastic term}}

(2.21)

Since we want to define the evolution on a distribution level, we take the expectation on both sides, which eliminates the stochastic term since the Itô integral has zero expectation:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[d\phi(\bm{X}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{X}_{t})\right]dt+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\sigma_{t}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t})^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\right]}_{=0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{X}_{t})\right]dt

(2.22)

Dividing both sides by
dt
, we have:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\phi(\bm{X}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{X}_{t})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{X}_{t})\right]

(2.23)

By definition, the expectations can be written in integral form as
\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}(\bm{x})}[\cdot]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\cdot)p_{t}d\bm{x}
which gives:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{x})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{x})\right]p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

(2.24)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(\bm{x})\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}}_{\text{drift term}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma_{t}^{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}}_{\text{diffusion term}}

(2.25)

For the
drift term
,
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\nabla\phi(\bm{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we can apply integration by parts on each coordinate to get:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\cdot\nabla\phi(\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\bm{f}_{i}(\bm{x},t)\frac{\partial\phi(\bm{x})}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\underbrace{\frac{\partial\phi(\bm{x})}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}}}_{dv}\underbrace{\big(\bm{f}_{i}(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})\big)}_{u}d\bm{x}

(1st integration by parts)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\underbrace{\phi(\bm{x})}_{v}\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}}\big(\bm{f}_{i}(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})\big)}_{du}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(\bm{x})\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}}\big(\bm{f}_{i}(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})\big)}_{=\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x}))}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\boxed{-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(\bm{x})\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x}))d\bm{x}}

(drift term)

where all boundary terms vanish since
\phi
has compact support. For the
diffusion term
, we apply integration by parts twice as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma_{t}^{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t}(\bm{x})\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}\partial\bm{x}_{i}}\phi(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\underbrace{p_{t}(\bm{x})}_{u}\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}}\phi(\bm{x})\right)}_{dv}d\bm{x}

(1st integration by parts)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}}p_{t}(\bm{x})}_{u}\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}}\phi(\bm{x})}_{dv}d\bm{x}

(2nd integration by parts)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(\bm{x})\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}\partial\bm{x}_{i}}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(\bm{x})\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}\partial\bm{x}_{i}}p_{t}(\bm{x})}_{\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\boxed{\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(\bm{x})\Delta p_{t}d\bm{x}}

(diffusion term)

Substituting the simplified drift and diffusion terms back into (
2.25
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(\bm{x})\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x}))d\bm{x}}_{\text{drift term}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(\bm{x})\sigma_{t}^{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}}_{\text{diffusion term}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(\bm{x})\bigg(-\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x}))+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})\bigg)d\bm{x}

(2.26)

Since we defined
\phi(\bm{x})
such that this equality holds for
any arbitrary test function
, then the integrands must be equal which recovers the
Fokker-Planck
(FP) equation:

\displaystyle\boxed{\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x}))+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})}

(Fokker-Planck Equation)

Furthermore, the density
p_{t}
that solves the FP equation is
unique
givne a fixed initial condition
15
15

15

For rigorous proof of uniqueness, see
bogachev2021uniqueness
.
\square

By testing against an arbitrary smooth test function and equating integrands, we recover the Fokker–Planck equation that governs the time evolution of the density
p_{t}(\bm{x})
. We observe that the drift term
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
induces the transport of mass via divergence, while the diffusion coefficient
\sigma_{t}
contributes to smoothing through the Laplacian operator. The Fokker–Planck equation therefore provides a forward-time description of the stochastic dynamics, complementing the underlying stochastic differential equation.

Remark 2.13

(Fokker-Planck Equation Generalizes the Continuity Equation)
.

The Fokker-Planck equation can be interpreted as a
stochastic generalization of the classic continuity equation
from the (
Dynamic OT Problem
), which ensures the conservation of probability mass, defined as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})+\nabla\cdot\big(p_{t}(\bm{x})\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\big)=0

(Continuity Equation)

which expresses that probability mass is transported along the flow induced by the velocity
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)
. The Fokker-Planck equation is equal to the continuity equation with an additional Laplacian term
\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}
that captures the
spreading of mass caused by Brownian motion
.

Intuitively, the Laplacian is the
divergence of the gradient

\Delta p_{t}=\nabla\cdot\nabla p_{t}
which measures whether the direction of steepest increase in probability density
\nabla p_{t}
is spreading out (positive divergence) or converging (negative divergence), so the positive diffusion coefficient increases the
spreading out of probability density
.

Using this intuition, we can easily extend the Fokker-Planck equation to controlled processes by introducing the scaled control
\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
into the drift contribution, yielding the
controlled Fokker-Planck equation
that governs the evolution of the controlled marginal density.

Corollary 2.14

(Controlled Fokker-Planck Equation)
.

Let
(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be a stochastic process governed by the SDE:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\left(\bm{f}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\right)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},

(2.27)

where
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
is the control drift,
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
is the reference drift and
\sigma_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
is the scalar diffusion coefficient. Let
p_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the marginal density or probability law of
\bm{X}^{u}_{t}
, such that
\bm{X}^{u}_{t}\sim p_{t}
and
\text{Law}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u})=p_{t}
. Then
p_{t}
evolves according to the
controlled Fokker-Planck equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot\left(\left(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\right)p_{t}(\bm{x})\right)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})

(Controlled Fokker-Planck Equation)

Equivalently, expanding the divergence term gives

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot\left(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})\right)-\nabla\cdot\bigl(\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})\bigr)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})

(2.28)

We have shown that uncontrolled and controlled Itô processes can not only be written as stochastic differential equations (SDEs) that govern the forward-time evolution of individual trajectories
\bm{X}_{t}
, but also admit an
equivalent deterministic representation through the Fokker-Planck equation
, which is a deterministic partial differential equation (PDE) that governs the forward-time evolution of the
marginal density

p_{t}
that characterizes the probability law of the SDE trajectories.

However, the Schrödinger bridge problem is inherently a two-sided problem enforced by both initial
and terminal
marginal distribution constraints. Therefore, we need to consider how constraints or functions evaluated on the terminal distribution
p_{T}
evolve
backward in time
through the stochastic process to determine the probability of an intermediate state under the terminal constraint. This evolution is captured by the
Feynman-Kac equation
, which we analyze next.

Theorem 2.15

(Feynman-Kac Equation)
.

Consider a scalar function
r(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
that solves the linear PDE with a terminal constraint
r(\bm{x},T)=\Phi(\bm{x})
and running cost
c(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
16
16

16

Note that from now on,
c(\bm{x},t)
will be used to denote the running cost, which is distinct from
c(\bm{x},\bm{y})
which is used in Section
1
to denote the transport cost from
\bm{x}
to
\bm{y}
defined as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}r(\bm{x},t)+\langle\bm{f}(\bm{x},t),\nabla r(\bm{x},t)\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta r(\bm{x},t)-c(\bm{x},t)r(\bm{x},t)=0,\hskip 9.24994ptr(\bm{x},T)=\Phi(\bm{x})

(Feynman-Kac PDE)

where
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
is the reference drift and
\sigma_{t}
is the diffusion coefficient of an SDE that generates a stochastic process
(\bm{X}_{\tau})_{\tau\in[t,T]}
defined by:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)d\tau+\sigma_{\tau}d\bm{B}_{\tau},\quad\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}

(2.29)

Then, the solution
r
that solves the (
Feynman-Kac PDE
) can be written as the following expectation over stochastic paths:

\displaystyle r(\bm{x},t)=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\int_{t}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{s},s)ds\right)\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(Feynman-Kac Formula)

which is called the
Feynman-Kac formula
.

Derivation.
Intuitively,
r(\bm{x},t)
is the expected future value of the terminal constraint
\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})
after discounting the running cost
c(\bm{X}_{s},s)
given the current state
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
at time
t
. With this intuition in mind, we derive the Feynman-Kac formula in steps.

Step 1: Define the Stochastic Processes.

We can consider the following stochastic process that describes the evolution of the accumulated running cost over
[\tau,t]
:

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{\tau}:=\underbrace{\exp\left(-\int_{t}^{\tau}c(\bm{X}_{s},s)ds\right)}_{=:\bm{Z}_{\tau}}r(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau),\hskip 9.24994pt\tau\in[t,T]

(2.30)

Since the exponential term is itself a stochastic process that changes with
\tau
, we can define
\bm{Z}_{\tau}:=\exp\left(-\int_{t}^{\tau}c(\bm{X}_{s},s)ds\right)
and
\bm{M}_{\tau}:=\bm{Z}_{\tau}r(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)
. Now, we aim to show that
\bm{M}_{\tau}
is a
martingale
whose expected value does not change over time (Definition
2.10
). In other words, we aim to show that the
expected future reward

\mathbb{E}[\bm{M}_{T}]
is equal to the current value
\bm{M}_{t}
, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}[\bm{M}_{T}]=\bm{M}_{t}
.

Step 2: Apply Itô’s Formula.

Applying
Itô’s product rule
to
\bm{M}_{s}
and
r(\bm{X}_{s},s)
, we get the following expression for
d\bm{M}_{s}
:

\displaystyle d\bm{M}_{\tau}=\bm{Z}_{\tau}dr(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)+r(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)d\bm{Z}_{s}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{d\bm{Z}_{s}dr(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)}_{=O(d\tau^{2})=0}}=\bm{Z}_{\tau}\underbrace{dr(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)}_{(\bigstar)}+r(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)\underbrace{d\bm{Z}_{\tau}}_{(\diamond)}

(2.31)

To expand
(\bigstar)
, we can apply (
Itô’s Formula
) to get:

\displaystyle dr(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)

\displaystyle=\bigg[\partial_{\tau}r(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\langle\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau),\nabla r(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)\rangle+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta r(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)}_{\text{uncontrolled generator applied to }r}}\bigg]d\tau+\nabla r(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\tau}r(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)+\mathcal{A}_{t}r(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau))d\tau+\nabla r(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{\tau}

(
\bigstar
)

where we recognize the unconditional generator
\mathcal{A}_{t}
from (
Uncontrolled Generator
). To expand
(\diamond)
, we denote
\bm{A}_{\tau}:=-\int_{t}^{\tau}c(\bm{X}_{s},s)ds
and apply the chain rule:

\displaystyle d\bm{Z}_{\tau}=d(e^{\bm{A}_{\tau}})=\underbrace{e^{\bm{A}_{\tau}}}_{=\bm{Z}_{\tau}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}d\bm{A}_{\tau}}=\bm{Z}_{\tau}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{d\bm{A}_{\tau}}{d\tau}}d\tau=\bm{Z}_{\tau}({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-c(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)}d\tau)=-c(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)\bm{Z}_{\tau}d\tau

(
\diamond
)

where the expression for
\frac{d\bm{A}_{\tau}}{d\tau}=-c(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)
follows from the fundamental theorem of calculus for integrals with variable upper limit. Substituting (

★

‣
2.4
) and (

⋄

‣
2.4
) back into (
2.31
), we get:

\displaystyle d\bm{M}_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\bm{Z}_{\tau}\underbrace{\left[(\partial_{\tau}r+\mathcal{A}r)d\tau+\nabla r^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{\tau}\right]}_{(\bigstar)}+r\underbrace{\left(-c\bm{Z}_{\tau}d\tau\right)}_{(\diamond)}

\displaystyle=\bm{Z}_{\tau}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\left(\partial_{\tau}r+\mathcal{A}r-cr\right)}_{=0}}d\tau+\bm{Z}_{\tau}\nabla r^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{\tau}

(2.32)

where we observe that the drift term is exactly equal to the (
Feynman-Kac PDE
), which is equal to zero given the solution
r(\bm{x},t)
. Therefore, the drift disappears, and
d\bm{M}_{\tau}=\bm{Z}_{\tau}\nabla r^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{\tau}
contains only a diffusion term and is a
martingale
with constant zero-expectation. Therefore, we have:

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{T}=\bm{M}_{t}+\int_{t}^{T}d\bm{M}_{\tau}=\bm{M}_{t}+\int_{t}^{T}\bm{Z}_{\tau}\nabla r^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{\tau}

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}[\bm{M}_{T}]=\mathbb{E}[\bm{M}_{t}]+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\bm{Z}_{\tau}\nabla r^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{\tau}\right]}_{\text{Itô integral }=0}

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}[\bm{M}_{T}]=\mathbb{E}[\bm{M}_{t}]=\bm{M}_{t}

(2.33)

where the final equality follows from the fact that
\bm{M}_{t}
is the starting time, and the accumulated cost over
[t,t]
is 0.

Step 3: Substitute Terminal Constraint.

By our definition in (
2.30
), we have:

\displaystyle\bm{M}_{T}=\exp\left(-\int_{t}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{s},s)ds\right)\underbrace{r(\bm{X}_{T},T)}_{=\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})}=\exp\left(-\int_{t}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{s},s)ds\right)\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})

(2.34)

Given that
\bm{M}_{t}=r(\bm{x},t)
and
\bm{M}_{t}=\mathbb{E}[\bm{M}_{T}]
, we have shown that the solution
r(\bm{x},t)
takes the form:

\displaystyle\boxed{r(\bm{x},t)=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\int_{t}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{s},s)ds\right)\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]}

(Feynman-Kac Formula)

which is exactly the (
Feynman-Kac Formula
).
\square

A special case of the Feynman-Kac formula when there is no running cost (
c\equiv 0
) is the
Kolmogorov backward equation
, which describes the expected future reward as depending only on the expected value over terminal states.

Corollary 2.16

(Kolmogorov Backward Equation)
.

Let
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a terminal constraint function and define
r(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
as the expected future reward from a intermediate state
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
:

\displaystyle r(\bm{x},t):=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{Q}}\left[\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(Feynman-Kac Representation)

where
\mathbb{Q}
is the reference path measure defined by the SDE
d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)ds+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
. Then
r(\bm{x},t)
satisfies the
Kolmogorov backward equation
defined as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}r(\bm{x},t)+\langle\bm{f}(\bm{x},t),\nabla r(\bm{x},t)\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta r(\bm{x},t)=0,\hskip 9.24994ptr(\bm{x},T)=\Phi(\bm{x})

(Kolmogorov Backward Equation)

Together, the (
Fokker-Planck Equation
) and (
Feynman-Kac Formula
) describe the two complementary ways in which stochastic differential equations evolve in time. The
Fokker–Planck equation
characterizes the forward evolution of probability densities
p_{t}
, describing how the distribution of particles transported by an SDE spreads and flows through state space.

In contrast, the
Feynman-Kac formula
provides a backward evolution of functions along stochastic trajectories, expressing solutions to certain partial differential equations as expectations over future paths of the stochastic process. In this sense, the Fokker-Planck equation propagates probability mass forward in time, while the Feynman-Kac equation propagates value functions or potentials backward through the stochastic dynamics. This forward–backward structure will play a central role in the theory of Schrödinger bridges, where the optimal dynamics are characterized by the interaction between forward-evolving densities and backward-evolving potentials that together determine the controlled stochastic process connecting the prescribed endpoint distributions.

2.5
Girsanov’s Theorem

Now that we have established
path measures
and how they evolve both on an individual trajectory level via stochastic differential equations and on a density level via the Fokker-Planck partial differential equation, we will shift our focus to describing the
relationship between path measures
. This is the
core objective
of the Schrödinger bridge problem, which is to define a new path measure that minimally diverges from a reference path measure with marginal constraints.

We begin our discussion with
Girsanov’s theorem
, which characterizes the probability density ratio of a single stochastic process under different path measures. In this section, we derive Girsanov’s theorem in the special case where the reference path measure is standard Brownian motion with identity diffusion matrix. The key idea is to understand how a change of drift modifies the underlying path measure. To understand the intuition, we build the result from scratch using the discrete-time structure of Brownian motion
17
17

17

This follows closely from the derivation presented in
sarkka2019applied
.
. For notational simplicity, we will consider a constant diffusion coefficient
\sigma
over time, and omit the time subscript.

Recall from Definition
2.4
that Brownian motion can be written as a sequence of
independent Gaussian increments
which can be written in discrete time with step size
\Delta t:=t_{k+1}-t_{k}
as:

\displaystyle\sigma\mathbb{B}:\quad\bm{B}_{t_{k+1}}=\bm{B}_{t_{k}}+\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}\bm{z}=:\bm{B}_{t_{k}}+\sigma\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}},\quad\bm{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})

(2.35)

where
\sigma
is the diffusion coefficient and
\sigma\mathbb{B}
to denotes the pure Brownian motion process with diffusion coefficient
\sigma
. Then, we can write the Brownian increment
\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}
as being sampled from a
d
-dimensional Gaussian with covariance
\Delta t\bm{I}_{d}
, denoted
\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\Delta t\bm{I}_{d})
. The
transition probability
under
\sigma\mathbb{B}
is a Markov transition density
p(\bm{B}_{t+\Delta t}|\bm{B}_{0:t})=p(\bm{B}_{t+\Delta t}|\bm{B}_{t})
which is a
d
-dimensional Gaussian centered at
\bm{B}_{t_{k}}
with covariance
\sigma^{2}\Delta t\bm{I}_{d}
given by:

\displaystyle\sigma\mathbb{B}(\bm{B}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{B}_{t_{k}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\bm{B}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{B}_{t_{k}})=\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2}\Delta t)^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\bm{B}_{t_{k+1}}-\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}\Delta t}\right)

(2.36)

Then, we can write the joint probability of the discrete-time Brownian process
(\bm{B}_{t_{k}})_{k\in\{1,\dots,K\}}=\bm{B}_{t_{1}},\dots,\bm{B}_{t_{n}}
where
t_{k+1}=t_{k}+\Delta t
as:

\displaystyle\sigma\mathbb{B}(\bm{B}_{t_{1}},\dots,\bm{B}_{t_{K}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\sigma\mathbb{B}(\bm{B}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{B}_{t_{k}})=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left[\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2}\Delta t)^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\bm{B}_{t_{k+1}}-\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}\Delta t}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left[\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2}\Delta t)^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\sigma^{2}\|\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}\Delta t}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left[\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2}\Delta t)^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}}{2\Delta t}\right)\right]

(2.37)

Now, we consider the Itô process with a control drift
\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)
that evolves via the SDE given by (
2.7
) which evolves in discrete increments defined by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}:\quad\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}=\bm{X}_{t_{k}}+\sigma\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\Delta t+\sigma\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}

(2.38)

which is the discrete Brownian process in (
2.35
) with an extra drift term that generates the path measure
\mathbb{P}
. Since the drift depends only on the current state
\bm{X}_{t}
, the transition probability is also a Markov transition density
\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}|(\bm{X}_{t_{\ell}})_{\ell\in\{1,\dots,k\}})=\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{X}_{t_{k}})
and can be written as a Gaussian centered at
\bm{X}_{t_{k}}+\sigma\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\Delta t
with covariance
\sigma^{2}\Delta t\bm{I}_{d}
given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{X}_{t_{k}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{X}_{t_{k}}+\sigma\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\Delta t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2}\Delta t)^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}-(\bm{X}_{t_{k}}+\sigma\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\Delta t)\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}\Delta t}\right)

(2.39)

with joint probability density of the full discrete process
(\bm{X}_{t_{k}})_{k\in\{1,\dots,K\}}=\bm{X}_{t_{1}},\dots,\bm{X}_{t_{K}}
given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{t_{1}},\dots,\bm{X}_{t_{K}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{X}_{t_{k}})

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left[\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2}\Delta t)^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}-(\bm{X}_{t_{k}}+\sigma\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\Delta t)\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}\Delta t}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left[\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2}\Delta t)^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}-\bm{X}_{t_{k}}-\sigma\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\Delta t\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}\Delta t}\right)\right]

(2.40)

Now, suppose we have a path
(\bm{X}_{t_{k}})_{k\in\{1,\dots,K\}}
generated
under
the Brownian process
\sigma\mathbb{B}
such that
\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}=\bm{X}_{t_{k}}+\sigma\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}
. Then, we can define the ratio of the path probability under
\mathbb{P}
with respect to
\sigma\mathbb{B}
as:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathbb{B}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}})_{k\in\{1,\dots,K\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{t_{1}},\dots,\bm{X}_{t_{K}})}{\sigma\mathbb{B}(\bm{X}_{t_{1}},\dots,\bm{X}_{t_{K}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left[\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2}\Delta t)^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}-\bm{X}_{t_{k}}}-\sigma\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\Delta t\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}\Delta t}\right)\right]}{\prod_{k=1}^{K}\left[\frac{1}{(2\pi\sigma^{2}\Delta t)^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}}{2\Delta t}\right)\right]}

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\exp\left(-\frac{\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}}-\sigma\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\Delta t\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}\Delta t}+\frac{\|\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}}{2\Delta t}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\exp\left(-\frac{\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}}-\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\Delta t\|^{2}}{2\Delta t}+\frac{\|\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}}{2\Delta t}\right)

(2.41)

where the second-last equality follows from the fact that the increment
\bm{X}_{t_{k}}\mapsto\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}
was made under the reference process such that
\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}=\bm{X}_{t_{k}}+\sigma\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}
, which allowed us to factor out the
\sigma^{2}
and cancel with the denominator. Expanding
\|\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}-\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\Delta t\|^{2}=\|\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}-2\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})^{\top}\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\Delta t+\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\|^{2}\Delta t^{2}
gives us:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathbb{B}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}})_{k\in\{1,\dots,K\}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{K}\exp\left(\frac{-\|\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}+2\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})^{\top}\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\Delta t-\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\|^{2}\Delta t^{2}+\|\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}\Delta t}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{2\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})^{\top}\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\Delta t-\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\|^{2}\Delta t^{2}}{2\Delta t}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})\|^{2}\Delta t\right)

(2.42)

which depends only on the Brownian increments
\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}
and the drift
\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},t_{k})
. Taking the continuous time limit as
\Delta t\to 0
, we have
\sum_{k}\bm{u}_{t_{k}}\Delta\bm{B}_{t_{k}}\to\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
and
\sum_{k}\|\bm{u}_{t_{k}}\|^{2}\Delta t\to\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}_{t}\|^{2}dt
, resulting in the following form of ratio between path measures:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathbb{B}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt+\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)d\bm{B}_{t}\right)

(2.43)

This ratio
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\sigma\mathbb{B}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})
is also the
Radon-Nikodym derivative (RND)
between
\mathbb{P}
and
\sigma\mathbb{B}
, which can be used to transform the likelihood of a path under
\sigma\mathbb{B}
to its likelihood under
\mathbb{P}
. From this intuition, we can state the formal version of
Girsanov’s theorem

(
girsanov1960transforming
;
karatzas2014brownian
;
oksendal2003stochastic
)
.

Theorem 2.17

(Girsanov’s Theorem)
.

Consider the
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
(\bm{B}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
adapted to the filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
. Given two path measures
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\ll\mathbb{P}
, define the density process
(\bm{Z}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
of the ratio:

\displaystyle\bm{Z}_{t}:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]

(2.44)

which is the likelihood ratio given the information up to time
t
. Then, there exists a predictable process
(\bm{\theta}_{s})_{s\in[0,T]}
such that:

\displaystyle\bm{Z}_{t}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\bm{\theta}_{s}\|^{2}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\theta}_{s}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{s}\right),\hskip 9.24994ptt\in[0,T]

(2.45)

Furthermore, the stochastic process
(\bm{B}^{\prime}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
defined as:

\displaystyle\bm{B}^{\prime}_{t}:=\bm{B}_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\theta}_{s}ds

(Girsanov’s Theorem)

is a standard Brownian motion under
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
, such that for any discrete time intervals
0=t_{0}\leq\dots\leq t_{k}\leq\dots\leq t_{K}\leq T
the increments
\Delta\bm{B}^{\prime}_{t_{k}}:=\bm{B}^{\prime}_{t_{k+1}}-\bm{B}^{\prime}_{t_{k}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},(t_{k+1}-t_{k})\bm{I}_{d})
and independent.

Intuitively, Girsanov’s theorem provides a way to change the probability measure on path space while leaving the underlying trajectories unchanged. By defining
\bm{\theta}_{t}
such that the drift of the original path probability cancels out after applying the transformation, Girsanov’s theorem states that the
\bm{\theta}_{t}
-tilted process becomes a standard Brownian motion under the transformed probability measure. To ensure that the resulting path measure obtained from reweighting with Girsanov’s theorem is valid, we establish
Novikov’s condition
, which states an integrability condition that ensures that the exponential density process is a
true martingale
satisfying (
Martingale Property
) that has constant expectation and thus defines a valid change of measure.

Lemma 2.18

(Novikov’s Condition)
.

Let
(\bm{B}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion adapted to a filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
and let
(\bm{\theta}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
be a
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted process that is square integrable over every finite time interval, such that
\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{\theta}_{t}\|^{2}dt<\infty
almost surely. Then, if the exponential process given by:

\displaystyle\bm{Z}_{t}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\bm{\theta}_{s}\|^{2}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\theta}_{s}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{s}\right)

(2.46)

satisfies the
Novikov’s condition
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\bm{\theta}_{s}\|^{2}ds\right)\right]<\infty

(Novikov’s condition)

Then,
(\bm{Z}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is a true martingale satisfying (
Martingale Property
) with constant expectation
\mathbb{E}[Z_{t}]=1
for
t\in[0,T]
.

The purpose of
Novikov’s Condition
is to ensure
\bm{Z}_{t}
does not lose mass and is properly normalized. In other words, it ensures that the exponential tilt used in Girsanov’s theorem can serve as a
valid likelihood ratio between path measures
and defines a
proper change of probability measure on path space
. Leveraging Girsanov’s theorem and Novikov’s Condition, we can now define changes in measure and KL divergences in path space as local drift changes in their stochastic differential equations.

2.6
Path Measure Radon-Nikodym Derivative and KL Divergence

To understand the SB problem, it is crucial to understand the measurement used to quantify
distance
between two path measures
\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
known as the
Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence
(also referred to as the
relative entropy
or
I-divergence
), denoted
\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})

(
csiszar1975divergence
)
.

Definition 2.19

(KL Divergence Between Path Measures)
.

Given two probability measures over the space of paths
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, the KL divergence is given by

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\|\mathbb{P})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}\right]=\int_{C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{0:T})

(2.47)

where
\bm{X}_{0:T}
is a stochastic process and
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}
is the Radon-Nikodym derivative between
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{P}
.

Since we have defined
path measures
as solutions to SDEs in Section
2.3
, we can now introduce how to write the KL divergence in integral form. First, we derive the
Radon-Nikodym (RN) derivative
with respect to the
drift
and
diffusion
of two Itô processes.

Figure 5:
Change of Measure Using Radon-Nikodym Derivative.
Sample trajectories of a diffusion process are shown under two probability measures.
Top left:
trajectories under the reference measure
\mathbb{Q}
, governed by the uncontrolled reference drift
\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)
.
Bottom left:
trajectories under the controlled measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
, where the dynamics include an additional control drift
\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)
.
Right:
the same reference trajectories are reweighted according to the Radon–Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}
, which assigns higher likelihood to paths aligned with the control and lower likelihood to paths that oppose it. The color intensity represents the log-likelihood ratio
\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}
, illustrating how the controlled dynamics can be interpreted as a change of measure on the same underlying path space.

Theorem 2.20

(Radon-Nikodym Derivative of Controlled Itô Path Measures
(
sarkka2019applied
)
)
.

Consider two controlled Itô path measures
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
with control drifts
\bm{u}
and
\tilde{\bm{u}}
, where
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}\ll\mathbb{P}^{u}
18
18

18

\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}\ll\mathbb{P}^{u}
means that
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and any event with zero probability under
\mathbb{P}^{u}
also has zero probability under
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
. Assuming the same reference drift
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
and diffusion coefficient
\sigma_{t}
, they are defined by the SDEs:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{u}:\quad d\bm{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}^{u}_{t},\quad\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}

(
\bm{u}
-SDE)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}:\quad d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{t}

\displaystyle=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\tilde{\bm{u}}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},\quad\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}=\bm{x}

(
\tilde{\bm{u}}
-SDE)

The Radon-Nikodym derivative of the corresponding path measures
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T})

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt+\int_{0}^{T}\big(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u}\big)(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}^{u}_{t}\bigg)

(Path RND)

where
\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}
denotes a stochastic process generated under
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and
\bm{B}^{u}_{t}
denotes the Brownian motion under
\mathbb{P}^{u}
.

Proof.
The
key idea
of the Radon-Nikodym derivative is to define how likely a stochastic process
\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}:=(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
defined as a standard Brownian motion under
\mathbb{P}^{u}
is under the path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
. Since a Brownian motion under
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
differ only in their control drifts, we define the change in the drifts as:

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{t}:=(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(2.48)

Following (
Girsanov’s Theorem
), we can define an density process
\bm{Z}_{t}
as:

\displaystyle\bm{Z}_{t}:=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\bm{\theta}_{s}\|^{2}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\theta}_{s}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{s}\right)\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\bm{Z}_{T}\right]=1

(2.49)

where the condition
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\bm{Z}_{T}\right]=1
follows from the assumption that
\bm{Z}_{t}
satisfies (
Novikov’s condition
) and is a true martingale. From (
Girsanov’s Theorem
), we know that
\bm{Z}_{T}
defines the density ratio of a new measure
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{u}
given by:

\displaystyle\bm{Z}_{t}:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]

(2.50)

Then, according to (
Girsanov’s Theorem
), we have that the Brownian motion
\bm{B}^{\prime}
under the new measure
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
is a standard Brownian motion defined by:

\displaystyle\bm{B}^{\prime}_{t}=\bm{B}^{u}_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\theta}_{s}ds\implies d\bm{B}^{u}_{t}=d\bm{B}^{\prime}_{t}+\bm{\theta}_{t}dt

(2.51)

Now, we can rewrite (

u

-SDE
) under the Brownian motion
\bm{B}^{\prime}
as:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}^{u}_{t}

\displaystyle=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}d\bm{B}^{u}_{t}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(d\bm{B}^{\prime}_{t}+\bm{\theta}_{t}dt)}

\displaystyle=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}(\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}+\bm{\theta}_{t}})dt+\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}d\bm{B}^{\prime}_{t}}

(2.52)

Substituting the definition
\bm{\theta}_{t}:=(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)
, we have:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}^{u}_{t}

\displaystyle=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}(\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}+\tilde{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)-\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)})dt+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}d\bm{B}^{\tilde{u}}_{t}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\tilde{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)})dt+\bm{\Sigma}_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}d\bm{B}^{\tilde{u}}_{t}}

(2.53)

which exactly matches (

~
u

-SDE
), so we conclude that
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}=\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
and
\bm{B}^{\prime}=\bm{B}^{\tilde{u}}
. Then, we have that
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}
is equal to
\bm{Z}_{T}
given by:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T})

\displaystyle=\bm{Z}_{T}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{\theta}_{s}}\|^{2}ds+\int_{0}^{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{\theta}_{s}^{\top}}d\bm{B}^{u}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)}\|^{2}ds+\int_{0}^{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)^{\top}}d\bm{B}^{u}_{s}\right)

(2.54)

which is exactly the Radon-Nikodym derivative that reweights stochastic processes under
\mathbb{P}^{u}
generated with (

u

-SDE
) with their probabilities under
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
defined by (

~
u

-SDE
).
\square

From Definition
2.19
, we defined the KL divergence between path measures as the expectation of the logarithm of the Radon-Nikodym derivative between two Itô processes. Using Theorem
2.20
, we can define the corresponding integral form for the KL divergence.

Corollary 2.21

(KL Divergence of Itô Path Measures)
.

Consider two controlled path measures
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
and
\mathbb{P}^{u}
, where
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}\ll\mathbb{P}^{u}
, with control drifts
\bm{u}
and
\tilde{\bm{u}}
and SDEs (

u

-SDE
) and (

~
u

-SDE
). The
KL divergence
of
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{u}
is given by:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}\|\mathbb{P}^{u})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\tilde{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)-\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)\|^{2}ds\right]

(Path KL Divergence)

where
\bm{X}_{0:T}^{\tilde{u}}
denotes stochastic trajectories sampled under the law of
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
. When
\mathbb{P}^{u}:=\mathbb{Q}
is the
reference path measure
with zero control
\bm{u}\equiv 0
with SDE
d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
, then the KL divergence reduces to:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\tilde{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)\|^{2}ds\right]

(2.55)

Proof.
Starting from our definition of (
Path RND
), we can expand the expression for path measure KL divergence from Definition
2.19
as:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}\|\mathbb{P}^{u})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}\left[\log{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T})}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)}\|^{2}ds+\int_{0}^{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)^{\top}}d\bm{B}^{u}_{s}}\right]

(2.56)

where the superscript in
\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T}
is used to explicitly denote that they are stochastic processes generated under
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
. Recall that the Itô integral of a standard Brownian motion vanishes under the expectation over its associated path measure. However, since the expectation in (
2.56
) is under
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
and the Brownian motion is under
\mathbb{P}^{u}
, we need to rewrite
d\bm{B}^{u}_{s}
in terms of
d\bm{B}^{\tilde{u}}_{s}
for the Itô integral to vanish. To do this, we apply (
Girsanov’s Theorem
) which states that the Brownian motion
\bm{B}^{\tilde{u}}
under the measure
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
is a standard Brownian motion defined by:

\displaystyle\bm{B}^{\tilde{u}}_{t}=\bm{B}_{t}^{u}-\int_{0}^{t}\bm{\theta}_{s}ds\implies d\bm{B}_{t}^{u}=d\bm{B}^{\tilde{u}}_{t}+\bm{\theta}_{t}dt

(2.57)

where we defined
(\bm{\theta}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
as in Theorem
2.20
as the difference in control drifts
\bm{\theta}_{t}:=(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)
evaluated along controlled trajectories generated under
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}
. Therefore, we can rewrite (
2.56
) as:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}\|\mathbb{P}^{u})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}\left[-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)}\|^{2}ds+\int_{0}^{t}(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(d\bm{B}^{\tilde{u}}_{s}+(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{s},s)ds)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}\bigg[-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)}\|^{2}ds+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}d\bm{B}^{\tilde{u}}_{s}}}_{\text{vanishes under expectation}}+\int_{0}^{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\|(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)\|^{2}}ds\bigg]

\displaystyle=\boxed{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\tilde{\bm{u}}-\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)}\|^{2}ds\bigg]}

(2.58)

which is the integral form of the path measure KL divergence. By simply substituting
\bm{u}\equiv 0
, we get the KL divergence for the case where
\mathbb{P}^{u}=\mathbb{Q}
is the reference path measure no control drift:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T})\right]=\boxed{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{u}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\tilde{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}^{\tilde{u}}_{t},t)\|^{2}ds\right]}

(2.59)

which is the form of the KL divergence that we will use to define the dynamic SB objective.
\square

The Radon–Nikodym derivative between controlled diffusion path measures provides an explicit representation of how changes in drift modify the probability of trajectories (Figure
5
). Using (
Girsanov’s Theorem
), this change of measure produces a
quadratic control cost
whose expectation reduces to the
path-space KL divergence
between the two measures. The final integral shows that minimizing the KL divergence between controlled path measures is equivalent to the expectation of the squared difference between the drifts over sampled stochastic paths.

2.7
Schrödinger Bridge with Arbitrary Reference Dynamics

Recall from Section
2.3
the family of controlled path measures
\mathbb{P}^{u}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
which characterize the probability law of stochastic processes
\bm{X}_{0:T}\in C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
generated by the controlled SDE:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Controlled SDE)

where
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
is the reference drift,
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
is the control drift and
\sigma_{t}
is the scalar diffusion coefficient. We also defined the
reference path measure
to be the special case where
\bm{u}\equiv 0
and the drift consists only of
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
.

Within the family of controlled path measures, the
dynamic Schrödinger bridge (SB) problem
seeks the control drift
\bm{u}^{\star}
that minimally perturbs the reference path measure while steering the process between prescribed marginal distributions.

More precisely, minimizing the perturbation from the reference path measure is defined as
minimizing the KL divergence
, which, following Corollary
2.21
, is equivalent to minimizing the kinetic energy produced from perturbing the reference dynamics with the control drift. Consequently, the dynamic SB can be interpreted as selecting the control drift that minimally perturbs the reference dynamics while satisfying the marginal distribution constraints
\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}
and
\bm{X}_{T}\sim\pi_{T}
.

Definition 2.22

(Dynamic Schrödinger Bridge with Arbitrary Reference Dynamics)
.

Let
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the prescribed initial and terminal distributions. The
dynamic Schrödinger bridge problem
, where the reference dynamics are defined by a drift
\bm{f}
, aims to determine the optimal control drift
\bm{u}^{\star}
that minimizes the KL divergence of the induced path measures subject to the marginal constraints:

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{Q})=\inf_{\bm{u}\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt\right]

(Dynamic SB)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}d\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
\bm{X}^{u}_{0}\sim\pi_{0},\quad\bm{X}^{u}_{T}\sim\pi_{T}\end{cases}

where the KL divergence follows from Corollary
2.21
and we define:

(i)

\mathcal{U}=\{\bm{u}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})|\exists C>0,\;\forall(\bm{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T],\;\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\leq C(1+\|\bm{x}\|)\}
is the set of all
feasible control drifts
which contains all smooth control fields that don’t grow faster than linearly, ensuring the controlled stochastic dynamics remain well-behaved
(
nusken2021solving
)
19
19

19

For the remainder of this guide, we assume optimization is over the set of feasible controls and omit stating it explicitly as a constraint
.

(ii)

\mathbb{P}^{u}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
as the path measure induced by the controlled SDE and
\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}
as a stochastic process generated under the controlled SDE with probability law
\mathbb{P}^{u}
.

Solving (
Dynamic SB
) yields the optimal pair
(\bm{u}^{\star},\mathbb{P}^{\star})
defines the Schrödinger bridge between
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
relative to
\bm{f}
.
