Rather than an expectation over stochastic processes under
\mathbb{P}^{u}
, we can also write the problem in terms of the time-dependent marginal densities
p_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
defined over the space of probability distributions over the state
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\displaystyle\inf_{(\bm{u},p_{t})}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}\right]p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

(Density Dynamic SB Problem)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot\big(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))\big)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})\\
p_{0}=\pi_{0},\quad p_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

where the marginal distributions satisfy
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
and the evolution of
p_{t}
satisfies the (
Controlled Fokker-Planck Equation
) derived in Section
2.4
.

Since the constrained optimization problem in (
Density Dynamic SB Problem
) requires minimizing with respect to both
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
and
p_{t}(\bm{x})
which are
coupled
via the Fokker-Planck PDE constraint, we cannot directly minimize this objective as any variation in
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
will implicitly change
p_{t}(\bm{x})
. Therefore, we introduce a
Lagrange multiplier

\psi_{t}
that turns (
Density Dynamic SB Problem
) to an
unconstrained optimization problem
with the Lagrangian:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(p,\bm{u},\psi_{t})

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}p_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle+\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\bigg(\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})+\nabla\cdot\big(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))\big)-\sigma_{t}^{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})\bigg)\bigg\}d\bm{x}dt

(2.60)

This optimization problem yields the pair of density and control drifts
(\bm{u}^{\star},p^{\star}_{t})
that satisfy the following
optimality conditions

(
caluya2021wasserstein
)
.

Proposition 2.23

(Optimality Conditions for Dynamic Schrödinger Bridge
(
caluya2021wasserstein
)
)
.

The pair of optimal state PDF and optimal control
(\bm{u}^{\star},p^{\star}_{t})
that minimize (
2.60
) is the solution to the pair of PDEs defined as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\psi_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}\|^{2}+\langle\nabla\psi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle=-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\psi_{t}\\
\partial_{t}p_{t}^{\star}+\nabla\cdot(p_{t}^{\star}(\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\psi_{t}))=\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta p^{\star}_{t}\end{cases}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}p^{\star}_{0}=\pi_{0}\\
p^{\star}_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(HJB-FP System)

where
\psi_{t}(\bm{x})
is the Lagrange multiplier. The optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}
can also be written in terms of
\psi_{t}
as:

\displaystyle\forall(\bm{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T],\quad\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=\sigma_{t}\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})

(2.61)

Proof.
Now, we will break down the proof of this statement, which follows the proof of Proposition 1 in
(
caluya2021wasserstein
)
. Expanding the Lagrangian from (
2.60
) we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(p,\bm{u},\psi):=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt}_{(\bigstar)}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\nabla\cdot\big(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))\big)d\bm{x}dt}_{(\blacklozenge)}-\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt}_{(\blacktriangle)}

(2.62)

Since we want to avoid derivatives with respect to
p^{\star}
, we move the derivatives onto the Lagrange multiplier
\psi_{t}(\bm{x})
using integration by parts. For the term (
\bigstar
), we have:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{0}^{T}\underbrace{\psi_{t}(\bm{x})}_{u}\underbrace{\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})}_{dv}dtd\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\underbrace{[\psi_{t}(\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{x})]_{t=0}^{T}}_{\text{constant}}-\int_{0}^{T}p_{t}(\bm{x})\partial_{t}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})dt\right)d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{0}^{T}p_{t}(\bm{x})\partial_{t}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})dtd\bm{x}

(2.63)

where the boundary term reduces to constants
\int\psi_{0}(\bm{x})\pi_{0}(\bm{x})dtd\bm{x}
and
\int\psi_{T}(\bm{x})\pi_{T}(\bm{x})dtd\bm{x}
, which do not depend on the optimization variable
p_{t}
and can be dropped from the Lagrangian. For the term (
\blacklozenge
), we can apply integration by parts with the divergence identity
\int\psi_{t}\nabla\cdot\bm{v}=-\int\bm{v}\cdot\nabla\psi_{t}
since the boundary condition vanishes at infinity
20
20

20

The marginal density
p_{t}
must integrate to 1 and since
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is unbounded, it must vanish as
\|\bm{x}\|\to\infty
.
.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\nabla

\displaystyle\cdot\big(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))\big)d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t}(\bm{x})\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))d\bm{x}dt

(2.64)

Finally, we apply integration by parts twice to simplify the term (
\blacktriangle
):

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt=-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}\partial\bm{x}_{j}}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\underbrace{\psi_{t}(\bm{x})}_{u}\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{x}_{j}}p_{t}(\bm{x})\right)}_{dv}d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})}_{u}\underbrace{\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{x}_{j}}p_{t}(\bm{x})}_{dv}d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=-\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t}(\bm{x}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\bm{x}_{i}\partial\bm{x}_{j}}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})}d\bm{x}dt}_{\text{sum over $i=j$ gives the Laplacian of }\psi_{t}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}p_{t}(\bm{x})\Delta\psi_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

(2.65)

Now, substituting all terms back into (
2.62
), we get:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(p_{t},\bm{u},\psi)=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{0}^{T}p_{t}(\bm{x})\partial_{t}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})dtd\bm{x}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t}(\bm{x})\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))d\bm{x}dt-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}p_{t}(\bm{x})\Delta\psi_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}-\partial_{t}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})-\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\bigg]p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

(2.66)

Isolating the terms that depend on
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
, we have the minimization problem:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{u}}\bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\cdot\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)}_{(\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))^{\top}(\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x}))}}=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}-\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)^{\top}\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\bigg\}

(2.67)

By completing the square, we get:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{u}}

\displaystyle\bigg\{\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}-\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)^{\top}\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}}}_{\text{square}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{u}}\bigg\{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)-\sigma_{t}\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}}_{\text{minimizing this yields }\bm{u}^{\star}}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}\bigg\}

(2.68)

Since the second term doesn’t depend on
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
, minimizing the first term yields the expression (
2.61
) for the optimal
\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)
:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=\sigma_{t}\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})

(2.69)

Substituting this expression into the Lagrangian in (
2.62
), we get:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(p,\bm{u}^{\star},\psi)=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\sigma_{t}\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})}_{\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)}}\|^{2}-\partial_{t}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})-\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\sigma_{t}\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})}_{\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)}})-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\bigg\}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg\{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}}-\partial_{t}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})-\langle\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\sigma_{t}^{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\bigg\}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg\{-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}-\partial_{t}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})-\langle\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\bigg\}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

(2.70)

Since
\bm{u}^{\star}
determines the optimal
p^{\star}_{t}
via the Fokker-Planck constraint, the Lagrangian cannot vary with respect to
p^{\star}_{t}
, so the expression within the curly brackets must vanish for optimality to hold over arbitrary
p_{t}
:

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}-\partial_{t}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})-\langle\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\psi_{t}(\bm{x})=0

(2.71)

or equivalently:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}+\langle\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\psi_{t}(\bm{x})

(2.72)

which is exactly the
Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB)
equation in (
HJB-FP System
). Substituting the optimal expression for
\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)
into the FP equation in (
HJB-FP System
), we obtain the FP equation for the optimal density function
p^{\star}_{t}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot\big(p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\underbrace{\sigma_{t}\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})}_{\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)})\big)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})

(2.73)

which gives the
Fokker-Planck (FP)
equation (
HJB-FP System
). Finally, the boundary conditions
p^{\star}_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
p^{\star}_{T}=\pi_{T}
follow directly from (
Density Dynamic SB Problem
), which yields the system of non-linear PDEs:

\displaystyle\boxed{\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\psi_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}\|^{2}+\langle\nabla\psi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle=-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\psi_{t}\\
\partial_{t}p_{t}^{\star}+\nabla\cdot(p_{t}^{\star}(\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\psi_{t}))=\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta p^{\star}_{t}\end{cases}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}p^{\star}_{0}=\pi_{0}\\
p^{\star}_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}}

(HJB-FP System)

which define the optimal pair
(\psi_{t},p^{\star}_{t})
and equivalently
(\bm{u}^{\star},p_{t}^{\star})
that solve the dynamic SB problem.
\square

Although the coupled nonlinear PDE system in (
HJB-FP System
) fully characterizes the
optimal control–density pair

(\bm{u}^{\star},p^{\star}_{t})
, solving this system remains challenging in general, particularly under nonlinear prior dynamics and arbitrary marginal constraints. A key step toward tractability is the Hopf–Cole transform, which converts the nonlinear system in (
HJB-FP System
) into an equivalent pair of
linear PDEs
.

Before moving on to deriving these linear PDE optimality conditions, let’s first briefly review several commonly used reference processes
\mathbb{Q}
that appear across SB formulations and modern generative modeling frameworks.

Reference Processes for Schrödinger Bridges

We now define a few common reference processes
\mathbb{Q}
, in addition to pure Brownian motion
\sigma\mathbb{B}
, to reveal the connection between various generative modeling paradigms and the dynamic Schrödinger bridge problem.

Ornstein–Uhlenbeck (OU) processes.

The class of
Ornstein–Uhlenbeck
(OU) processes is a common form of reference process in SB literature
(
vargas2021solving
;
shi2023diffusion
;
de2021diffusion
)
and takes the form:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=-\beta\bm{X}_{t}dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Ornstein–Uhlenbeck SDE)

Unlike pure Brownian motion, the OU process introduces a deterministic drift
-\beta\bm{X}_{t}
that pulls the system toward the origin, creating
mean-reverting
stochastic processes. While Brownian motion diverges in variance, OU processes approach a stationary Gaussian distribution.

Variance Exploding SDEs.

The classic
score-based generative modeling
framework
(
huang2021variational
;
song2019generative
;
song2020score
)
adopts the class of
variance exploding SDEs
(VESDEs) as the reference process, which takes the form:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\sqrt{d\sigma^{2}_{t}/dt}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}

(Variance Exploding SDE)

where
\sigma_{t}^{2}
is the variance that increases with time. By integrating the SDE over time
[0,t]
, the variance is defined as
\beta_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds
, which increases over time. Therefore, the marginal density is given by
\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\beta_{t}\bm{I}_{d})
.

Variance Preserving SDEs.

Another class of reference processes are the
variance-preserving SDEs
(VPSDEs) that underlie the widely-adopted
denoising diffusion probabalistic model
(DDPM) framework
(
ho2020denoising
;
sohl2015deep
;
song2020score
)
, which take the form:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{t}\bm{X}_{t}dt+\sqrt{\beta_{t}}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}

(Variance Preserving SDE)

Unlike VESDEs, the drift and diffusion terms in VPSDEs are carefully balanced so that the total variance of the process remains approximately constant, and the marginal distribution follows a constant-variance Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})
.

Other reference processes have also been explored, including alternative definitions of the coefficient
\sigma_{t}
in VESDEs
(
karras2022elucidating
;
song2023consistency
)
, sub-VPSDEs
(
song2020score
)
, and fractional Brownian motion
(
nobis2025fractional
)
, which we will explore in depth in Section
5.6
.

Having defined the dynamic SB problem given an arbitrary reference process
\mathbb{Q}
, the system of non-linear PDEs that define the optimal control-density pair
(\bm{u}^{\star},p_{t}^{\star})
, and some examples of reference processes
\mathbb{Q}
commonly seen in literature, we will move on to the derivation of the Hopf-Cole transform. This transform converts the nonlinear PDE conditions to a system of linear PDE conditions, where the solution is a pair of time-evolving Schrödinger potentials
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
that define the marginal density of the SB, which are analogous to the static Schrödinger potentials
(\varphi,\hat{\varphi})
that define the optimal static SB problem
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
from Section
1.4
.

2.8
Hopf-Cole Transform

The Hopf-Cole transform allows us to transform the
non-linear PDEs coupled over the full trajectory
into a system of
linear PDEs that are only coupled via their boundary constraints
. This is achieved with a change-of-variables from the
optimal control–density pair

(\bm{u}^{\star},p^{\star}_{t})
to a pair of potential functions
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
that define a
coupled system of linear PDEs
. To derive this, we first recall the (
HJB-FP System
) as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}\|^{2}+\langle\nabla\psi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\psi_{t}

(HJB Equation)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}^{\star}+\nabla\cdot(p_{t}^{\star}(\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\psi_{t}))

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta p^{\star}_{t}

(FP Equation)

To understand the intuition behind the Hopf-Cole transform, we can make the simple observation that the quadratic term
\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi\|^{2}
is the
only
non-linear term in the HJB-FP system. Therefore, the goal of the transform is to answer:
how do we define a change-of-variables that makes this linear?

That is exactly the goal of the
Hopf-Cole Transform
, which defines a change-of-variables to the non-linear HJB-FP system such that they can be solved via a system of
linear PDEs
, whose optimality conditions align
exactly
to the solution of the non-linear system.

Theorem 2.24

(Hopf-Cole Transform)
.

Given an reference process defined by the deterministic drift
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
, diffusion coefficient
\sigma_{t}
, and boundary marginal distributions
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we can apply the following change of variables
(\psi,p_{t}^{\star})\mapsto(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
defined as:

\displaystyle\psi_{t}(\bm{x})=\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x}),\quad p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\varphi(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}(\bm{x})

(Hopf-Cole Transform)

which transforms (
HJB Equation
) and (
FP Equation
) into a system of
linear PDEs
for
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
given by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}+\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}\\
\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\hat{\varphi}_{t}\bm{f})=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}\end{cases}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}p^{\star}_{0}=\varphi_{0}\hat{\varphi}_{0}\\
p^{\star}_{T}=\varphi_{T}\hat{\varphi}_{T}\end{cases}

(Hopf-Cole PDEs)

which define the dynamics and terminal conditions of
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
. Furthermore, the optimal control can be written as:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})

(Optimal Control with SB Potential)

Derivation.
To derive this from first principles, we can start with a well-known property of the Laplacian operation, which is that
the Laplacian of a logarithm produces squared gradients
. We can leverage this property to write the Laplacian
\Delta\psi_{t}
as a squared gradient to cancel out the quadratic term. Specifically, let us define an
ansatz
for the change of variables
\psi\mapsto\varphi
as:

\displaystyle\psi_{t}(\bm{x})=C\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\iff\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=\exp\left(\frac{\psi_{t}(\bm{x})}{C}\right)

(2.74)

where
C
is some unknown scalar constant. We now express each term containing
\psi_{t}
in terms of
\varphi
.

(i)

The quadratic term becomes:

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\psi_{t}}\|^{2}=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(C\log\varphi_{t})}\|^{2}=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}C^{2}}{2}\left\|\frac{\nabla\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}\right\|^{2}=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}C^{2}}{2}\frac{\|\nabla\varphi_{t}\|^{2}}{\varphi_{t}^{2}}

(2.75)

(ii)

The Laplacian term becomes:

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\Delta\psi_{t}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\cdot\nabla(C\log\varphi_{t})}\right)=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}C{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\cdot\left(\frac{\nabla\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}\right)}=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}C{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\cdot\left(\varphi_{t}^{-1}\nabla\varphi_{t}\right)}

(2.76)

Applying the product rule of divergences
\nabla\cdot(u\bm{v})=(\nabla u)\cdot\bm{v}+u(\nabla\cdot\bm{v})
, we get:

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\psi

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}C\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\cdot\left(\varphi^{-1}\nabla\varphi\right)}\right)=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}C\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla(\varphi^{-1})\cdot\nabla\varphi+\varphi^{-1}(\nabla\cdot\nabla\varphi)}\right)

(2.77)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}C\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\Delta\varphi}{\varphi}-\frac{\|\nabla\varphi\|^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}}\right)=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}C\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\|\nabla\varphi\|^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}-\frac{\Delta\varphi}{\varphi}}\right)

(2.78)

To determine the value of
C
that allows the quadratic term to cancel, we set them equal to each other:

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}C^{2}}{2}\frac{\|\nabla\varphi\|^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}C\frac{\|\nabla\varphi\|^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}\implies C=1

(2.79)

Now, we have defined the mapping
\psi_{t}\mapsto\varphi_{t}
as
\psi_{t}(\bm{x})=\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
which makes the HJB PDE linear. Substituting
\psi_{t}(\bm{x})=\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
into the (
FP Equation
), we get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\log\varphi_{t}+\langle\nabla\log\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\log\varphi_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\log\varphi_{t}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varphi_{t}}\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}+\frac{1}{\varphi_{t}}\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\|\nabla\varphi_{t}\|^{2}}{\varphi_{t}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{\Delta\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}-\frac{\|\nabla\varphi_{t}\|^{2}}{\varphi_{t}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varphi_{t}}\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}+\frac{1}{\varphi_{t}}\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\|\nabla\varphi_{t}\|^{2}}{\varphi_{t}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\Delta\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\|\nabla\varphi_{t}\|^{2}}{\varphi_{t}^{2}}

(2.80)

Subtracting
\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\|\nabla\varphi\|^{2}}{\varphi^{2}}
from both sides and multiplying by
\varphi_{t}
, we get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}+\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}

(2.81)

which is exactly the
first equation
in (
Hopf-Cole PDEs
). Now, we need to define a change-of-variables for
p_{t}
that decouples
p_{t}^{\star}
and
\nabla\psi_{t}
in the (
FP Equation
). Substituting our first mapping
\psi_{t}(\bm{x})=\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
into the Fokker-Planck equation, we get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p^{\star}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\psi_{t}}))

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p^{\star}_{t}

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p^{\star}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log\varphi_{t})})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p^{\star}_{t}

(2.82)

Observing this equation, we see that the term
\nabla\log\varphi_{t}
appears as an additional drift, which reweights the trajectories according to the potential
\varphi_{t}
, but also couples the density
p^{\star}_{t}
with the potential
\varphi_{t}
. To decouple this interaction, we can divide the density by
\varphi_{t}
to get the change of variables
\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})=\frac{p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})}{\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})}
such that the optimal density factorizes as:

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})=\frac{p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})}{\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})}\implies p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})e^{\psi_{t}(\bm{x})}

(2.83)

which separates the dynamics into forward and backward components. Substituting
p^{\star}_{t}=\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}
into each term in the (
FP Equation
), we get:

(i)

The time derivative becomes:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t})=\left(\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}\right)\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\varphi_{t}\left(\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\right)

(2.84)

and substituting the expression
\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial t}=-\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}
from the first PDE, we get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p^{\star}_{t}=\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}}\right)\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\varphi_{t}\left(\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\right)={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\hat{\varphi}_{t}\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\Delta\varphi_{t}}+\varphi_{t}\left(\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\right)

(2.85)

(ii)

The drift term becomes:

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot

\displaystyle({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p^{\star}_{t}}(\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log\varphi_{t}))=\nabla\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}}(\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log\varphi_{t}))=\nabla\cdot\bigg({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\bigg(\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\frac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\bm{f})+\nabla\cdot\bigg(\sigma^{2}_{t}(\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t})\frac{\nabla_{\bm{x}}\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}\bigg)=\nabla\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\bm{f})+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\cdot(\hat{\varphi}_{t}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\varphi_{t})

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla(\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}),\bm{f}\rangle+(\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t})\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}(\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{t}+\hat{\varphi}_{t}\Delta\varphi_{t})

\displaystyle=\hat{\varphi}_{t}\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle+\varphi_{t}\langle\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t},\bm{f}\rangle+(\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t})\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{t}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\Delta\varphi_{t}

(2.86)

(iii)

The Laplacian term becomes:

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta(\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t})=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\nabla\cdot(\hat{\varphi}_{t}\nabla\varphi_{t}+\varphi_{t}\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}(\nabla\cdot(\hat{\varphi}_{t}\nabla\varphi_{t})+\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{t}\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}))=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}(\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{t}+\hat{\varphi}\Delta\varphi+\nabla\varphi_{t}\cdot\nabla\hat{\varphi}+\varphi_{t}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}(\hat{\varphi}_{t}\Delta\varphi_{t}+2\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{t}+\varphi_{t}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t})=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\Delta\varphi_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\varphi_{t}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}

(2.87)

Putting it together and canceling terms, we get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p^{\star}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(p_{t}^{\star}\nabla\log\varphi_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}^{\star}

\displaystyle\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\hat{\varphi}_{t}\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\Delta\varphi_{t}}+\varphi_{t}\left(\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\right)}_{(\ref{eq:hopf-cole-proof1})}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle}+\varphi_{t}\langle\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t},\bm{f}\rangle+(\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t})\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{t}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma^{2}_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\Delta\varphi_{t}}}_{(\ref{eq:hopf-cole-proof2})}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\Delta\varphi_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{t}}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\varphi_{t}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}}_{(\ref{eq:hopf-cole-proof3})}

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\right)+\varphi_{t}\langle\nabla\hat{\varphi},\bm{f}\rangle+(\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t})\nabla\cdot\bm{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\varphi_{t}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\langle\nabla\hat{\varphi},\bm{f}\rangle+\hat{\varphi}_{t}\nabla\cdot\bm{f}}_{=\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}\hat{\varphi}_{t})}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\hat{\varphi}_{t}\bm{f})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}

(2.88)

which recovers the
second equation
in (
Hopf-Cole PDEs
). Now, we have derived the Hopf-Cole transform
(\psi,p_{t}^{\star})\mapsto(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
that solve a system of linear PDE equations:

\displaystyle\boxed{\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}+\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}\\
\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\hat{\varphi}_{t}\bm{f})=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}\end{cases}}

(Hopf-Cole PDEs)

which
uniquely
define the optimal control-density pair
(\bm{u}^{\star},p^{\star}_{t})
of the Schrödinger bridge:

\displaystyle\boxed{\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x}),\quad p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})}

(Optimal Control-Density Pair)

yielding the boundary constraints
p_{0}^{\star}(\bm{x})=\pi_{0}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{0}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{x})
and
p_{T}^{\star}(\bm{x})=\pi_{T}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})
.
\square

Given these optimality constraints, we can derive a system of equations that explicitly define the forward and backward potential functions.

Corollary 2.25

(Forward–Backward Schrödinger Potentials)
.

Let
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
denote the Schrödinger potentials obtained through the Hopf-Cole transform, which define the solution to the (
Dynamic OT Problem
) as:

\displaystyle p_{t}^{\star}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x}),\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})

(2.89)

Then,
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
can be represented as the solution to a system of equations with the transition density under the reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
given by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{y}|\bm{x})\varphi_{T}(\bm{y})d\bm{y}\\
\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{y})d\bm{y}\end{cases}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}\pi_{0}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{0}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{x})\\
\pi_{T}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})\end{cases}

(Schrödinger Potentials)

subject to the boundary factorization constraints.

Proof.
Observe that the system of linear PDEs derived in Theorem
2.24
aligns with the form of the (
Kolmogorov Backward Equation
) defined in Corollary
2.16
, which we recall is defined for some function
r(\bm{x},t)
as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}r(\bm{x},t)+\langle\bm{f}(\bm{x},t),\nabla r(\bm{x},t)\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta r(\bm{x},t)=0,\hskip 9.24994ptr(\bm{x},T)=\Phi(\bm{x})

(Kolmogorov Backward Equation)

which admits the (
Feynman-Kac Representation
):

\displaystyle r(\bm{x},t):=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{Q}}\left[\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(Feynman-Kac Representation)

The forward potential
\varphi_{t}
satisfies the first linear PDE in (
Hopf-Cole PDEs
), which is equivalent to the backward Kolmogorov equation with
r(\bm{x},t):=\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
and
\Phi(\bm{x})=\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})
associated with the reference process
\mathbb{Q}
. Therefore,
\varphi_{t}
can be written with the Feynman-Kac representation as:

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{Q}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(2.90)

Equivalently, since the conditional density of
\bm{X}_{T}
given
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
is defined by the transition density under the reference process
\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\cdot|\bm{x})=\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T}=\cdot|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})
, we can write the Feynman-Kac representation the
linear integral operator
:

\displaystyle\boxed{\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})\varphi_{T}(\bm{x}_{T})d\bm{x}_{T}}

(Forward Potential)

Similarly, the backward potential
\hat{\varphi}_{t}
satisfies the second linear PDE in (
Hopf-Cole PDEs
), which is equivalent to the backward Kolmogorov equation with
r(\bm{x},t):=\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})
and terminal constraint
\Phi(\bm{x})=\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{x})
. Therefore,
\hat{\varphi}_{t}
can be written with the Feynman-Kac representation as:

\displaystyle\boxed{\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:t}\sim\mathbb{Q}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X_{0}})}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{x}_{0})d\bm{x}_{0}}

(Backward Potential)

which are exactly the equations defined in (
Schrödinger Potentials
).
\square

This result shows that the forward potential
\varphi_{t}
propogates backward from the terminal constraint
\pi_{T}=\varphi_{T}\hat{\varphi}_{T}
via a linear integral operator over the expected distribution of
\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})
and the backward potential propogates forward from the reversed terminal constraint
\pi_{0}=\varphi_{0}\hat{\varphi}_{0}
via a linear integral operator in the reverse time over the expected distribution of
\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})
.

Recall from our discussion on the static Schrödinger bridge problem in Section
1.4
where we define the pair of SB potentials
(\varphi,\hat{\varphi})
as the solution to the (
Schrödinger System
). Crucially, we can interpret the Schrödinger potentials
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
obtained through the Hopf–Cole transform as the
dynamic or continuous-time analogues of the Schrödinger potentials
. In the static formulation, the optimal coupling between the marginals
(\pi_{0},\pi_{T})
under the reference kernel
\mathcal{K}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=e^{-c(\bm{x},\bm{y})}
admits the factorized form:

\displaystyle\pi_{0,T}^{\star}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=e^{\varphi(\bm{x})+\hat{\varphi}(\bm{y})-c(\bm{x},\bm{y})}=e^{\varphi(\bm{x})}\mathcal{K}(\bm{x},\bm{y})e^{\hat{\varphi}(\bm{y})}

(Solution to Static SB)

The (
Dynamic SB
) problem generalizes this structure from couplings of endpoints to path measures of stochastic processes. In this setting, the time-dependent potentials
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
propagate according to the forward–backward linear PDE system derived above, and their product recovers the optimal marginal density at each time
p_{t}^{\star}=\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}
.

As shown in Theorem
1.10
, the static Schrödinger system admits a
unique solution up to an additive constant
. Since the solution to the dynamic SB problem
p^{\star}_{t}
is also unique by the strict convexity of the KL divergence, the product
\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}
is unique, and the individual potentials
\varphi_{t}
and
\hat{\varphi}_{t}
are
unique up to a multiplicative constant
which leaves their product invariant.

2.9
Schrödinger Bridges as Entropy-Regularized Dynamic Optimal Transport

One of the most important perspectives on the Schrödinger bridge problem is its close relationship to optimal transport. In Section
1
, we derived the static SB problem directly from the entropic OT problem, with a simple reparameterization of the reference coupling.

In this section, we make a similar connection to the
Benamou and Brenier (dynamic) formulation of the OT problem
defined in Section
2.1
. We will show that by reparameterizing the control drift in the controlled Fokker–Planck equation, the stochastic dynamics can be expressed as a deterministic continuity equation describing the transport of probability mass by a velocity field. Under this transformation, the dynamic SB objective decomposes into a kinetic transport energy that aligns with the dynamic OT objective, with additional
entropy-regularization terms
induced by diffusion.

Proposition 2.26

(Dynamic Optimal Transport Form of Schrödinger Bridge)
.

Consider the dynamic Schrödinger bridge problem written in terms of the marginal density in (
Controlled Fokker-Planck Equation
) as:

\displaystyle\inf_{(\bm{u},p_{t})}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt\right]

(Density Dynamic SB Objective)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot\big(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))\big)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})\\
p_{0}=\pi_{0},\quad p_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

which enforces the marginal constraints
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
and Fokker-Planck equation constraint. Then, by reparameterizing
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t):=\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)+\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})
, the dynamic SB problem takes an
equivalent form of a dynamic optimal transport problem
given by:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{v}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\int_{0}^{T}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{8}\|\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle\bigg]dt

(Entropy-Regularized Dynamic OT)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)))\\
p_{0}=\pi_{0},\hskip 9.24994ptp_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

where
\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{f}_{t}+\sigma_{t}\bm{v}))
is the continuity equation constraint.

Proof.
In this proof, our goal is to absorb the stochastic term in the Fokker-Planck constraint into the objective functional, such that the constrained objective is in the form of the (
Continuity Equation
), which can be interpreted as a special case of the Fokker-Planck equation (Remark
2.13
).

Step 1: Rewrite Fokker-Planck Equation as Continuity Equation.

We start by rewriting the Fokker-Planck equation by absorbing the Laplacian diffusion term into the divergence term to get the continuity equation with a newly defined drift field
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\big(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))\big)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\cdot}\big(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))\big)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\cdot}(\nabla p_{t}(\bm{x}))

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\left(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\nabla p_{t}(\bm{x})\right)

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\bigg({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}(\bm{x})}\bigg(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})}\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\bigg(p_{t}(\bm{x})\bigg(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\left(\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)-\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\right)}_{=:\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)}}\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{v}(\bm{x},t))

(Continuity Equation)

where we define a new velocity
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t):=\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)-\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})
that satisfies a continuity equation. We rearrange to write
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
in terms of the new velocity
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)
as:

\displaystyle\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)=\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)-\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle\implies\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)=\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)+\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})

(2.91)

Substituting this expression (
2.91
) for
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
into the objective functional for the (
Density Dynamic SB Objective
), we get:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}

\displaystyle\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}}\right]p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{2}\left\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)+\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\right\|^{2}}\right]p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[\frac{1}{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\left(\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+2\left\langle\bm{v}(\bm{x},t),\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\right\rangle+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{4}\|\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}\right)}\right]p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg[\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}}_{\text{kinetic energy}}+\underbrace{\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\left\langle\bm{v}(\bm{x},t),\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\right\rangle}_{\text{cross term}}+\underbrace{\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{8}\|\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}}_{\text{Fisher information}}\bigg]p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

(2.92)

which is the objective that corresponds to the (
Continuity Equation
). Since the kinetic energy
\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}
is convex in
\bm{v}
, we want to write the objective such that the drift being optimized (i.e.,
\bm{v}
) appears only in the kinetic energy. To do this, we will rewrite the cross term dependent on
\bm{v}
in the next step.

Step 2: Expand the Cross-Term.

Observing (
2.92
), the cross term is dependent on both of the variables being optimized
\bm{v}
and
p_{t}
, which yields challenges in practice. Isolating the cross term, we aim to expand it to remove the cross dependency:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\left\langle\bm{v}(\bm{x},t),\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\right\rangle p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

(Cross Term)

Intuitively, we can consider the expression
\langle\bm{v},\nabla\log p_{t}\rangle p_{t}=\bm{v}\cdot\nabla p_{t}
as measuring the alignment between the velocity field
\bm{v}
and the gradient of the density. In other words, it answers:
is velocity field pushing probability mass toward regions of high density or away from them?
This has a direct connection to
entropy
, which measures the concentration of probability density. Let
H(p_{t})=\int p_{t}\log p_{t}d\bm{x}
denote the entropy. Since the objective integrates the cross term over time
t\in[0,T]
, we consider the
change
in entropy between the terminal marginals:

\displaystyle H(p_{T})-H(p_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\partial_{t}H(p_{t})dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left(\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t}\log p_{t}d\bm{x}\right)dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg(\log p_{t}\partial_{t}p_{t}+p_{t}\partial_{t}\log p_{t}\bigg)d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\log p_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}p_{t}}d\bm{x}dt

(2.93)

Substituting the continuity equation constraint
\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{v}))
, we have:

\displaystyle H(p_{T})-H(p_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\log p_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\big(-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{v})\big)}d\bm{x}dt

(2.94)

Then, applying the integration of parts identity for divergence
21
21

21

which states for a scalar function
\phi
and vector
\bm{\xi}
, the following holds:
\int\phi(-\nabla\cdot\bm{\xi})d\bm{x}=\int\langle\nabla\phi,\bm{\xi}\rangle d\bm{x}
, we can rewrite (
2.94
) as:

\displaystyle H(p_{T})-H(p_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg\langle\nabla\log p_{t},{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{v})}\bigg\rangle d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg\langle\nabla\log p_{t},(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{v})\bigg\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}}d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big\langle\nabla\log p_{t},\bm{f}\big\rangle p_{t}d\bm{x}dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma_{t}\big\langle\nabla\log p_{t},\bm{v}\big\rangle p_{t}d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}}\big\langle\nabla\log p_{t},\bm{v}\big\rangle p_{t}d\bm{x}dt=\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{2}}(H(p_{T})-H(p_{0}))}_{\text{constant}}-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{2}}\big\langle\nabla\log p_{t},\bm{f}\big\rangle p_{t}d\bm{x}dt

(2.95)

In the (
Density Dynamic SB Objective
), the marginal distributions
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
are fixed, so the entropy difference term
H(p_{T})-H(p_{0})
is a constant and can be dropped in the objective. Therefore, substituting (
2.94
) into the objective functional, we have:

\displaystyle\boxed{\inf_{(p_{t},\bm{v},g)}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{8}\|\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle}\bigg]p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt}

(2.96)

Equivalently, we can write (
2.96
) as an expectation over the marginal density
p_{t}
corresponding to the velocity field
\bm{v}_{t}
given by:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{v}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\int_{0}^{T}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{8}\|\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle\bigg]dt

(2.97)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)))\\
p_{0}=\pi_{0},\quad p_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(2.98)

which concludes our derivation of the dynamical OT form of the dynamic SB problem.
\square

This derivation yields an objective with three distinct terms that capture both the transport and diffusion terms in the original Fokker-Planck constraint.
Intuitively, each term can be interpreted as follows:

(i)

The
kinetic energy term
\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}
measures the deterministic cost of transporting probability mass along the velocity field
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)
. This term is exactly the kinetic energy appearing in the (
Dynamic OT Problem
), where the optimal velocity minimizes the kinetic energy required to move mass on the deterministic flow between the marginal distributions.

(ii)

The
Fisher information term
\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{4}\|\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}
measures the sharpness of the marginal distribution. Smooth distributions yield low Fisher information, while sharp and concentrated distributions yield high Fisher information. This term appears from the diffusion term in the Fokker-Planck equation as the diffusion in the SDE acts to smooth out the distribution.

(iii)

The
cross term or drift interaction
\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle
measures the interaction between the reference drift
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
and the density evolution
\nabla\log p_{t}
. This term reflects how the prior dynamics influence the evolution of the distribution by either aligning with or opposing the natural directions of increasing probability mass.

This result shows that the (
Dynamic SB Problem
) problem can be explicitly rewritten as an entropy-regularized version of the (
Dynamic OT Problem
), where the optimization over stochastic path measures
\mathbb{P}^{u}
reduces to an optimization over deterministic density flows
p_{t}
and velocity fields
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)
. In the limit of vanishing diffusion (
\sigma_{t}\to 0
), the entropy regularization from the Fisher information and cross-term vanish, and the formulation recovers the classic (
Dynamic OT Problem
).

2.10
Closing Remarks for Section
2

In this section, we took the crucial step of lifting the static formulation of the Schrödinger bridge (SB) problem to the space of continuous-time
path measures
. Starting from the dynamic optimal transport (OT) problem, we show that the static OT problem with a quadratic cost function can be written in an equivalent
dynamic
form, which aims to find the optimal probability flow that smoothly transports mass between the prescribed marginals via a velocity field over a continuous time interval, yielding the straight probability flow between distributions. Given that most real-world systems do not evolve in straight lines between distributions, we extend the entropic OT problem to the space of
stochastic processes
, where path measures are determined by both a deterministic drift and random fluctuations in the form of Brownian motion, and entropy regularization is performed with a reference stochastic process.

To understand the dynamic SB problem, we first introduce the governing theory of path measures and Itô processes, from Itô’s formula, which describes the evolution of functions evaluated on stochastic processes, to the Fokker-Planck and Feynman-Kac equations, which characterize how probability densities and functions evolve over stochastic processes through partial differential equations (PDEs). Just like the static case, we introduce the concept of relative entropy in the space of path measures, which can be expressed explicitly in terms of the difference between the drifts of two stochastic differential equations using Girsanov’s theorem.

Building on this framework, we analyzed the dynamic SB problem by expressing the KL minimization over path measures as a minimization of the
kinetic energy of a control drift
, corresponding to the minimal perturbation required to steer the reference SDE so that its marginals match the prescribed constraints. We leverage Lagrange multipliers to derive the optimality conditions for the dynamic SB problem, which result in a pair of
non-linear PDEs
describing the forward and backward dynamics of the optimal solution
(\psi_{t},p^{\star}_{t})
. Finally, we introduce the Hopf-Cole transform as a method of linearizing the non-linear PDEs with a simple change-of-variables, revealing that the optimal SB dynamics can be factorized into the product of a pair of forward and backward potentials
p_{t}^{\star}=\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}
which solve a pair of linear PDEs.

While we have analyzed the dynamic SB problem through the lenses of minimizing the KL divergence between stochastic path measures, it admits an alternative interpretation as an
optimal control problem
, where the goal is to determine the control drift that minimizes the expected future cost of steering a stochastic system toward a desired terminal distribution. This is precisely the idea of
stochastic optimal control
(SOC), which leverages Bellman’s Principle of Optimality to define an optimal control as the minimizer to the expected
cost-to-go
from an intermediate state to all possible terminal states under an SDE. In the next section, we explore this perspective in detail, first introducing the theory of SOC and its properties before explicitly reformulating the dynamic SB problem as an SOC problem.

3
Schrödinger Bridge Problem as Optimal Control

In this section, we reformulate the Schrödinger bridge (SB) problem through the lens of
stochastic optimal control (SOC)
theory, providing a dynamic and decision-theoretic perspective on entropy-regularized transport. Rather than directly optimizing over path measures, this view interprets the SB problem as learning an optimal control that steers a reference stochastic process between prescribed marginals while minimizing a control cost.

We begin in Section
3.1
by introducing the general SOC framework, including Bellman’s Principle of Optimality and the value function that characterizes optimal policies under terminal constraints. In Section
3.2
, we establish the connection between SB and SOC by showing that the optimal bridge corresponds to an
optimal control drift
applied to the reference dynamics. Finally, in Section
3.3
, we develop practical training objectives that enable solving the resulting control problem in high-dimensional settings. Since we work with multiple path measures with different controls, we denote the stochastic process generated with a
specific
control
\bm{u}
with the superscripted notation
\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}=(\bm{X}_{t}^{u})_{t\in[0,T]}
to clearly denote the path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
that the process was generated under.

3.1
Stochastic Optimal Control

While the Schrödinger bridge problem aims to optimize the intermediate bridge between fixed endpoints, it is natural to consider an alternative variational perspective where given a particle sampled from the initial distribution
\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}
, we want to
optimize its path such that it reaches a state in the target distribution while minimially deviating from the reference SDE.
This is the key idea behind
stochastic optimal control (SOC)
theory
(
nusken2021solving
)
, which seeks an
optimal control drift

\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)
that corrects the particles trajectory such that it takes the path of
minimal cost
toward the target distribution
\pi_{T}
.

Definition 3.1

(Stochastic Optimal Control (SOC) Objective)
.

Given an running cost
c(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
and a terminal cost
\Phi(\bm{x}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we consider the following
stochastic optimal control
(SOC) objective:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{u}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\right)dt+\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})\right]

(SOC Objective)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad d\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\left(\bm{f}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\right)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{X}^{u}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}

where
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
is the control drift which produces the path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
,
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
is the drift of the reference process
\mathbb{Q}
,
\sigma_{t}
is the diffusion coefficient, and
\bm{B}_{t}
is
d
-dimensional Brownain motion.

Under this objective, we can define the
cost functional

J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})
being optimized as the
cost-to-go
from any fixed point
(\bm{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]
at time
t
under the control
\bm{u}
as the expected running cost and terminal cost of integrating the controlled SDE from
\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}
over
s\in[t,T]
.

\displaystyle J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}):=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\right)ds+\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})\bigg|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(Cost Functional)

Given the cost-to-go for an arbitrary control
\bm{u}
, we define the
value function

V_{t}(\bm{x}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
as the
optimal cost-to-go
obtained with the optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}
.

\displaystyle V_{t}(\bm{x}):=J^{\star}(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}^{\star}):=\inf_{\bm{u}}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})

(Value Function)

which solves the
Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB) equation
, similarly to the Lagrange multiplier
\psi_{t}(\bm{x})
from Section
2.7
. Before defining the HJB for the value function, we first define
Bellman’s Principle of Optimality
, also referred to as the
dynamic programming principle
, which provides an intuitive and rigorous foundation for stochastic optimal control theory.

Definition 3.2

(Bellman’s Principle of Optimality)
.

Let
V_{t}(\bm{x})
denote the value function or optimal cost-to-go of a stochastic control problem starting from state
\bm{x}
at time
t
. Then, the optimal control satisfies
Bellman’s principle of optimality
, which states that for all intermediate time steps
t\leq\tau\leq T
the optimal cost-to-go is equal to the cost incurred over the interval
[t,\tau]
and the future cost-to-go
V_{\tau}(\bm{X}_{\tau})
starting from
\bm{X}_{\tau}
over time
[\tau,T]
, which yields the following
expanded expression or the value function
:

\displaystyle V_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{u}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{t}^{\tau}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\int_{\tau}^{T}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\right)ds+\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})\bigg|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{u}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{t}^{\tau}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\right)ds}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}V_{\tau}(\bm{X}^{u}_{\tau})}\bigg|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(Bellman’s Principle of Optimality)

Intuitively, this means that in an optimally controlled process, regardless of the initial state
\bm{x}
and changes over
[t,\tau]
, the remaining controlled process from
\bm{X}_{\tau}
follows the optimal control law for the state resulting from the
first decision
. Taking the infinitesimal limit when
\tau\to t
and
\tau-t\to 0
, this property yields the
Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (HJB) equations

(
bardi1997optimal
)
, which characterizes the evolution of the value function.

Lemma 3.3

(Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman (HJB) Equations)
.

Given the infinitesimal generator
\mathcal{A}_{t}
of the
uncontrolled SDE

d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
that acts on the value function
V_{t}(\bm{x})
as:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{x}):=\langle\bm{f}(\bm{x},t),\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta V_{t}(\bm{x})

(3.1)

Then,
V_{t}(\bm{x})
solves the
Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman
equation defined as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})=-(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{x})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}-c(\bm{x},t),\quad V_{T}(\bm{x})=\Phi(\bm{x})

(Value HJB Equation)

Proof.
The proof follows from (
Bellman’s Principle of Optimality
), where we define
\tau:=t+\Delta t
as a small time step
\Delta t
to get the following expanded expression for the value function:

\displaystyle V_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{u}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\int_{t+\Delta t}^{T}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\right)ds+\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})\bigg|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{u}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\right)ds}+\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\int_{t+\Delta t}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\right)ds+\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})}}_{=:V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t})}\bigg|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{u}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\right)ds}+V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t})\bigg|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{u}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\left[\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\right)\Delta t\right]}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})+\inf_{\bm{u}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t})\big|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]}

(3.2)

Applying (
Controlled Itô Formula with

A
u

t

), the change in the value function over the interval
[t,t+\Delta t]
expands into:

\displaystyle V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t})-V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})=\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{dV_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})}_{\text{Itô's formula}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\overset{(\ref{thm:ito-formula})}{=}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}(\partial_{s}+\mathcal{A}^{u}_{s})V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})ds+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t+\Delta t}\sigma_{s}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})^{\top}d\bm{B}_{s}}_{\text{Itô integral vanishes under expectation}}

(3.3)

Moving
V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})
to the right-hand side and applying the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[\cdot|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}]
to both sides, we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t})|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})+\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}(\partial_{s}+\mathcal{A}^{u}_{s})V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})ds\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

\displaystyle=V_{t}(\bm{x})+(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{A}^{u}_{t})V_{t}(\bm{x})\Delta t+o(\Delta t)

(3.4)

Substituting this expression (
3.4
) into (
3.2
), we obtain the equation:

\displaystyle V_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{u}}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\bigg)\Delta t}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}V_{t}(\bm{x})+(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{A}^{u}_{t})V_{t}(\bm{x})\Delta t}\bigg]+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})

(3.5)

Now, we can subtract
V_{t}(\bm{x})
from both sides, divide by
\Delta t
and take the continuous time limit
\Delta t\to 0
to get the expression:

0

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{u}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+c(\bm{x},t)+(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{A}^{u}_{t})V_{t}(\bm{x})\right]

(3.6)

Completing the square for the
\bm{u}
-dependent terms, we get the form of the minimizer:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{u}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+

\displaystyle\langle\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x}),\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\rangle\bigg]=\inf_{\bm{u}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+\langle\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x}),\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\bm{u}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\implies\boxed{\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=-\sigma_{t}\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})}

(3.7)

Plugging the expression for the optimal
\bm{u}^{\star}
into (
3.6
) and rewriting the controlled generator in terms of the uncontrolled generator with (
2.17
), we recover the HJB equation:

0

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)}\|^{2}+c(\bm{x},t)+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\partial_{t}+\mathcal{A}^{u^{\star}}_{t})V_{t}(\bm{x})}

0

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)}\|^{2}+c(\bm{x},t)+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})+(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{x})+\langle\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x}),\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)}\rangle}

0

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}}+c(\bm{x},t)+\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})+(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{x}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\sigma_{t}^{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{x})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}-c(\bm{x},t)

(Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman Equation)

which is the HJB equation stated in (
Value HJB Equation
). Intuitively, this states that the time evolution of the value function
\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})
is equal to the instantaneous change in value induced by the
uncontrolled dynamics

-(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{x})
, the cost that arises from tilting the dynamics with the optimal control
\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}
, and the immediate running cost
-c(\bm{x},t)
.
\square

Recall from Section
2.7
that the optimality conditions for (
Dynamic SB Problem
) with arbitrary reference dynamics are defined by the pair
(\psi_{t},p^{\star}_{t})
, where
\psi_{t}
is the Lagrange multiplier of the constrained optimization problem and
p^{\star}_{t}
is the optimal marginal density evolution over
t\in[0,T]
. Then, we showed that
(\psi_{t},p_{t}^{\star})
that characterize the dynamic SB problem satisfy a system of
non-linear PDEs
called the (
HJB-FP System
) which can then be transformed into a system of
linear PDEs
(
Hopf-Cole PDEs
) via the (
Hopf-Cole Transform
). Leveraging this idea, we can also write the SOC problem described by the (
Value HJB Equation
) into a
linear PDE
via the
Hopf-Cole transform
.

Corollary 3.4

(Hopf-Cole Transform for Value Function)
.

Let
V_{t}(\bm{x})
be the value function that defines the solution to the (
SOC Objective
) and satisfies the (
Value HJB Equation
). Then, we define the change of variables:

\displaystyle V_{t}(\bm{x})=-\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\iff\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}

(3.8)

where
\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
satisfies the
linear PDE
defined as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})+\langle\bm{f}(\bm{x},t),\nabla\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})-c(\bm{x},t)\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})=e^{-\Phi(\bm{x})}

(Hopf-Cole Value PDE)

Proof.
First, we recall the (
Value HJB Equation
) given by:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{x})}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}+c(\bm{x},t)=0,\hskip 9.24994ptV_{T}(\bm{x})=\Phi(\bm{x})

(3.9)

\displaystyle\text{where}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{x})}=\langle\bm{f}(\bm{x},t),\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta V_{t}(\bm{x})

(3.10)

Following the analogous steps from Section
2.8
, we express each term containing
V_{t}
in terms of
\varphi
:

(i)

Time Derivative:

\partial_{t}V_{t}=-\partial_{t}\log\varphi_{t}=-\frac{\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}
.

(ii)

Gradient Term:

\nabla V_{t}=-\nabla\log\varphi_{t}=-\frac{\nabla\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}\implies\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}\|^{2}=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\|\nabla\varphi_{t}\|^{2}}{\varphi_{t}^{2}}
.

(iii)

Generator Term:
First, we expand the Laplacian term in
(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})
as:

\displaystyle\Delta V_{t}

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\nabla(-\log\varphi_{t})=-\nabla\cdot\left(\frac{\nabla\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}\right)=-\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{t}^{-1}\nabla\varphi_{t})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{-\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{-1})\cdot\nabla\varphi_{t}+\varphi_{t}^{-1}(\nabla\cdot\nabla\varphi_{t})}_{\text{product rule of divergences}}=-\frac{\Delta\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}+\frac{\|\nabla\varphi_{t}\|^{2}}{\varphi_{t}^{2}}

(3.11)

Then, the generator becomes:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\bm{f},{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\frac{\nabla\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}}\bigg\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\frac{\Delta\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}+\frac{\|\nabla\varphi_{t}\|^{2}}{\varphi_{t}^{2}}}\right)=-\frac{\langle\bm{f},\nabla\varphi_{t}\rangle}{\varphi_{t}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\Delta\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\|\nabla\varphi_{t}\|^{2}}{\varphi_{t}^{2}}

(3.12)

Substituting each term back into the HJB equation (
3.9
) and cancelling terms, we get:

\displaystyle\underbrace{-\frac{\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}}_{\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})}\underbrace{-\frac{\langle\bm{f},\nabla\varphi_{t}\rangle}{\varphi_{t}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\Delta\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\|\nabla\varphi_{t}\|^{2}}{\varphi_{t}^{2}}}}_{(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{x})}-\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\|\nabla\varphi_{t}\|^{2}}{\varphi_{t}^{2}}}}_{\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}}+c(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}-\frac{\langle\bm{f},\nabla\varphi_{t}\rangle}{\varphi_{t}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\Delta\varphi_{t}}{\varphi_{t}}+c(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}+\langle\bm{f},\nabla\varphi_{t}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}-c(\bm{x},t)\varphi_{t}

\displaystyle=0

(3.13)

where the final line follows from multiplying both sides by
-\varphi_{t}
. Given the terminal condition
V_{T}(\bm{x})=\Phi(\bm{x})
defined in (
3.9
), we apply the change of variables to get
\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})=e^{-V_{T}(\bm{x})}=e^{-\Phi(\bm{x})}
. Putting this together with (
3.13
), we have that
\varphi_{t}
satisfies the transformed
linear PDE
given by:

\displaystyle\boxed{\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})+\langle\bm{f}(\bm{x},t),\nabla\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})-c(\bm{x},t)\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})=e^{-\Phi(\bm{x})}}

(Hopf-Cole Value PDE)

which concludes our derivation of the Hopf-Cole transform for the value function that solves (
SOC Objective
).
\square

It is worth noticing that the Hopf-Cole transform that we performed in Section
2.8
resulted in
two linear PDEs
that describe the evolution of the forward and backward Schrödinger potentials
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
, whereas we only derived a single linear PDE for the SOC problem. This is because while the (
Dynamic SB Problem
) has two terminal constraints at both marginals
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
, the (
SOC Objective
) only has one terminal constraint for the value function at
V_{T}(\bm{x})=\Phi(\bm{x})
, resulting in a single potential change-of-variables
\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}
that satisfies the backward (
Hopf-Cole Value PDE
) with terminal constraint
\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})=e^{-\Phi(\bm{x})}
. Despite this difference in the two formulations, we make the following remark, which we explore further in Section
3.2
.

Remark 3.5
.

Although the Schrödinger bridge problem imposes constraints on both the initial and terminal distributions, it can still be reformulated as an SOC problem. The key observation is that the initial distribution can be treated as the
starting distribution
of the controlled process, while the terminal constraint can be enforced through an appropriate terminal cost that penalizes deviations from the desired terminal marginal.

We have shown that the value function
V_{t}
can be analyzed in the form of a linear PDE. We now present an alternative perspective by analyzing the evolution of
V_{t}
along stochastic trajectories generated under the uncontrolled reference measure
\mathbb{Q}
. In particular, we show that the stochastic process
\bm{X}_{0:T}
together with the value function evaluated along the trajectory satisfies a system of
forward-backward stochastic differential equations (FBSDEs)
. In this formulation, the forward equation describes the evolution of the uncontrolled diffusion process, while the backward equation governs the dynamics of the value function along the stochastic path.

Proposition 3.6

(Forward-Backward Stochastic Differential Equations)
.

The optimal controlled process can be defined with a pair of forward and backward SDEs (FBSDEs) which define the evolution of the uncontrolled process
\bm{X}_{0:T}
and the value function
V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
, given by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
dV_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})=\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\|^{2}-c(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right)dt+\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})=\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})\end{cases}

(3.14)

In addition, for the controlled process
(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
, the FBSDEs are given by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
dV_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})=\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})\|^{2}-c(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\langle\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t),\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})\rangle\right)dt+\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
V_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})=\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})\end{cases}

(3.15)

where
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is the terminal cost and
\sigma_{t}
is the diffusion coefficient.

Proof.
Applying (
2.3
) to the value function, we have:

\displaystyle dV_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})=\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})dt+(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{X}_{t})dt+\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})^{\top}\sigma_{s}d\bm{B}_{t}

(3.16)

From (
Value HJB Equation
), we can substitute
\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})+(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{X}_{t})=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\|^{2}-c(\bm{X}_{t},t)
to get the backward evolution of the value function over the uncontrolled process as:

\displaystyle dV_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})

\displaystyle=\bigg({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\|^{2}-c(\bm{X}_{t},t)}\bigg)dt+\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})

\displaystyle=\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})

(3.17)

Applying (
Controlled Itô Formula with

A
u

t

) and the same steps as above to the value function evaluated along the controlled process
\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}
, we get:

\displaystyle dV_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})

\displaystyle=\big(\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\mathcal{A}^{u}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})}\big)dt+\sigma_{t}\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(
Controlled Itô Formula with

A
u

t

)

\displaystyle\overset{\ref{eq:generator-control-to-uncontrolled}}{=}\big(\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})+\langle\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u},t),\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u})\rangle}\big)dt+\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(3.18)

Substituting the expression for
\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})+(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{X}_{t})
from (
Value HJB Equation
), we have:

\displaystyle dV_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})

\displaystyle=\bigg({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})\|^{2}-c(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)}+\langle\sigma_{s}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t),\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})\rangle\bigg)ds+\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(3.19)

\displaystyle V_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})

\displaystyle=\Phi(\bm{X}_{T}^{u})

(3.20)

which recovers the backward evolution of the value function over the controlled process from the terminal constraint
V_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})=\Phi(\bm{X}_{T}^{u})
.
\square

The FBSDE representation of the value function establishes the evolution of the value function under a stochastic process in the form of a backward SDE. By integrating this backward SDE along the trajectory and taking expectations, we can derive an explicit relationship between the cost functional
J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})
for an arbitrary control
\bm{u}
and the value function
V_{t}(\bm{x}):=J^{\star}(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}^{\star})
defined with the
optimal control

\bm{u}^{\star}
, which can be written with respect to the value function as shown in the following Proposition.

Proposition 3.7

(Optimal Control and Value Function)
.

Given a reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
, the relationship between the
cost functional
, or cost-to-go,
J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})
and the
value function

V_{t}(\bm{x})
is defined as:

\displaystyle J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})=V_{t}(\bm{x})+\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\sigma_{s}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})+\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}\bigg|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(Cost Functional)

where
\mathbb{Q}
is defined by the
uncontrolled
SDE
d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
. Furthermore, the optimal control satisfies:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=-\sigma_{t}\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})

(Optimal Control)

Proof.
First, we can write the value change over the time interval
s\in[t,T]
using Itô’s lemma from Theorem
2.7
for the controlled stochastic process
(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
and the value function
V_{t}(\bm{x})\in C^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T])
to get the time derivative:

\displaystyle dV_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})=\left[\partial_{s}V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})+\langle\bm{f}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)+\sigma_{s}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s),\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{s}^{2}\Delta V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})\right]ds+\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})^{\top}\sigma_{s}d\bm{B}_{s}

(3.21)

Then, we can derive the value difference over the interval
s\in[t,T]
as:

\displaystyle V_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})-V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})=\int_{t}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}dV_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})}

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{T}\bigg({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{s}V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})+\mathcal{A}_{s}V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})}+\langle\sigma_{s}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s),\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})\rangle\bigg)ds+\int_{t}^{T}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})^{\top}\sigma_{s}d\bm{B}_{s}

(3.22)

We can substitute the expression for
\partial_{s}V_{s}(\bm{X}_{s})+(\mathcal{A}_{s}V_{s})(\bm{X}_{t})
from (
Value HJB Equation
) and complete the square to get:

\displaystyle\partial_{s}V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})+

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{s}V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})+\langle\sigma_{s}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s),\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})\rangle

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})\|^{2}-c(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)}+\langle\sigma_{s}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s),\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})\|^{2}+\langle\sigma_{s}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s),\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})\rangle+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}}-c(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\sigma_{s}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})+\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}-c(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)

(3.23)

Substituting (
3.23
) back into (
3.22
) and setting
V_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})=\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})
, we can rearrange and take the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[\cdot|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}]
to get:

\displaystyle\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})-V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})=\int_{t}^{T}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{2}\|\sigma_{s}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})+\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}-c(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)}\bigg]ds+\int_{t}^{T}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})^{\top}\sigma_{s}d\bm{B}_{s}

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\sigma_{s}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})+\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}ds-\underbrace{\int_{t}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}-c(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\right)ds}_{(\bigstar)}+\int_{t}^{T}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})^{\top}\sigma_{s}d\bm{B}_{s}

(3.24)

Observing that both
\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})
and
(\bigstar)
appear in the conditional expectation of the cost functional
J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})
, we can rearrange and take expectations
\mathbb{E}[\cdot|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]
to get:

\displaystyle(\bigstar)+\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})

\displaystyle=V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})+\int_{t}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\sigma_{s}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})+\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}+\int_{t}^{T}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})^{\top}\sigma_{s}d\bm{B}_{s}

\displaystyle\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}[}(\bigstar)+\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\big|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}]}}_{=:J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}\bigg[}V_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})+\int_{t}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\sigma_{s}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})+\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}+\underbrace{\int_{t}^{T}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})^{\top}\sigma_{s}d\bm{B}_{s}}_{\text{vanishes under expectation}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bigg|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\bigg]}
