Recognizing that the left-hand side is the definition of the
cost-to-go
function
J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})
and the expectation of the Itô integral vanishes, we conclude:

\displaystyle\boxed{J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})=V_{t}(\bm{x})+\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\sigma_{s}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s})+\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}\bigg|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]}

(Cost Functional)

which establishes the relationship between the
value function
defined with the
optimal control

\bm{u}^{\star}
and the
cost-to-go
under the arbitrary control
\bm{u}
. Since, the value function is equal to the
optimal cost-to-go
by definition, where
V_{t}(\bm{x}):=J^{\star}(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}^{\star})
, the exepectation must vanish at optimality. Therefore, we can derive the expression for the optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)
from the condition:

\displaystyle\forall(\bm{x},s)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T],\quad\|\sigma_{s}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{x})+\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},s)\|^{2}=0\implies\boxed{\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},s)=-\sigma_{s}\nabla V_{s}(\bm{x})}

(3.25)

which recovers the expression in (
Optimal Control
).
\square

The value function can alternatively be expressed using expectations over an uncontrolled reference measure
\mathbb{Q}

(
kappen2012optimal
;
domingo2024stochastic
)
. This representation, derived using the (
Feynman-Kac Formula
), will later allow us to connect stochastic optimal control with the Schrödinger bridge formulation based on path measures.

Proposition 3.8

(Closed-Form Expression for Value Function (Equation 8 in
domingo2024stochastic
))
.

Given a reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
, the
value function

V_{t}(\bm{x})
can be derived independently of the optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}
as:

\displaystyle V_{t}(\bm{x})=-\log\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{Q}}\left[\exp\left(-\int_{t}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{s},s)ds+\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})\right)\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(3.26)

where
\mathbb{Q}
is defined by the
uncontrolled
SDE
d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
.

Proof.
This proof combines the (
Value HJB Equation
), (
Hopf-Cole Value PDE
), and the (
Feynman-Kac Formula
). First, we recall that the value function solves (
Value HJB Equation
), defined as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{t}+\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\|\sigma_{t}^{\top}\nabla V_{t}\|^{2}+c=0

(3.27)

which we show in Corollary
3.4
be transformed to a (
Hopf-Cole Value PDE
) via the change in variables
\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}
defined as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})+\langle\bm{f}(\bm{x},t),\nabla\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})-c(\bm{x},t)\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=0,\hskip 9.24994pt\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})=e^{-\Phi(\bm{x})}

(3.28)

Applying the (
Feynman-Kac Formula
), we can write
\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
as an expectation over uncontrolled stochastic processes as:

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{Q}}\left[\exp\left(-\int_{t}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{s},s)ds\right)e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})}\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{Q}}\left[\exp\left(-\int_{t}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{s},s)ds-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})\right)\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(3.29)

Taking the logarithm on both sides and inverting the sign, we get:

\displaystyle V_{t}(\bm{x})=-\log{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{Q}}\left[\exp\left(-\int_{t}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{s},s)ds-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})\right)\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]}

(3.30)

which defines the closed form expression for the value function as an
expectation over uncontrolled stochastic processes
.
\square

The Feynman-Kac representation above expresses the value function as a log-expectation with respect to the reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
. While this formulation provides a closed-form characterization of the optimal cost-to-go, it does not yet explicitly describe how the
optimal controlled process differs from the reference dynamics
. To make this relationship concrete, we interpret SOC as a
change of measure on path space
, where the optimal path
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is obtained by reweighting the reference process
\mathbb{Q}
using the
change in the value function
.

Proposition 3.9

(Radon-Nikodym Derivative Between Optimal and Reference Path Measure)
.

The
Radon-Nikodym Derivative
(RND) between the optimal controlled path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
and the reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt}

(Optimal Path RND)

which directly yields the optimal path measure:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0:T})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt}

(Optimal Path Measure)

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt}\right]

(Normalization Constant)

where
Z
is the normalization constant. Furthermore, given that the exponential factor depends only on the endpoints
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})
, the joint endpoint law of the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})
is given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt}

(3.31)

Proof.
To prove this, we leverage the form of the RND from Theorem
2.20
where the two path measures are generated via the SDEs:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}:

\displaystyle\quad d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(3.32)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}:

\displaystyle\quad d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(3.33)

which yields the RND:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},t)}\|^{2}dt+\int_{0}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},t)}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\right)

(3.34)

Substituting the definition of the optimal control with the value function
\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},t)=-\sigma_{t}\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
, we get:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\sigma_{t}\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})}\|^{2}dt+\int_{0}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\sigma_{t}\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\right)

(3.35)

Recall that (
Value HJB Equation
) also contains the expression
\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\|^{2}
which can be isolated as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})=-(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{x})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}-c(\bm{x},t)\implies\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}=\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})+(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{x})+c(\bm{x},t)

(3.36)

Substituting this into the integral, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\left(\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})+(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{X}_{t})+c(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right)}dt-\int_{0}^{T}\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(-\int_{0}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\bigg(\left(\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})+(\mathcal{A}_{t}V_{t})(\bm{X}_{t})\right)dt-\nabla V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\bigg)}_{dV_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})}}-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt\bigg)

(3.37)

Since integrating the Itô integral of the value process is equal to the value difference, we can write:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})

\displaystyle=\exp\bigg(-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}dV_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})}_{(V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})-V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0}))}}-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{x},t)dt\bigg)

\displaystyle=\boxed{\exp\bigg(-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{x},t)dt\bigg)}

(3.38)

Multiplying both sides by the reference path measure and scaling by a normalization factor, we get the expression for the optimal path measure as:

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\frac{1}{Z}e^{-V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})}

(3.39)

where
Z
is defined such that the distribution integrates to one:

\displaystyle\int\frac{1}{Z}e^{-V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})-\int\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{x},t)dt\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[e^{-V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt}\right]=1

\displaystyle\implies\boxed{Z:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[e^{-V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt}\right]}

(3.40)

which concludes our derivation of the expression for the optimal RND and optimal path measure. Since the exponential scaling factor depends only on the endpoints
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})
, we can derive the expression for the endpoint law:

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})e^{-V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt}}

(3.41)

which means that the endpoint law of the optimal path measure is an exponential tilting of the reference path measure by the initial and terminal value functions. Substituting the terminal constraint
V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})=\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})
yields the final expressions in Proposition
3.9
.
\square

The expression for (
Optimal Path Measure
) reveals the
key challenge
in solving the SOC problem. That is, the optimal path measure is a
tilted
version of the reference path measure by not only the terminal cost
-V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})
but also the initial value function
V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})
, which is defined as an expectation over stochastic paths as defined in Proposition
3.8
and is
intractable
. This motivates the definition of
memoryless reference processes
, which removes the dependency of the optimal path on the initial value function.

Memoryless Reference Process Eliminates Initial Value Function Bias

Consider a reference process
\mathbb{Q}
where the joint distribution of initial and terminal states is
independent
, such that the endpoint distribution factorizes:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})=q_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})q_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})

(3.42)

From Proposition
3.9
, we show that the endpoint distribution of the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is an exponential tilting of the reference law, given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}

(3.43)

where we consider the common case with no running cost function
c\equiv 0
. Then, the terminal distribution under the optimal process
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
satisfies:

\displaystyle p^{\star}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})d\bm{X}_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})}e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}d\bm{X}_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}q_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})q_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})}e^{V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}d\bm{X}_{0}=q_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}q_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})e^{V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}d\bm{X}_{0}}_{\text{constant normalization}}}

\displaystyle=\boxed{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{Z}}q_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})}}

(3.44)

where the initial value function integrates to a constant normalization factor, eliminating the need to compute
V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})
explicitly. Furthermore, we can define
\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})
such that the terminal marginal is exactly the target distribution
\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})
as:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z}q_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})}=\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})

\displaystyle\implies\frac{q_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}{Z\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}=e^{\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})}

\displaystyle\implies\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})=\log\frac{q_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}{\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}-\log Z

(3.45)

which yields a tractable objective that does not require computing
V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})
. Examples of memoryless reference processes include:

(i)

Linear reference drift
\bm{f}:=\bm{A}_{t}\bm{X}_{t}+\bm{b}_{t}
, for some
\bm{A}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
\bm{b}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and noise that grows with time such that the initial distribution is a standard zero-mean Gaussian
\bm{X}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})
. This includes the variance-preserving SDE used in
song2020score
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}:\;d\bm{X}_{t}=-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{t}\bm{X}_{t}dt+\sqrt{\beta_{t}}d\bm{B}_{t},\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{X}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})\implies\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\approx q_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})q_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})

(ii)

Brownian motion reference drift
\bm{f}:=\bm{0}
and a initial distribution that is a Dirac delta
\pi_{0}:=\delta_{0}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}:\;d\bm{X}_{t}=\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{0}\implies\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\approx\delta_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})q_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})

While this shows that we can solve the SOC problem without computing the initial value function
V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})
by explicitly designing the reference process to be
memoryless
, this construction restricts us to a limited class of relatively uninformative reference dynamics, which may not provide a meaningful prior for a given system. To overcome this limitation, we will reformulate the SOC objective by absorbing the initial value function into the terminal cost, which allows for a broader class of reference processes and more expressive prior dynamics.

3.2
Schrödinger Bridges with Stochastic Optimal Control

In this section, we will adapt stochastic optimal control (SOC) theory for solving the (
Dynamic SB Problem
) with arbitrary prior dynamics and initial distributions. First, we recall that the Hopf-Cole transform from Theorem
2.24
expresses the optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}
and probability density
p^{\star}_{t}
using a pair of
forward-backward SB potentials

(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
as:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x}),\hskip 9.24994ptp^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})

(3.46)

where
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
are defined in (
Schrödinger Potentials
) as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{y}|\bm{x})\varphi_{T}(\bm{y})d\bm{y}\\
\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{y})d\bm{y}\end{cases}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}\pi_{0}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{0}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{x})\\
\pi_{T}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})\end{cases}

(Schrödinger Potentials)

Now, we can rewrite the original SOC problem in (
3.1
) by observing that the optimal control can be written in
two equivalent ways
: using the
value function

V_{t}(\bm{x})
(Proposition
3.7
)
or
the
Schrödinger potential

\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
(
3.46
). This allows us to derive the
relationship between the value function and the SB potential
as:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=-\sigma_{t}\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}V_{t}(\bm{x})=-\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})}

(SB Value Function)

Since the terminal cost is given by the terminal value function
\Phi(\bm{x})=V_{T}(\bm{x})
, we can also write the terminal cost in terms of the SB potentials as:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\Phi(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=V_{T}(\bm{x})=-\log\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})=-\log\frac{\pi_{T}(\bm{x})}{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}=\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})}{\pi_{T}(\bm{x})}}

(SB Terminal Cost)

where we substitute
\pi_{T}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}(\bm{x})\implies\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})=\frac{\pi_{T}(\bm{x})}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})}
from (
Schrödinger Potentials
). Substituting (
SB Value Function
) and (
SB Terminal Cost
) into the results from Proposition
3.9
, we can write the optimal path RND and path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
that solves the dynamic SB problem as:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})\right)=\exp\left(-\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}{\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}-\log\varphi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})\right)

(Optimal SB Path RND)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0:T})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})\exp\left(-\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}{\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}-\log\varphi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})\right)

(Optimal SB Path Measure)

Using (
SB Value Function
) and (
SB Terminal Cost
), we also define the
alternative Schrödinger bridge form of the SOC problem
which yields the optimal SB solution.

Definition 3.10

(Schrödinger Bridge with Stochastic Optimal Control (SB-SOC))
.

Given the Schrödinger potentials
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
that satisfy the linear PDEs in (
Hopf-Cole PDEs
), the (
SOC Objective
) can be expressed in terms of
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
as:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{u}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt+\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})}{\pi_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})}\right]

(SB-SOC Objective)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad d\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{X}^{u}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}

where
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
is the control drift which produces the path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
,
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
is the drift of the reference process
\mathbb{Q}
,
\sigma_{t}
is the diffusion coefficient, and
d\bm{B}_{t}
is
d
-dimensional Brownian motion.

Given the (
SB-SOC Objective
), we can define an
cost-to-go
analogously to (
Cost Functional
) from any fixed point
(\bm{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]
at time
t
under the control
\bm{u}
as:

\displaystyle J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}):=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},s)\|^{2}ds+\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})}{\pi_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})}\bigg|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(3.47)

We can also show that (
SB-SOC Objective
) eliminates the initial value bias and does not require defining a memoryless reference process as discussed in Box
3.1
.

Proposition 3.11

(Schrödinger Potential Eliminates the Initial Value Bias)
.

The (
SB-SOC Objective
) does not require computing the initial value function
V_{0}(\bm{x})
. In particular, under the optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}
, the induced path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
automatically satisfies the terminal marginal constraint
p_{T}^{\star}(\bm{x}_{T})=\pi_{T}(\bm{x}_{T})
independently of the initial value function
V_{0}(x)
.

Proof.

Starting from the SB-SOC representation of the (
Optimal SB Path Measure
), we can write the optimal endpoint law as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\exp\left(-\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}{\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}-\log\varphi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})\right)

(3.48)

Integrating over
\bm{X}_{0}
, we have that the terminal marginal of the optimal controlled path
p^{\star}_{T}
is equal to:

\displaystyle p_{T}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{T})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\exp\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}{\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}-\log\varphi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}\right)d\bm{X}_{0}

(3.49)

Rearranging the exponential term, we have:

\displaystyle p_{T}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{T})={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\frac{1}{\varphi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}}d\bm{X}_{0}

(3.50)

Using the factorization
\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})=\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{0})\pi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})
and the boundary condition
\pi_{0}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{0}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{x})
, we get:
as follows:

\displaystyle p_{T}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{T})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{0})\pi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}{\pi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}}d\bm{X}_{0}=\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{0}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}d\bm{X}_{0}}_{=\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}=\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})

(3.51)

which shows that the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
under the optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}
that solves (
SB-SOC Objective
) exactly matches the terminal distribution
p^{\star}_{T}=\pi_{T}
, without dependence on the initial value
V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})
.
\square

We can now characterize the full optimal path measure induced by the optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}
. In particular, we will show that the Schrödinger potentials
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
provide a clean factorization of the optimal bridge measure with respect to the reference process
\mathbb{Q}
, which reveals
how the endpoint potentials reweight trajectories of the reference dynamics to produce the Schrödinger bridge. Furthermore, the optimal path density
\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0:T})
, as well as its associated marginal density
p^{\star}_{t}
and joint density
p^{\star}_{s,t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})
, can be written explicitly in terms of the potentials as stated below.

Proposition 3.12

(Optimal Path Density of SB-SOC)
.

The Schrödinger bridge path measure that solves (
SB-SOC Objective
) can be written with respect to the SB potentials as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}{\pi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{0})\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})

(SB-SOC Path)

and the marginal density at time
t
can be factorized as:

\displaystyle p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\hat{\varphi}_{t}(x)\varphi_{t}(x)

(SB-SOC Density)

Additionally, for any
s\leq t
, the joint density of
\bm{X}_{s}=\bm{y}
and
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
satisfies:

\displaystyle p^{\star}_{s,t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})=\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}|\bm{X}_{s}=\bm{y})\hat{\varphi}_{s}(\bm{y})\varphi_{t}(\bm{x}),\quad s\leq t

(SB-SOC Joint Density)

Proof.
Using the alternative definition of the value function, we can also derive the optimal SB path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
with respect to the Schrödinger potentials. From Proposition
3.9
, we showed that the optimal path measure can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0:T})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})\exp\left(-V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt\right)

(3.52)

Since the dynamic SB problem is defined as a KL minimization problem, which only produces a quadratic control cost, we have
c\equiv 0
. Therefore, substituting the (
SB Terminal Cost
) and (
SB Value Function
), we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0:T})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})\exp\left(-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}\right)=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})\exp\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\log\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\log\varphi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}\frac{1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\varphi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}}=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}{\pi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\underbrace{\frac{\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})}{\pi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}}_{=\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{0})}\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{0})\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})

(3.53)

where we use the identity
\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{0})\pi_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})
. To obtain the marginal density at time
t
given by
p^{\star}_{t}
we can define the distribution over paths
\bm{X}_{0:T}
where
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
and integrate over all paths
\bm{X}_{0:T}
to get:

\displaystyle p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\int\delta(\bm{X}_{t}-\bm{x})\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})\mathbb{Q}(d\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{0})

(3.54)

Since the integrand is multiplied only by the potentials evaluated at
\bm{X}_{0}
and
\bm{X}_{T}
, we integrate out the path coordinates leaving only
\bm{X}_{0}
and
\bm{X}_{T}
and apply the Markov property of the reference path measure to get a simplified expression for the marginal density:

\displaystyle p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\int{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T},\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}|\bm{X}_{0})}\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}d\bm{X}_{0}d\bm{X}_{T}}

\displaystyle=\int\int{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}|\bm{X}_{0})}\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}d\bm{X}_{0}d\bm{X}_{T}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\int\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})d\bm{X}_{T}\right)}_{={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\hat{\varphi}_{t}(x)}}\underbrace{\left(\int\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}|\bm{X}_{0})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})d\bm{X}_{0}\right)}_{={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\varphi_{t}(x)}}

\displaystyle=\boxed{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\hat{\varphi}_{t}(x)}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\varphi_{t}(x)}}

(3.55)

To derive the joint distribution for arbitrary
s\leq t
, we can include additional conditioning on
\bm{X}_{s}=\bm{y}
and apply the Markov property of
\mathbb{Q}
similarly to (
SB-SOC Density
) to get:

\displaystyle p^{\star}_{s,t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\int\mathbb{Q}({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{X}_{T}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}},{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\bm{X}_{s}=\bm{y}}|\bm{X}_{0})\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})d\bm{X}_{0}d\bm{X}_{T}

\displaystyle=\int\int{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}|\bm{X}_{s}=\bm{y})}{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{s}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{0})}\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}d\bm{X}_{0}d\bm{X}_{T}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}|\bm{X}_{s}=\bm{y})}\underbrace{\left(\int\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})d\bm{X}_{T}\right)}_{={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\hat{\varphi}_{t}(x)}}\underbrace{\left(\int\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}|\bm{X}_{0})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})d\bm{X}_{0}\right)}_{={\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\varphi_{t}(x)}}

\displaystyle=\boxed{\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}|\bm{X}_{s}=\bm{y})\hat{\varphi}_{t}(x)\varphi_{t}(x)}

(3.56)

which is the joint probability density of
\bm{X}_{s}=\bm{y}
at time
s
and
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
at time
t
under the optimal SB-SOC path measure.
\square

Together, these results show that the SOC formulation of the Schrödinger bridge can be fully characterized by the reference dynamics
\mathbb{Q}
and the terminal Schrödinger potentials
\varphi_{0}
and
\hat{\varphi}_{T}
, with the intermediate potentials defined via the (
Schrödinger Potentials
) without the need to explicitly compute the value function. Building on this reformulation, we can now derive
tractable training objectives
that estimate the optimal control by sampling stochastic paths from proposal SDEs and minimizing divergences between control drift with respect to the reference drift.

3.3
Objectives for Solving the SOC Problem

In this section, we introduce three different objective functions that can be used to solve the SOC problem defined in (
SOC Objective
): the
relative-entropy loss

\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}
, the
log-variance loss

\mathcal{L}_{\text{LV}}
, and the
cross-entropy loss

\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}

(
nusken2021solving
)
.

Since the SOC objective is inherently a KL divergence between the controlled path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
generated with
\bm{u}
and the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}:=\mathbb{P}^{\bm{u}^{\star}}
generated with the
optimal control

\bm{u}^{\star}
, it is natural to consider a loss function that minimizes the KL divergence or the
reverse KL divergence
between
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
defined as:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}\right],\quad\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\star}\|\mathbb{P}^{u})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\star}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\right]

(3.57)

which both yield a
unique minimizer
at
\mathbb{P}^{u}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}
when
\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\star}\|\mathbb{P}^{u})=0
. These two divergences are exactly what define the
relative-entropy
(RE) and
cross-entropy
(CE) losses
(
nusken2021solving
)
. In the following definitions, we will define both its
path measure form
and derive its
path-integral form
with respect to the control drifts
\bm{u}
.

Definition 3.13

(Relative Entropy (RE) Loss)
.

The
relative entropy
(RE) loss between the controlled path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is defined as the KL divergence:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}(\mathbb{P}^{u},\mathbb{P}^{\star}):=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}\right]

(Relative Entropy Objective)

Let
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
denote the control that generates
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and let
\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}=(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
denote a stochastic process under the (
Controlled SDE
). Then, the RE loss takes the path-integral form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}(\bm{u}):=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt+\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})+\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)dt\right]

(RE Loss)

Derivation.
To derive this loss, we leverage (
Girsanov’s Theorem
) and our derivation for the Radon-Nikodym derivative between the optimal and reference path measure derived in Proposition
3.12
. Since
\mathbb{P}^{u}
is the
controlled
version of the reference process
\mathbb{Q}
, we write:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}+\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg[\log\underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}}_{\text{Girsanov}}-\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}\bigg]

(3.58)

Since
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and
\mathbb{Q}
differ only by the control drift
\sigma\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
, we can apply (
Path RND
) to get write the first term
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}
as:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt+\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\right)

(3.59)

Substituting this and (
Optimal Path RND
), we get the integral form of the relative-entropy objective
\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}(\bm{u})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T})}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T})}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt+\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}V_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})-V_{0}(\bm{X}^{u}_{0})+\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)dt\bigg)}\bigg]

(3.60)

By definition, we have
V_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})=\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})
and the initial distribution is fixed at
\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}
so
V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})
is constant. Then, we get the final form of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}(\bm{u})
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}(\bm{u}):=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt+\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})+\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)dt\right]

(3.61)

which is exactly (
RE Loss
) in our definition.
\square

While the relative-entropy loss has a
unique minimizer
when
\mathbb{P}^{u}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}
, optimizing
\bm{u}
using the gradient
\nabla_{u}\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}(\bm{u})
with respect to
\bm{u}
or its parameters
\nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}(\bm{u}_{\theta})
, requires differentiating through the full SDE trajectories
(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
due to the expectation over
\mathbb{P}^{u}
. In practice, if
(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
is generated via an SDE solver, like the Euler-Maruyama method, this requires storing the full computational graph at each simulation step, which is memory-intensive.

To obtain a practical estimator, the
REINFORCE trick
allows us to rewrite (
Relative Entropy Objective
) as an expectation under a stop-gradient sampling measure
\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}
, where
\bm{Q}^{\bar{u}}:=\texttt{stopgrad}(\bm{Q}^{u})
is the non-gradient-tracking controlled generator
(
williams1992simple
;
ranganath2014black
;
mnih2014neural
)
.

Definition 3.14

(REINFORCE Relative Entropy (RERF) Loss)
.

The
REINFORCE relative entropy
(RERF) loss is defined as an expectation over
\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}
, where
\bm{Q}^{\bar{u}}:=\texttt{stopgrad}(\bm{Q}^{u})
is the non-gradient-tracking controlled generator:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{RERF}}(\mathbb{P}^{u},\mathbb{P}^{\star}):=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}+C\right)\right]

(RERF Loss)

where
C\in\mathbb{R}
is any constant. Crucially, the gradient aligns with the gradient of the relative-entropy loss, i.e.,
\nabla_{\bm{u}}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{P}^{u})=\nabla_{\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{RERF}}(\mathbb{P}^{u},\mathbb{P}^{\star})
.

While the (
RERF Loss
) provides an unbiased estimator of the relative-entropy gradient that satisfies
\nabla_{\bm{u}}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{P}^{u})=\nabla_{\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{RERF}}(\mathbb{P}^{u},\mathbb{P}^{\star})
, it is important to note that it should be interpreted as a computational surrogate rather than a true loss function, as decreasing its value does not necessarily correspond to a monotonic reduction of the KL divergence itself. Instead, its role is to provide a tractable estimator of the gradient needed to learn the optimal control that induces the target path measure.

An alternative objective that is a true loss function and doesn’t require an expectation over
\mathbb{P}^{u}
is the
cross-entropy (CE) loss
, which is simply the
reverse KL divergence
, where the expectation is over the fixed optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
instead of
\mathbb{P}^{u}
.

Definition 3.15

(Cross Entropy (CE) Loss)
.

The
cross entropy
(CE) loss between the controlled path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is defined as the KL divergence:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}(\mathbb{P}^{u},\mathbb{P}^{\star}):=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\star}\|\mathbb{P}^{u})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\star}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\right]

(Cross Entropy Objective)

Let
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)
denote an arbitrary
fixed
control drift that generates
\mathbb{P}^{v}
and let
\bm{X}^{v}_{0:T}=(\bm{X}^{v}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
denote the stochastic process under
\mathbb{P}^{v}
. Then, the CE loss takes the path-integral form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}(\bm{u})=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}\sim\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg[\exp\left(-g(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{v}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{v}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}(\bm{u}\cdot\bm{v})(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}-g(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt\right)\bigg]+C

(CE Loss)

Derivation.
This proof follows similar steps to the relative-entropy

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\star}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{v}}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{v}}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg[\underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{v}}}_{(\bigstar)}\underbrace{\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}-\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}\right)}_{(\diamond)}\bigg]

(3.62)

Applying Girsanov’s theorem and substituting (
Optimal Path RND
) into (
\diamond
), we get the integral form of the relative-entropy objective
\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}
as:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}^{v}_{0:T})}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}^{v}_{0:T})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-V_{T}(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}^{v}_{0})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}^{\mathbb{Q}}_{t}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}-g(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt+C}_{(\diamond)}

(3.63)

Since the Brownian motion of the RND is under the reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
in the denominator, we need to transform it to match the path measure under which
\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}
is generated. By (
Girsanov’s Theorem
), we substitute
d\bm{B}^{\mathbb{Q}}_{t}=d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}+\bm{v}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt
which gives us:

\displaystyle-V_{T}(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}^{v}_{0})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}+\bm{v}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt)}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\int_{0}^{T}(\bm{u}\cdot\bm{v})(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}}-g(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt+C}_{(\diamond)}

(3.64)

where we set
V_{T}(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})=g(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})
and denote
V_{0}(\bm{X}^{v}_{0})
as a constant. For
(\bigstar)
, we can derive the (
Path RND
) of
\mathbb{Q}
with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{v}
, where the difference between the control drifts is
-\bm{v}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)
, and substitute (
Optimal Path RND
) to get:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}^{v}_{0:T})\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{v}}(\bm{X}^{v}_{0:T})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{Z}\exp\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-V_{T}(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}^{v}_{0})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt}\right)}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{v}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{v}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}\right)}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{Z}\exp\left(-g(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{v}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{v}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}\right)}_{(\bigstar)}

(3.65)

Substituting the expanded forms for
(\bigstar)
and
(\diamond)
, we get:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}\sim\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg[\underbrace{\exp\left(-g(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{v}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{v}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}\right)}_{(\bigstar)}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}(\bm{u}\cdot\bm{v})(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}-g(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt\right)}_{(\diamond)}\bigg]+C

(3.66)

which recovers the (
CE Loss
) from our definition.
\square

Since the paths
\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}
are generated under the fixed controlled process
\mathbb{P}^{v}
instead of the path that is being optimized
\mathbb{P}^{u}
, it is considered an
off-policy objective
. This admits a much more computationally tractable objective as the gradient
\nabla_{\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}(\bm{u})
no longer depends on the SDE trajectories, and computing the objective does not require differentiating through or maintaining the computational graph of the SDE solver. Typically, the off-policy control
\bm{v}
is defined as the non-gradient-tracked control being optimized
\bm{v}:=\texttt{stopgrad}(\bm{u})
, which generates paths from the controlled SDE without maintaining the computational graph used to generate it. We also note that while both
\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}
are
uniquely minimized
at
\mathbb{P}^{u}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}
, the
\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}
is
convex in
\mathbb{P}^{u}
, which yields a more favorable optimization landscape.

Remark 3.16

(Convexity of the Cross-Entropy Objective)
.

The cross-entropy loss
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CE}}(\mathbb{P}^{u},\mathbb{P}^{\star})=\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\star}\|\mathbb{P}^{u})
is
convex in the controlled path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
. This follows from the fact that
\mathbb{P}^{u}\mapsto\log\mathbb{P}^{u}
is convex when the reference distribution
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is fixed. Therefore, minimizing the CE objective corresponds to a convex optimization problem in the space of path measures, and the global minimum is achieved when
\mathbb{P}^{u}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}
.

Now that we have defined two objectives corresponding to the KL divergence between path measures, we consider an alternative class of objectives that aims to minimize the
variance
between the controlled and optimal path measures. To build the intuition behind the variance-based losses, we recall that a stochastic process
\bm{X}_{0:T}^{u}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}
generated under a controlled process can be
reweighted
by the Radon-Nikodym derivative such that the law matches the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T})}_{\text{optimal measure}}=\underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T})}_{\text{importance weight}}\underbrace{\mathbb{P}^{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T})}_{\text{paths under }\bm{u}}

(3.67)

However, when
\mathbb{P}^{u}
deviates far from the optimal measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
, the importance weights will vary significantly for different paths
\bm{X}_{0:T}^{u}
, with some weights being huge and others small. This means that the
variance of the importance weight
can be a measure of the
similarity between
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
. Crucially, the variance is only zero when the importance weight is a constant that is not dependent on the path
\bm{X}_{0:T}^{u}
. Since the RND is constant only when the two measures are equal
\mathbb{P}^{u}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}
up to a normalization, we have:

\displaystyle\text{Var}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}^{u}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}(\bm{X}_{0:T}^{u})\right)=0\iff\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}(\bm{X}_{0:T}^{u})=\text{const.}\iff\mathbb{P}^{u}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}

(3.68)

Similar to the cross-entropy objective, we want to prevent taking gradients under paths generated directly from the controlled path measure that we are optimizing
\mathbb{P}^{u}
, so we define an arbitrary, fixed control
\bm{v}
and sample paths
\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}\sim\mathbb{P}^{v}
. Using these paths
off-policy paths
, the importance weights are still minimized when the RND is constant. Using this idea, we define the
variance loss
and
log-variance loss
.

Definition 3.17

(Variance and Log-Variance Losses)
.

The
variance
and
log-variance
(LV) loss between the controlled path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is defined as the KL divergence:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}}(\mathbb{P}^{u},\mathbb{P}^{\star}):=\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\right),\quad\mathcal{L}^{\text{log}}_{\text{Var}}(\mathbb{P}^{u},\mathbb{P}^{\star}):=\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\right)

(3.69)

Let
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
denote the control being optimized and
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)
denote an arbitrary fixed control that generates the stochastic paths
(\bm{X}_{t}^{v})_{t\in[0,T]}
. Then, the variance and log-variance losses take the path-integral form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}}(\bm{u}):=\frac{1}{Z^{2}}\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left(e^{\mathcal{F}_{u,v}-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T}^{v})}\right),\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{L}^{\text{log}}_{\text{Var}}(\bm{u}):=\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left(\mathcal{F}_{u,v}-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T}^{v})\right)

(Variance Losses)

where
\mathcal{F}_{u,v}
is given by the expression:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{u,v}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t}^{v},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}(\bm{u}\cdot\bm{v})(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t}^{v},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}_{t}^{v},t)dt

(3.70)

Derivation.
We can break down the variance losses as follows:

\displaystyle\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{{P}}^{u}}\bigg),\hskip 9.24994pt\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\right)=\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}-\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{{Q}}}\bigg)

(3.71)

Expanding these terms similar form as (
3.63
), where
d\bm{B}^{\mathbb{Q}}_{t}=d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}+\bm{v}(\bm{X}_{t}^{v},t)dt
, we get:

\displaystyle\text{Var}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}\sim\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}(\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v})\right)=\text{Var}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}\sim\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{{P}}^{u}}(\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v})\bigg)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{Z^{2}}}\text{Var}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}\sim\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\exp\bigg(\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}(\bm{u}\cdot\bm{v})(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}}_{=:\mathcal{F}_{u,v}}-g(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt\bigg)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\text{Var}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}\sim\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}(\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v})\right)=\text{Var}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}\sim\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg(\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v})-\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{{Q}}}(\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v})\bigg)

\displaystyle=\text{Var}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}\sim\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}(\bm{u}\cdot\bm{v})(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}}_{=:\mathcal{F}_{u,v}}}-g(\bm{X}^{v}_{T})-\int_{0}^{T}c(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt+C\bigg)

Then, we can define
\mathcal{F}_{u,v}:=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}(\bm{u}\cdot\bm{v})(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{v}_{t},t)^{\top}d\bm{B}^{v}_{t}
and rewrite the variance losses as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}}(\bm{u}):=\frac{1}{Z^{2}}\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left(e^{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathcal{F}_{u,v}}-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T}^{v})}\right),\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{L}^{\text{log}}_{\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}}(\bm{u}):=\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathcal{F}_{u,v}}-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T}^{v})\right)

(3.72)

which recovers the (
Variance Losses
) from our definition.
\square

Comparing the (
CE Loss
) and (
Variance Losses
), we observe that they both sample paths from an arbitrary fixed path measure
\mathbb{P}^{v}
with control
\bm{v}
. However, the (
CE Loss
) does not change with different choices of
\bm{v}
, since the dependence on
\mathbb{P}^{v}

cancels out
when scaling with the RND
\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{v}
. In contrast, the variance-based objectives change with respect to different definitions of
\bm{v}
, since the distribution under which the variance is calculated
\mathbb{P}^{v}
, changes with different
\bm{v}
.

Remark 3.18

(Variance Loss Depends on Sampling Distribution)
.

The variance-based objectives in (
Variance Losses
) depend on the definition of
\bm{v}
used to sample
\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}\sim\mathbb{P}^{v}
. However, it holds that the variance is minimized exactly when the controlled measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
matches the optimal measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
:

\displaystyle\forall\bm{u}\in\mathcal{U},\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{L}_{\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}}(\bm{u})=0\iff\bm{u}=\bm{u}^{\star}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{and}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathcal{L}^{\text{log}}_{\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}}(\bm{u})=0\iff\bm{u}=\bm{u}^{\star}

(3.73)

This is easy to see given that the RND between the optimal path measure and itself must be equal to one, i.e.,
\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}\equiv 1
, regardless of the path on which it is evaluated, and the variance of a constant is zero.

We have introduced three variations of the SOC objective, which are all derived from the RND between path measures. The
key insight
is that all three objectives can be
estimated from paths generated from a tractable SDE
, enabling practical learning of the optimal bridge dynamics. Although the objectives differ in their sampling laws, training stability, and convergence guarantees, they are all minimized when the controlled dynamics recover the optimal bridge
\mathbb{P}^{u}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}
.

3.4
Closing Remarks for Section
3

In this section, we introduced the stochastic optimal control (SOC) problem, which defines a path-space variational objective that minimizes a running cost and terminal cost function generated by a
controlled SDE
. Leveraging dynamic programming theory, we introduce the
value function

V_{t}
, which solves the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation similarly to the Lagrangian
\psi_{t}
defined in Section
2.7
and connect it to the optimal control drift
\bm{u}^{\star}
and optimal path measure Radon-Nikodym derivative.

A
key observation
is that the quadratic control cost in the SOC objective is equivalent to the KL divergence between the controlled and reference Itô processes from Corollary
2.21
. This connection allows us to reformulate the dynamic Schrödinger bridge problem as an SOC objective in which the terminal cost is expressed as a log-ratio involving the backward Schrödinger potential
\hat{\varphi}_{T}
and the terminal marginal constraint
\pi_{T}
. This reformulation avoids the need for explicit couplings between
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
, and we show that the backward potential
\hat{\varphi}_{T}
effectively absorbs the dependence on the initial value
V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})
appearing in classical SOC formulations, making the framework applicable to arbitrary initial distributions and reference dynamics.

Building on this formulation, we introduced several tractable training objectives that are uniquely minimized by the optimal control drift
\bm{u}^{\star}
and its corresponding path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
. A
key step
in deriving these objectives is using Girsanov’s theorem to express the Radon–Nikodym derivative between the controlled and reference path measures directly in terms of the control drift, yielding closed-form expressions and a practical way to evaluate and optimize path-space divergences using trajectories simulated from the controlled SDE.

Now that we have explored several complementary formulations of the SB problem, from the static formulation to dynamic formulations with connections to SOC, we next turn to the practical construction of Schrödinger bridges. While the SOC objectives introduced in Section
3.3
already provide a glimpse of how such optimization can be carried out, the next section will present several concrete algorithms and computational strategies for building stochastic bridges and Schrödinger bridges in practice.

4
Building Schrödinger Bridges

While the Schrödinger bridge problem provides a variational characterization of the optimal stochastic transport between two marginals, this formulation alone does not immediately reveal how such bridges can be constructed in practice. In this section, we move from the abstract formulation to explore several complementary mechanisms for building stochastic bridges between prescribed endpoint distributions.

We begin by interpreting Schrödinger bridges as a mixture of conditional bridges (Section
4.1
), which provides an intuitive pathwise construction. We then study time reversal (Section
4.2
) and the resulting forward–backward SDE representation (Section
4.3
), revealing how drift corrections arise from the score of the evolving distribution. Next, we present Doob’s
h
-transform (Section
4.4
) as a change-of-measure construction that enforces endpoint constraints, followed by an interpretation in terms of Markovian and reciprocal projections (Section
4.5
) as entropy-minimizing projections in path space. Finally, we connect these ideas to the stochastic interpolant framework (Section
4.6
), which provides practical parameterizations of bridges used in modern generative modeling.

Throughout this section, we will use a slight abuse of notation and define the
transition density
from
\bm{X}_{t}
at time
t
to
\bm{X}_{\tau}
at a later time
\tau\geq t
under a path measure as
\mathbb{P}_{\tau|t}(\bm{x}_{\tau}|\bm{x}_{t})
or equivalently
\mathbb{P}_{\tau|t}(\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bm{x}_{\tau}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}_{t})
. For instance, the transition density from
\bm{x}
at time
t
to
\bm{x}_{T}
at time
T
under the reference measure
\mathbb{Q}
is denoted
\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})=\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})
.

4.1
Mixture of Conditional Bridges

A useful way to understand the structure of Schrödinger bridges is through endpoint conditioning. Rather than viewing the bridge as a single global stochastic process, we can interpret it as a
mixture of conditional bridges
under the reference process
\mathbb{Q}
connecting specific endpoint pairs
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
drawn from a coupling
\pi_{0,T}
. Each conditional bridge describes the distribution of paths under the reference dynamics conditioned on fixed endpoints, which recovers the optimal Schrödinger bridge when conditioned on the
optimal coupling

\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
that solves the static Schrödinger bridge problem.

Proposition 4.1

(Mixture of Endpoint-Conditioned Bridges)
.

Consider the dynamic SB problem with reference process
\mathbb{Q}
and marginal constraints
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
defined as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}=\underset{\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\arg\min}\left\{\text{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{Q}\right):\mathbb{P}^{u}_{0}=\pi_{0},\mathbb{P}^{u}_{T}=\pi_{T}\right\}

(4.1)

Then, the unique minimizer
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
can be written as a
mixture of endpoint-conditioned bridges

\mathbb{Q}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\int\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\pi^{\star}_{0,T}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})

(Mixture of Bridges)

where
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
is the optimal coupling that solves the
static Schrödinger bridge problem
with the reference coupling defined as the endpoint law of the reference process
\mathbb{Q}_{0,T}
given by:

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}_{0,T}=\underset{\pi_{0,T}\in\Pi(\pi_{0},\pi_{T})}{\arg\min}\text{KL}(\pi_{0,T}\|\mathbb{Q}_{0,T})

(4.2)

Proof.
By the law of total probability, we can decompose the reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T})\mathbb{Q}_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})

(4.3)

By definition, the conditional law of the reference process
\mathbb{Q}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
is the bridge connecting
\bm{x}_{0}
and
\bm{x}_{T}
. Similarly, we can decompose any candidate path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
with endpoint law
\mathbb{P}_{0,T}\equiv\pi_{0,T}
as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T})\pi_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})

(4.4)

Applying the (
KL Divergence Chain Rule
), we can split the dynamic SB objective into the KL divergence of the endpoint couplings and conditional KL divergence between the endpoint-conditioned path distributions:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{Q})=\underbrace{\text{KL}(\pi_{0,T}\|\mathbb{Q}_{0,T})}_{(\bigstar)}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim\pi_{0,T}}\left[\text{KL}\big(\mathbb{P}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\|\mathbb{Q}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\big)\right]}_{(\diamond)}}

(4.5)

Clearly, for any fixed endpoint law
\pi_{0,T}
the minimizer of (
\diamond
) is achieved when
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})=\mathbb{Q}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
and the KL is zero. Therefore,
solving the dynamic SB over the restricted space of path measures where
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})=\mathbb{Q}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
reduces to solving the static SB problem
which yields the optimal endpoint law:

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}_{0,T}=\underset{\pi_{0,T}\in\Pi(\pi_{0},\pi_{T})}{\arg\min}\text{KL}(\pi_{0,T}\|\mathbb{Q}_{0,T})

(4.6)

Since we have shown that the optimal
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
satisfies
\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})=\mathbb{Q}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
and
\mathbb{P}^{\star}_{0,T}=\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
, we can conclude that the minimizer is given by:

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\int\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\pi^{\star}_{0,T}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})}

(4.7)

which is
unique
following proof of uniqueness of the static SB problem in Proposition
1.9
.
\square

We note that this result is a generalization of the result from
follmer2006random
, which states that the solution to the dynamic SB problem with a pure Brownian reference process is given by the
mixture of endpoint-conditioned Brownian bridges
weighted by the
entropic OT plan
with quadratic transport cost.

Corollary 4.2

(Mixture of Brownian Bridges (
follmer2006random
))
.

Consider the dynamic SB problem with Brownian reference process
\mathbb{Q}:d\bm{X}_{t}=\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
and marginal constraints
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, the unique minimizer
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
can be written as a
mixture of endpoint-conditioned Brownian bridges

\mathbb{Q}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\int\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\pi^{\star}_{0,T}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})

(Mixture of Brownian Bridges)

where
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
solves the (
Entropic OT Problem
) with quadratic state cost
c(\bm{x},\bm{y}):=\|\bm{x}-\bm{y}\|^{2}
.

This factorized definition of the (
Dynamic SB Problem
) can be used to define a tractable objective based on
conditional stochastic optimal control
, which samples pairs from the optimal marginal law
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
and optimizes the interpolating controlled dynamics such that they minimize the KL divergence from the reference drift.

Proposition 4.3

(Conditional Stochastic Optimal Control (Proposition 2 in
liu2023generalized
))
.

Consider the controlled path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
, where the marginal density
p_{t}
can be factorized as
p_{t}(\bm{x})=\int p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\pi_{0,T}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})
. Given the optimal endpoint distribution
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
that solves the
static SB problem
with reference distribution
\mathbb{Q}_{0,T}
, the
dynamic SB problem
objective decomposes into a
mixture of conditional stochastic optimal control problems
with endpoints sampled from
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{u}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}_{0,T}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\|^{2}\right]dt

(Conditional SOC Objective)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T}))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

Proof.
We recall the dynamic SB problem with the Fokker-Planck constraint as:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{u}}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}\right]dt\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot\left((\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))p_{t}(\bm{x})\right)+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})\\
p_{0}=\pi_{0},\hskip 9.24994ptp_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(4.8)

Step 1: Decompose the Minimization Objective.

Let
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
denote the optimal endpoint coupling solving the static SB problem with reference endpoint law
\mathbb{Q}_{0,T}
. Then, the marginal density
p_{t}
can be factorized as:

\displaystyle p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\int p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\pi^{\star}_{0,T}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})

(4.9)

Under mild regularity assumptions allowing Leibniz’s rule, Fubini’s theorem, and differentiation under the integral sign
22
22

22

We assume standard regularity conditions ensuring that expectations and time integrals can be interchanged and that differentiation under the integral sign is valid. Such conditions are typically satisfied for diffusion processes with smooth drift and nondegenerate diffusion coefficients.
, we can separate the optimal endpoint law
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
out of the integral, condition on the endpoints, and decompose the objective as:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}\right]dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}_{0,T}}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}(\cdot|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T}})}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T}})\|^{2}\right]\right]dt

(law of total expectation)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}_{0,T}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{0}^{T}}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\|^{2}\right]\right]dt

(Fubini’s theorem)

which recovers the conditional SOC objective in (
Conditional SOC Objective
).

Step 2: Derive the Conditional Fokker-Planck Constraint.

We still need to show that the conditional dynamics
p_{t}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
satisfy the conditional Fokker-Planck constraint. To do this, we substitute the factorization for
p_{t}^{\star}
in (
4.9
) into each term of the Fokker-Planck constraint defined as:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})}_{(\bigstar)}=\underbrace{-\nabla\cdot\left((\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))p_{t}(\bm{x})\right)}_{(\diamond)}+\underbrace{\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})}_{(\blacklozenge)}

(4.10)

Starting off, the time derivative
(\bigstar)
can be written as an expectation over the optimal coupling as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\pi^{\star}_{0,T}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})}

\displaystyle=\int\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}\right]\pi^{\star}_{0,T}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}_{0,T}}\left[\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right]

(4.11)

where the integral can be separated out as
\pi_{0,T}^{\star}
is not dependent on time. Next, we rearrange the divergence term
(\diamond)
as:

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\left((\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))p_{t}(\bm{x})\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\left((\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))\int p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\pi_{0,T}^{\star}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\left(\int(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\pi_{0,T}^{\star}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})\right)

(linearity of integration)

\displaystyle=\int{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\cdot\left((\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right)}\pi_{0,T}^{\star}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})

(Leibniz rule)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0,T}^{\star}}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\cdot\left((\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right)}\bigg]

(4.12)

Finally, we write the Laplacian term
(\blacklozenge)
as expectation over the optimal coupling given by:

\displaystyle\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\nabla{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}(\bm{x})}=\nabla\cdot\nabla\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\pi_{0,T}^{\star}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})}\right)

\displaystyle=\int{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\left(\nabla\cdot\nabla p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right)}\pi_{0,T}^{\star}(d\bm{x}_{0},d\bm{x}_{T})

(Leibniz rule)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0,T}^{\star}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\cdot\nabla p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0,T}^{\star}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}\right]

(4.13)

Substituting each of the conditional expressions into (
4.10
), we get:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}_{0,T}}\left[\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right]}_{(\bigstar)}=\underbrace{-\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0,T}^{\star}}\bigg[\nabla\cdot\left((\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right)\bigg]}_{(\diamond)}+\underbrace{\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0,T}^{\star}}\left[\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right]}_{(\blacklozenge)}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}_{0,T}}\left[\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})+\nabla\cdot\left((\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right)-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right]=0

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\boxed{\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})+\nabla\cdot\left((\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right)-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})=0}

(Conditional FP Equation)

where the last implication follows from the fact that for the expectation to equal zero pointwise for all
\bm{x}
, then the terms inside the expectation must equal zero for
\pi_{0,T}^{\star}
-almost every
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
. The final line is exactly the (
Controlled Fokker-Planck Equation
) conditioned on the endpoints
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
.

Since we have shown that the conditional density
p_{t}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
satsifies the (
Conditional FP Equation
), we can conclude that it is a valid density evolution with endpoint constraints
p_{0}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}_{0,T}}[\delta_{\bm{x}_{0}}]
and
p_{T}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{\star}_{0,T}}[\delta_{\bm{x}_{T}}]
. By equivalence between the conditional Fokker-Planck equation and the controlled SDE generated by
\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
, we can also derive the corresponding SDE representation:

\displaystyle\boxed{d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T}))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0},\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}}

(4.14)

which shows that once the endpoints are sampled from the optimal coupling
\pi_{0,T}^{\star}
, the (
Dynamic SB Problem
) decomposes into a mixture of conditional SOC problems.
\square

This mixture conditional SOC problem decomposes the path space KL minimization to the optimal measure (
Mixture of Bridges
) from Proposition
4.1
into a static SB problem and a family of conditional bridge problems. This formulation avoids optimizing directly over full path measures and allows us to solve the dynamic SB problem through a
tractable two-stage procedure
: first, estimate the optimal static coupling
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim\pi_{0,T}^{\star}
, then learn the corresponding conditional stochastic control law
\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
. We will leverage this idea in Section
6.4
to efficiently learn parameterized Schrödinger bridges with score and flow matching.

4.2
Time Reversal

A useful approach for constructing stochastic bridges is to analyze the time reversal of a stochastic process, where we leverage the
key idea
that conditioning on a terminal constraint induces a
modified reverse-time dynamics
whose drift differs from the original forward drift. Rather than directly enforcing the endpoint condition, we reinterpret the forward process as a backward process and derive the forward Markovian dynamics that reproduce the same marginal density evolution under time reversal, revealing how information about the terminal distribution propagates backward through the SDE dynamics.

Figure 6:
Time-Reversal of Stochastic Differential Equations.

Left:
A forward diffusion process over
t\in[0,T]
evolves from an initial state
\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}
under the forward-time SDE
d\bm{X}_{t}
, producing marginal densities
p_{t}(\bm{x})
that evolve via the Fokker-Planck equation.
Right:
The corresponding reverse-time stochastic process generates trajectories that traverse the same sequence of marginal densities in the reversed time coordinate
s=T-t\in[0,T]
. Its drift includes an additional score correction
\nabla\log p_{T-s}(\bm{x})
that accounts for the probability flow of the forward dynamics. This reverse SDE allows trajectories to be simulated starting from the terminal distribution such that they reconstruct the initial distribution.

Consider a
forward-time stochastic process
defined on the time-horizon
t\in[0,T]
generated from the SDE:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}

(4.15)

One method of building a stochastic bridge that evolves via (
4.15
) but is
conditioned to reach a target state

\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}
is to reverse the time coordinate of the original forward process such that it can be conditioned on the target state and then, derive a
reverse stochastic process
that matches density evolution of the forward process with time-reversal.

Proposition 4.4

(Time Reversal Formula)
.

Consider a forward-time SDE of the form:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(4.16)

where
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
is the drift and
\sigma_{t}
is the scalar diffusion coefficient. Let
p_{t}(\bm{x})
denote the marginal density of
\bm{X}_{t}
at time
t
. Then, the time-reversed process
\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}:=\bm{X}_{T-s}
follows the SDE:

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\left[-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log p(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)\right]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}

(4.17)

where
\tilde{\bm{B}}_{s}
is the Brownian motion with respect to the reverse-time filtration
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}=\sigma(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{\tau}:0\leq\tau\leq s)
. Together, the pair of forward and reverse-time SDEs are given by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\left[-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log p(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)\right]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}\end{cases}

(Time Reversal Formula)

Proof.
Intuitively, defining an SDE that simulates stochastic processes on the reversed time coordinate such that the marginals match those of the forward time stochastic processes requires first
reparameterizing the time coordinate of the forward process
and then
deriving a backward drift that
propogates
particles on a reversed time coordinate
such that it matches the corresponding marginal on the reparameterized forward process. To this end, we break the proof into two steps.

Step 1: Define the Forward Process with Time-Reversal.

This is a simple reparameterization of the time coordinate such that it follows
s=T-t
that starts at
t=0
and
s=T
and terminates at
t=T
and
s=0
. We denote this process as
\overleftarrow{\bm{X}}_{s}=\bm{X}_{T-t}
This reparameterization allows us to define a initial condition
\overleftarrow{\bm{X}}_{0}=\bm{x}_{T}
and the density
\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})
such that
q(\bm{x},0)=\pi_{T}
. With the same drift and diffusion terms, we define the time-reversed process with the SDE:

\displaystyle d\overleftarrow{\bm{X}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\bm{f}(\overleftarrow{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)}_{:=\overleftarrow{\bm{f}}(\bm{X}_{s},s)}ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\bm{B}_{s},\quad\overleftarrow{\bm{X}}_{s}=\bm{x}_{T}

(4.18)

The
marginal density

p_{t}(\bm{x})
evolves via the Fokker-Planck equation derived in Section
2.4
as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)p(\bm{x},t))+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p(\bm{x},t)

(4.19)

To write this as an evolution over the time coordinate
s=T-t
, we perform a change of variables as:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\underbrace{\frac{\partial s}{\partial t}}_{=-1}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{p(\bm{x},T-s)}_{\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})}}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{p(\bm{x},T-s)}_{\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})}})+\frac{\sigma_{T-s}^{2}}{2}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{p(\bm{x},T-s)}_{\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x}))+\frac{\sigma_{T-s}^{2}}{2}\Delta\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

(4.20)

Multiplying both sides by
-1
, we get that the evolution of time-reversed density
\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})
follows the PDE:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x}))-\frac{\sigma_{T-s}^{2}}{2}\Delta\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

(4.21)

Step 2: Derive the Fokker-Planck Equation of the Reverse Stochastic Process.

Although reversing the time coordinate defines the density evolution conditioned on a target distribution, it does
not
tell us the drift and diffusion to follow when
simulating paths backward in time
since the drift and diffusion are still defined in the forward direction.

We define
(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s})_{s\in[0,T]}
as the Itô process that evolves backward in time. Let
\bm{b}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},s)
be a placeholder for the unknown drift. Since
(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s})_{s\in[0,T]}
must be adapted to the backward filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{s})_{s\in[0,T]}
such that it depends only on the past and present given the backward direction, we denote this new
\mathcal{F}_{s}
-adapted Brownian motion as
d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}
. To define the diffusion coefficient of the reverse process, we denote
\tilde{\sigma}_{s}\equiv\sigma_{T-s}
. Therefore, we can write the SDE of the backward stochastic process as:

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\bm{b}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},s)ds+\tilde{\sigma}_{s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}

(4.22)

and the corresponding Fokker-Planck equation as:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot(\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x}))+\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{s}^{2}}{2}\Delta\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

(4.23)

Setting (
4.23
) and (
4.21
) equal to each other, we have:

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot(\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x}))+\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{s}^{2}}{2}\Delta\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x}))-\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{s}^{2}}{2}\Delta\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot(\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x}))-\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x}))

\displaystyle=\tilde{\sigma}_{s}^{2}\Delta\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\bigg(\big[\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)+\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)\big]\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})\bigg)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\sigma}_{s}^{2}\Delta\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

(4.24)

By definition, the Laplacian is the divergence of the gradient
\Delta\equiv\nabla\cdot\nabla
, so we can write:

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\bigg(\big[\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)+\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)\big]\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})\bigg)

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{s}^{2}\nabla\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})\right)

(4.25)

Since the terms within the divergences are unknown and we aim to determine an expression for the backward drift
\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)
with respect to the forward drift and diffusion, we apply a similar technique to the one used to derive the Fokker-Planck equation in Section
2.4
by integrating both sides with an
arbitrary test function

\phi(\bm{x})
.
