\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\cdot\bigg(\big[\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)+\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)\big]\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})\bigg)\phi(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\cdot\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{s}^{2}\nabla\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})\right)\phi(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

(4.26)

Applying the integration by parts identity
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\cdot f(\bm{x})\phi(\bm{x})d\bm{x}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\bm{x})\cdot\nabla\phi(\bm{x})d\bm{x}
23
23

23

which assumes
f
is continuously differentiable and
\phi
is smooth with sufficient decay at infinity such that the boundary term from integration by parts vanishes.
to both sides, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\bigg(\big[\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)+\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)\big]\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})\bigg)}_{(\bigstar)}}\cdot\nabla\phi(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\left(\tilde{\sigma}_{s}^{2}\nabla\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})\right)}_{(\diamond)}}\cdot\nabla\phi(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

(4.27)

Since we defined
\phi(\bm{x})
to be any arbitrary test function,
(\bigstar)
and
(\diamond)
must be equal to each other for all
\bm{x}
, and thus we can write:

\displaystyle\big[\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)+\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)\big]\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})=\tilde{\sigma}_{s}^{2}\nabla\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

(4.28)

Rearranging and dividing both sides by
\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})\geq 0
, we have:

\displaystyle\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})+\tilde{\sigma}_{s}^{2}\nabla\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)

\displaystyle=-\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)+\tilde{\sigma}_{s}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\frac{\nabla\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})}{\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})}}_{\text{score function}}}

(4.29)

Since
\frac{\nabla\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})}{\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})}=\nabla\log\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})
which is the
score function
of the density
\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})
, we can write the final
backward drift
as:

\displaystyle\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)

\displaystyle=-\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)+\tilde{\sigma}_{s}^{2}\nabla\log\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

(4.30)

which can also be expressed in terms of the forward density
\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})=p(\bm{x},T-s)
and forward diffusion
\tilde{\sigma}_{s}=\sigma_{T-s}
as:

\displaystyle\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)

\displaystyle=-\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log p(\bm{x},T-s)

(4.31)

Substituting
\bm{b}(\bm{x},s)
into (
4.23
), we get the Fokker-Planck equation satisfied by the backward stochastic process (or
bridge
) as:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial s}p_{T-s}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\big[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(-\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log p_{T-s}(\bm{x}))}p_{T-s}(\bm{x})\big]+\frac{\sigma_{T-s}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{T-s}(\bm{x})

(4.32)

and the corresponding SDE given by:

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\left[-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log p(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)\right]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}

which completes the pair of
forward and reverse-time SDEs
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\left[-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log p(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)\right]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}\end{cases}

(4.33)

and we conclude our proof.
\square

Using (
Time Reversal Formula
), denoising diffusion can be viewed as a special case of the Schrödinger bridge problem where the reference process is a variance-explooding SDE with zero drift.

Denoising Diffusion as a Special Case of the Schrödinger Bridge

Here, we will show that Schrödinger bridges are a
generalization
of score-based diffusion models introduced in
(
song2019generative
;
song2020improved
;
song2020score
;
huang2021variational
)
. Specifically, score-based models are a special case where the forward-time stochastic process is the variance exploding SDE with zero-drift:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}\sim p_{\text{data}}

(4.34)

where the variance of the noise accumulates over time following
\beta_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds
. Applying the time reversal described above, we can derive the SDE of the backward stochastic process
\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\bm{X}_{T-s}
as:

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\underbrace{-\bm{f}(\bm{x},T-s)}_{=0\text{ (no drift)}}+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})\bigg]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\left[\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})\right]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}

(4.35)

where the initial distribution is a Gaussian with large variance
\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}_{0},\beta_{T})
. Since the variance of the forward process is
\beta_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds
, we can derive the variance of the backward process as the total variance subtracted by the variance reduced in the backward process up to time
s
given by
\tilde{\beta}_{s}=\beta_{T}-\beta_{s}
. Therefore, the density of the backward process can be written explicitly as a Gaussian with mean
\bm{x}
and variance
\tilde{\beta}_{s}
, denoted
\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})=\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}_{0},\beta_{T}-\beta_{s})
. Then, the score function can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi(\beta_{T}-\beta_{s}))^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{0}\|^{2}}{2(\beta_{T}-\beta_{s})}\right)

\displaystyle\log\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\frac{d}{2}\log(2\pi(\beta_{T}-\beta_{s}))-\frac{1}{2(\beta_{T}-\beta_{s})}\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{0}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\nabla\log\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2(\beta_{T}-\beta_{s})}\nabla\left(\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{0}\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2(\beta_{T}-\beta_{s})}\cdot 2(\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{0})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{0}}{\beta_{T}-\beta_{s}}

(4.36)

Substituting this expression for the score into the backward SDE, we get the SDE of a
Brownian bridge
:

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\frac{\bm{x}_{0}-\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}}{\beta_{T}-\beta_{s}}\bigg]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}

(4.37)

In practice, we do not know clean samples
\bm{x}_{0}\sim p_{\text{data}}
during the backward process, so instead of initializing the distribution from
\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{x}_{0},\beta_{T}\bm{I}_{d})
, we approximate it with
\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\beta_{T}\bm{I}_{d})
, which works when
\beta_{T}
is large relative to the data variance.

We can also define the time-reversal formula for a
controlled
SDE by simply replacing the drift
\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)
with a controlled drift
(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t))
and following the same derivation.

Corollary 4.5

(Time Reversal for Controlled SDEs)
.

Consider a forward-time controlled SDE of the form:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(4.38)

where
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
is the control drift and
\sigma_{t}
is the scalar diffusion coefficient. Let
p_{t}(\bm{x})
denote the marginal density of
\bm{X}_{t}
at time
t
. Then, the time-reversed process
\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}:=\bm{X}_{T-s}
follows the SDE:

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\left[-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)-\sigma_{T-s}\bm{u}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log p(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)\right]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}

(4.39)

and the pair of forward and reverse-time SDEs is given by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\left[-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)-\sigma_{T-s}\bm{u}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log p(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)\right]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}\end{cases}

(4.40)

The
key takeaway
from this section is that reversing a forward-time stochastic process is not as simple as inverting the time coordinate of the trajectories. While the forward process propagates densities
p_{t}
according to its drift
\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)
and diffusion coefficients, running the process backward requires deriving a
new stochastic process
whose drift correctly propagates the marginal densities in the reverse time direction such that it reconstructs the same marginal densities as the forward process
s=T-t\in[0,T]
.

The final (
Time Reversal Formula
) reveals that the reverse-time drift includes the negative forward drift
-\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{s},T-s)
and an
additional correction proportional to the score function

\nabla\log p_{T-s}(\bm{x})
, which compensates for the spreading of probability density from the forward diffusion. This insight is fundamental to modern generative modeling frameworks, such as score-based diffusion models and Schrödinger bridge methods, where learning the score function enables simulation of reverse-time dynamics and thus the generation of samples from complex target distributions.

4.3
Forward-Backward Stochastic Differential Equations

Figure 7:
Time Reversal vs. Forward-Backward SDE.

Top
: Reversing an uncontrolled forward diffusion requires correcting the drift using the score function
\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})
, which depends on the evolving marginal density and can lead to unstable or uncontrolled trajectories.
Bottom
: In the more general Schrödinger bridge setting, the forward process evolves via a control drift
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t):=\sigma_{t}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
which contains the forward Schrödinger potential
\varphi_{t}
. This controlled forward process induces a corresponding backward SDE with the control
\sigma_{t}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})
, containing the backward Schrödinger potential
\hat{\varphi}_{t}
. This enables accurate transport between two structured distributions with optimal bridge dynamics.

A
key limitation
of the standard time-reversal formula from Section
4.2
is that it assumes an
uncontrolled
forward SDE, where the drift
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
is deterministic and can be easily simulated in either direction, such as denoising diffusion, where the forward process is a known variance-exploding SDE. In the general Schrödinger bridge setting, however, the goal is to interpolate between two
structured distributions
, and the optimal forward drift that transports
\pi_{0}
to
\pi_{T}
is itself
unknown
and must be solved for as part of the optimization problem. As a result, (
Time Reversal Formula
) is no longer sufficient on its own, and we must instead derive a coupled pair of
forward-backward SDEs
that characterizes both the forward and backward controlled dynamics.

Recall the (
Dynamic SB Problem
), where the optimal control and optimal marginal density
(\bm{u}^{\star},p_{t}^{\star})
are defined by the non-linear (
HJB-FP System
) as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\psi_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}\|^{2}+\langle\nabla\psi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle=-\sigma_{t}^{2}\Delta\psi_{t}\\
\partial_{t}p^{\star}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\psi))=\sigma_{t}^{2}\Delta p^{\star}_{t}\end{cases}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}p^{\star}_{0}(\bm{x})=\pi_{0}\\
p^{\star}_{T}(\bm{x})=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(4.41)

While the Hopf-Cole transform described in Section
2.8
transforms the non-linear PDEs into linear PDEs, solving them remains challenging. In Section
6.1
, we show that score-based generative modeling can be derived from the time reversal of a forward SDE described in Section
4.2
. This naturally leads to the question:
how can we turn the PDEs defining the solution to the dynamic SB problem into SDEs that can be solved with likelihood training?

To answer this, we can leverage the theory of
forward-backward SDEs

(
exarchos2018stochastic
)
. This theory extends the Feynman-Kac theory from Section
2.4
, which represents solutions of certain linear parabolic PDEs as expectations over trajectories of a forward stochastic process. Forward–backward SDE theory generalizes this idea by introducing a
coupled system consisting of a forward SDE that generates trajectories and a backward stochastic process that evolves along those trajectories
and encodes the solution of the PDE.

Forward-Backward SDE Theory

Consider a function
\psi_{t}(\bm{x}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}\in C^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T])
and a
parabolic PDE
of the form:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\psi_{t}+\langle\bm{f},\nabla\psi_{t}\rangle+\bm{h}(\bm{x},t,\psi_{t},\sigma_{t}\nabla\psi_{t})=0,\quad\psi_{T}(\bm{x})=\Phi(\bm{x})

(4.42)

where
\psi_{T}(\bm{x})=\Phi(\bm{x})
is the terminal constraint. Suppose the state
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
is a stochastic process
\bm{X}_{0:T}
that evolves via a forward SDE defined as:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}

(4.43)

Then, we can define the stochastic process
\bm{Y}_{t}:=\psi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
and
\bm{Z}_{t}:=\sigma_{t}\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
which evolves via the
backward SDE
defined as:

\displaystyle d\bm{Y}_{t}=-\bm{h}(\bm{X}_{t},t,\bm{Y}_{t},\bm{Z}_{t})dt+\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{Y}_{T}=\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})

(4.44)

such that the solution
\psi_{t}
to (
4.42
) is equivalent to the solution to the forward-backward SDEs defined in (
4.43
) and (
4.44
).

While this gives us a way to convert a single non-linear PDE into a pair of FBSDEs, the
dynamic SB problem with arbitrary reference dynamics
is defined by a
pair of coupled PDEs
given in Section
2.8
by the Hopf-Cole transform. Therefore, we must derive a
system of forward-backward SDEs that describes the evolution of both potentials
.

Forward-Backward SDEs for Dynamic Schrödinger Bridge

In Section
2.8
, we transformed the non-linear (
HJB-FP System
) with the
Hopf-Cole transform
to get the system of
linear PDEs
that defines the solution to the Schrödinger bridge problem with arbitrary reference dynamics:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}+\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle=-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}\\
\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\hat{\varphi}_{t}\bm{f})=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}\end{cases}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}p^{\star}_{0}=\varphi_{0}\hat{\varphi}_{0}\\
p^{\star}_{T}=\varphi_{T}\hat{\varphi}_{T}\end{cases}

(4.45)

whre
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
are the pair of
Schrödinger potentials
that
uniquely
characterize the optimal control and optimal state PDF
(\bm{u}^{\star},p_{t}^{\star})
given by:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})

(4.46)

\displaystyle p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})

(4.47)

Given the
stochastic process

\bm{X}_{0:T}
, we can define the random variables associated with the forward and backward potentials as:

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{t}\equiv\bm{Y}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t},t)=\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t}),\quad

\displaystyle\bm{Z}_{t}\equiv\bm{Z}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t},t)=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})

(4.48)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}\equiv\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t},t)=\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t}),\quad

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\equiv\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t},t)=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})

(4.49)

Then, the forward-backward SDEs that define the evolution of these random variables are given by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t}=\left(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{Z}_{t}\right)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
d\bm{Y}_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}_{t}\|^{2}dt+\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
d\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}=\big[\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}-\bm{f})+\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\|^{2}+\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\big]dt+\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\end{cases}\text{s.t.}\begin{cases}\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}\\
\bm{Y}_{T}+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{T}=\log\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\\
\bm{Y}_{t}+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}=\log p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\\
\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},t)=\bm{Z}_{t}\end{cases}

(4.50)

where the solution to the FBSDEs in (
4.50
) is the solution to the linear PDEs in (
4.45
).

Derivation.

Step 1: Derive the Stochastic Processes that Encode Optimality Conditions.

Recall from Theorem
2.24
that the dynamic SB problem has the control drift
\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
which defines the forward SDE:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\left[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\right]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(4.51)

Furthermore, the optimal marginal density
p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})
is defined by both the forward and backward potentials
p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})
, which can be transformed to a logarithm as:

\displaystyle\log p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})+\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})

(4.52)

Therefore, both the optimal control drift and marginal density can be expressed as the logarithm of the SB potentials
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
. The evolution of
\log\varphi_{t}
and
\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}
along the stochastic trajectories
\bm{X}_{0:T}
can be described by a pair of stochastic processes
(\bm{Y}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
and
(\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
defined as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\bm{Y}_{t}:=\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\\
\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}:=\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\end{cases}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\log p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})=\bm{Y}_{t}+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}

(4.53)

Unlike score-based generative modeling, the score function
\nabla\log q_{t}(\bm{x})
is replaced with the gradient of the log potentials
\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
and
\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})
. Since the gradient of a random variable defined by a stochastic process is also a random variable, we define the auxillary stochastic processes
(\bm{Z}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
and
(\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
which track the
gradient
of the log potentials:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\bm{Z}_{t}:=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\\
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}:=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\end{cases}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},t)=\bm{Z}_{t}

(4.54)

which transforms the forward SDE into:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\left[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{Z}_{t}\right]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(4.55)

Now that we have defined the time-indexed random variables
(\bm{Y}_{t},\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t})
and
(\bm{Z}_{t},\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t})
that encode the optimality constraints, we can derive the corresponding forward-backward SDEs.

Step 2: Derive the Forward-Backward SDEs.

To determine the time-evolution of
\bm{Y}_{t}
and
\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}
, we apply (
Itô’s Formula
). For simplicity, we will drop explicit
(\bm{X}_{t},t)
dependence and denote
\varphi_{t}\equiv\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
and
\bm{f}\equiv\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)
throughout the derivation. Starting with
\bm{Y}_{t}=\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
, we apply Itô’s formula to get the SDE:

\displaystyle d\log\varphi_{t}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\partial_{t}\log\varphi_{t}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\langle\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t},\nabla\log\varphi_{t}\rangle}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\log\varphi_{t}\bigg]dt+\nabla\log\varphi_{t}^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\underbrace{\partial_{t}\log\varphi_{t}}_{\text{given by PDE}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\langle\bm{f},\nabla\log\varphi_{t}\rangle+\sigma^{2}_{t}\|\nabla\log\varphi_{t}\|^{2}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\log\varphi_{t}\bigg]dt+\nabla\log\varphi_{t}^{\top}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(4.56)

From the (
Hopf-Cole PDEs
), we have that the potential function
\varphi_{t}
evolves via the linear PDE given by:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}=-\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}

(4.57)

Using the chain rule, we have
\partial_{t}\log\varphi_{t}=\frac{\partial\log\varphi_{t}}{\partial\varphi_{t}}\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}
, so the PDE gives us the following form of the time evolution for
\log\varphi_{t}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\log\varphi_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\log\varphi_{t}}{\partial\varphi_{t}}\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}=\frac{1}{\varphi_{t}}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\bigg\langle\underbrace{\frac{\nabla\varphi_{t}}{\varphi}}_{=\nabla\log\varphi_{t}},\bm{f}\bigg\rangle-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\frac{1}{\varphi_{t}}\Delta\varphi_{t}={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\left\langle\nabla\log\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\right\rangle-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\log\varphi_{t}}

(4.58)

Substituting the expression for
\partial_{t}\log\varphi_{t}
into the SDE (
4.56
), we get:

\displaystyle d\log\varphi_{t}

\displaystyle=\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\partial_{t}\log\varphi_{t}}_{\text{given by PDE}}}+\langle\bm{f},\nabla\log\varphi_{t}\rangle+\sigma^{2}_{t}\|\nabla\log\varphi_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\log\varphi_{t}\bigg]dt+(\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t})^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\left\langle\nabla\log\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\right\rangle-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\log\varphi_{t}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\langle\bm{f},\nabla\log\varphi_{t}\rangle}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\|\nabla\log\varphi_{t}\|^{2}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\log\varphi_{t}}\bigg]dt+(\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t})^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle=\|\underbrace{\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}}_{=:\bm{Z}_{t}}\|^{2}dt+\underbrace{(\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t})^{\top}}_{=:\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}_{t}\|^{2}dt+\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

(4.59)

which is exactly the SDE for
\bm{Y}_{t}
:

\displaystyle\boxed{d\bm{Y}_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}_{t}\|^{2}dt+\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}}

(4.60)

Now, we apply Itô’s formula to
\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}=\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
to get:

\displaystyle d\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t},t)=\bigg[\partial_{t}\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}+(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log\varphi_{t})^{\top}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\bigg]dt+(\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\underbrace{\partial_{t}\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}_{\text{given by PDE}}+\langle\bm{f},\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\rangle+\langle\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log\varphi_{t},\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\bigg]dt+(\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

(4.61)

Similarly to
\varphi_{t}
, the (
Hopf-Cole PDEs
) defines the time evolution of
\hat{\varphi}
via the following linear PDE:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(\hat{\varphi}_{t}\bm{f})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}

(4.62)

Applying the chain rule, we have:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\partial\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}=\frac{1}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\left(-\nabla\cdot(\hat{\varphi}_{t}\bm{f})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=-\underbrace{\frac{1}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}(\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}}_{\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\cdot\bm{f})-\frac{1}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\frac{\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}=-\langle\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t},\bm{f}\rangle-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}

(4.63)

Substituting this expression into the SDE (
4.61
), we can cancel like terms to get:

\displaystyle d\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})

\displaystyle=\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\langle\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t},\bm{f}\rangle-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}}+\langle\bm{f},\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt+\langle\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log\varphi_{t},\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\bigg]dt+(\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bigg[-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}+\langle\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log\varphi_{t},\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\bigg]dt+(\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

(4.64)

We can expand the Laplacian using the product rule:

\displaystyle\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\hat{\varphi}_{t}^{-1}\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t})={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla(\hat{\varphi}_{t}^{-1})}^{\top}\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\hat{\varphi}_{t}^{-1}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(-\hat{\varphi}_{t}^{-2}\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t})}^{\top}\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\hat{\varphi}_{t}^{-1}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}=-\frac{\|\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}\|^{2}}{\varphi^{2}_{t}}+\frac{\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}

\displaystyle=-\|\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}

(4.65)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}=\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\|\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\|^{2}

(4.66)

Substituting the expression for
\frac{\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}
back into (
4.64
), we get:

\displaystyle d\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})

\displaystyle=\bigg[-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\|\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\|^{2}}\right)+\langle\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log\varphi_{t},\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\rangle+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\bigg]dt+(\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\underbrace{-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}^{2}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}}_{\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}-\bm{f})}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\|^{2}}+\langle\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log\varphi_{t},\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\rangle\bigg]dt+(\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\nabla\cdot({\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}_{=:\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}}}-\bm{f})+\frac{1}{2}\|{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}_{=:\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}}}\|^{2}+\langle\underbrace{\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}}_{=:\bm{Z}_{t}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}_{=:\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}}}\rangle\bigg]dt+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{(\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})^{\top}}_{=:\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\top}}}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\nabla\cdot({\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}}-\bm{f})+\frac{1}{2}\|{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}}\|^{2}+\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}}\bigg]dt+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\top}}d\bm{B}_{t}

(4.67)

which gives us the SDE for
\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}
:

\displaystyle\boxed{d\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}=\bigg[\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}-\bm{f})+\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\|^{2}+\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\bigg]dt+\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}}

(4.68)

Finally, by combining the three SDEs, we have shown that the solution to the dynamic SB problem is equivalent to the solution to the system of three FBSDEs.

\displaystyle\boxed{\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t}=\left(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{Z}_{t}\right)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=(-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{s})ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\tilde{\bm{B}}_{s}\\
d\bm{Y}_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}_{t}\|^{2}dt+\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
d\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}=\big[\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}-\bm{f})+\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\|^{2}+\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\big]dt+\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\end{cases}}

(Forward-Backward SDEs)

which describe the evolution of the Schrödinger bridge potentials
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
that characterize the optimal control drift
\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},t)=\bm{Z}_{t}
and marginal density
\log p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})=\bm{Y}_{t}+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}
as SDEs that can be simulated forward and backward in time.
\square

We now connect the FBSDE representation to the time-reversal representation and show that the Schrödinger bridge potentials yield a
generalization
of the time-reversal strategy to settings where the forward SDE has an additional
non-deterministic control drift

\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
.

Remark 4.6

(Forward-Backward SDE Theory Generalizes the Time-Reversal Formula)
.

The forward–backward SDE formulation provides a natural generalization of the classical time-reversal formula for uncontrolled forward SDEs. In the special case where the forward dynamics contain no control drift (i.e.,
\bm{Z}_{t}\equiv\bm{0}
), the forward SDE reduces to the reference diffusion, and the backward control
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}
becomes proportional to the
score
of the marginal density
p^{\star}_{t}
:

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{t}=\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\equiv 0

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\log p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})=0+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}=\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t})

(4.69)

which yields a reverse-time SDE that is exactly the time-reversal SDE derived in (
Time Reversal Formula
):

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\left[-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log p(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)\right]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}

(4.70)

that characterize the generation process of
score-based diffusion models
. Therefore, we can interpret forward-backward SDEs as a generalization of the time-reversal formula and the dynamic SB problem as a generalization of score-based diffusion.

In Section
6.2
, we will turn this FBSDE construction into a tractable likelihood-based training objective. However, before introducing any training formulations, we continue our exploration of building Schrödinger bridges. Next, we discuss endpoint conditioning via Doob’s
h
-transform, which provides additional insight into the structure of Schrödinger bridges.

4.4
Doob’s
h
-Transform

The
central goal
when solving the Schrödinger bridge problem is to derive a controlled stochastic process by minimally shaping a reference stochastic process that originates an initial distribution
\pi_{0}
by
conditioning
it on a terminal distribution
\pi_{T}
. One way to achieve this conditioned process is using
Doob’s
h
-Transform

(
rogers2000diffusions
;
sarkka2019applied
)
, which introduces a
tilting function
, known as the
h
-function, such that multiplying the transition density of the reference stochastic process by the
h
-function and re-normalizing produces the transition density of the optimal Schrödinger bridge.

Proposition 4.7

(Doob’s
h
-Transform)
.

Under a reference stochastic process
\mathbb{Q}
, let
\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})
denote the transition kernel from
\bm{x}
at
t
to
\bm{y}
at time
\tau\geq t
. Define a function
h(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
that satisfies the following space-time Markov consistency property:

\displaystyle h(\bm{x},t)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})h(\bm{y},\tau)d\bm{y}

(
h
-Function)

Then, we define the stochastic process
\mathbb{P}^{h}
by
tilting
the reference process
\mathbb{Q}
as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{h}(\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})=\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})\frac{h(\bm{y},\tau)}{h(\bm{x},t)}

(Doob’s
h
-Transform)

where
\mathbb{P}^{h}(\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})
is the tilted transition kernel of
\mathbb{P}^{h}
. Then,
\mathbb{P}^{h}
is Markov and has the associated SDE:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\left[\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t)\right]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Doob’s
h
-Transform SDE)

Proof.
First, we show that the tilted transition density integrates to one:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{P}^{h}(\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})d\bm{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})\frac{h(\bm{y},\tau)}{h(\bm{x},t)}d\bm{y}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h(\bm{x},t)}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})h(\bm{y},\tau)d\bm{y}}_{=:h(\bm{x},t)}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}=1}

(4.71)

which proves that
\mathbb{P}^{h}
is a valid Markov process which depends only on
\bm{X}_{t}
. Next, we will derive the corresponding SDE that generates
\mathbb{P}^{h}
by defining a smooth test function
\phi(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}\in C^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T])
and derive the expression for the generator
\mathcal{A}_{t}
and using Itô’s formula to recover the expression for the drift field
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)
. By definition, the generator of a stochastic process is given by:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}^{h}_{t}\phi)(\bm{x})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left\{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}^{h}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]}-\phi(\bm{x})}{\Delta t}\right\}

(4.72)

From (
Doob’s

h

-Transform
), we have that the expectation of the test function evaluated on the tilted measure can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{h}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{h(\bm{x},t)}\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)h(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]

(4.73)

which can be substituted back into (
4.72
) to get:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}^{h}_{t}\phi)(\bm{X}_{t})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left\{\frac{{\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}h(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]}-\phi(\bm{x}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}h(\bm{x},t)}}{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}h(\bm{x},t)}\Delta t}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h(\bm{x},t)}\underbrace{\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left\{\frac{{\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)h(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]-\phi(\bm{x})h(\bm{x},t)}{\Delta t}\right\}}_{=:{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathcal{A}_{t}(\phi(\bm{x})h(\bm{x},t))}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h(\bm{x},t)}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathcal{A}_{t}(\phi(\bm{x})h(\bm{x},t))}

(4.74)

where
\mathcal{A}_{t}
is the generator of the untilted reference process defined in (
Uncontrolled Generator
). Now, using the expanded form of the generator derived in (
Uncontrolled Generator
) and denoting
\phi\equiv\phi(\bm{x},t)
and
h\equiv h(\bm{x},t)
for simplicity, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{h}_{t}\phi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}(\phi h)}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\langle\bm{f},\nabla(\phi h)\rangle}+{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta(\phi h)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\phi\partial_{t}h}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\langle\bm{f},h\nabla\phi\rangle+\langle\bm{f},\phi\nabla h\rangle}+{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\nabla\cdot(h\nabla\phi+\phi\nabla h)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\phi\partial_{t}h}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}h\langle\bm{f},\nabla\phi\rangle+\phi\langle\bm{f},\nabla h\rangle}+{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}(h\Delta\phi+\phi\Delta h+2\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla h)}\right]

Since, we defined the
h
-function as a conditional expectation
h(\bm{x},t)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h(\bm{X}_{\tau},\tau)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]
, it satisfies the (
Martingale Property
), and the expression for the unconditional generator vanishes:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{h}_{t}\phi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\phi\underbrace{\left[\partial_{t}h+\langle\bm{f},\nabla h\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta h\right]}_{=\mathcal{A}_{t}h=0\text{ ($h$ is a Martingale)}}+\frac{1}{h}\left[h\langle\bm{f},\nabla\phi\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}(h\Delta\phi+2\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla h)\right]

\displaystyle=\langle\bm{f},\nabla\phi\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\phi\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\frac{\nabla h}{h}}_{\nabla\log h}}

\displaystyle=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{f}},\nabla\phi\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi+\langle\nabla\phi,{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log h}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log h}_{\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)}},\nabla\phi\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi

(4.76)

which means the control drift of the Doob
h
-transformed process is defined as
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t):=\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t)
and the corresponding SDE is:

\displaystyle\boxed{d\bm{X}_{t}=\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t)}\right]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}}

(Doob’s
h
-Transform SDE)

and we conclude our proof.
\square

The previous derivation explicitly shows how reweighting a reference diffusion with a positive space–time function
h(\bm{x},t)
modifies the infinitesimal generator of the process. The following Corollary summarizes this result and provides an
expression for the generator of the reweighted path measure
in compact form.

Corollary 4.8

(Generator of Reweighted Path Measure)
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}
be a reference path measure with infinitesimal generator
\mathcal{A}_{t}
and
h(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
be the
h
-function defined in (

h

-Function
). Then, the path measure
reweighted by
h
is the
h
-transform of
\mathbb{Q}
with generator defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t}^{h}\phi(\bm{x}):=\frac{\mathcal{A}_{t}(\phi(\bm{x})h(\bm{x},t))-\phi(\bm{x})\mathcal{A}_{t}h(\bm{x},t)}{h(\bm{x},t)}=\mathcal{A}_{t}\phi(\bm{x})+\langle\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t),\nabla\phi(\bm{x})\rangle

(4.77)

Therefore, Doob’s
h
-transform provides a precise mechanism for incorporating endpoint information into the dynamics of a reference process. This mechanism allows us to define an
h
-function that exactly recovers the Markov dynamics of the optimal Schrödinger bridge.

Corollary 4.9

(Schrödinger Bridge as Doob’s
h
-Transform)
.

Given the Schrödinger potentials
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
that generate the solution to the (
Dynamic SB Problem
), we can define the
h
-function as:

\displaystyle h(\bm{x},t):=\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})d\bm{y}

(
h
-Function for SB)

Then, the optimal Schrödinger bridge path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is the Doob’s
h
-transform of
\mathbb{Q}
, where for any
0\leq t\leq\tau\leq T
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})=\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})\frac{h(\bm{y},\tau)}{h(\bm{x},t)}

(Doob’s
h
-Transform for SB)

Equivalently,
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is Markov and has the associated SDE:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\left[\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t)\right]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Doob’s
h
-Transform SDE)

Proof Sketch.
From the derivation of the (
Schrödinger Potentials
), the optimal path measure satisfies:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})

(4.78)

Conditioning on
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
and using the Markov property of
\mathbb{Q}
, we observe that the future evolution from time
t
to
\tau
depends
only on the terminal reweighting
through:

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]

(4.79)

Therefore, the conditional transition kernel under
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is obtained by tilting the reference kernel by the ratio
\frac{\varphi_{\tau}(\bm{y})}{\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})}
, which is exactly Doob’s
h
-transform.
\square

This perspective shows that the Schrödinger bridge corresponds to a precise reweighting the reference process
\mathbb{Q}
through a harmonic function
h(\bm{x},t):=\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
which modifies the forward drift by
\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t)
. The resulting process therefore evolves according to the controlled drift
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t)
, which can be interpreted as the
minimal modification of the reference dynamics required to enforce the desired endpoint constraints
.

4.5
Markovian and Reciprocal Projections

In many settings, the Schrödinger bridge yields a path measure whose dependencies span the entire trajectory, making direct simulation difficult. This raises a natural question:
Can we approximate a general bridge by a Markov process that is easier to simulate, while remaining as close as possible in relative entropy?

The
Markovian projection
provides a principled answer to this question. Given an arbitrary path measure, we project it onto the space of Markov measures
\mathcal{M}
by minimizing KL divergence over processes whose future states depend
only on the present state
. This produces the closest Markov approximation to the original bridge. Formally, we define the
space of Markov measures
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}:=\big\{\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))\;|\;\forall 0\leq s<t\leq T,\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{M}}[f(\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{s}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{M}}[f(\bm{X}_{t})|\mathcal{F}_{s}]\big\}

(4.80)

where
\mathcal{F}_{s}
denotes the filtration generated by the process up to time
s
(Definition
2.3
).

In the Schrödinger bridge setting, we seek to construct a stochastic bridge between empirical endpoint distributions
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
that is close in relative entropy to a reference measure
\mathbb{Q}
defined by the SDE:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}

\displaystyle:d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Reference SDE)

Given a coupling
\pi_{0,T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
between the endpoint distributions, we will consider the path measure generated by
mixture of endpoint-conditioned bridges
defined as:

\displaystyle\Pi=\pi_{0,T}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))

(Mixture of Bridges)

which can be interpreted as constructing a bridge by first sampling endpoints
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim\Pi_{0,T}
and then generating the intermediate bridge dynamics according to the reference path measure conditioned on the endpoints. For fixed
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
, the corresponding
conditional bridge dynamics
under the reference measure
\mathbb{Q}
are given by the (
Doob’s

h

-Transform SDE
) defined as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})

\displaystyle:d\bm{X}_{t}=\left[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})\right]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Bridge SDE)

where
\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})
is the probability of reaching
\bm{x}_{T}
from the current state
\bm{X}_{t}
via the SDE of
\mathbb{Q}
. Since the drift in (
Bridge SDE
) is
conditioned on the future endpoint

\bm{x}_{T}
, it is not Markov with respect to
\bm{X}_{t}
. Therefore, simulating the SDE requires evaluating the transition density
\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})
at every step, which in general is computationally intractable.

To obtain a tractable process, we instead construct the
Markovian projection of the bridge measure
\Pi
, which is the Markov process whose drift depends only on the current state
\bm{X}_{t}
and that minimizes the KL divergence to the original bridge measure.

Proposition 4.10

(Markovian Projection (Proposition 2 in
shi2023diffusion
))
.

Consider a
mixture of bridges

\Pi=\Pi_{0,T}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}
that bridges distributions
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
via the endpoint law
\Pi_{0,T}
, where each conditional bridge is defined by (
Bridge SDE
) generated from the reference measure. Then, the
Markovian projection
of
\Pi
is denoted:

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}^{\star}:=\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)\in\mathcal{M}

(Markovian Projection)

with the associated SDE:

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}^{\star}:\;

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\left[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Markovian Projection SDE)

\displaystyle\text{s.t. }\quad\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|t}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(Markovian Projection Drift)

where
\sigma_{t}>0
. Then,
\mathbb{M}^{\star}
satisfies the following properties:

(i)

It is the Markov measure
\mathbb{M}^{\star}\in\mathcal{M}
that
minimizes the reverse KL divergence
with the mixture of bridges
\Pi
such that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}^{\star}

\displaystyle=\underset{\mathbb{M}}{\arg\min}\{\text{KL}\left(\Pi|\mathbb{M}\right):\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{M}\}

(4.81)

\displaystyle\text{KL}\left(\Pi|\mathbb{M}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}\bigg[\big\|\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|0,t}}\big[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}\big]-\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\big\|^{2}\bigg]

(4.82)

(ii)

It preserves the time marginals of
\Pi_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
such that:

\displaystyle\forall t\in[0,T],\quad\mathbb{M}^{\star}_{t}=\Pi_{t}

(4.83)

Proof.
We break down the proof in three steps.

Step 1: Derive the Optimal Control Drift of the Markovian Projection.

Recall that the bridge path measure can be obtained by reweighting the reference measure
\mathbb{Q}
by the Radon–Nikodym derivative of the endpoint coupling
\Pi_{0,T}
with respect to the reference endpoint law
\mathbb{Q}_{0,T}
:

\displaystyle\pi_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T}):=\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{0,T}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}_{0,T}}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\implies\Pi(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\pi_{0,T}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})

(4.84)

Conditioning on the initial state
\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}
, the corresponding
conditional bridge law
becomes:

\displaystyle\Pi(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0})=h(\bm{X}_{T},T)\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}),\hskip 9.24994pt\text{where}\hskip 9.24994pth(\bm{X}_{T},T):=\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0})}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0})}

(4.85)

This means that given an initial state
\bm{x}_{0}
, the bridge law is obtained by reweighting the reference diffusion by a terminal weight. This is exactly the (

h

-Function
) from (
Doob’s

h

-Transform
) for time
t\to T
with
h
-function defined as:

\displaystyle h(\bm{x},t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})h(\bm{X}_{T},T)d\bm{x}_{T}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h(\bm{X}_{T},T)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x},\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}]

(Doob’s
h
-transform)

which is the
conditional expectation of the endpoint weight under the reference process
. Corollary
4.8
establishes the fact that reweighting paths from
\mathbb{Q}
by the
h
-function changes the generator by adding the drift
\langle\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t),\nabla\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle
which is still Markov. Therefore, the bridge measure
\Pi^{\cdot|0=\bm{x}_{0}}
conditioned on
\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}
follows the SDE:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\left[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\log h(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(4.86)

To derive the form of the correction term
\nabla\log h(\bm{X}_{t},t)
in terms of
\mathbb{Q}
, we can differentiate (
Doob’s

h

-transform
) with respect to
\bm{x}
to get:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla}h(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})h(\bm{X}_{T},T)d\bm{x}_{T}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h(\bm{X}_{T},T){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla}\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})d\bm{x}_{T}

(4.87)

To get the expression for the gradient of the logarithm, we divide both sides by
h(\bm{x},t)
to get:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{h(\bm{X}_{T},T)}{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}h(\bm{x},t)}}\nabla\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})d\bm{x}_{T}

(4.88)

Since we want to obtain a
score-like
function for the reference process, we can rewrite
\nabla\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}=\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}
then substitute the identity
h(\bm{x},T)=\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{T|0}}{d\mathbb{Q}_{T|0}}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0})
from (
4.85
) to get:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}h(\bm{X}_{T},T)}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})}}{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}h(\bm{x},t)}}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})d\bm{x}_{T}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\Pi_{T|0}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x}_{0})}\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathbb{Q}_{T|0}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x}_{0})}h(\bm{x},t)}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})d\bm{x}_{T}

(4.89)

The ratio
\frac{\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})}{\mathbb{Q}_{T|0}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x}_{0})}
between reference conditionals can be rewritten using the
Markov property of the reference measure
which states that the distribution of
\bm{x},\bm{x}_{T}
given
\bm{x}_{0}
is equal, regardless of the order of sampling
0\to T\to t
or
0\to t\to T
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{T|0}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x}_{0})\mathbb{Q}_{t|0,T}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0})\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})}{\mathbb{Q}_{T|0}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x}_{0})}}=\frac{\mathbb{Q}_{t|0,T}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}{\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0})}

(4.90)

Substituting the expression into (
4.89
), we get:

\displaystyle\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\Pi_{T|0}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x}_{0})\mathbb{Q}_{t|0,T}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0})h(\bm{x},t)}}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})d\bm{x}_{T}

(4.91)

Now, we observe that the
numerator

\Pi_{T|0}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x}_{0})\mathbb{Q}_{t|0,T}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
is equivalent to the joint law of
(\bm{x},\bm{x}_{T})
given
\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}
:

\displaystyle\Pi_{T|0}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x}_{0})\mathbb{Q}_{t|0,T}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})=\Pi_{t,T|0}(\bm{x},\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x}_{0})

(Numerator of (
4.91
))

Furthermore, marginalizing the conditional bridge law
\Pi(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0})=h(\bm{X}_{T},T)\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0})
in (
4.85
) to time
t
gives us the following expression for the
denominator
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0})h(\bm{x},t)=\Pi_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0})

(Denominator of (
4.91
))

by the definition of (
Doob’s

h

-transform
) as
h(\bm{x},t)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[h(\bm{X}_{T},T)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x},\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}]
. Plugging these expressions into (
4.91
) yields:

\displaystyle\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\frac{\Pi_{t,T|0}(\bm{x},\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x}_{0})}{\Pi_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0})}}_{=\Pi_{T|0,t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x},\bm{x}_{0}}}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})d\bm{x}_{T}

(Bayes’ rule)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})\mathrm{d}\Pi_{T|0,t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x},\bm{x}_{0})}_{\text{expectation over $\Pi$ given $\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}$ and $\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}$}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|t,0}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})\big|\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0},\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(4.92)

which can be used to express the correction drift in (
4.86
) as a conditional expectation of endpoint-conditioned drifts. Substituting this expression into the SDE for
\Pi_{t|0}
defined in (
4.86
), we get an SDE with
non-Markov drift
:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\left[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|t,0}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})\big|\bm{X}_{t},\bm{X}_{0}\right]\right]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(4.93)

Step 2: Show that Markovian Projection is the Minimizer of Reverse KL Divergence.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be an arbitrary Markov path measure that evolves via the SDE
d\bm{X}_{t}=[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
. Then, we can write the KL divergence between
\Pi
and
\mathbb{M}
with the same diffusion coefficient
\sigma_{t}
and using Corollary
2.21
as the squared difference between their drifts:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\Pi\|\mathbb{M})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}\left[\|\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|0,t}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})\big|\bm{X}_{t},\bm{X}_{0}\right]-\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}\right]dt

(4.94)

Minimizing this KL yields the optimal
\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)
that best approximates the non-Markovian drift
\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|t,0}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})\big|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{t}\right]
using only
\bm{X}_{t}
across all time steps
t
. For any random variable
\bm{Z}
, the minimizer of the expected squared loss with respect to a function is equal to the expectation of the random variable given only the input to the function:

\displaystyle\underset{\bm{b}(\bm{X}_{t})}{\arg\min}\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{Z}-\bm{b}(\bm{X}_{t})\|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\bm{Z}|\bm{X}_{t}]

(4.95)

Therefore, the minimizer is the optimal Markovian drift given by:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|t}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})\big|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(4.96)

which concludes the proof of the first condition
(i)
in Proposition
4.10
.

Step 3: Prove Equality of the Marginal Distributions.

To prove the second condition
(ii)
which states that
\mathbb{M}^{\star}_{t}=\Pi_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, we first establish that
\mathbb{M}^{\star}_{t}
and
\Pi_{t}
solve the same Fokker-Planck equation given by:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{X}_{t},t)=-\nabla\cdot\left((\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},t))p_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\right)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})

(Fokker-Planck Equation)

Since the divergence extracts only the drift that depends on
\bm{X}_{t}
, and the drift for
\Pi
contains only an additional dependence on
\bm{X}_{0}
, the
effective drift
in the (
Fokker-Planck Equation
) is equal for
\Pi_{t}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\star}_{t}
. Given that both diffusion coefficients are also equal, we conclude that they both satisfy the same (
Fokker-Planck Equation
). Following the uniqueness of the solution to the Fokker-Planck equation given fixed initial conditions
24
24

24

For proof, see
(
bogachev2021uniqueness
)
, we conclude that the time marginals are equal
\mathbb{M}^{\star}_{t}=\Pi_{t}
.
\square

While the Markovian projection provides a way of simulating endpoint-conditioned bridges with only dependence on the current state, it generally fails to preserve the
bridge measure
of
\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}\equiv\mathbb{Q}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
, which is the distribution over bridge paths conditioned on a pair of endpoints
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim\Pi_{0,T}
. To define a measure that exactly matches the bridge of the endpoint-conditioned reference measure
\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}
, we define the
reciprocal projection
which projects any path measure to the
reciprocal class

\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
of
\mathbb{Q}
. To understand the reciprocal projection, we first define a
reciprocal process
and a unique property that will become useful in later sections.

Proposition 4.11

(Reciprocal Processes (Lemma 1.4 in
jamison1974reciprocal
))
.

A stochastic process
(\bm{X}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
on a measurable state space
\mathcal{X}
is considered a
reciprocal process
if for any
0\leq s<t<\tau\leq T
and any bounded measurable function
\phi(\bm{x}):\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
, the
reciprocal property
holds:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{0:s},\bm{X}_{\tau:T}]=\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{s},\bm{X}_{\tau}]

(Reciprocal Property)

which means that the interior of any interval
(s,\tau)
is
conditionally independent
of the rest of the trajectory given the two boundary states
(\bm{X}_{s},\bm{X}_{\tau})
. While reciprocal processes are generally not Markov, fixing either boundary state (
\bm{X}_{s}
or
\bm{X}_{\tau})
to a constant yields a Markov process.

Proof.
To prove the Markov property of reciprocal processes given a fixed constant endpoint, we first observe that conditioning on a fixed constant does not change the conditional expectation. Suppose first that
\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bar{\bm{x}}
almost surely. Let
s\leq t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<t<\tau
and
\phi(\bm{x}):\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
be any bounded and measurable test function. Since
\bm{X}_{\tau}
is a constant, conditioning on
\bm{X}_{\tau}
does not change the conditional expectation:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t_{1}},\dots,\bm{X}_{t_{k}}]=\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t_{1}},\dots,\bm{X}_{t_{k}},{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{X}_{\tau}}].

(4.97)

By the
reciprocal property
, once the two boundary states
(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{\tau})
are given, the interior of the interval
(t_{k},\tau)
is conditionally independent of the past before
t_{n}
. Therefore, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t_{1}},\dots,\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{\tau}]=\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{\tau}].

(4.98)

Since
\bm{X}_{\tau}=\bar{\bm{x}}
is constant, this reduces to:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{\tau}]=\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t_{k}}].

(4.99)

Therefore, we conclude that the future state
\bm{X}_{t}
can be determined with only knowledge of the current state
\bm{X}_{t_{k}}
and independent of the past trajectory prior to
t_{k}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t_{1}},\dots,\bm{X}_{t_{k}}]=\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t_{k}}],

(4.100)

which is exactly the
Markov property
. If instead
\bm{X}_{s}
is fixed, the same conclusion follows by time reversal, since both the reciprocal and Markov properties are preserved under reversing time.
\square

Since we have shown that
reciprocal processes
are characterized by their
conditional independence structure
, we will now apply this idea to level of path measures. In particular, reciprocal processes naturally arise as mixtures of Markov bridges sharing the same conditional dynamics between endpoints. This observation leads to the notion of the
reciprocal class

\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
of a reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
.

Intuitively, all path measures in the reciprocal class
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
generate trajectories with the
same bridge dynamics
as
\mathbb{Q}
, differing only in how probability mass is assigned to the endpoint pairs
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
. Concretely, this class consists of all path measures obtained by reweighting the endpoint distribution of
\mathbb{Q}
while preserving its conditional bridge law.

Definition 4.12

(Reciprocal Class)
.

The
reciprocal class
of
\mathbb{Q}
is the collection of all path measures that share the same
conditional bridge distribution
given fixed endpoints
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim\Pi_{0,T}
but may differ in their endpoint law, which defines how the endpoints are weighted. Formally, we define the reciprocal class
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q}):=\{\Pi\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})):\Pi=\Pi_{0,T}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}\}

(4.101)

where
\Pi_{0,T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes an arbitrary endpoint coupling and
\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}
is the endpoint-conditioned bridge distribution of
\mathbb{Q}
. Equivalently, a path measure belongs in the reciprocal class
\Pi\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
if it admits the
mixture-of-bridges representation
:

\displaystyle\Pi(\cdot)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\mathrm{d}\Pi_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})

(4.102)

which can be written equivalently as
\Pi=\Pi_{0,T}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}
.

Having characterized the reciprocal class
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
as the family of path measures that share the same bridge dynamics as the reference process
\mathbb{Q}
, we now consider how to
approximate an arbitrary path measure

\mathbb{P}
by an element of the reciprocal class. A natural approach is to choose the measure in
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
that is closest to
\mathbb{P}
in relative entropy. This is exactly the goal of the
reciprocal projection
, which we show is the
unique minimizer
of the KL divergence from
\mathbb{P}
.

Proposition 4.13

(Reciprocal Projection (Proposition 4 in
shi2023diffusion
))
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}
be a reference path measure and
\mathbb{P}
be an arbitrary path measure that we wish to project onto the reciprocal class of
\mathbb{Q}
. The
reciprocal projection
of
\mathbb{P}
onto the reciprocal class
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
is defined as:

\displaystyle\Pi^{\star}:=\text{proj}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})}(\mathbb{P})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})

(Reciprocal Projection)

Then,
\Pi^{\star}
is the element of the reciprocal class which
minimizes the KL divergence
from
\mathbb{P}
:

\displaystyle\Pi^{\star}=\underset{\Pi\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})}{\arg\min}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi)

(4.103)

Furthermore, the reciprocal projection admits the
mixture-of-bridges representation
:

\displaystyle\Pi^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\iff\Pi^{\star}=\mathbb{P}_{0,T}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}

(4.104)

where
\mathbb{P}_{0,T}
denotes the endpoint distribution of
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
is the reference bridge conditioned on the endpoints.

Proof.
We start by applying the (
KL Divergence Chain Rule
) from Lemma
1.4
to decompose
\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi)
into the sum of the
divergence between endpoints
and the
divergence in bridge measure
:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{0,T}\|\Pi_{0,T})}_{\text{endpoint divergence}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\sim\mathbb{P}_{0,T}}\left[\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\cdot|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\|\Pi_{\cdot|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}))\right]}_{\text{bridge divergence}}

(4.105)

Since all elements of the reciprocal class
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
have the same bridge as
\mathbb{Q}
such that
\Pi_{\cdot|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})=\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
, we can write:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi)

\displaystyle=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{0,T}\|\Pi_{0,T})+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\sim\mathbb{P}_{0,T}}\left[\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\cdot|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\|{\color[rgb]{0.3125,0.6992,0.8438}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})})\right]}_{\text{not dependent on $\Pi$}}

(4.106)

Since the bridge divergence is no longer dependent on the measure
\Pi
which we are optimizing, the only term left to minimize is the endpoint divergence
\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{0,T}\|\Pi_{0,T})
, which is minimized
uniquely
when:

\displaystyle\Pi^{\star}_{0,T}=\mathbb{P}_{0,T}

(4.107)

which follows the
strict convexity of the KL divergence
. Given that
\Pi^{\star}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
and
\Pi^{\star}_{0,T}=\mathbb{P}_{0,T}
, we can write:

\displaystyle\Pi^{\star}(\cdot)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})

(4.108)

which can equivalently be written as
\Pi^{\star}=\mathbb{P}_{0,T}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}
and is exactly our definition of the (
Reciprocal Projection
).
\square

Now that we have shown that any path measure can be projected onto the reciprocal class
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
of a bridge measure
\mathbb{Q}
such that the bridge is preserved, we will show that there exists a
unique Markov measure in the reciprocal class

\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
which is also the solution to the (
2.2
).

Proposition 4.14

(Solution to Dynamic Schrödinger Bridge)
.

The
Markov measure in the reciprocal class
of
\mathbb{Q}
, i.e.,
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
, that satisfies
\mathbb{M}_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
\mathbb{M}_{T}=\pi_{T}
is the unique solution to the Schrödinger bridge
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}
.

Proof.
By definition of the reciprocal class
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
,
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{Q}
and share the exact same bridge such that
\mathbb{M}_{t|0,T}=\mathbb{Q}_{t|0,T}
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Thus, the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{M}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})
depends only on the endpoints
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})
and can be written as:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{M}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\xi(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})

(4.109)

where
\xi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a measurable function. Since we define
\mathbb{P}
to be Markov and the reference measure
\mathbb{Q}
is Markov by construction, conditioning on some intermediate state
\bm{X}_{t}
yields the factorization:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{M}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{t})}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T}|\bm{X}_{t})}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{M}(\bm{X}_{0:t},\bm{X}_{t:T}|\bm{X}_{t})}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:t},\bm{X}_{t:T}|\bm{X}_{t})}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{M}(\bm{X}_{0:t}|\bm{X}_{t})}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:t}|\bm{X}_{t})}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{M}(\bm{X}_{t:T}|\bm{X}_{t})}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{t:T}|\bm{X}_{t})}

(4.110)

where the past and future given
\bm{X}_{t}
are
independent
. Given that the RND depends only on the endpoints via the measurable function
\xi(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})
, there exist two measurable functions
a,b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
that depend only on each endpoint, respectively, such that:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{M}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\xi(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})=a(\bm{X}_{0})b(\bm{X}_{T})

(4.111)

Recall from Section
1
that the optimal endpoint law
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
that solves the static SB problem factorizes into Schrödinger potentials
(\varphi,\hat{\varphi})
that are
unique
up to a constant. Therefore, we conclude:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{M}}{d\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\frac{d\pi^{\star}_{0,T}}{dq}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})=e^{\varphi(\bm{X}_{0})}e^{\hat{\varphi}(\bm{X}_{T})}

(4.112)

and we have shown that the Markov measure in the reciprocal class
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
is
unique
and equals the Schrödinger bridge
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}
.
\square

Building on the ideas of Markovian and reciprocal projections, we can define the
Iterative Markovian Fitting
(IMF) scheme
(
shi2023diffusion
)
which constructs the optimal SB via alternating Markovian and reciprocal projections of an SDE which becomes the foundation of the practical DSBM algorithm which fits a parameterized velocity field by matching the control drift.

Figure 8:
Iterative Markovian Fitting (IMF) vs. Iterative Proportional Fitting (IPF).
While IPF alternates projections onto the marginal constraint sets
\Pi(\pi_{0},\cdot)
and
\Pi(\cdot,\pi_{T})
, intermediate iterations generally do not preserve the endpoint coupling. In contrast, IMF performs Markovian and reciprocal projections that maintain both marginal constraints
\mathbb{P}^{n}\in\Pi(\pi_{0},\pi_{T})
at every iteration, progressively refining the path measure until convergence to the optimal bridge measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
.

Iterative Markovian Fitting
(IMF) generates a sequence of path measures
(\mathbb{P}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
alternating Markovian projections and reciprocal projections
defined as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{2n+1}=\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbb{P}^{2n}),\quad\mathbb{P}^{2n+2}=\text{proj}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})}(\mathbb{P}^{2n+1})

(4.113)

where the first path measure in the sequence
\mathbb{P}^{0}
is the path measure in the reciprocal class of the reference measure
\mathbb{P}^{0}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
that satisfies the marginal constraints
\mathbb{P}^{0}_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
\mathbb{P}^{0}_{T}=\pi_{T}
. The IMF procedure is grounded in three
key theoretical results
which are summarized as follows:

(i)

Pythagorean Identity (Lemma
4.15
):
The KL divergence between a mixture of bridges
\Pi\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
in the recprocal class of
\mathbb{Q}
and an arbitrary Markov measure
\mathbb{M}\in\mathcal{M}
satisfies the Pythagorean identity:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\Pi\|\mathbb{M})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\text{KL}(\Pi|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)})}_{\text{distance to Markovian projection}}+\underbrace{\text{KL}({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)}\|\mathbb{M})}_{\text{remaining distance to target}}

(4.114)

Similarly, the KL divergence between a path measure
\mathbb{P}
and its reciprocal projection
\text{proj}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})}(\mathbb{P})
satisfies the Pythagorean identity defined as:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\text{proj}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})}}(\mathbb{P}))}_{\text{distance to reciprocal projection}}+\underbrace{\text{KL}({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\text{proj}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})}(\mathbb{P})}\|\Pi)}_{\text{remaining distance to target}}

(4.115)

(ii)

Monotone Improvement (Proposition
4.16
):
The distance to the optimal SB measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is
strictly decreasing
with each iteration of IMF, such that:

\displaystyle\text{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}^{n+1}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star}\right)\leq\text{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star}\right),\quad\lim_{n\to+\infty}\text{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{n+1}\right)=0

(4.116)

(iii)

Convergence Guarantee (Theroem
4.17
):
The sequence of path measures generated via IMF
(\mathbb{P}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to a unique fixed point
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
that is exactly the SB measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}\text{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star}\right)=0\quad\text{where}\quad\mathbb{P}^{\star}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}

(4.117)

To understand why each of these statements holds and build our intuition on the structure of Markovian and reciprocal projections, we will prove each of them and conclude with the convergence of the IMF procedure.

Lemma 4.15

(Pythagorean Theorem of Markovian and Reciprocal Projections (Lemma 6 in
shi2023diffusion
))
.

Let
\Pi\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
be a bridge measure in the reciprocal class of
\mathbb{Q}
and
\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)
be the Markovian projection of
\Pi
. Given some arbitrary Markov measure that has finite KL divergence with
\Pi
and
\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)
, the following identity holds:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\Pi\|\mathbb{M})=\text{KL}(\Pi\|\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi))+\text{KL}(\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)\|\mathbb{M})

(Markovian Projection Identity)

which means that the KL divergence between
\Pi
and
\mathbb{M}
is the sum of the KL incurred from projecting
\Pi
to the Markov class and the KL incurred from projecting
\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)
to the Markov measure
\mathbb{M}
. Similarly, for any arbitrary path measure
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
projected onto the reciprocal class
\text{proj}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})}(\mathbb{P})
, the KL divergence with
\Pi\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
can be decomposed as:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi)=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\text{proj}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})}(\mathbb{P}))+\text{KL}(\text{proj}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})}(\mathbb{P})\|\Pi)

(Reciprocal Projection Identity)

Proof.

Step 1: Derive the Markovian Projection Identity.

Intuitively, this step aims to derive the KL divergence to the Markov measure, which can be interpreted as the
information lost
when going from conditioning on
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{t})
in
\Pi
to conditioning only on
\bm{X}_{t}
in
\mathbb{M}
. To break this down, we define the
key component
that appears in both the drift of the Markovian projection and the mixture of bridges
\Pi
as:

\displaystyle\bm{G}_{t}:=\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})

(4.118)

Then, rewriting (
Markovian Projection Drift
) and the drift of the reciprocal process with additional conditioning on
\bm{X}_{0}
in terms of
\bm{G}_{t}
, we have:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\bm{A}_{t}:=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}\left[\bm{G}_{t}|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{t}\right]}_{\text{drift of reciprocal measure }\Pi},\hskip 9.24994pt\underbrace{\bm{B}_{t}:=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}\left[\bm{G}_{t}|\bm{X}_{t}\right]}_{\text{drift of Markovian measure }\mathbb{M}}

(4.119)

Using these definitions, we can rewrite the Markovian projection identity in terms of the integral differences between drifts as derived in Section
2.6
. First, recall the identity:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\text{KL}(\Pi\|\mathbb{M})}_{(\bigstar)}=\underbrace{\text{KL}(\Pi\|\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi))}_{(\diamond)}+\underbrace{\text{KL}(\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)\|\mathbb{M})}_{(\blacklozenge)}

(4.120)

For
(\bigstar)
defined as the KL divergence between
\Pi\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
with control drift
\sigma_{t}\bm{A}_{t}
and the Markov measure
\mathbb{M}
with control drift
\bm{u}
, we can write as the integral difference:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\Pi\|\mathbb{M})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}\left[\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)-\sigma_{t}\bm{A}_{t}\|^{2}\right]dt

(4.121)

For
(\diamond)
, the Markovian projection
\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)
has control drift
\sigma_{t}\bm{B}_{t}
which removes conditioning on
\bm{X}_{0}
and yields following KL divergence with
\Pi
:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\Pi\|\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}\left[\|\sigma_{t}\bm{A}_{t}-\sigma_{t}\bm{B}_{t}\|^{2}\right]dt

(4.122)

Finally, for
(\blacklozenge)
, the KL divergence between the Markovian projection
\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)
has control drift
\sigma_{t}\bm{B}_{t}
and the Markov measure
\mathbb{M}
with drift
\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)
is given by:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)\|\mathbb{M})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}\left[\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)-\sigma_{t}\bm{B}_{t}\|^{2}\right]dt

(4.123)

Now, summing
(\diamond)
and
(\blacklozenge)
from the left-hand side of (
4.120
) and observing that projecting onto the subspace spanned by
\bm{B}_{t}
gives
\langle\bm{A}_{t},\bm{B}_{t}\rangle=\|\bm{B}_{t}\|^{2}
and
\bm{B}_{t}=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi}[\bm{A}_{t}|\bm{X}_{t}]
, we have:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\text{KL}(\Pi\|\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi))}_{(\diamond)}+\underbrace{\text{KL}(\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)\|\mathbb{M})}_{(\blacklozenge)}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}\left[\|\sigma_{t}\bm{A}_{t}-\sigma_{t}\bm{B}_{t}\|^{2}\right]dt+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}\left[\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)-\sigma_{t}\bm{B}_{t}\|^{2}\right]dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}\bigg[\sigma_{t}^{2}\|\bm{A}_{t}\|^{2}-\underbrace{2\sigma_{t}^{2}\langle\bm{A}_{t},\bm{B}_{t}\rangle}_{=2\sigma_{t}^{2}\|\bm{B}_{t}\|^{2}}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\|\bm{B}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}-2\sigma_{t}\langle\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t),\bm{B}_{t}\rangle+\sigma_{t}^{2}\|\bm{B}_{t}\|^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}\bigg[\sigma_{t}^{2}\|\bm{A}_{t}\|^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-2\sigma_{t}^{2}\|\bm{B}_{t}\|^{2}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}^{2}\|\bm{B}_{t}\|^{2}}+\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}-2\sigma_{t}\langle\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t),\bm{B}_{t}\rangle+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}^{2}\|\bm{B}_{t}\|^{2}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}\bigg[\sigma_{t}^{2}\|\bm{A}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}-2\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\langle\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t),\bm{B}_{t}\rangle}_{=\langle\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t),\bm{A}_{t}\rangle}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}\bigg[\sigma_{t}^{2}\|\bm{A}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}-2\sigma_{t}\langle\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t),\bm{A}_{t}\rangle\bigg]

(given
\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{t}}[\langle\bm{u},\bm{B}_{t}\rangle]=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}[\langle\bm{u},\bm{A}_{t}\rangle]
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0,t}}\left[\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)-\sigma_{t}\bm{A}_{t}\|^{2}\right]dt=\boxed{\text{KL}(\Pi\|\mathbb{M})}

(4.124)

where the final equality exactly yields the KL divergence between
\text{KL}(\Pi\|\mathbb{M})
, which proves (
Markovian Projection Identity
). Next, we prove the (
Reciprocal Projection Identity
) with fewer steps.

Step 2: Derive the Reciprocal Projection Identity.

Let
\Pi^{\star}=\text{proj}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})}(\mathbb{P})
denote the reciprocal projection of
\mathbb{P}
. By the definition of reciprocal projection,
\Pi^{\star}
has the same endpoint law as
\mathbb{P}
and the same conditional bridge as
\mathbb{Q}
, defined as:

\displaystyle\Pi^{\star}=\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}

(4.125)

Applying the (
KL Divergence Chain Rule
), we have:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi^{\star}}+\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi^{\star}}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi}\right]=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi^{\star})+\int\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}

(4.126)

Now, we leverage the key property of measures in the same reciprocal class
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
that share the same
conditional bridge
as
\mathbb{Q}
. Therefore, the log RND depends only on the endpoints
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})
. In addition, the original path measure
\mathbb{P}
and its reciprocal projection
\Pi^{\star}
share the same
endpoint law
, so we can write (
4.126
) as:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi)

\displaystyle=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi^{\star})+\int\underbrace{\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi}(\bm{X}_{0:T})}_{\text{depends on $(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})$ only}}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi^{\star})+\int\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi^{\star}_{0,T}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{0,T}}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})}_{=\mathrm{d}\Pi^{\star}_{0,T}}}
