\displaystyle=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi^{\star})+\underbrace{\int\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\Pi^{\star}_{0,T}}{\mathrm{d}\Pi_{0,T}}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\mathrm{d}\Pi^{\star}_{0,T}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})}_{=\text{KL}(\Pi^{\star}\|\Pi)}=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\Pi^{\star})+\text{KL}(\Pi^{\star}\|\Pi)

(4.127)

which recovers the (
Reciprocal Projection Identity
) by subsituting back
\Pi^{\star}=\text{proj}_{\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})}(\mathbb{P})
.
\square

Using Lemma
4.15
, we can prove that iterating between Markov and reciprocal projections yields a monotonically decreasing KL divergence to the Schrödinger bridge measure, which will be the foundation for our final convergence proof.

Proposition 4.16

(Monotonically Decreasing KL Divergence (Proposition 7 in
shi2023diffusion
))
.

Given a sequence of Markov and reciprocal projections
(\mathbb{P}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and the Schrödinger bridge path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
, the reverse KL divergences between
\mathbb{P}^{n}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
decreases monotonically:

\displaystyle\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n+1}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})\leq\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})\leq\infty

(4.128)

and in the limit
n\to\infty
, the KL divergence between subsequent projections converges to zero:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{n+1})=0

(4.129)

Proof.
Recall that the Schrödinger bridge is the unique path measure that is Markov
\mathbb{P}^{\star}\in\mathcal{M}
and in the reciprocal class
\mathbb{P}^{\star}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
. Therefore, both (
Markovian Projection Identity
) and (
Reciprocal Projection Identity
) from Lemma
4.15
hold with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
. This means that for all
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, the following identity holds:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})=\underbrace{\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{n+1})}_{\geq 0}+\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n+1}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})\implies\boxed{\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})\geq\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n+1}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})}

(4.130)

where
\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
is either the
Markovian projection of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
if
\mathbb{P}^{n}
was generated from a reciprocal projection or the
reciprocal projection of
\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
if
\mathbb{P}^{n}
was generated from a Markovian projection. This proves the first part of the proposition.

Next, we show that at the limit as
n\to\infty
, the KL between iterations converges to zero
. To do this, we can compute the accumulated KL divergence over
N
iterations using the same identity from (
4.130
). To do this, we apply the identity for each iteration
n=0,\dots,N
to get:

\displaystyle n=0

\displaystyle:\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{0}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{0}\|\mathbb{P}^{1})+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})}

\displaystyle n=1

\displaystyle:{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})}=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\|\mathbb{P}^{2})+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{2}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})}

\displaystyle n=N

\displaystyle:{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{N}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})}=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{N}\|\mathbb{P}^{N+1})+\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{N+1}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})

(4.131)

Observing that the intermediate KL divergences with
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
(indicated in
blue
) cancel after summing over
n=0,\dots,N
(telescoping identity), we can write:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{0}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})}_{\text{fixed }\leq\infty}=\sum_{n=0}^{N}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{n+1})+\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{N+1}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})\implies\sum_{n=0}^{N}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{n+1})\leq\infty

(4.132)

where we observe that the KL divergence between the initial path measure
\mathbb{P}^{0}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is fixed and bounded. Since (
4.132
) holds as we increase
N
to infinity, we have shown that the series of non-negative KL divergences is bounded
\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{n+1})
is finite and bounded, the additive terms must converge to zero:

\displaystyle\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\quad\sum_{n=0}^{N}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{n+1})\leq\infty\implies\boxed{\lim_{n\to\infty}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{n+1})=0}

(4.133)

and we conclude our proof.
\square

This leads to the final result, which ensures that an IMF sequence converges to the optimal SB path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
.

Proposition 4.17

(Iterative Markovian Fitting Converges to the Unique Schrödinger Bridge)
.

The sequence of path measures
(\mathbb{P}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
generated from alternating Markovian and reciprocal projections of the IMF algorithm has a unique fixed point
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
which equals the Schrödinger bridge. Furthermore, in the limit
n\to\infty
, the KL divergence converges to the fixed point:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})=0

(4.134)

Proof Sketch.
By Proposition
4.16
, we know that each path measure in the sequence
(\mathbb{P}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
decreases the KL divergence to
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
, so the sequence remains trapped in a compact region in path space that is
bounded below by zero
, since KL divergence is non-negative. Therefore, both the sequence of Markovian projections and reciprocal projections converge to their optimal fixed points
\mathbb{M}^{\star}\in\mathcal{M}
and
\Pi^{\star}\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
. From Proposition
4.16
, we also have that the the KL divergence between each iteration converges to zero, which implies that the fixed points of the Markovian and reciprocal projections coincide exactly
25
25

25

Note that
\mathbb{M}^{\star}
and
\Pi^{\star}
are used to denote the Markovian and reciprocal projections for any path measure, but they only coincide at the unique fixed point when they are equal to the Schrödinger bridge.
:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\|\mathbb{P}^{n+1})=0\implies\text{KL}(\mathbb{M}^{\star}\|\Pi)=0\implies\boxed{\mathbb{M}^{\star}=\Pi^{\star}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}}

(4.135)

which means that the shared limit of the Markov and reciprocal projections is both Markov and in the reciprocal class
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
, and therefore, must be the Schrödinger bridge
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
. Given that both subsequences converge to
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
, we have that the full sequence
(\mathbb{P}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
also converges to
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
and the KL divergence converges to zero.
26
26

26

for more rigorous proof, see
(
shi2023diffusion
)

\square

The relationship between Markov and reciprocal projections reveals a
key structural property
of the Schrödinger bridge. The (
Markovian Projection
) enforces the Markov property by selecting the closest Markov process in relative entropy to some bridge measure, and the (
Reciprocal Projection
) adjusts the path measure so that the endpoint marginals match the prescribed distributions, while preserving the bridge structure inherited from the reference process.

Crucially, the Schrödinger bridge
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
lies exactly at the
equilibrium
of these two constraints, as it is the unique path measure that simultaneously satisfies the endpoint conditions and remains the closest Markov measure to the reference bridge dynamics. We further show that alternating between performing Markovian and reciprocal projections yields a unique fixed point that coincides with
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
, which is exactly what defines the Iterative Markovian Fitting (IMF) procedure. We will revisit the IMF procedure in Section
6.3
, where we apply this procedure in the context of generative modeling.

4.6
Stochastic Interpolants to Schrödinger Bridges

The
stochastic interpolants
framework
(
albergo2025stochastic
)
can be used to construct the Schrödinger bridge solution. First, we will provide some background on the framework, which will naturally lead to its extension in solving the SB problem.

Definition 4.18

(Stochastic Interpolant
(
albergo2025stochastic
)
)
.

Let
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be two probability densities on the state space. The
stochastic interpolant
between
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
is a stochastic process
\bm{X}_{0:T}
of the form:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{t}=I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\gamma(t)\bm{z},\quad t\in[0,T]

(4.136)

where the following are satisfied:

(i)

The map
I\in C^{2}((C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{d},[0,T])
has boundary conditions
I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},0)=\bm{x}_{0}
and
I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},T)=\bm{x}_{T}
and controlled time variation:

\displaystyle\exists C_{1}<\infty\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt|\partial_{t}I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)|\leq C_{1}|\bm{x}_{T}-\bm{x}_{0}|,\hskip 9.24994pt\forall(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

(4.137)

(ii)

The scalar noise function
\gamma:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)=0
and
\gamma(t)>0
for all
t\in(0,T)
. In addition,
\gamma^{2}\in C^{2}([0,T])
.

(iii)

The pair
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
are sampled from a probability measure
\pi_{0,T}
whose marginals are
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
defined as:

\displaystyle\pi_{0,T}(d\bm{x}_{0},\mathbb{R}^{d})=\pi_{0}(\bm{x}_{0})d\bm{x}_{0},\quad\pi_{0,T}(\mathbb{R}^{d},d\bm{x}_{T})=\pi_{T}(\bm{x}_{T})d\bm{x}_{T}

(4.138)

(iv)

The Gaussian random variable
\bm{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})
is independent of
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
.

Intuitively, the stochastic interpolant is a general method of connecting samples from two marginal distributions with a
deterministic path

I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)
that is perturbed by a time-dependent Gaussian diffusion
\gamma(t)\bm{z}
along the interior of the time interval. By definition, the noise
\gamma(t)\bm{z}
vanishes at
t=0
and
t=T
, ensuring that the process matches exactly the terminal marginals
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
.

Theorem 4.19

(Properties of Stochastic Interpolants (Theorem 2.6 in
albergo2025stochastic
))
.

The stochastic interpolant
\bm{x}_{t}=I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)
satisfies the following properties:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}+\nabla\cdot(p_{t}\bm{v})=0

(4.139)

where the velocity is defined as the expectation of the time derivative:

\displaystyle\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)=\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}[\dot{\bm{x}}_{t}|\bm{x}_{t}=\bm{x}]=\mathbb{E}[\partial_{t}I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\dot{\gamma}(t)\bm{z}|\bm{x}_{t}=\bm{x}]

(4.140)

which is bounded on the domain of the density function
p_{t}(\bm{x})
given by:

\displaystyle\forall t\in[0,T]:\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}<\infty

(4.141)

Proof.
We start with the definition of the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}(\bm{\omega},t)[p_{t}]
of the density
p_{t}(\bm{x})
for the random variable
\bm{x}_{t}=I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\gamma(t)\bm{z}
, given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\bm{\omega},t)[p_{t}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}_{t}}p_{t}(\bm{x})=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}_{t}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot(I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\gamma(t)\bm{z})}\right]

(4.142)

where
\bm{\omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the
d
-dimensional Fourier frequency variable. To show that
p_{t}(\bm{x})
satisfies the continuity equation in (
4.139
), we can take the time derivative of its Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}(\bm{\omega},t)[p_{t}]
to get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathcal{F}(\bm{\omega},t)[p_{t}]

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot(I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\gamma(t)\bm{z})}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}}e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot(I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\gamma(t)\bm{z})}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}_{t}}\partial_{t}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}i\bm{\omega}}\cdot(I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\gamma(t)\bm{z})\big)\right]={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}i\bm{\omega}}\cdot\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}_{t}}(\partial_{t}I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\dot{\gamma}(t)\bm{z})\right]}_{(\bigstar)}

(4.143)

Now, we can apply the law of total expectation
\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[X|Y]]
to write
(\bigstar)
with respect to a conditional expectation on
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathcal{F}(\bm{\omega},t)[p_{t}]

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}i\bm{\omega}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}_{t}}(\partial_{t}I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\dot{\gamma}(t)\bm{z})\right]

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}i\bm{\omega}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}\big[(\partial_{t}I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\dot{\gamma}(t)\bm{z})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\big]}\right]

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}i\bm{\omega}}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\big[(\partial_{t}I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\dot{\gamma}(t)\bm{z})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\big]}_{=:\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)}}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}i\bm{\omega}}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

(4.144)

Next, we show that the Fourier transform of
-\nabla\cdot(\bm{v}p_{t})
is exactly
i\bm{\omega}\mathcal{F}(\bm{\omega},t)[\bm{v}p_{t}]
. Given that
\nabla\cdot(\bm{v}p_{t})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\partial_{\bm{x}_{j}}(\bm{v}p_{t})_{j}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\bm{\omega},t)[-\nabla\cdot(\bm{v}p_{t})]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\underbrace{e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}}}_{u}\underbrace{\partial_{\bm{x}_{j}}(\bm{v}p_{t})_{j}}_{dv}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left[e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}}(\bm{v}p_{t})\right]_{-\infty}^{\infty}}_{=0}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{v}p_{t})_{j}\partial_{\bm{x}_{j}}\left(e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}}\right)d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{v}p_{t})_{j}\partial_{\bm{x}_{j}}\left(e^{i\sum_{\ell=1}^{d}\bm{\omega}_{\ell}\bm{x}_{\ell}}\right)d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\bm{v}p_{t})_{j}\left(i\bm{\omega}_{j}e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}}\right)d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}i\bm{\omega}_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}}(\bm{v}p_{t})_{j}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=i\bm{\omega}\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\bm{\omega}\cdot\bm{x}}\bm{v}p_{t}d\bm{x}

(4.145)

which proves that
\bm{v}
and
p_{t}
satisfies the continuity equation in the real space:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathcal{F}(\bm{\omega},t)[p_{t}]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(\bm{\omega},t)[-\nabla\cdot(\bm{v}p_{t})]\implies\boxed{\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(\bm{v}p_{t})}

(4.146)

and we conclude our proof.
\square

Having established the fundamental properties of stochastic interpolants, we now observe that they induce a family of time-evolving densities
(p_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
governed by the continuity equation (
4.139
). This equation characterizes deterministic mass transport under a velocity field
\bm{v}
, and forms the core dynamical constraint underlying optimal transport. In particular, stochastic interpolants provide a constructive way to define admissible trajectories that transport probability mass between endpoint distributions.

However, while stochastic interpolants describe valid transport dynamics, they do not yet specify which trajectory is
optimal
. The SB problem resolves this ambiguity by selecting, among all admissible paths satisfying the same marginal constraints, the one that is closest to a reference stochastic process in the sense of minimizing path-space KL divergence. As we now show, this optimality can be expressed through stochastic control, where the velocity field
\bm{v}
is parameterized via a controlled drift that minimizes a quadratic control cost while matching the prescribed marginals.

We begin by recalling the (
Dynamic SB Problem
), which aims to determine the optimal control drift and density evolution
(\bm{u}^{\star},p_{t}^{\star})
that solve the minimization problem:

\displaystyle\inf_{(\bm{u},p_{t})}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt\right]\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}\bm{u})+\epsilon\Delta p_{t}\\
p_{0}=\pi_{0},\hskip 9.24994ptp_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(4.147)

which can be rewritten as solving (
HJB-FP System
) given by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\psi_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}\|^{2}=-\epsilon\Delta\psi_{t}\\
\partial_{t}p_{t}^{\star}+\nabla\cdot(p_{t}^{\star}\bm{u}^{\star})=\epsilon\Delta p^{\star}_{t}\end{cases}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}p^{\star}_{0}=\pi_{0}\\
p^{\star}_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(4.148)

where we simplify the setting by setting
\bm{f}\equiv 0
and
\epsilon=\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}
.

Before we construct the stochastic interpolant that solves the SB problem, we will define an invertible map that generates the optimal SB density
p_{t}^{\star}
that solves (
4.147
).

Definition 4.20

(Invertible Map)
.

Define an invertible map
M:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
M,M^{-1}\in C^{1}([0,T],(C^{d}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{d})
such that:

\displaystyle p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=M(\cdot,t)_{\#}\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})

(4.149)

In other words, given a Gaussian random variable
\bm{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})
, we have
\bm{x}_{t}=M(\bm{z},t)\sim p^{\star}_{t}
.

Given the existence of the invertible map, we can now derive the
stochastic interpolant
that solves the SB problem.

Lemma 4.21

(Stochastic Interpolant Form of SB Solution (Lemma 3.12 in
albergo2025stochastic
)
.

Given the existence of an invertible map
M
defined in Definition
4.20
, the optimal density
p_{t}^{\star}
that solves the dynamic SB problem can be written as a
stochastic interpolant
of the form:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{t}=M\big({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\alpha(t)M^{-1}(\bm{x}_{0},0)+\beta(t)M^{-1}(\bm{x}_{T},T)},t\big)+\gamma(t)

(4.150)

where
\alpha^{2}(t)+\beta^{2}(t)+\gamma^{2}(t)=1
. This corresponds to defining the interpolant function from Definition
4.18
as
I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)=M(\alpha(t)M^{-1}(\bm{x}_{0},0)+\beta(t)M^{-1}(\bm{x}_{T},T))
.

Proof.
Intuitively, this Lemma states that the density
p_{t}^{\star}
that solves the HJB-FP system defined in (
4.148
) is exactly the distribution of the random variable obtained defined in (
4.150
).

By definition of the map
M(\cdot,t)
which
transports
a standard Gaussian to the target density
p_{t}^{\star}
at time
t
, so the
inverse map

M^{-1}(\cdot,t)
must transport the target density
p_{t}^{\star}
back to a standard Gaussian, such that:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{0}\sim\pi_{0},\quad

\displaystyle M^{-1}(\bm{x}_{0},0)\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})

(4.151)

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{T}\sim\pi_{T},\quad

\displaystyle M^{-1}(\bm{x}_{T},T)\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})

(4.152)

Since
\bm{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})
is also sampled from a standard Gaussian and
\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},\bm{z}
are drawn independently in the stochastic interpolant construction, we have that the linear combination of independent standard Gaussians given by:

\displaystyle\alpha(t)M^{-1}(\bm{x}_{0},0)+\beta(t)M^{-1}(\bm{x}_{T},T)+\gamma(t)\bm{z}\sim{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\alpha^{2}(t)\bm{I}_{d}+\beta^{2}(t)\bm{I}_{d}+\gamma^{2}(t)\bm{I}_{d})}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})

(4.153)

is a Gaussian with zero-mean and covariance
(\alpha^{2}(t)+\beta^{2}(t)+\gamma^{2}(t))\bm{I}_{d}
. Since we defined
\alpha^{2}(t)+\beta^{2}(t)+\gamma^{2}(t)=1
, then this is just a standard Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})
. Therefore, we can apply the definition of the map
M(\cdot,t)
on the linear combination (
4.153
) which yields a random variable from the target distribution
p_{t}^{\star}
:

\displaystyle\bm{x}_{t}=M\big({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\alpha(t)M^{-1}(\bm{x}_{0},0)+\beta(t)M^{-1}(\bm{x}_{T},T)},t\big)+\gamma(t)\bm{z}\sim{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}^{\star}}

(4.154)

which concludes the proof.
\square

Lemma
4.21
shows that the density
p_{t}^{\star}
that solves the HJB-FP system can be obtained as the distribution of a random variable constructed by interpolation of samples from the terminal marginals
\bm{x}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}
and
\bm{x}_{T}\sim\pi_{T}
in Gaussian space, which establishes the form of the optimal interpolant
I^{\star}
that generates the Schrödinger bridge marginals. Now, we can derive an objective whose minimizer recovers this optimal interpolant
I^{\star}
.

Proposition 4.22

(Solving SB problem with Stochastic Interpolants (Theorem 3.13 in
albergo2025stochastic
)
.

Define a scalar function
\gamma(t):[0,T]\to[0,1)
which returns zero at the terminal time points (i.e.,
\gamma(0)=\gamma(T)=0
), returns non-zero at intermediate times (i.e.,
\forall t\in(0,T),\quad\gamma(t)>0
), and satisfies
\gamma\in C^{2}((0,T))
and
\gamma^{2}\in C^{1}([0,T])
.

Then, given independent
\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},\bm{z}
solving the max-min problem over
\hat{I}\in C^{1}([0,T],(C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{d})
and
\hat{\bm{u}}\in C^{0}([0,T],(C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{d})
given by:

\displaystyle\underset{\hat{I}}{\max}\underset{\hat{\bm{u}}}{\min}\int_{0}^{T}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}-\left(\partial_{t}\hat{I}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\left(\dot{\gamma}(t)-\epsilon\gamma^{-1}(t)\right)\bm{z}\right)\cdot\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{x},t)\bigg]dt

(4.155)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}\bm{x}_{t}=\hat{I}(t,\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})+\gamma(t)\bm{z}\\
\bm{x}_{0}\sim\pi_{0},\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{x}_{T}\sim\pi_{T},\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})\end{cases}

(4.156)

where, given the existence of the invertible map
M
, all optimal
(I^{\star},\bm{u}^{\star})
produces the stochastic interpolant
\bm{x}_{t}=I^{\star}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\gamma(t)\bm{z}
with marginals
p_{t}^{\star}
that satisfy the continuity equation of the form:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(\hat{p}_{t}\hat{\bm{v}})

(4.157)

where
\hat{\bm{v}}
is the effective velocity field that accounts for the Gaussian perturbation induced by
\gamma(t)\bm{z}
.

Proof.

To prove this, we begin by defining the effective velocity
\hat{\bm{v}}
of the stochastic interpolant and rewriting the objective as an optimization over
\hat{\bm{v}}
. Then, we write the constrained objective using Lagrange multipliers and solve for the optimal solution. First, we rewrite the Fokker-Planck equation into a continuity constraint that must be satisfied by the effective velocity:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(\hat{p}_{t}\bm{v})+\epsilon\Delta\hat{p}_{t}

\displaystyle\implies\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(\hat{p}_{t}\bm{v})+\epsilon\nabla\cdot\nabla\hat{p}_{t}

\displaystyle\implies\boxed{\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(\hat{p}_{t}\underbrace{(\bm{v}-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\epsilon\nabla\log\hat{p}_{t}})}_{=:\hat{\bm{v}}})}

(4.158)

To define the effective velocity that accounts for the contribution of the Gaussian perturbation, we need to incorporate
\epsilon\nabla\log\hat{p}_{t}
. Since the stochastic interpolant is defined as
\bm{x}_{t}=I(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\gamma(t)\bm{z}
, the conditional distribution of
\bm{x}_{t}
given
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
is Gaussian with variance
\gamma^{2}(t)
. A standard identity for Gaussian variables implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]=-\gamma(t)\nabla\log\hat{p}_{t}(\bm{x}),

(4.159)

where
\hat{p}_{t}
is the density of
\bm{x}_{t}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle-\epsilon\gamma^{-1}(t)\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]

\displaystyle=\epsilon\nabla\log\hat{p}_{t}(\bm{x})

(4.160)

Now, we can define the average effective velocity as:

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{v}}(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[\partial_{t}\hat{I}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+(\dot{\gamma}(t){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\epsilon\gamma^{-1}(t)})\bm{z}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]

(4.161)

where
-\epsilon\gamma^{-1}(t)

corrects for the Gaussian perturabtion
. Then, rewriting the expectation in the original objective as the integral, we have:

\displaystyle\underset{\hat{I}}{\max}\underset{\hat{\bm{u}}}{\min}\int_{0}^{T}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{u}}(\hat{\bm{x}}_{t},t)\|^{2}-\left(\partial_{t}\hat{I}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\left(\dot{\gamma}(t)-\epsilon\gamma^{-1}(t)\right)\bm{z}\right)\cdot\hat{\bm{u}}(\hat{\bm{x}}_{t},t)\bigg]dt

\displaystyle=\underset{\hat{p},\bm{v}}{\max}\underset{\hat{\bm{u}}}{\min}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}\left[\partial_{t}\hat{I}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\left(\dot{\gamma}(t)-\epsilon\gamma^{-1}(t)\right)\bm{z}\big|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]}\cdot\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{x},t)\bigg]\hat{p}_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\underset{\hat{p},\bm{v}}{\max}\underset{\hat{\bm{u}}}{\min}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}\hat{p}_{t}(\bm{x})-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\hat{\bm{v}}(\bm{x},t)}\cdot\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{x},t)\bigg]\hat{p}_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

(4.162)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\partial_{t}\hat{p}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\bm{v}\hat{p}_{t})=\epsilon\Delta\hat{p}_{t},\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{p}_{0}=\pi_{0},\hskip 9.24994pt\hat{p}_{T}=\pi_{T}

Rewriting (
4.162
) as an unconstrained objective with Lagrange multipliers
\psi_{t}(\bm{x}),\eta_{0}(\bm{x})
and
\eta_{T}(\bm{x})
, we have:

\displaystyle\underset{\hat{p},\hat{\bm{v}}}{\max}\underset{\hat{\bm{u}}}{\min}

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}\hat{p}_{t}(\bm{x})-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\hat{\bm{v}}(\bm{x},t)}\cdot\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{x},t)\bigg]\hat{p}_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\left(\partial_{t}\hat{p}_{t}(\bm{x})+\nabla\cdot(\hat{\bm{v}}(\bm{x},t)\hat{p}_{t}(\bm{x}))\right)d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta_{0}(\bm{x})\left(\hat{p}_{0}(\bm{x})-\pi_{0}(\bm{x})\right)dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta_{T}(\bm{x})\left(\hat{p}_{T}(\bm{x})-\pi_{T}(\bm{x})\right)dt\bigg)

(4.163)

The optimal
(p_{t}^{\star},\bm{u}^{\star})
that minimizes the Lagrangian pointwise satisfies the following optimality conditions:

(i)

Varying with respect to
\psi_{t}(\bm{x})
enforces:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}\hat{p}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})=0}

(4.164)

(ii)

Varying with respect to
\hat{\bm{u}}
enforces:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{u}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{u}}\|^{2}p_{t}-\hat{\bm{u}}\cdot(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})\right)=\hat{\bm{u}}\hat{p}_{t}-(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})=0\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}^{\star}=\hat{\bm{v}}}

(4.165)

(iii)

Since
(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})
appears in
-\hat{\bm{u}}\cdot(\hat{\bm{v}}^{\star}\hat{p}_{t})
and
\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\nabla\cdot(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})
, we can integrate by parts to get:

\displaystyle-\int\hat{\bm{u}}\cdot(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})-\int\psi_{t}\nabla\cdot(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})

\displaystyle=-\int\hat{\bm{u}}\cdot(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})-\underbrace{[\psi_{t}(\bm{x})(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})]_{-\infty}^{\infty}}_{=0}+\int\nabla\psi_{t}\cdot(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})

\displaystyle=-\int\hat{\bm{u}}\cdot(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})+\int\nabla\psi_{t}\cdot(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})

\displaystyle=\int(\nabla\psi_{t}-\hat{\bm{u}})\cdot(\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t})

\displaystyle\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\psi_{t}-\bm{u}^{\star}=0}

(4.166)

(iv)

Isolating the terms that depend on
\hat{p}_{t}
(excluding those that involve
\hat{\bm{v}}\hat{p}_{t}
, which we accounted for previously) and integrating by parts to factor out
\hat{p}_{t}
, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{u}}\|^{2}\hat{p}_{t}-\psi_{t}\partial_{t}\hat{p}_{t}\right]d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{u}}\|^{2}\hat{p}_{t}+(\partial_{t}\psi_{t})\hat{p}_{t}\right]d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{u}}\|^{2}+(\partial_{t}\psi_{t})\right]}_{\text{vanishes pointwise at minimum}}}\hat{p}_{t}d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}\psi_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}^{\star}\|^{2}=0}

(4.167)

(v)

Varying with respect to
\eta_{0}(\bm{x})
and
\eta_{T}(\bm{x})
yields
p_{0}^{\star}=\pi_{0}
and
p^{\star}_{T}=\pi_{T}
.

Putting it all together, (ii) and (iii) enforce
\bm{u}^{\star}=\nabla\psi_{t}
, which can be substituted into (i) to get the following system:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\psi_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}\|^{2}=0\\
\partial_{t}p^{\star}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(p_{t}^{\star}\nabla\psi_{t})=0\end{cases}\quad\textbf{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}p_{0}=\pi_{0}\\
p_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(4.168)

which is exactly the HJB-FP system satisfied by the solution to the SB problem with given in (
4.148
) with vanishing diffusion, since the Gaussian diffusion is absorbed into the marginal density
p_{t}
through the effective velocity
\hat{\bm{v}}
. Given the existance of the invertible map from Definition
4.20
that yields the optimal density
p^{\star}_{t}
, we have that the pair
(I^{\star},\bm{u}^{\star})
that solves the original max-min objective in (
4.155
) exists which we have shown yields the optimal density
\bm{x}_{t}\sim p^{\star}_{t}
for
\bm{x}_{t}=I^{\star}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\gamma(t)\bm{z}
.
\square

This result shows that stochastic interpolants provide a alternative representation of the Schrödinger bridge dynamics
(\bm{u}^{\star},p^{\star}_{t})
, where the optimal control and velocity fields coincide and are given by the gradient of the Lagrange multiplier
\nabla\psi_{t}
, yielding the coupled HJB-FP system that define the optimality conditions of the SB solution. Therefore, sampling from the stochastic interpolant
\bm{x}_{t}=I^{\star}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\gamma(t)\bm{z}
, where
\bm{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})
generates trajectories whose marginals match the marginal density flow
p_{t}^{\star}
of the SB path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
.

4.7
Closing Remarks for Section
4

This section explored the theoretical foundations for building a Schrödinger bridge using several approaches. While each approach originates from a different mathematical viewpoint, they ultimately converge to a unified form of a Markov control drift that minimally corrects the uncontrolled reference dynamics such that they reconstruct the prescribed marginal distributions.

The
key takeaway
of this section is that stochastic bridges are not arbitrary conditioned processes, but rather minimal-entropy corrections of a reference diffusion that preserve the bridge structure while introducing the smallest possible dynamical adjustment. This adjustment consistently appears as a gradient of a logarithmic potential, providing a unifying perspective to the structure of the Schrödinger bridge.

The different constructions introduced in this section can therefore be understood as alternative ways of identifying the optimal control drift that modifies the reference dynamics:

(i)

Mixture of Conditional Bridges (Section
4.1
):
This section shows that the dynamic SB
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
can be expressed as a mixture of endpoint-conditioned stochastic bridges given samples from the optimal endpoint law
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
, which can be constructed with a conditional drift
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t;\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
. This decomposition separates the SB problem into estimating the optimal static coupling and learning the conditional bridge dynamics.

(ii)

Time-reversal formula (Section
4.2
):
This section shows that the time-reversal formula yields a backward correction term
\nabla\log p_{T-s}(\bm{x})
that corresponds to the
score function
in score-based generative modeling. This formulation models the endpoint-conditioned bridge for
uncontrolled
forward process.

(iii)

Forward-backward SDEs (Section
4.3
):
This section analyzes how the theory of forward–backward theory generalizes the time-reversal formula for a forward process containing a non-deterministic control drift
\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
which yields a backward control drift
\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})
that evolves via a system of forward-backward SDEs.

(iv)

Doob’s
h
-transform (Section
4.4
):
This section defines an
h
-function
h(\bm{x},t)
that
reweights path transitions
by its potential at time
\tau
. The reweighted path measure
\mathbb{P}^{h}
is defined by an SDE with a correction term
\nabla\log h(\bm{x},t)
. By defining
h(\bm{x},t):=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\varphi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]
, we show that the tilted path measure recovers the Schrödinger bridge
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
.

(v)

Markov and reciprocal projections (Section
4.5
):
This section shows that the optimal Schrödinger bridge
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
can be interpreted as the equilibrium point between projections onto the space of Markov path measures and the reciprocal class
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
containing mixtures of bridges under the reference path
\mathbb{Q}
. In this formulation, the optimal drift is expressed as an expectation over the target-conditioned path measure
\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|t}}[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t}]
.

(vi)

Stochastic interpolants (Section
4.6
):
The stochastic interpolant framework represents the bridge by expressing the intermediate state as
\bm{x}_{t}=I^{\star}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T},t)+\gamma(t)\bm{z}
, where
I^{\star}
is the optimal interpolant between samples
\bm{x}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}
and
\bm{x}_{T}\sim\pi_{T}
and
\bm{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})
is Gaussian noise. We show that the induced velocity field satisfies the same optimality conditions that characterize the Schrödinger bridge dynamics.

While this section provides a principled framework for constructing stochastic bridges between prescribed endpoint distributions that solve the (
Dynamic SB Problem
), recent advances in generative modeling have motivated a variety of
specialized Schrödinger bridge formulations
tailored to different modeling assumptions and problem settings. These variants extend the original framework in several directions, ranging from alternative reference processes to mean-field interactions, unbalanced mass transport, and multi-marginal and multi-modal constraints. In the next section, we analyze several of these problem variations and discuss how they modify our dynamic SB problem formulation while preserving its
core principle
: constructing stochastic dynamics that minimally deviate from a reference process while matching prescribed marginal distributions.

5
Variations of the Schrödinger Bridge Problem

In previous sections, we have established the foundational theories and intuition behind the classical static and dynamic Schrödinger bridge problem and have shown how to derive stochastic bridges from scratch using various techniques. Now, we are ready to describe diverse variations of the SB problem that have been introduced in conjunction to novel generative modeling techniques, each of which are specialized for different settings and tasks.

Specifically, we analyze the Gaussian SB problem (Section
5.1
), the generalized SB problem (Section
5.2
), the multi-marginal SB problem (Section
5.3
), the unbalanced SB problem (Section
5.4
), the branched SB problem (Section
5.5
), and finally the fractional SB problem (Section
5.6
). For each problem variation, we provide the intuition and formal definition of the problem, the necessary theoretical background, and relevant proofs and derivations that are useful for interpreting the problem and its optimal solution.

5.1
Gaussian Schrödinger Bridge Problem

While the (
Dynamic SB Problem
) defined in Section
2
does not admit a close form solution in general, in the special case where the marginal distributions
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}
are Gaussian distributions defined as
\pi_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{0}),\pi_{T}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{T},\bm{\Sigma}_{T})
, the SB solution can be solved in closed form
(
bunne2023schrodinger
)
. This special case of the dynamic SB problem is called the Gaussian Schrödinger Bridge (SB) problem
(
bunne2023schrodinger
;
mallasto2022entropy
)
. Beforing defining the Gaussian SB problem, we start by defining the
Gaussian formulation of the entropic OT problem

(
bojilov2016matching
;
janati2020entropic
;
mallasto2022entropy
)
, which will become crucial for our later derivation of the closed-form solution in the Gaussian SB setting.

Lemma 5.1

(Static Entropy-Regularized Gaussian Optimal Transport)
.

Let
\pi_{0}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})
and
\pi_{T}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})
be Gaussian probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are the mean vectors and
\bm{\Sigma}
. Consider the entropy-regularized optimal transport problem

\displaystyle\min_{\pi_{0,T}\in\Pi(\pi_{0},\pi_{T})}\int\|\bm{x}_{T}-\bm{x}_{0}\|^{2}d\pi(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})+2\sigma^{2}\text{KL}\left(\pi_{0,T}\|\pi_{0}\otimes\pi_{T}\right)

(5.1)

where
\sigma\geq 0
and
\Pi(\pi_{0},\pi_{T})
denotes the set of couplings with marginals
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
. Then, the unique optimal coupling
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
is Gaussian and satisfies

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}_{0,T}\sim\mathcal{N}\!\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mu}_{0}\\
\bm{\mu}_{T}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}&\bm{C}_{\sigma}\\
\bm{C}_{\sigma}^{\top}&\bm{\Sigma}_{T}\end{bmatrix}\right)

(5.2)

where

\displaystyle\bm{C}_{\sigma}:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{1/2}\bm{D}_{\sigma}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{-1/2}-\sigma^{2}\bm{I}_{d}\right),\quad\text{where}\quad\bm{D}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle:=\left(4\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{1/2}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{1/2}+\sigma^{4}\bm{I}_{d}\right)^{1/2}

(5.3)

In particular, when
\sigma=0
, the solution reduces to the classical Gaussian optimal transport coupling with quadratic transport cost.

Intuition.
While we omit the full proof
27
27

27

See
janati2020entropic
for proof.
, we observe that when transporting between two Gaussian distributions under entropy regularization, the
optimal coupling remains Gaussian
and is fully characterized by how the two variables are correlated. Since the marginals are fixed to be
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
, the only degree of freedom is the cross-covariance
\bm{C}_{\sigma}
, which determines how samples are paired.

The objective balances two competing effects: the quadratic cost
\|\bm{x}_{T}-\bm{x}_{0}\|^{2}
encourages pairs
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
to be as close as possible, whereas the entropy regularization term
\text{KL}\left(\pi_{0,T}\|\pi_{0}\otimes\pi_{T}\right)
penalizes deviations from independence, pushing the coupling toward the product measure where
\bm{x}_{0}
and
\bm{x}_{T}
are uncorrelated. Therefore, the optimal cross-covariance
\bm{C}_{\sigma}
can be interpreted as the optimal trade-off between these two objectives.

Given this result, we are ready to define the
Gaussian Schrödinger Bridge
(SB) problem and derive its closed form solution.

Definition 5.2

(Gaussian Schrödinger Bridge Problem)
.

Let
\pi_{0}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})
and
\pi_{T}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma})
be Gaussian probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are the mean vectors and
\bm{\Sigma},\bm{\Sigma}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are the covariances, and let
\mathbb{Q}
be a reference path measure. The
Gaussian Schrödinger bridge
seeks the path measure that matches the Gaussian marginals while minimizing the relative entropy with respect to
\mathbb{Q}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}=\underset{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))}{\arg\min}\big\{\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q}):\pi_{0}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma}),\pi_{T}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}^{\prime},\bm{\Sigma}^{\prime})\big\}

(Gaussian SB Problem)

which can also be written in the form of a (
Entropy-Regularized Dynamic OT
)
28
28

28

to align notation with
(
bunne2023schrodinger
)
, we denote the full drift
\bm{f}_{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{x},t)
without scaling by
\sigma_{t}
, which results the additional factor of
\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{4}
in the Fisher information term.
as:

\displaystyle\inf_{(p_{t},\bm{v})}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{4}_{t}}{8}\|\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}\right]dt\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}\bm{v})\\
p_{0}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\Sigma}),\hskip 9.24994ptp_{T}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}^{\prime},\bm{\Sigma}^{\prime})\end{cases}

(5.4)

where we set
\bm{f}\equiv 0
.

To obtain a tractable characterization of this problem, we exploit the special structure of Gaussian measures, which are uniquely defined by their mean and covariance. When the marginals remain Gaussian along the interpolation, the evolution of the process is fully characterized by the trajectories of its
mean

\bm{\mu}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
covariance

\bm{\Sigma}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. In this setting, the Gaussian Schrödinger bridge can be interpreted as minimizing the energy of the change in covariance matrices as they move along the manifold of symmetric positive definite matrices
\bm{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{S}_{++}^{d}
, whose
tangent space
is the space of symmetric matrices, whose
tangent space
is the space of symmetric matrices:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\Sigma}}\mathbb{S}^{d}_{++}:=\{\bm{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}:\bm{U}^{\top}=\bm{U}\}

(5.5)

The natural geometry governing optimal transport between Gaussian covariances is called the
Bures-Wasserstein manifold

(
takatsu2010wasserstein
;
bhatia2019bures
)
. This Riemannian manifold defines the metric structure in the space of covariance matrices induced by the Wasserstein distance between Gaussian distributions, which characterizes optimality of transport between covariances. The geometry of this manifold can be described using the
Lyapunov operator
, which we define below.

Definition 5.3

(Lyapunov Operator and Bures-Wasserstein Manifold)
.

Given a covariance matrix
\bm{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{S}^{d}_{++}
and tangent matrix
\bm{U}\in\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\Sigma}}\mathbb{S}^{d}_{++}
, the Lyapunov operator
\mathcal{L}_{\bm{\Sigma}}[\bm{U}]:\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\Sigma}}\mathbb{S}^{d}_{++}\to\mathbb{S}^{d}_{++}
is the operator that returns the unique symmetric matrix
\bm{A}
that solves:

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}\bm{A}+\bm{A}\bm{\Sigma}=\bm{U}

(5.6)

which defines the Riemannian metric of the Bures-Wasserstein manifold given by:

\displaystyle\langle\bm{U},\bm{V}\rangle_{\bm{\Sigma}}:=\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}(\mathcal{L}_{\bm{\Sigma}}[\bm{U}]\bm{V}),\quad\bm{U},\bm{V}\in\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\Sigma}}\mathbb{S}^{d}_{++}

(5.7)

where
\bm{U},\bm{V}\in\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\Sigma}}\mathbb{S}^{d}_{++}
are two tangent vectors in the tangent space of the covariance matrices.

Using the Lyapunov operator, the (
Gaussian SB Problem
) can be reformulated as an
action minimization problem
on the Bures-Wasserstein manifold of covariance matrices. This geometric formulation reveals that the optimal covariance trajectory evolves along curves minimizing a kinetic energy functional augmented by a diffusion correction term.

Figure 9:
Gaussian Schrödinger Bridge Problem.
The Gaussian SB between initial and terminal Gaussian distributions
\pi_{0}=\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
and
\pi_{T}=\mathcal{N}(\mu^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime})
reduces to solving an action minimization problem on the Bures–Wasserstein manifold of covariance matrices, where the covariance trajectory
(\Sigma_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
balances a kinetic energy term describing the rate of covariance change and a potential energy term induced by the diffusion of the reference process.

Proposition 5.4

(Gaussian Schrödinger Bridge Problem as Action Minimization (Theorem 2 of
(
bunne2023schrodinger
)
))
.

The solution to the (
Gaussian SB Problem
) where
\mathbb{Q}
is defined as pure Brownian motion
\mathbb{Q}:d\bm{X}_{t}=\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
is equivalent to the solution of the
action minimization
problem on the Bures-Wasserstein manifold, defined as:

\displaystyle\inf_{(\bm{\Sigma}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}}\int_{0}^{T}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}\|^{2}_{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}+\mathcal{U}_{\sigma}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t})\right]dt\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}=\bm{\Sigma}\\
\bm{\Sigma}_{T}=\bm{\Sigma}^{\prime}\end{cases}

(5.8)

where
\mathcal{U}_{\sigma}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}):=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{4}}{8}\text{Tr}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\right)
is the potential energy that captures the entropic contribution of the diffusion term and
\|\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}\|^{2}_{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}=\langle\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t},\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}\rangle_{\bm{\Sigma}}
is the Riemmannian metric defined in (
5.7
). Furthermore, the solution satisfies the
Euler-Lagrange equation
in Bures-Wasserstein geometry:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\nabla_{\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}}\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}=-\text{grad}\left(-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{4}}{8}\text{Tr}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\right)\\
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}=\bm{\Sigma},\quad\bm{\Sigma}_{T}=\bm{\Sigma}^{\prime}\end{cases}

(Euler-Lagrange Equation)

where
\nabla_{\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}}\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}
is the acceleration defined by the Riemannian gradient in the Bures-Wasserstein geometry.

Proof Sketch.
While we don’t prove all required lemmas rigorously, we break down the key components of this proof in three steps
29
29

29

for rigorous proof, see
(
bunne2023schrodinger
)
Appendix B.2
.

Step 1: Define the Lyapunov Operator.

A
key component
of this proof is that the Lyapunov operator
\mathcal{L}_{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}[\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}]
of the
velocity of the covariance matrix along the curve
\bm{\Sigma}_{t}
takes the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}[\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}]={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}

(5.9)

where
\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
can be interpreted as the linear velocity field that transports the Gaussian distribution defined as:

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}:=t\bm{\Sigma}^{\prime}+(T-t)\bm{C}_{\sigma}-(T-t)\bm{\Sigma}-t\bm{C}_{\sigma}^{\top}+\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}(T-2t)\bm{I}_{d}

(5.10)

Recall from Definition
5.3
that the Lyapunov operator
\mathcal{L}_{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}[\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}]
is the symmetric matrix
\bm{A}
that solves
\bm{\Sigma}_{t}\bm{A}+\bm{A}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}=\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}
and defines the Riemannian metric
\|\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}\|^{2}_{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}=\text{Tr}(\bm{A}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}\bm{A})
. Therefore, kinetic energy term appearing in the action functional can be written as:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}\|^{2}_{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}=\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathcal{L}_{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}[\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}]}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathcal{L}_{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}[\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}]}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\big({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}^{\top}}\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}_{\bm{I}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}\big)=\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\big(\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}\big)

(5.11)

Substituting this expression for the kinetic energy into the integrand of the action functional in (
5.8
) yields:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}\|^{2}_{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}+\mathcal{U}_{\sigma}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t})=\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\big(\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}\big)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{4}}{8}\text{Tr}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\right)=\text{Tr}\left(\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{4}}{8}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\right)

(5.12)

which yields an expression to the action functional written entirely in terms of the matrix
\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}
and the inverse covariance
\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}
.

Step 2: Use Continuity Equation to Derive Optimal Velocity.

To finish the proof, we aim to show that the action minimization problem in (
5.8
) is equivalent to the (
Gaussian SB Problem
) with
\mathbb{Q}:=\sigma_{t}\mathbb{B}
. To do this, we consider the special form of the (
Gaussian SB Problem
) with zero-mean marginals
30
30

30

since the mean follows the straight-line interpolation between the terminal marginals, we focus our attention on the covariance dynamics.
:

\displaystyle\inf_{(p_{t},\bm{v})}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{4}}{8}\|\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}\right]dt\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}\bm{v})\\
p_{0}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}),\hskip 9.24994ptp_{T}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}^{\prime})\end{cases}

(5.13)

Then, the solution yields a Gaussian density
p_{t}(\bm{x})=\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{t})
with zero-mean and covariance
\bm{\Sigma}_{t}
defined as:

\displaystyle p_{t}(\bm{x})=(2\pi)^{-\frac{1}{d}}(\text{det}\bm{\Sigma}_{t})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\bm{x}\right)

(5.14)

To derive the form of the optimal velocity
\bm{v}
, we can expand both sides of the continuity constraint given by:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\partial_{t}p_{t}}_{(\bigstar)}=\underbrace{-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}\bm{v})}_{(\diamond)}

(5.15)

For
(\bigstar)
, we differentiate the Gaussian form of
p_{t}(\bm{x})
with respect to time using the Jacobi formula
31
31

31

which states for any matrix valued function
\bm{A}_{t}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, the time derivative of its determinant is equal to
\frac{d}{dt}\text{det}(\bm{A}_{t})=\text{det}(\bm{A}_{t})\cdot\text{Tr}(\bm{A}_{t}^{-1})\cdot\frac{d}{dt}\bm{A}_{t}
, we get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=p_{t}(\bm{x})\left(\frac{1}{2}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\bm{x}-\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t})\right)

(
\bigstar
)

For
(\diamond)
, we use the chain rule of divergence and the gradient of the log-density
\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\bm{x}
to get:

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(p_{t}\bm{v})

\displaystyle=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla p_{t}},\bm{v}\rangle+p_{t}(\nabla\cdot\bm{v})=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}\nabla\log p_{t}},\bm{v}\rangle+p_{t}(\nabla\cdot\bm{v})

\displaystyle=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}(-\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\bm{x})},\bm{v}\rangle+p_{t}(\nabla\cdot\bm{v})=p_{t}\left(\langle-\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\bm{x},\bm{v}\rangle+\nabla\cdot\bm{v}\right)

(
\diamond
)

Plugging
(\bigstar)
and
(\diamond)
back into (
5.15
), we have:

\displaystyle p_{t}(\bm{x})\left(\frac{1}{2}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\bm{x}-\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t})\right)=p_{t}(\bm{x})\left(\langle\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\bm{x},\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\rangle-\nabla\cdot\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\right)

\displaystyle\implies\begin{cases}\nabla\cdot\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)=\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t})\\
\langle\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\bm{x},\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}\bm{x},\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\bm{x}\rangle\end{cases}

(5.16)

which means that the optimal velocity field must be linear in
\bm{x}
with the form:

\displaystyle\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)=\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}\bm{x}

(5.17)

Step 3: Prove Equivalence of the Action Minimization and Gaussian SB Problem.

Using the linear form of the velocity field in (
5.17
), the expectations appearing in the objective (
5.13
) can now be computed explicitly:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}]}_{(\bigstar)}+\frac{\sigma^{4}}{8}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}]}_{(\diamond)}

(5.18)

Since
\bm{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{t})
, we have
\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\top}]=\bm{\Sigma}_{t}
. Using this and the velocity field from (
5.17
), the kinetic energy term
(\bigstar)
can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\text{Tr}\left(\tilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}\right)\right]=\text{Tr}\bigg(\tilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\top}\right]}_{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\bigg(\tilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}_{t}\underbrace{\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}}}_{=\bm{I}}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}\bigg)=\text{Tr}\big(\tilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}\big)

(5.19)

Similarly, using
\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\bm{x}
and
\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}\bm{x}^{\top}]=\bm{\Sigma}_{t}\implies\mathbb{E}[\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{A}\bm{x}]=\text{Tr}(\bm{A}\bm{\Sigma}_{t})
, the score term
(\diamond)
reduces to:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{x}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-2}\bm{x}}\|^{2}\right]=\text{Tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-2}\bm{\Sigma}_{t})=\text{Tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1})

(5.20)

Plugging
(\bigstar)
and
(\diamond)
back into (
5.18
), we get:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{\text{Tr}\big(\tilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}\big)}_{(\bigstar)}+\frac{\sigma^{4}}{8}\underbrace{\text{Tr}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1})}_{(\diamond)}=\text{Tr}\left(\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\bm{S}}^{\top}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}_{t}\tilde{\bm{S}}_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{4}}{8}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\right)

(5.21)

which exactly aligns with the form of the action function derived in (
5.12
), and we have shown that solving the action minimization problem is equivalent to solving the (
Gaussian SB Problem
).
\square

This result shows that the infinite-dimensional
Gaussian SB Problem
) reduces to a finite-dimensional action functional defined entirely on the trajectory of covariance matrices. Intuitively, we have shown that the optimal evolution of the covariance follows a curve on the manifold of symmetric positive definite matrices
\mathbb{S}_{++}^{d}
where the first term measures the
kinetic energy of the covariance transport in the Bures–Wasserstein geometry
, and the second term acts as a potential induced by the diffusion term. The Schrödinger bridge, therefore, corresponds to the curve that minimizes this action while matching the endpoint covariances
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}=\bm{\Sigma}
and
\bm{\Sigma}_{T}=\bm{\Sigma}^{\prime}
. Next, we show that the solution for both problems yields a
closed-form solution
which can be written explicitly as an SDE.

Theorem 5.5

(Closed-Form Solution to Gaussian SB problem (Theorem 3 in
bunne2023schrodinger
))
.

The solution to the Gaussian SB problem
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
with linear reference measure
\mathbb{Q}
defined with the SDE
d\bm{X}^{\mathbb{Q}}_{t}=(c_{t}\bm{X}_{t}+\bm{\alpha}_{t})dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
is itself a Gaussian Markov stochastic process
\bm{X}_{0:T}
where the intermediate marginals are Gaussians
p_{t}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{t},\bm{\Sigma}_{t})
, with mean and covariance defined by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\bm{\mu}^{\star}_{t}&=\bar{r}_{t}\bm{\mu}_{0}+r_{t}\bm{\mu}_{T}+\bm{\zeta}(t)-r_{t}\bm{\zeta}(T)\\
\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}_{t}&=\bar{r}_{t}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+r_{t}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}+r_{t}\bar{r}_{t}(\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}+\bm{C}_{\sigma^{\star}}^{\top})+\kappa(t,t)(1-\rho_{t})\bm{I}\end{cases}

(5.22)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}r_{t}:=\frac{\kappa(t,T)}{\kappa(T,T)},\quad r_{t}:=\tau_{t}-r_{t}\tau_{T},\quad\sigma_{\star}:=\sqrt{\tau_{T}^{-1}\kappa(T,T)}\\
\bm{\zeta}(t):=\tau_{t}\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}_{s}ds,\quad\rho_{t}:=\frac{\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-2}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds}{\int_{0}^{T}\tau_{s}^{-2}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds}\end{cases}

The time evolution of
\bm{X}_{t}
follows a closed-form SDE:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}_{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}\bm{f}_{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{x},t):=\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})+\dot{\bm{\mu}}_{t}\\
\bm{P}_{t}:=\dot{r}_{t}(r_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}+\bar{r}_{t}\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}})\\
\bm{Q}_{t}:=-\dot{\bar{r}}_{t}(\bar{r}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+r_{t}\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}})\\
\bm{S}_{t}:=\bm{P}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{\top}+(c_{t}\kappa(t,t)(1-\rho_{t})-\sigma_{t}^{2}\rho_{t})\bm{I}\end{cases}

(5.23)

where the matrix
\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}
is symmetric.

Proof.
Following
bunne2023schrodinger
, we break down the proof in several steps.

Step 1: Solving the Static Gaussian SB problem.

First, we will solve the
static Gaussian SB
defined as the minimization problem:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbb{P}_{0,T}}\bigg\{\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{0,T}\|\mathbb{Q}_{0,T}):p_{0}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{0}),p_{T}=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{T},\bm{\Sigma}_{T})\bigg\}

(5.24)

where
\mathbb{Q}_{0,T}
is the endpoint law of the reference Gaussian process. By definition of linear SDEs of the form
d\bm{X}_{t}=(c_{t}\bm{X}_{t}+\bm{\alpha}_{t})dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
, the intermediate state
\bm{X}_{t}
can be written as
(
platen2010numerical
)
:

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{t}=\tau_{t}\left(\bm{X}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}_{s}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-1}\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\right),\quad\tau_{t}:=\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}c_{s}ds\right)

(5.25)

Conditioned on an initial state
\bm{X}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{0},\bm{\Sigma}_{0})
, the mean and covariance of
\bm{X}_{t}
takes the form:

\displaystyle\bm{\eta}(t)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{0}]=\tau_{t}\left(\bm{X}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}_{s}ds\right)

(5.26)

\displaystyle\bm{\kappa}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left[(\bm{X}_{t}-\bm{\eta}(t))(\bm{X}_{t}-\bm{\eta}(t^{\prime}))^{\top}\big|\bm{X}_{0}\right]=\left(\tau_{t}\tau_{t^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-2}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds\right)\bm{I}

(5.27)

Therefore, under the linear Gaussian measure
\mathbb{Q}
, the probability of the pair
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})
is a Gaussian defined by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\eta}(T),\bm{\kappa}(T,T)\bm{I})

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\text{det}(\bm{\kappa}(T,T)\bm{I})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}(\bm{x}_{T}-\bm{\eta}(T))(\kappa(T,T)\bm{I})^{-1}(\bm{x}_{T}-\bm{\eta}(T))\right)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\text{det}(\bm{\kappa}(T,T)\bm{I})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}\|\bm{x}_{T}-\tau_{T}\bm{x}_{0}-\bm{\zeta}(T)\|^{2}\right)

(5.28)

where the third equality follows from setting
\eta(T):=\tau_{T}\bm{x}_{0}+\tau_{T}\int_{0}^{T}\tau_{s}^{-1}\bm{\alpha}_{s}ds=\tau_{T}\bm{x}_{0}+\bm{\zeta}(T)
and the fact that the covariance
\kappa(T,T)\bm{I}
is isotropic. Now, we can expand the KL divergence and substitute (
5.28
) for
\mathbb{Q}_{0,T}
to get:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{0,T}\|\mathbb{Q}_{0,T})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}_{0,T}}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\log\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\log\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}_{0,T})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\log\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\log\left((2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\text{det}(\kappa(T,T)\bm{I})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\kappa(T,T)}\|\bm{x}_{T}-\tau_{T}\bm{x}_{0}-\bm{\zeta}(T)\|^{2}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\log\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(-\frac{d}{2}\log(2\pi)-\frac{1}{2}\text{det}(\kappa(T,T)\bm{I})-\frac{1}{2\kappa(T,T)}\|\bm{x}_{T}-\tau_{T}\bm{x}_{0}-\bm{\zeta}(T)\|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\log\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}+\underbrace{\frac{d}{2}\log(2\pi)+\frac{1}{2}\text{det}(\kappa(T,T)\bm{I})}_{\text{constant w.r.t. }\mathbb{P}_{0,T}}+\frac{1}{2\kappa(T,T)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\bm{x}_{T}-\tau_{T}\bm{x}_{0}-\bm{\zeta}(T)\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\log\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}+\frac{1}{2\kappa(T,T)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\bm{x}_{T}-\tau_{T}\bm{x}_{0}-\bm{\zeta}(T)\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})+\text{const.}

(5.29)

Now, we aim to simplify this objective such that rather than matching
\bm{X}_{T}
to
\tau_{T}\bm{X}_{0}+\bm{\zeta}(T)
over
\mathbb{P}_{0,T}
, where
\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}
are sampled from two disjoint marginals, we can directly match the initial marginal transformed to time
T
to the target marginal at time
T
. Concretely, we define a transformed marginal
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
defined by applying a change-of-variables:

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}=\tau_{T}\bm{X}_{0}+\bm{\zeta}(T)\sim\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{0},\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{0})\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{0}=\tau_{T}\bm{\mu}_{0}+\bm{\zeta}(T)\\
\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{0}=\tau_{T}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}\end{cases}

(5.30)

which yields the joint distribution
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T}
with marginals
\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{0},\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{0})
and
\bm{X}_{T}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{T},\bm{\Sigma}_{T})
. Since the change of variables is invertible and the map
\mathbb{P}_{0,T}\mapsto\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T}
is bijective, optimizing (
5.29
) over
\mathbb{P}_{0,T}
is equivalent to optimizing over
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T}
.

Under
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T}
, the quadratic cost becomes
\|\bm{x}_{T}-\tau_{T}\bm{x}_{0}-\bm{\zeta}(T)\|^{2}=\|\bm{x}_{T}-\tilde{\bm{x}}_{0}\|^{2}
and the entropy term
(\log\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0,T}
is shifted by a constant that does not depend on
\mathbb{P}_{0,T}
, yielding the KL divergence:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T}\|\mathbb{Q}_{0,T})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\log\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T})\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T}+\frac{1}{2\kappa(T,T)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\bm{x}_{T}-\tilde{\bm{x}}_{0}\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})+C

(5.31)

where
C
is some constant. Since multiplying the objective by a constant
\kappa(T,T)>0
does not change the minimizer, the optimization problem reduces to a standard entropic OT problem, defined as:

\displaystyle\min_{\mathbb{P}_{0,T}}\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{0,T}\|\mathbb{Q}_{0,T})

\displaystyle=\min_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T}}\text{KL}(\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T}\|\mathbb{Q}_{0,T})

\displaystyle=\min_{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T}}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{x}_{T}-\tilde{\bm{x}}_{0}\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T}+\kappa(T,T)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\log\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T})\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{0,T}\right\}

(5.32)

where
\kappa(T,T)
is now the entropic regularization weight
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}:=\kappa(T,T)
. From Lemma
5.1
, the solution of (
5.32
) is given by the joint Gaussian:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{\star}_{0,T}=\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mu}_{0}\\
\bm{\mu}_{T}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{0}&\tilde{\bm{C}}_{\tilde{\sigma}}\\
\tilde{\bm{C}}_{\tilde{\sigma}}^{\top}&\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{T}\end{bmatrix}\right),\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}\tilde{\sigma}=\sqrt{\kappa(T,T)}\\
\tilde{\bm{C}}_{\tilde{\sigma}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{\bm{D}}_{\tilde{\sigma}}\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\tilde{\sigma}^{2}\bm{I}\right)\\
\tilde{\bm{D}}_{\tilde{\sigma}}=\left(4\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\tilde{\sigma}^{4}\bm{I}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\end{cases}

(5.33)

Applying the inverse transform
\bm{X}_{0}=\tau_{T}^{-1}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}-\bm{\zeta}(T))
and the identity
\text{Cov}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})=\text{Cov}(\tau_{T}^{-1}\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})=\tau_{T}^{-1}\text{Cov}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})
, we recover the optimal joint Gaussian
\mathbb{P}_{0,T}
that solves the static Gaussian SB problem:

\displaystyle\pi_{0,T}^{\star}=\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mu}_{0}\\
\bm{\mu}_{T}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}&\tau_{T}^{-1}\tilde{\bm{C}}_{\tilde{\sigma}}\\
\tau_{T}^{-1}\tilde{\bm{C}}_{\tilde{\sigma}}^{\top}&\bm{\Sigma}_{T}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mu}_{0}\\
\bm{\mu}_{T}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}&\bm{C}_{\sigma^{\star}}\\
\bm{C}_{\sigma^{\star}}^{\top}&\bm{\Sigma}_{T}\end{bmatrix}\right)

(5.34)

where
\bm{C}_{\sigma^{\star}}:=\tau_{T}^{-1}\tilde{\bm{C}}_{\tilde{\sigma}}
. From our definition
\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{0}=\tau_{T}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}
in (
5.30
) and
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}:=\kappa(T,T)
, we expand
\tilde{\bm{D}}_{\tilde{\sigma}}
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{D}}_{\tilde{\sigma}}=\left(4(\tau_{T}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}(\tau_{T}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}-\kappa(T,T)^{2}\bm{I}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(4\tau_{T}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\kappa(T,T)^{2}\bm{I}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\tau_{T}\bigg(\underbrace{4\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\frac{\kappa(T,T)^{2}}{\tau_{T}^{2}}\bm{I}}_{\bm{D}_{\sigma_{\star}}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(5.35)

which means
\sigma_{\star}^{4}:=\frac{\kappa(T,T)^{2}}{\tau_{T}^{2}}
and
\sigma_{\star}^{2}=\frac{\kappa(T,T)}{\tau_{T}}
, proving the definition of
\sigma_{\star}
in Theorem
5.5
.

Step 2: Deriving the Endpoint-Conditioned Gaussian Bridge.

In this step, we aim to derive a closed form solution for the distribution of
\bm{X}_{t}

conditioned
on the endpoint pair
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})
, where the stochastic process follows a linear SDE defined in (
5.25
). Conditioned on the initial state
\bm{X}_{0}
, we use the mean
\bm{\eta}(t)
defined in (
5.26
) and covariance
\bm{\kappa}(t,t^{\prime})
defined in (
5.27
) to write the joint Gaussian distribution of
\bm{X}_{t},\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{0}
as:

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{t},\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{0}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mu}_{0}\\
\bm{\mu}_{T}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Sigma}_{00}&\bm{\Sigma}_{01}\\
\bm{\Sigma}_{10}&\bm{\Sigma}_{11}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\eta}(0)\\
\bm{\eta}(T)\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\kappa}(t,t)\bm{I}_{d}&\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\bm{I}_{d}\\
\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\bm{I}_{d}&\bm{\kappa}(T,T)\bm{I}_{d}\end{bmatrix}\right)

(5.36)

To compute the conditional distribution
\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{T}
from the joint Gaussian
(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{X}_{T})
, we use the fact that any conditional distribution of a joint Gaussian random vector remains Gaussian, with mean and covariance given by:

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}\sim\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\bm{\mu}},\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}),\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}=\bm{\mu}_{0}+\bm{\Sigma}_{01}\bm{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{1})\\
\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}=\bm{\Sigma}_{00}-\bm{\Sigma}_{01}\bm{\Sigma}_{11}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{10}\end{cases}

(5.37)

Therefore, from (
5.36
), we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}]

\displaystyle=\bm{\eta}(t)+\frac{\bm{\kappa}(t,T)}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}(\bm{X}_{T}-\bm{\eta}(T))

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:guassian-sde-mean})}{=}\tau_{t}\bm{X}_{0}+\bm{\zeta}(t)+\frac{\bm{\kappa}(t,T)}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}(\bm{X}_{T}-\tau_{T}\bm{X}_{0}-\bm{\zeta}(T))

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\tau_{t}-\frac{\bm{\kappa}(t,T)}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}\tau_{T}\right)}_{=:\bar{r}_{t}}\bm{X}_{0}+\underbrace{\frac{\bm{\kappa}(t,T)}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}}_{=:r_{t}}\bm{X}_{T}+\bm{\zeta}(t)-\underbrace{\frac{\bm{\kappa}(t,T)}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}}_{=:r_{t}}\bm{\zeta}(T)

\displaystyle=\bar{r}_{t}\bm{X}_{0}+r_{t}\bm{X}_{T}+\bm{\zeta}(t)-r_{t}\bm{\zeta}(T)

(5.38)

which means the expected bridge at
\bm{X}_{t}
is a weighted interpolation of the endpoints, with
\bar{r}_{t}
defining the weight of the starting point
\bm{X}_{0}
and
r_{t}
defining the weight of the end point, with a deterministic correction term
\bm{\zeta}(t)-r_{t}\bm{\zeta}(T)
. Similarly, we can derive the cross-covariance for any
t^{\prime}\geq t
as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\bm{X}_{t}-\mathbb{E}[\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}]\right)\left(\bm{X}_{t^{\prime}}-\mathbb{E}[\bm{X}_{t^{\prime}}|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}]\right)^{\top}\big|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}\right]=\left(\bm{\kappa}(t,t^{\prime})-\frac{\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\bm{\kappa}(t^{\prime},T)}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}\right)\bm{I}_{d}

(5.39)

which follows from the covariance identity
\text{Cov}(\bm{X}|\bm{Y})=\text{Cov}(\bm{X})-\text{Cov}(\bm{X},\bm{Y})\text{Cov}(\bm{Y})^{-1}\text{Cov}(\bm{Y},\bm{X})
. Since the mean and covariance uniquely define a Gaussian process, the law of the conditional bridge is equivalent to the expected mean of the bridge with some random fluctuations defined by a zero-mean Gaussian random variable
\bm{\xi}_{t}
and covariance process (
5.39
):

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{law}}{=}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}\right]}_{\text{deterministic given }\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}}+\underbrace{\bm{\xi}_{t}}_{\text{random fluctuation}}

\displaystyle=\bar{r}_{t}\bm{X}_{0}+r_{t}\bm{X}_{T}+\bm{\zeta}(t)-r_{t}\bm{\zeta}(T)+\bm{\xi}_{t}

(5.40)

which proves that the Gaussian process conditioned on endpoints is simply a deterministic interpolation with independent residual noise
\bm{\xi}_{t}
.

Step 3: Constructing the Gaussian SB from Endpoint Conditioned Bridges.

Now, we can derive the solution to the Gaussian SB problem (
5.2
) as a mixture of endpoint conditioned briges, where the endpoints are sampled from the optimal joint distribution
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\sim\mathbb{P}_{0,T}^{\star}
for
\pi_{0,T}^{\star}
defined in (
5.34
) as the solution to the static Gaussian SB. First, we recall the (
KL Divergence Chain Rule
) which states:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{0,T}\|\mathbb{Q}_{0,T})+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0,T}}\left[\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\cdot|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\|\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}))\right]

(5.41)

Therefore, the Gaussian SB can be constructed by first sampling
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\sim\mathbb{P}_{0,T}^{\star}
via (
5.34
) which yields the law:

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{t}\overset{\text{law}}{=}\bar{r}_{t}\bm{X}_{0}+r_{t}\bm{X}_{T}+\bm{\zeta}(t)-r_{t}\bm{\zeta}(T)+\bm{\xi}_{t}

(5.42)

where the covariance of the bridge measure is given by (
5.27
) with
t^{\prime}=t
:

\displaystyle\text{Cov}(\bm{\xi}_{t})\overset{(\ref{eq:gaussian-cov-process})}{=}\bigg(\bm{\kappa}(t,t)-\underbrace{\frac{\bm{\kappa}(t,T)^{2}}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}}_{r_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)}\bigg)\bm{I}_{d}=\bm{\kappa}(t,t)\bigg(1-\underbrace{\frac{\bm{\kappa}(t,T)^{2}}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)\bm{\kappa}(t,t)}}_{=:\rho_{t}}\bigg)\bm{I}=:\bm{\kappa}(t,t)(1-\rho_{t})\bm{I}

(5.43)

Applying the linearity of expectation, we derive the mean
\bm{\mu}_{t}^{\star}
of the Gaussian SB as:

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}_{t}^{\star}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0,T}^{\star}}[\bm{X}_{t}]

\displaystyle=\bar{r}_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0,T}^{\star}}[\bm{X}_{0}]+r_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0,T}^{\star}}[\bm{X}_{T}]+\bm{\zeta}(t)-r_{t}\bm{\zeta}(T)+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}_{0,T}^{\star}}[\bm{\xi}_{t}]

\displaystyle=\bar{r}_{t}\bm{\mu}_{0}+r_{t}\bm{\mu}_{T}+\bm{\zeta}(t)-r_{t}\bm{\zeta}(T)

(5.44)

Applying the covariance identity
32
32

32

\text{Cov}(a\bm{A}+b\bm{B})=a^{2}\text{Cov}(\bm{A})+b^{2}\text{Cov}(\bm{B})+ab\text{Cov}(\bm{A},\bm{B})+ab\text{Cov}(\bm{B},\bm{A})
, we derive the covariance
\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}_{t}
of the Gaussian SB as:

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}_{t}=\text{Cov}(\bm{X}_{t})

\displaystyle=\text{Cov}\left(\bar{r}_{t}\bm{X}_{0}+r_{t}\bm{X}_{T}+\bm{\zeta}(t)-r_{t}\bm{\zeta}(T)+\bm{\xi}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Cov}\left(\bar{r}_{t}\bm{X}_{0}+r_{t}\bm{X}_{T}\right)+\text{Cov}\left(\bm{\xi}_{t}\right)

(5.45)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:cov-noise-gaussian})}{=}\bar{r}_{t}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+r_{t}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}+r_{t}\bar{r}_{t}\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}+r_{t}\bar{r}_{t}\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}^{\top}+\bm{\kappa}(t,t)(1-\rho_{t})\bm{I}

\displaystyle=\bar{r}_{t}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+r_{t}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}+r_{t}\bar{r}_{t}(\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}+\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}^{\top})+\bm{\kappa}(t,t)(1-\rho_{t})\bm{I}

(5.46)

which concludes the first part of the proof that states the mean and covariance of the optimal marginal distributions of the Gaussian SB, given by:

\displaystyle\boxed{\begin{cases}\bm{\mu}^{\star}_{t}&=\bar{r}_{t}\bm{\mu}_{0}+r_{t}\bm{\mu}_{T}+\bm{\zeta}(t)-r_{t}\bm{\zeta}(T)\\
\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}_{t}&=\bar{r}_{t}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+r_{t}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}+r_{t}\bar{r}_{t}(\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}+\bm{C}_{\sigma^{\star}}^{\top})+\kappa(t,t)(1-\rho_{t})\bm{I}_{d}\end{cases}}

(5.47)

Since the endpoints are defined by a joint Gaussian
\pi_{0,T}^{\star}
and the bridge is a linear interpolation of the endpoints with added Gaussian noise
\bm{\xi}_{t}
, we conclude that the optimal SB measure is indeed Gaussian. Furthermore, applying the ideas from Section
4.5
, we have that the solution to the Gaussian SB problem is the unique measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
that is both Markov and in the reciprocal class
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
.

Step 4: Deriving the SDE of the Gaussian Bridge.

To simulate the solution to the Gaussian SB in practice, it is useful to derive the
associated SDE
which defines the forward propagation of the bridge given an intermediate state.

Let
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth test function on the SB process
\bm{X}_{0:T}
. Then, the core of this derivation is to show that
\phi(\bm{X}_{t})
is an Itô process satisfying Itô’s formula (
2.7
) with a
generator

\mathcal{A}_{t}
of the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t}\phi(\bm{x},t):=\partial_{t}\phi(\bm{x},t)+\bm{f}_{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{x},t)^{\top}\nabla\phi(\bm{x},t)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{x},t)

(5.48)

for a control drift
\bm{f}_{\mathcal{N}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
that defines the SDE of
\bm{X}_{t}
given by
d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}_{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
. To do this, we must match the time derivative of
\phi(\bm{X}_{t},t)
with (
5.48
) by taking the continuous time limit:

\displaystyle\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left\{\frac{\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]-\phi(\bm{x},t)}{\Delta t}\right\}

(5.49)

The first step is to derive
\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]
and take the infinitesimal time limit
\Delta t\to 0
. Since the conditional expectation is a Gaussian, we aim to derive its
mean

\bm{\mu}_{t+\Delta t}:=\mathbb{E}[\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]=\bm{\mu}_{t}+\bm{\Sigma}_{t,t+\Delta t}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})
(where
\bm{\Sigma}_{t,t+\Delta t}
denotes the cross-covariance) and
covariance

\bm{\Sigma}_{t+\Delta t}:=\text{Cov}(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})
using the
first-order approximation
. First, we compute the time derivative
\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\star}_{t}
as:

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}^{\star}_{t}:=\partial_{t}\bm{\Sigma}^{\star}_{t}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\left(\bar{r}_{t}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+r_{t}^{2}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}+r_{t}\bar{r}_{t}(\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}+\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}^{\top})+\left(\bm{\kappa}(t,t)-\frac{\bm{\kappa}(t,T)^{2}}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}\right)\bm{I}\right)

\displaystyle=2\bar{r}_{t}\dot{\bar{r}}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+2r_{t}\dot{r}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}+(\dot{r}_{t}\bar{r}_{t}+r_{t}\dot{\bar{r}}_{t})(\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}+\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}^{\top})+\bigg(\partial_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,t)-2\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\underbrace{\partial_{t}\frac{\bm{\kappa}(t,T)}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}}_{=\dot{r}_{t}}\bigg)\bm{I}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\dot{r}_{t}\left(r_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}+\bar{r}_{t}\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}\right)}_{\bm{P}_{t}}+\underbrace{\dot{r}_{t}\left(r_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}+\bar{r}_{t}\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}^{\top}\right)}_{\bm{P}_{t}^{\top}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\dot{\bar{r}}_{t}\left(\bar{r}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+r_{t}\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}\right)}_{-\bm{Q}_{t}}+\underbrace{\dot{\bar{r}}_{t}\left(\bar{r}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+r_{t}\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}^{\top}\right)}_{-\bm{Q}_{t}^{\top}}+\bigg(\partial_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,t)-2\dot{r}_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\bigg)\bm{I}

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{P}_{t}+\bm{P}^{\top}_{t}\right)-\left(\bm{Q}_{t}+\bm{Q}^{\top}_{t}\right)+\bigg(\partial_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,t)-2\dot{r}_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\bigg)\bm{I}

(5.50)

Next, we derive the cross-covariance
\bm{\Sigma}_{t,t+\Delta t}
using the definition in (
5.39
) given by
\bm{\Sigma}_{t,t+\Delta t}:=\mathbb{E}\left[(\bm{X}_{t}-\bm{\mu}_{t})(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}-\bm{\mu}_{t+\Delta t})^{\top}\right]
where we make the following decomposition:

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{t}-\bm{\mu}_{t}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:xt-law-gaussian})}{=}\bar{r}_{t}(\bm{X}_{0}-\bm{\mu}_{0})+r_{t}(\bm{X}_{T}-\bm{\mu}_{T})+\bm{\xi}_{t}

(5.51)

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}-\bm{\mu}_{t+\Delta t}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:xt-law-gaussian})}{=}\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{X}_{0}-\bm{\mu}_{0})+r_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{X}_{T}-\bm{\mu}_{T})+\bm{\xi}_{t+\Delta t}

(5.52)

Setting
\bm{A}:=\bm{X}_{0}-\bm{\mu}_{0}
,
\bm{B}:=\bm{X}_{T}-\bm{\mu}_{T}
, we can write the cross covariance as:

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{t,t+\Delta t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[(\bar{r}_{t}\bm{A}+r_{t}\bm{B}+\bm{\xi}_{t})(\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}\bm{A}+r_{t+\Delta t}\bm{B}+\bm{\xi}_{t+\Delta t})^{\top}\right]

\displaystyle=\bar{r}_{t}\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\bm{A}\bm{A}^{\top}]}_{=:\bm{\Sigma}_{0}}+\bar{r}_{t}r_{t+\Delta t}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\bm{A}\bm{B}^{\top}]}_{=:\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}}+\bar{r}_{t}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\bm{A}\bm{\xi}_{t+\Delta t}^{\top}]}_{=0}+r_{t}\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\bm{B}\bm{A}^{\top}]}_{=:\bm{C}^{\top}_{\sigma_{\star}}}

\displaystyle+r_{t}r_{t+\Delta t}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\bm{B}\bm{B}^{\top}]}_{\bm{\Sigma}_{T}}+r_{t}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\bm{B}\bm{\xi}_{t}^{\top}]}_{=0}+\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\bm{\xi}_{t}\bm{A}^{\top}]}_{=0}+r_{t+\Delta t}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\bm{\xi}_{t}\bm{B}^{\top}]}_{=0}+\mathbb{E}[\bm{\xi}_{t}\bm{\xi}_{t+\Delta t}^{\top}]

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:gaussian-cov-process})}{=}\bar{r}_{t}\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+\bar{r}_{t}r_{t+\Delta t}\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}+r_{t}\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}\bm{C}^{\top}_{\sigma_{\star}}+r_{t}r_{t+\Delta t}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}+\left(\bm{\kappa}(t,t+\Delta t)-\frac{\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\bm{\kappa}(t+\Delta t,T)}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}\right)\bm{I}
