\displaystyle=\bar{r}_{t}\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+\bar{r}_{t}r_{t+\Delta t}\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}+r_{t}\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}\bm{C}^{\top}_{\sigma_{\star}}+r_{t}r_{t+\Delta t}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}+(\bm{\kappa}(t,t+\Delta t)-r_{t+\Delta t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T))\bm{I}

(5.53)

where all covariances with only a single noise term
\bm{\xi}_{t}
vanish since the noise is
independent
of
\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T}
. Since
\bm{\Sigma}_{t}
has the same structure as
\bm{\Sigma}_{t+\Delta t}
, we can write the cross covariance as
\bm{\Sigma}_{t,t+\Delta t}=\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta\bm{\Sigma}
, where
\Delta\bm{\Sigma}
is the change in covariance from
t\to t+\Delta t
, which gives the following expression:

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{t,t+\Delta t}=

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\bar{r}_{t}(\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\bar{r}_{t}})\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+\bar{r}_{t}(r_{t+\Delta t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-r_{t}})\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}+r_{t}(\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\bar{r}_{t}})\bm{C}^{\top}_{\sigma_{\star}}+r_{t}(r_{t+\Delta t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-r_{t}})\bm{\Sigma}_{T}

\displaystyle+(\bm{\kappa}(t,t+\Delta t){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\bm{\kappa}(t,t)}-r_{t+\Delta t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}+r_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)})\bm{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+(\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}-\bar{r}_{t})\left(\bar{r}_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{0}+r_{t}\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}^{\top}\right)+(r_{t+\Delta t}-r_{t})\left(\bar{r}_{t}\bm{C}_{\sigma_{\star}}+r_{t}\bm{\Sigma}_{T}\right)

\displaystyle+(\bm{\kappa}(t,t+\Delta t)-\bm{\kappa}(t,t)-r_{t+\Delta t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)+r_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T))\bm{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\frac{\bar{r}_{t+\Delta t}-\bar{r}_{t}}{\dot{r}_{t}}\bm{P}_{t}-\frac{r_{t+\Delta t}-r_{t}}{\dot{\bar{r}}_{t}}\bm{Q}^{\top}_{t}+(\bm{\kappa}(t,t+\Delta t){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{-\kappa}(t,t)}-r_{t+\Delta t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}+r_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)})\bm{I}

(5.54)

Applying the
first-order approximation
which preserves all terms scaled by
h
and denoting the higher-order terms with
o(\Delta t)
, we have:

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}_{t,t+\Delta t}=\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\underbrace{\left\{\bm{P}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{\top}+\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\partial\bm{\kappa}}{dt^{\prime}}(t,t)}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\dot{r}_{t}}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\right]\bm{I}\right\}}_{=:\bm{S}_{t}}+o(\Delta t)

(5.55)

\displaystyle=\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\left\{\bm{P}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{\top}+\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}\left(\tau_{t}\tau_{t^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-2}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds\right)\bigg|_{t^{\prime}=t}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\frac{1}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}\partial_{t}\left(\tau_{t}\tau_{T}\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-2}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds\right)}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\right]\bm{I}\right\}+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle=\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\bigg\{\bm{P}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{\top}+\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\tau_{t}\dot{\tau}_{t}\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-2}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds}_{c_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,t)}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\frac{1}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}\bigg(\underbrace{\dot{\tau}_{t}\tau_{T}\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-2}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds}_{c_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)}+\tau_{t}\tau_{T}\underbrace{\partial_{t}\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-2}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds}_{=\tau_{t}^{-2}\sigma_{t}^{2}\text{ (Leibniz rule)}}\bigg)}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\bigg]\bm{I}\bigg\}+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle=\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\bigg\{\bm{P}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{\top}+\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}c_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,t)}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bigg(\frac{c_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}+\frac{\tau_{T}\sigma_{t}^{2}}{\tau_{t}\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}}\bigg)\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\bigg]\bm{I}\bigg\}+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle=\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\bigg\{\bm{P}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{\top}+\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}c_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,t)}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\frac{c_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)^{2}}{\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}}_{c_{t}\rho_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,t)}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\underbrace{\frac{\tau_{T}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)}{\tau_{t}\bm{\kappa}(T,T)}}_{=:\rho_{t}}}\bigg]\bm{I}\bigg\}+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle=\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\underbrace{\big\{\bm{P}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{\top}+\big[c_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,t)(1-\rho_{t})+\sigma_{t}^{2}\rho_{t}\big]\bm{I}\big\}}_{=:\bm{S}_{t}}}+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle=\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\bm{S}_{t}}+o(\Delta t)

(5.56)

which gives us the expression for the cross covariance
\bm{\Sigma}_{t,t+\Delta t}=\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\bm{S}_{t}+o(\Delta t)
. Now, we can expand the mean
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t}
and covariance
\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t+\Delta t}
of the conditional process
\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}|\bm{X}_{t}
using the same logic as (
5.37
) to get:

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t}

\displaystyle=\bm{\mu}_{t+\Delta t}+\bm{\Sigma}^{\top}_{t,t+\Delta t}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})

(5.57)

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t+\Delta t}

\displaystyle=\bm{\Sigma}_{t+\Delta t}+\bm{\Sigma}^{\top}_{t,t+\Delta t}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\bm{\Sigma}_{t,t+\Delta t}

(5.58)

Starting with the mean
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t}
, we substitute (
5.56
) to get:

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{\mu}_{t+\Delta t}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\bm{S}_{t})}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}+o(\Delta t)}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{\mu}_{t}+\Delta t\dot{\bm{\mu}}_{t}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\underbrace{\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}}_{=\bm{I}}+h\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1})}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}+o(\Delta t)}

\displaystyle=\bm{\mu}_{t}+\Delta t\dot{\bm{\mu}}_{t}+\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t}+(h\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1})(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle=\boxed{\bm{x}+h(\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})+\dot{\bm{\mu}}_{t})+o(\Delta t)}

(5.59)

Then, substituting (
5.56
) into the covariance, we get:

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t+\Delta t}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{\Sigma}_{t+\Delta t}}-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\bm{S}_{t})^{\top}}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\bm{S}_{t})+o(\Delta t)}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}}-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\bm{S}_{t})^{\top}}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\bm{S}_{t})+o(\Delta t)}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}}-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\bm{I}+h\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1})}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\bm{S}_{t})+o(\Delta t)}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}}-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\bm{\Sigma}_{t}+\Delta t\bm{S}_{t}+\Delta t\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}+\underbrace{h^{2}\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}\bm{S}_{t}}_{o(\Delta t)})}+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle=h(\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}-\bm{S}_{t}-\bm{S}_{t}^{\top})+o(\Delta t)

(5.60)

and substituting the definition for
\bm{S}_{t}
from (
5.55
) and
\dot{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t}
from (
5.50
), we get:

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t+\Delta t}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:gaus-S-def2},\ref{eq:gaus-dot-cov-def})}{=}h\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\left(\bm{P}_{t}+\bm{P}^{\top}_{t}\right)-\left(\bm{Q}_{t}+\bm{Q}^{\top}_{t}\right)+\bigg(\partial_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,t)-2\dot{r}_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\bigg)\bm{I}}

\displaystyle-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{P}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{\top}+\left[\frac{\partial\bm{\kappa}}{dt^{\prime}}(t,t)-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\dot{r}_{t}}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\right]\bm{I}}-{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\bigg(\bm{P}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{\top}+\left[\frac{\partial\bm{\kappa}}{dt^{\prime}}(t,t)-\dot{r}_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)\right]\bm{I}\bigg)^{\top}}\bigg]+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle=h\bigg({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,t)}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}2\frac{\partial\bm{\kappa}}{dt^{\prime}}(t,t)}\bigg)\bm{I}+o(\Delta t)

(5.61)

Now, substituting (
5.27
) and decomposing
\frac{\partial\bm{\kappa}}{dt^{\prime}}(t,t)-\dot{r}_{t}\bm{\kappa}(t,T)
using the same procedure as (
5.55
-
5.56
), we have:

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t+\Delta t}

\displaystyle=h\bigg({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}\left(\tau_{t}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-2}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds\right)}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}2\tau_{t}\dot{\tau}\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-2}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds}\bigg)\bm{I}+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle=h\bigg({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}2\dot{\tau}_{t}\tau_{t}\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-2}\sigma_{t}^{2}ds+\sigma_{t}^{2}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}2\tau_{t}\dot{\tau}\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{s}^{-2}\sigma_{s}^{2}ds}\bigg)\bm{I}+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle=\boxed{h\sigma_{t}^{2}\bm{I}+o(\Delta t)}

(5.62)

Finally, we are ready to derive the form of
\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]
as the expected value at
\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}
. Since
\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
is Gaussian with mean
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t}
and covariance
\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t+\Delta t}
, we can write:

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}=\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t}+\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t+\Delta t}^{1/2}\bm{z},\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{d})

(5.63)

Applying the second-order Taylor expansion of
\phi(\tilde{\bm{x}}+\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)
around
\tilde{\bm{x}}=0
yields:

\displaystyle\phi(\tilde{\bm{x}}+\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)=\phi(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)+\nabla\phi^{\top}\tilde{\bm{x}}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\bm{x}}^{\top}\nabla^{2}\phi\tilde{\bm{x}}+o(\|\tilde{\bm{x}}\|^{2})

(5.64)

Given the Gaussian identities
\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}]=\bm{0}
and
\mathbb{E}[\bm{z}\bm{z}^{\top}]=\bm{I}_{d}
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{x}}]=\bm{0}
and
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{x}}\tilde{\bm{x}}^{\top}]=\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t+\Delta t}
. Using these identities and taking the conditional expectation, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\phi(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)}+{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\left(\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t+\Delta t}\nabla^{2}\phi(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)\right)}+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:gaus-proof-final})}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\phi(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)}+{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\frac{h\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)}+o(\Delta t)

(5.65)

where the second line follows from
\tilde{\bm{\Sigma}}_{t+\Delta t}=h\sigma_{t}^{2}\bm{I}+o(\Delta t)
from (
5.62
). From (
5.59
), the mean expands to
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t}=\bm{x}+h(\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})+\dot{\bm{\mu}}_{t})+o(\Delta t)=\bm{x}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t)
, where
h
is some infinitesimal time increment, we can apply the Taylor expansion around
\bm{x}
to get:

\displaystyle\phi(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)

\displaystyle=\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t)+h\big\langle\nabla\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t),\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})+\dot{\bm{\mu}}_{t}\big\rangle+o(\Delta t)

(5.66)

Then, substituting the expression (
5.66
) into (
5.65
), we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\phi(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)}+{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\frac{h\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)}+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t)+h\big\langle\nabla\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t),\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})+\dot{\bm{\mu}}_{t}\big\rangle}+{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\frac{h\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t)}+o(\Delta t)

\displaystyle=\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t)+h\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t)+\big\langle\nabla\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t),\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})+\dot{\bm{\mu}}_{t}\big\rangle\right)+o(\Delta t)

(5.67)

Dividing both sides by
h
and taking the limit as
h\to 0
, we recover the final form of the
generator

\mathcal{A}_{t}
for
\phi(\bm{x},t)
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t}\phi(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left\{\frac{\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]-\phi(\bm{x},t)}{h}\right\}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\bigg\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\frac{\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t)-\phi(\bm{x},t)}{h}}+{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t)}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}\big\langle\nabla\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t),\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})+\dot{\bm{\mu}}_{t}\big\rangle}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\phi(\bm{x},t)+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t)+\big\langle\nabla\phi(\bm{x},t+\Delta t),{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})+\dot{\bm{\mu}}_{t}}\big\rangle

(5.68)

where the control drift takes the form:

\displaystyle\boxed{\bm{f}_{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{x},t):=\underbrace{\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})}_{\text{shape-correcting term}}+\underbrace{\dot{\bm{\mu}}_{t}}_{\text{drift of mean}}}

(Gaussian SB Drift)

which can be decomposed into the mean drift of the Gaussian and a shape-correcting term that consists of the deviation from the mean
(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})
, how expensive it is under the covariance
\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}
, and how strongly to correct it
\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}
.

As we show in Proposition
2.23
, the optimal control drift of the non-linear SB problem can be written as a gradient field as
\bm{f}_{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{x},t)=\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})
of the Lagrange multiplier
\psi_{t}(\bm{x})
such that the Jacobian of
\bm{f}_{\mathcal{N}}
is symmetric or
\partial_{i}\bm{f}_{j}=\partial_{j}\bm{f}_{i}
. Since we have:

\displaystyle\nabla\bm{f}_{\mathcal{N}}(\bm{x},t)=\nabla(\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}(\bm{x}-\bm{\mu}_{t})+\dot{\bm{\mu}}_{t})=\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}

(5.69)

this implies that
\bm{S}_{t}^{\top}\bm{\Sigma}_{t}^{-1}
is symmetric and concludes our proof.
\square

From this result, we establish that, when the reference dynamics are linear–Gaussian, the (
Gaussian SB Problem
) admits an
exact closed-form solution that remains within the class of Gaussian Markov processes
, which is fully characterized by finite-dimensional evolutions of the mean and covariance and by an affine drift field that admits a gradient-field structure. This reveals the
core insight
that entropy-regularized transport between Gaussian marginals preserves the Gaussian structure of the distribution and induces a
potential-driven flow
that conserves distributional structure. This Gaussian case serves as a solvable model that provides a foundation for the upcoming sections on more specialized variations of the SB problem.

5.2
Generalized Schrödinger Bridge Problem

Prerequisite: Section
2.7
,
2.8
,
4.3

Figure 10:
Generalized Schrödinger Bridge Problem.
The generalized SB problem extends the dynamic SB problem by introducing a mean-field interaction cost
\mathcal{I}(x,p_{t})
that depends on the evolving marginal distribution. The resulting objective balances kinetic energy
\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}
with an interaction energy term integrated against the state density
p_{t}(x)
. The optimal dynamics transport particles between the prescribed marginals
(\pi_{0},\pi_{T})
while accounting for collective interactions encoded by
\mathcal{I}(x,p_{t})
, leading to a coupled Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman and Fokker-Planck system that characterizes the optimal bridge.

Up to this point, we have considered particles as acting independently via an optimal path along the Schrödinger bridge such that the total distribution over many particles matches the endpoint marginals. However, in many settings, particles evolve via stochastic trajectories that depend not only on their individual state and control but also
interactions with the population distribution

p_{t}
. Solving for the optimal evolution of particles is referred to as solving a
Mean-Field Game
, since the particles are influenced by the average dynamics of the population.

At equilibrium, each particle evolves via optimal control given the density of the particles
p_{t}
, and the population
p_{t}
is generated from these optimally controlled particles. To determine the optimal dynamics, we leverage the definition of the
value function

\psi_{t}(\bm{x}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
which defines the minimum cost of transporting
\bm{x}
at time
t
. The gradient of the function
\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
defines a potential field that adjusts the drift of the reference process so that the evolving distribution satisfies the endpoint constraints.

The value function
\psi_{t}
and marginal density
p_{t}
are coupled via a
Hamiltonian function

\mathcal{H}(\bm{x},\nabla\psi_{t},p_{t}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
, which describes the dynamics of the interacting particles and an
interaction function

\mathcal{I}(\bm{x},p_{t}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
that can be defined depending on the task. Given these functions, the pair of optimal value function and optimal state PDF
(\psi_{t},p^{\star}_{t})
solves the following pair of PDEs:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\psi_{t}+\mathcal{H}(\bm{x},\nabla\psi_{t},p^{\star}_{t})+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\psi_{t}=\mathcal{I}(\bm{x},p_{t}^{\star})&\psi_{T}(\bm{x})=\Phi(\bm{x})\\
\partial_{t}p^{\star}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(p_{t}^{\star}\nabla_{\nabla\psi_{t}}\mathcal{H}(\bm{x},\nabla\psi_{t},p^{\star}_{t}))-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta p^{\star}_{t}=0&p_{0}=\pi_{0},p_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(5.70)

where
\Phi(\bm{x}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a terminal cost that can be defined to enforce a target distribution in the case of Schrödinger bridges. Given a solution pair
(\psi_{t},p^{\star}_{t})
, the dynamics of each particle evolve via the SDE defined by:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=-\nabla_{\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})}\mathcal{H}(\bm{X}_{t},\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t}),p_{t})dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}

(5.71)

which satisfies the marginals defined by
p_{t}
as the number of particles goes to infinity.

Now that we have defined the Mean-Field Game problem, we can observe that the coupled PDEs in (
5.70
) resemble the (
HJB-FP System
) defined for the dynamic SB problem in Proposition
2.23
with the additional
mean field interaction

\mathcal{I}(\bm{x},p_{t}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, we can leverage this connection between mean-field games and dynamic SB to define the
Generalized Schrödinger Bridge Problem

(
chen2015optimal
;
chen2023density
;
liu2022deep
;
liu2023generalized
)
.

Definition 5.6

(Generalized Schrödinger Bridge Problem)
.

Given an interaction cost
\mathcal{I}(\bm{x},p_{t}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
, reference drift
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
, diffusion coefficient
\sigma_{t}
, and terminal marginal constraints
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the
generalized SB problem
can be written as:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{u}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0,T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}+\mathcal{I}(\bm{X}_{t},p_{t},t)\right)dt\bigg]

(Generalized SB Problem)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0},\quad\bm{X}_{T}\sim\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(5.72)

which can also be written as the density-space objective:

\displaystyle\inf_{(\bm{u},p_{t})}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+\mathcal{I}(\bm{x},p_{t},t)\right)p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt\bigg]

(5.73)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot\big(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))\big)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}\\
p_{0}=\pi_{0},\quad p_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(5.74)

Following the derivation of the nonlinear HJB-FP system in Section
2.7
, which defines the optimal pair
(\psi_{t},p^{\star}_{t})
and the Hopf-Cole transform in Section
2.8
, which defines the optimal potentials
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
, we can derive the optimality conditions of the
generalized SB problem
.

Definition 5.7

(Optimality Conditions of Generalized Schrödinger Bridge Problem)
.

Defining the Hamiltonian as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\bm{x},\nabla\psi_{t},p_{t}):=\frac{1}{2}\|\sigma_{t}\nabla\psi_{t}\|^{2}-\nabla\psi_{t}^{\top}\bm{f}(\bm{x},p_{t},t)

(5.75)

We can write the optimality conditions
(\psi_{t},p^{\star}_{t})
as the pair of coupled non-linear HJB-FP system with the
interaction term

\mathcal{I}(\bm{x},p_{t}^{\star}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
given by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\psi_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\psi_{t}\|^{2}+\langle\nabla\psi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\psi_{t}=\mathcal{I}(\bm{x},p^{\star}_{t})\\
\partial_{t}p^{\star}_{t}+\nabla\cdot(p_{t}^{\star}(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\psi_{t}))-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}^{\star}=0\end{cases}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}p_{0}=\pi_{0}\\
p_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(5.76)

To transform the system of non-linear PDEs to linear PDEs, we can apply the
Hopf-Cole transform
discussed in Section
2.8
, which defines the following change-of-variables:

\displaystyle\psi_{t}(\bm{x})=\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x}),\quad p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})

(5.77)

which satisfy the pair of
linear PDEs
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}=-\langle\nabla\varphi_{t},\bm{f}\rangle-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\varphi_{t}+\mathcal{I}\varphi_{t}\\
\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(\hat{\varphi}_{t}\bm{f})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}-\mathcal{I}\hat{\varphi}_{t}\end{cases}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}\pi_{0}=\varphi_{0}\hat{\varphi}_{0}\\
\pi_{T}=\varphi_{T}\hat{\varphi}_{T}\end{cases}

(5.78)

which differs from the linear PDEs from (
Hopf-Cole PDEs
) only by the interaction term
\mathcal{I}(\bm{x},p_{t}^{\star})
.

In Section
4.3
, we established a pair of
forward-backward SDEs
that define the evolution of the dynamic Schrödinger bridge problem, which describes the evolution of the Schrödinger potentials
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
from the system of linear PDEs derived via the Hopf-Cole transform (Section
2.8
). Given the modified Hopf-Cole linear PDEs for the generalized SB problem, we must define a new set of FBSDEs that integrates the mean-field interaction term
\mathcal{I}
.

Proposition 5.8

(Generalized SB Forward-Backward SDEs (Theorem 2 in
liu2022deep
))
.

Given the Schrödinger potentials
(\varphi_{t},\hat{\varphi}_{t})
that solve the Hopf-Cole linear PDEs from (
5.78
) and a stochastic process
\bm{X}_{0:T}
that satisfies the forward-time SDE, we define additional stochastic processes as:

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{t}=\bm{Y}(\bm{X}_{t},t):=\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t}),

\displaystyle\bm{Z}_{t}=\bm{Z}(\bm{X}_{t},t):=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})

(5.79)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}=\widehat{\bm{Y}}(\bm{X}_{t},t):=\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t}),

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}=\widehat{\bm{Z}}(\bm{X}_{t},t):=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}(\bm{X}_{t})

(5.80)

Then, the forward time evolution
t\in[0,T]
of
\bm{X}_{0:T}
,
(\bm{Y}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
and
(\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
are characterized by the FBSDEs:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},p^{\star}_{t})+\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Z}_{t}})dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
d\bm{Y}_{t}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Z}_{t}}\|^{2}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathcal{I}(\bm{X}_{t},p^{\star}_{t})}\right)dt+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Z}_{t}}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
d\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}}\|^{2}+\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}}-\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},p^{\star}_{t}))-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Z}_{t}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathcal{I}(\bm{X}_{t},p^{\star}_{t})}\right)dt+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
\end{cases}

(5.81)

To condition the dynamics on the target distribution
\pi_{T}
, on the time-reversed coordinate
s:=T-t\in[0,T]
, we define the time-reversed SDE
(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s})_{s\in[0,T]}
and the corresponding FBSDEs for
(\bm{Y}_{s})_{s\in[0,T]}
and
(\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{s})_{s\in[0,T]}
as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=(-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},p^{\star}_{s})+\sigma_{s}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{s}})ds+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{s}\\
d\bm{Y}_{s}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Z}_{s}}\|^{2}+\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Z}_{s}}+\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},p^{\star}_{s}))-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Z}_{s}}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{s}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},p^{\star}_{s})}\right)dt+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Z}_{s}}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{s}\\
d\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{s}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{s}}\|^{2}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathcal{I}(\bm{X}_{s},p_{s}^{\star})}\right)ds+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{s}}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{s}\\
\end{cases}

(5.82)

Given the SB optimality condition
p^{\star}_{t}=\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}
, we define the
interaction term

\mathcal{I}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\bm{X}_{t},p^{\star}_{t})=\mathcal{I}(\bm{X}_{t},\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}),\quad\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},p^{\star}_{t})=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t})

(5.83)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},p^{\star}_{s})=\mathcal{I}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},\varphi_{s}\hat{\varphi}_{s}),\quad\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},p^{\star}_{s})=\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},\varphi_{s}\hat{\varphi}_{s})

(5.84)

Proof Sketch.
This proof follows closely from the derivation in Section
4.3
, where we apply Itô’s formula to get
d\log\varphi_{t}
and
d\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}
, but instead of substituting the Hopf-Cole transform for the standard dynamic SB problem for
\partial_{t}\varphi_{t}
and
\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}
, we substitute the Hopf-Cole transform with the interaction term
\mathcal{I}
given in (
5.78
). The reverse-time FBSDEs follow the same steps after reformulating the HJB-FP PDEs with the reversed time coordinate
s:=T-t
and applying the Hopf-Cole transform.
\square

Compared to the dynamic SB FBSDEs derived in Section
4.3
, the only structural modification appears through the interaction term
\mathcal{I}(\bm{x},p_{t}^{\star})
, which introduces a mean-field dependence into both the forward and backward SDEs. The forward–backward structure remains similar to the dynamic SB FB-SDEs, but the system now encodes collective effects through the optimal marginal flow defined as
p^{\star}_{t}=\varphi_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}
.

Remark 5.9

(Generalization of Dynamic SB)
.

When the interaction term vanishes for all
(\bm{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]
, i.e.,
\mathcal{I}\equiv 0
, and the reference drift is independent of the marginal, i.e.,
\bm{f}(\bm{x},p_{t},t)=\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
, the
generalized SB problem
reduces to (
Dynamic SB Problem
) and can be interpreted as a generalization of dynamic SB to McKean-Vlasov settings with mean-field interactions.

In summary, the generalized Schrödinger bridge extends the classical dynamic SB formulation by introducing an interaction term
\mathcal{I}(\bm{x},p_{t})
, that allows the dynamics to depend on the evolving marginal distribution
p_{t}
. Crucially, we show that the extension of the dynamic SB problem to settings with mean-field interactions
preserves the fundamental structure of the dynamic SB problem
with the only difference being an additive interaction term. Therefore, many frameworks used to solve the dynamic SB problem can be adapted to solve the generalized SB problem.

5.3
Multi-Marginal Schrödinger Bridge Problem

The standard dynamic SB problem aims to determine the optimal bridge that maps particles from the initial distribution
\pi_{0}
to the terminal distribution
\pi_{T}
while minimizing the KL divergence to the reference measure. A natural extension of this problem is to consider
multiple marginal constraints
at multiple points along the time horizon, which can be applied to construct feasible trajectories between observed snapshots over coarse time intervals. This variation of the SB problem is considered the
Multi-Marginal Schrödinger Bridge Problem

(
chen2019multi
;
chen2023deep
;
theodoropoulos2025momentum
)
.

Figure 11:
Multi-Marginal Schrödinger Bridge Problem.
The multi-marginal Schrödinger bridge constructs an optimal stochastic process whose marginal distributions match a sequence of prescribed intermediate densities
\{\pi_{t_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{K}
. Starting from a reference stochastic process
\mathbb{Q}
, the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is obtained by minimizing the path-space KL divergence while matching the marginal constraints.

Definition 5.10

(Standard Multi-Marginal Schrödinger Bridge Problem)
.

Given a uncontrolled reference measure
\mathbb{Q}
multiple marginal constraints
\{\pi_{t_{k}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}_{k=1}^{K}
at sequential time points
0=t_{0}<\dots<t_{k}<\dots<t_{K}=T
, the standard
multi-marginal Schrödinger bridge problem
aims to determine a control
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
that minimizes:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{u}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt\right]

(Multi-Marginal SB Problem)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
\bm{X}_{t_{k}}\sim\pi_{t_{k}},\forall k\in\{0,\dots,K\}\end{cases}

(5.85)

where the optimal
\bm{u}^{\star}
generates the path measure
\mathbb{P^{\star}}
of minimal relative entropy with respect
\mathbb{Q}
among all controlled measures matching the prescribed marginals. This can equivalently be written as the density-space objective:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{u}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt\right]

(5.86)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot\big(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t))\big)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}\\
p_{t_{k}}=\pi_{t_{k}},\forall k\in\{0,\dots,K\}\end{cases}

(5.87)

Since intermediate marginal state distributions can have associated velocity information that determines how it propagates to the subsequent distribution such that
\pi_{t_{k}}(\bm{x},\bm{v})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, we re-formulate the multi-marginal SB problem in
phase space
, known as the
Momentum Multi-Marginal Schrödinger Bridge
problem
(
theodoropoulos2025momentum
;
chen2023deep
)
, where the state is given by a vector
(\bm{x},\bm{v})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. In this setting, the reference dynamics that follow a pair of second-order SDEs:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\bm{x}_{t}=\bm{v}_{t}dt\\
d\bm{v}_{t}=\bm{a}_{t}dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\end{cases}

(5.88)

where stochasticity is introduced into the second-order SDE. We define the marginal distribution generated by the SDEs in (
5.88
) over the phase space as
p_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, the distribution of the state as
\mu(\bm{x},t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})d\bm{v}
, and the distribution over velocity as
\xi(\bm{v},t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})d\bm{x}
.

Definition 5.11

(Momentum Multi-Marginal Schrödinger Bridge Problem)
.

Given multiple marginal distributions over the phase space
\pi_{t_{0}},\dots,\pi_{t_{K}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
defined at sequential time points
t_{0},\dots,t_{K}\in[0,T]
on the time horizon, the
multi-marginal SB problem
aims to determine the optimal acceleration
\bm{a}_{t}^{\star}\equiv\bm{a}^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
that solves the SOC problem:

\displaystyle\bm{a}^{\star}_{t}=\underset{\bm{a}_{t}}{\arg\min}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{a}_{t}\|^{2}dt\right]\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}d\bm{x}_{t}=\bm{v}_{t}dt\\
d\bm{v}_{t}=\bm{a}_{t}dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
(\bm{x}_{t_{k}},\bm{v}_{t_{k}})\sim\pi_{t_{k}},\forall k\in\{0,\dots,K\}\end{cases}

(5.89)

Equivalently, writing the problem in terms of the distribution
p_{t}
and the Fokker-Planck equation from Section
2.4
, we have:

\displaystyle\bm{a}^{\star}_{t}

\displaystyle=\underset{\bm{a}_{t}}{\arg\min}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{a}_{t}\|^{2}p_{t}d\bm{x}d\bm{v}dt\right]

(5.90)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})+\bm{v}\cdot\nabla p_{t}+\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot(\bm{a}_{t}p_{t})-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta_{\bm{v}}p_{t}=0\\
\mu(\bm{x},t_{k})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pi_{t_{k}}(\bm{x},\bm{v})d\bm{v},\forall k\in\{0,\dots,K\}\end{cases}

(5.91)

The diffusion term in the FP equation makes the dynamics
irreversible
since diffusion increases entropy over a single time direction. To absorb the diffusion term into a deterministic drift, we can expand the velocity term
\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta_{\bm{v}}p_{t}
as:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bm{v}}p_{t}=\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot(\nabla_{\bm{v}}p_{t})=\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot(p_{t}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t})

(5.92)

Substituting this into the FP equation in (
5.91
), we get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})+\bm{v}\cdot\nabla p_{t}+\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot\left(\bm{a}_{t}p_{t}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}}\right)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})+\bm{v}\cdot\nabla p_{t}+\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\left(\bm{a}_{t}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}\right)}_{=:\hat{\bm{a}}_{t}}}p_{t}

\displaystyle=0

(5.93)

Then, we can define
\hat{\bm{a}}_{t}:=\bm{a}_{t}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}
to get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})+\bm{v}\cdot\nabla p_{t}+\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot(\hat{\bm{a}}_{t}p_{t})=0

(5.94)

To write the cost functional in terms of
\hat{\bm{a}}
, we substitute
\bm{a}_{t}=\hat{\bm{a}}_{t}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}
in the squared cost to get:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{a}\|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left\|\hat{\bm{a}}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}\right\|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{a}}\|^{2}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{8}\|\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\hat{\bm{a}},\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}\rangle

(5.95)

and integrating the expanded form over time and phase space, we have:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{a}}\|^{2}p_{t}d\bm{x}d\bm{v}dt}_{\text{transport cost}}+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{8}\|\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}\|^{2}p_{t}d\bm{x}d\bm{v}dt}_{\text{velocity Fisher information}}+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{1}{2}\langle\hat{\bm{a}},\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}\rangle p_{t}d\bm{x}d\bm{v}dt}_{\text{cross term}}

(5.96)

where the first term is the transport cost. The second term is the velocity
Fisher information
(squared norm of the score function), which measures the sensitivity of the distribution under infinitesimal changes to the velocity. Since the diffusion only acts on the velocity coordinate in (
5.88
), this term acts as an uncertainty regularization after absorbing the diffusion into the deterministic drift. Finally, the cross term can be expanded as follows:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{1}{2}\langle\hat{\bm{a}},\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}\rangle p_{t}d\bm{x}d\bm{v}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{1}{2}\hat{\bm{a}}\cdot(p_{t}\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t})d\bm{x}d\bm{v}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{1}{2}\hat{\bm{a}}\cdot\nabla_{\bm{v}}p_{t}d\bm{x}d\bm{v}dt

(5.97)

We now observe that this cross term is the alignment of the transport velocity
\hat{\bm{a}}
with the density gradient
\nabla_{\bm{v}}p_{t}
in velocity space. Using the identity
\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot(p_{t}\hat{\bm{a}})=\hat{\bm{a}}\cdot\nabla_{\bm{v}}p_{t}+p_{t}(\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot\hat{\bm{a}})
and applying integration by parts in velocity space under vanishing boundary conditions
33
33

33

Assumes that
p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})\to 0
as
\|\bm{v}\|\to\infty
is satisfied.
, we get:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\bm{a}}\cdot\nabla_{\bm{v}}p_{t})d\bm{v}d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot(p_{t}\hat{\bm{a}}))d\bm{v}d\bm{x}dt}\underbrace{-\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{1}{2}p_{t}(\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot\hat{\bm{a}})d\bm{v}d\bm{x}dt}_{=0\text{ (divergence integrates to 0)}}

(5.98)

where the divergence
\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot\hat{\bm{a}}
integrated over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is 0 since probability mass is conserved, and since there are no boundaries on velocity, the flux must be 0. Now, substituting the continuity equation from (
5.94
), we get:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot(p_{t}\hat{\bm{a}}))d\bm{v}d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})d\bm{v}d\bm{x}dt-\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{1}{2}\bm{v}\cdot\nabla p_{t}d\bm{v}d\bm{x}dt}_{=0}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}p_{t}\log p_{t}d\bm{v}d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\int p_{t}\log p_{t}d\bm{v}d\bm{x}dt

(5.99)

Defining
F(t):=\int p_{t}\log p_{t}d\bm{v}d\bm{x}
gives
\partial_{t}F(t)=\partial_{t}\int p_{t}\log p_{t}d\bm{v}d\bm{x}
, so we can apply the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus which states
\int_{0}^{T}F^{\prime}(t)dt=F(T)-F(0)
to get:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int\frac{1}{2}\langle\hat{\bm{a}},\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}\rangle p_{t}d\bm{x}d\bm{v}dt

\displaystyle=\int\frac{1}{2}\left(p_{T}\log p_{T}-p_{0}\log p_{0}\right)d\bm{v}d\bm{x}

(5.100)

Finally, substituting this back into (
5.96
), we get the new form of the cost function with respect to
\hat{\bm{a}}_{t}
:

\displaystyle\underset{\bm{a}_{t}}{\arg\min}\int_{0}^{T}\int\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{a}_{t}\|^{2}p_{t}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{8}\|\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}\|^{2}p_{t}\right]d\bm{x}d\bm{v}dt+\underbrace{\int\frac{1}{2}\left(p_{T}\log p_{T}-p_{0}\log p_{0}\right)d\bm{x}d\bm{v}}_{\text{constant}}

(5.101)

In the SBM setting, the phase space marginals are constrained to
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
, so we can drop the second term of the minimization, and write the multi-marginal SB problem as:

\displaystyle\bm{a}^{\star}_{t}

\displaystyle=\underset{\bm{a}_{t}}{\arg\min}\int_{0}^{T}\int\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{a}_{t}\|^{2}p_{t}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{8}\|\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}\|^{2}p_{t}\right]d\bm{x}d\bm{v}dt

(5.102)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})+\bm{v}\cdot\nabla p_{t}+\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot(\hat{\bm{a}}_{t}p_{t})=0\\
\mu(\bm{x},t_{k})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\pi_{t_{k}}(\bm{x},\bm{v})d\bm{v},\quad\forall k\in\{0,\dots,K\}\end{cases}

(5.103)

This derivation shows that the multi-marginal Schrödinger bridge problem in phase space can be reformulated as an entropy-regularized dynamic optimal transport problem over joint position–velocity distributions. The quadratic acceleration cost
\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{a}_{t}\|^{2}
governs how trajectories bend in phase space, penalizing deviations from inertial motion and enforcing smooth transitions that interpolate through the prescribed intermediate marginals
\{\pi_{t_{k}}\}_{k=1}^{K}
. In addition, the Fisher information term
\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{8}\|\nabla_{\bm{v}}\log p_{t}\|^{2}
acts specifically in the velocity coordinate, regularizing the uncertainty and dispersion of velocities induced by stochasticity.

Just like how the static and dynamic Schrödinger bridge problems are an entropically regularized analogue of static and dynamic optimal transport, the multi-marginal SB problem can be interpreted as the stochastic, entropically regularized analogue of
measure-valued splines
. This perspective will help us understand the behavior of multi-marginal SB in the zero-noise limit.

Connection to Measure-Valued Splines

The
variational spline problem
aims to select the smoothest curve
(\bm{x}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
that interpolates between a set of sequential points
\bar{\bm{x}}_{t_{0}},\dots,\bar{\bm{x}}_{t_{K}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
at times
0=t_{0}<\dots<t_{k}<\dots<t_{K}=T
by minimizing:

\displaystyle\min_{(\bm{x}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\ddot{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}dt\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{x}_{t_{k}}=\bar{\bm{x}}_{t_{k}},\forall k\in\{0,\dots,K\}

(5.104)

where the minimization is taken over all twice-differentiable curves satisfying the interpolation constraints. The objective penalizes squared acceleration
\|\ddot{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}
, so the resulting trajectory is the smoothest curve connecting the prescribed points in the sense of minimizing the
total bending energy
.

This principle can be generalized from deterministic trajectories to evolving probability distributions by replacing the single curve
\bm{x}(t)
with a time-dependent probability density
p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})
defined over the phase space of particle positions and velocities
(\bm{x},\bm{v})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. Then, the distributional analogue of the variational spline problem, known as the
measure-valued spline problem
, becomes:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{a},p_{t}}

\displaystyle\left\{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\bm{a}(\bm{x},\bm{v},t)\|^{2}p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})d\bm{x}d\bm{v}dt\right\}

(Measure-Valued Spline Problem)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}d\bm{x}_{t}=\bm{v}dt,\hskip 9.24994ptd\bm{v}_{t}=\bm{a}_{t}dt\\
\partial_{t}p_{t}+\langle\bm{v},\nabla p_{t}\rangle+\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot(\bm{a}p_{t})=0\\
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})d\bm{v}=\pi_{t_{k}},\forall k\in\{0,\dots,K\}\end{cases}

where
\partial_{t}p_{t}+\langle\bm{v},\nabla p_{t}\rangle+\nabla_{\bm{v}}\cdot(\bm{a}p_{t})=0
is the phase-space continuity equation and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{v})d\bm{v}=\pi_{t_{k}}
enforces the position marginals. Intuitively, this problem yields the evolution of a probability distribution that passes through prescribed marginal distributions while minimizing the average squared acceleration of the particles.

Notice that this problem is exactly the (
Multi-Marginal SB Problem
) except with zero diffusion
\sigma_{t}\equiv 0
, which reduces the Fokker-Planck equation with the Laplacian term
\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta_{\bm{v}}p_{t}\equiv 0
into the classic phase-space continuity equation. To this end, the multi-marginal SB problem can be interpreted as a stochastic, entropy-regularized analogue of the measure-valued spline.

This formulation extends the dynamic Schrödinger bridge problem along two key directions: incorporating multiple intermediate marginal constraints and lifting the dynamics to phase space, where marginals can encode both position and velocity information. In the zero-noise limit, the problem recovers a deterministic measure-valued spline problem, revealing how SB generalizes smooth trajectory interpolation between distributions with stochastic uncertainty.

5.4
Unbalanced Schrödinger Bridge Problem

Since the Schrödinger bridge problems originate from the optimal mass transport (OMT) problem, where the probability mass across the full time horizon is conserved, and no mass is lost. In many applications, such as cell dynamics, probability mass is not necessarily conserved, and particles undergo growth and death, producing
unbalanced marginal distributions
. However, this presents the
key problem
of determining the way in which particles should transport and vanish along intermediate time points that minimizes the deviation from some reference dynamics while reconstructing the unbalanced marginals.

To account for the difference in mass between terminal marginals, we can relax the mass conservation constraint in the standard regularized OT problem by introducing a growth rate
g(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
into the minimization objective to get the
dynamic unbalanced optimal transport
problem
(
chizat2018interpolating
;
chizat2018unbalanced
)
.

Definition 5.12

(Dynamic Unbalanced Optimal Transport Problem)
.

Let
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be non-negative measures that may have different total mass. The
dynamic unbalanced optimal transport problem
seeks a time-dependent density
p_{t}
, transport velocity
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and growth rate
g(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
that solve the minimization:

\displaystyle\inf_{p_{t},\bm{v},g}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\{\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+\alpha\Psi(g(\bm{x},t))\right\}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

(5.105)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{x})\bm{v}(\bm{x},t))+g(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})\\
p_{0}=\pi_{0},\quad p_{T}=\pi_{T}\end{cases}

(5.106)

where
\Psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a non-negative function that penalizes changes in mass and
\alpha>0
is a hyperparameter that controls the penalty.

Notice that the
unbalanced continuity equation
constraint in the dynamic unbalanced OT problem
\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}\bm{v})+gp_{t}
contains an additional
gp_{t}
term compared to the standard continuity equation. This term models local mass creation and destruction, where the current density
p_{t}(\bm{x})
at
\bm{x}
grows or decays proportionally to
g(\bm{x},t)
. Therefore,
gp_{t}
accounts for the instantaneous change in mass density due to growth or decay, allowing the total mass to vary over time.

The growth penalty
\Psi(g)
, commonly defined as the quadratic growth
\Psi(g(\bm{x},t)):=|g(\bm{x},t)|^{2}
, regularizes the amount of mass creation or destruction along the transport path. By penalizing large growth rates, the optimization balances mass transport and mass variation, yielding the
minimal-cost combination of transport and local mass change
required to match the terminal marginals.

Figure 12:
Unbalanced Schrödinger Bridge Problem.
The unbalanced SB problem extends the classical formulation to settings where the endpoint marginals
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
have different total mass. In addition to the control
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
that transports probability, the dynamics contain a growth term
g(\bm{x},t)
which allows mass to be created or removed along trajectories, which contributes a growth penalty
\Psi(g)
in the minimization objective. The dynamics of the density evolve according to the unbalanced Fokker–Planck equation, which augments the standard continuity equation with the mass-change term
gp_{t}
.

Extending this formulation to SDEs of the form
d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
yields the
unbalanced Schrödinger bridge
problem, which seeks the
most likely stochastic process
between two unbalanced marginals
(
zhang2024learning
;
chen2022most
;
pariset2023unbalanced
)
.

Definition 5.13

(Unbalanced Schrödinger Bridge Problem)
.

Given a pair of unbalanced marginals
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, a reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
with drift
\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)
and a function that penalizes the growth rate
\Psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, the
unbalanced Schrödinger bridge (SB) problem
aims to determine the optimal tuple density evolution
p^{\star}_{t}
, control drift
\bm{u}^{\star}
, and growth rate
g^{\star}
that solve the minimization problem:

\displaystyle\inf_{p_{t},\bm{u},g}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+\alpha\Psi(g(\bm{x},t))\right]p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

(Unbalanced SB Problem)

subject to the marginal constraints
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
and an
unbalanced Fokker-Planck constraint
defined as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)))+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})+g(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})

(Unbalanced FP Equation)

where
g(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})
relaxes the mass conservation constraint of the standard Fokker-Planck equation to allow the mass to change proportionally to the growth rate.

Since the Schrödinger bridge formulation has three optimization parameters, the density
p_{t}
, the control drift
\bm{u}
,
and the growth rate

g
which are all coupled in the (
Unbalanced FP Equation
), optimizing a stochastic flow yields a difficult optimization problem. By reparameterizing the unbalanced Fokker-Planck constraint into a
unbalanced continuity equation
constraint, we can derive an alternative
entropy-regularized dynamic optimal transport form
of the (
Unbalanced SB Problem
), which was introduced to model stochastic unbalanced dynamical systems
(
baradat2021regularized
;
buze2023entropic
;
chen2022most
;
janati2020entropic
)
, and has recently been used to develop scalable computational methods for simulating high-dimensional real-world data
(
zhang2024learning
)
.

Proposition 5.14

(Entropy-Regularized Unbalanced Dynamic Optimal Transport Problem)
.

The (
Unbalanced SB Problem
) from Definition
5.13
can be written as:

\displaystyle\inf_{(p_{t},\bm{v},g)}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}\|^{2}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{8}\|\nabla\log p_{t}\|^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\nabla\log p_{t},\bm{f}\big\rangle-\frac{1}{2}(1+\log p_{t})g}+\alpha\Psi(g)\bigg]p_{t}d\bm{x}dt

(Regularized Unbalanced OT )

subject to the same marginal constraints
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
and the
unbalanced continuity equation constraint
defined as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{v}(\bm{x},t))+g(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})

(5.107)

where
g(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})
relaxes the mass conservation constraint of the standard (
Continuity Equation
)
34
34

34

Note that the regularized unbalanced optimal transport objective introduced in
(
zhang2024learning
)
is slightly different than the one stated here as we parameterize the control drift rather than the full velocity field, which results in an additional term containing the reference drift
\bm{f}
and different coefficient scaling.
.

Proof.
Starting with the (
Unbalanced FP Equation
), we follow similar steps as in (
Continuity Equation
) to derive a reparameterized form of the control drift that satisfies a
continuity equation constraint
with the additional growth term
g(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})
as follows:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\big(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)})\big)+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{x})}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}g(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\bigg(p_{t}(\bm{x})\bigg(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\left(\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)-\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\right)}_{=:\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)}}\bigg)\bigg)+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}g(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{x})(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{v}(\bm{x},t))+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}g(\bm{x},t)p_{t}(\bm{x})}

(Unbalanced Continuity Equation)

which is the unbalanced form of the Fokker-Planck constraint in (
5.14
). Defining
\bm{v}(\bm{x},t):=\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)-\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})
and rearranging to get the change-in-variables
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)=\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)+\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})
, we write the objective from Definition
5.13
as:

\displaystyle\inf_{(p_{t},\bm{v},g)}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}+\alpha\Psi(g(\bm{x},t))\right]p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\inf_{(p_{t},\bm{v},g)}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\bigg\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\bigg\|^{2}+\alpha\Psi(g(\bm{x},t))\right]p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\inf_{(p_{t},\bm{v},g)}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|+\sigma_{t}\langle\bm{v}(\bm{x},t),\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{4}\|\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}\right)+\alpha\Psi(g(\bm{x},t))\right]p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\inf_{(p_{t},\bm{v},g)}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg\{\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}}_{\text{kinetic energy}}+\underbrace{\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\left\langle\bm{v}(\bm{x},t),\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\right\rangle}_{\text{cross term}}+\underbrace{\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{8}\|\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}}_{\text{Fisher information}}+\underbrace{\alpha\Psi(g(\bm{x},t))}_{\text{growth penalty}}\bigg\}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}dt

(5.108)

which follows the same steps as the derivation in Section
2.9
. The expansion of the cross term deviates from the derivation in Section
2.9
as it incorporates the additional growth term in the continuity equation substitution:

\displaystyle H(p_{T})-H(p_{0})

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:ot-sb-proof2})}{=}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\log p_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}p_{t}}d\bm{x}dt

(5.109)

Substituting the
unbalanced continuity equation
constraint
\partial_{t}p_{t}=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{v}))+gp_{t}
, we have:

\displaystyle H(p_{T})-H(p_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\log p_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\big(-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{v})+gp_{t}\big)}d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\log p_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\big(-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{v})\big)}d\bm{x}dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\log p_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(gp_{t})}d\bm{x}dt

(5.110)

Then, applying the integration of parts identity on the divergence and following the steps in (
2.94
), we get:

\displaystyle H(p_{T})

\displaystyle-H(p_{0})=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg\langle\nabla\log p_{t},{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{v})}\bigg\rangle d\bm{x}dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\log p_{t})(gp_{t})d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big\langle\nabla\log p_{t},\bm{f}\big\rangle p_{t}d\bm{x}dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma_{t}\big\langle\nabla\log p_{t},\bm{v}\big\rangle p_{t}d\bm{x}dt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\log p_{t})(gp_{t})d\bm{x}dt

(5.111)

Dividing both sides by two and rearranging to isolate the cross term in (
5.108
), we have:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\sigma_{t}}{2}\big\langle\nabla\log p_{t},\bm{v}\big\rangle

\displaystyle p_{t}d\bm{x}dt=\frac{1}{2}(H(p_{T})-H(p_{0}))+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}\bigg[-\big\langle\nabla\log p_{t},\bm{f}\big\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}}-(1+\log p_{t})(g{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}})\bigg]d\bm{x}dt

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}(H(p_{T})-H(p_{0}))}_{\text{constant}}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg[-\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\nabla\log p_{t},\bm{f}\big\rangle-\frac{1}{2}(1+\log p_{t})g\bigg]{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}}d\bm{x}dt

(5.112)

Since the entropy difference is fixed given the marginal constraints
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
, the entropy difference term can be dropped, and we get:

\displaystyle\boxed{\inf_{(p_{t},\bm{u},g)}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}\|^{2}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{8}\|\nabla\log p_{t}\|^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\nabla\log p_{t},\bm{f}\big\rangle-\frac{1}{2}(1+\log p_{t})g}+\alpha\Psi(g)\bigg]p_{t}d\bm{x}dt}

(5.113)

subject to the marginal constraints
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
,
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
, and the (
Unbalanced FP Equation
), which reformulates the unbalanced SB problem as a
entropy-regularized dynamic OT problem
.
\square

The reformulation removes the coupling between
\bm{v}
and
\nabla\log p_{t}
in the cross interaction term
\langle\bm{v}(\bm{x},t),\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})\rangle
, leading to a more numerically stable and computationally tractable objective
(
zhang2024learning
)
. We can also observe that since the terminal marginals are fixed, the entropy difference is constant, and the objective depends solely on the path-dependent cost. This results in a problem with three
key components
: the quadratic cost of the velocity field
\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}\|^{2}
, the Fisher information term that penalizes sharp changes in density, and the contribution of the growth rate determined by the growth penalty
\Psi(g)
and the entropy change caused by creating or destroying mass through
(1+\log p_{t})
.

5.5
Branched Schrödinger Bridge Problem

Orthogonal to the problem of matching multiple subsequent marginals along the temporal evolution of probability mass is the problem of matching a complex terminal marginal distribution
\pi_{T}
with
multiple modes

\pi_{T}=\pi_{T,1}\oplus\dots\oplus\pi_{T,K}
. In practice, fitting a standard Schrödinger bridge to accurately transport density from an initial distribution
\pi_{0}
to a multi-modal distribution
\pi_{T}
suffers from several challenges.

Figure 13:
Branched Schrödinger Bridge Problem.
The branched Schrödinger bridge generalizes the classical formulation to transport an initial distribution
\pi_{0}
toward multiple target marginals
\{\pi_{T,k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
. A primary trajectory evolves from the source distribution and probabilistically branches into secondary branched paths, each governed by its own control and growth dynamics to reach a different terminal distribution. The objective minimizes the weighted kinetic energy of the controls across all branches, producing a stochastic branching flow that connects a single source to multiple target modes.

One of these challenges is
mode collapse
, in which the probability density is concentrated in only one or a few terminal modes, thereby missing the full distribution. To overcome this, one may increase the number of particles simulated from the initial distribution to improve the likelihood of discovering each terminal mode; however, this increases computational cost and does not guarantee accurate reconstruction of all modes and
their relative density weights
. Furthermore, Schrödinger bridges not only sample from the terminal distribution but also recover the energy-minimizing temporal bridge between the distributions. For multimodal target distributions, this optimal bridge can inform us about
branching times
and
mass redistribution
; however, it remains challenging to simulate effectively with standard SB methods. This is the motivation behind the
Branched Schrödinger Bridge Problem

(
tang2026branchsbm
)
, which enables simulation of branching and mass redistribution over a stochastic bridge to reconstruct multi-modal target distributions.

Since branching can be interpreted as the unbalanced flow of probability mass from a primary branch to multiple diverging trajectories, it can be formalized as the
sum
of Unbalanced Schrödinger bridges, where mass is progressively depleted from the primary branch and fed into the diverging paths to multiple terminal modes.

Definition 5.15

(Branched Schrödinger Bridge Problem)
.

Consider an initial distribution
\pi_{0}
and a terminal distribution with
K
distinct modes
\pi_{T}:=\pi_{T,1}\oplus\dots\oplus\pi_{T,K}
on some potential energy landscape defined by a state cost
c(\bm{x},t)
. Denoting the control drift of each branch as
\{\bm{u}_{k}(\bm{x},t)\}_{k=0}^{K}
, the growth rate of each branch as
\{g_{k}(\bm{x},t)\}_{k=0}^{K}
, and the accumulated weight of each branch as
\{w_{t,k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
, the Branched SB problem seeks the optimal set of control and growth rates
\{\bm{u}^{\star}_{k},g_{k}^{\star}\}_{k=0}^{K}
that solve the following minimization:

\displaystyle\inf_{\{\bm{u}_{k},g_{k}\}_{k=0}^{K}}\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{p_{t,0}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}_{0}(\bm{X}_{t,0},t)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}_{t,0},t)\right]w_{t,0}+

(5.114)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t,k}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}_{k}(\bm{X}_{t,k},t)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}_{t,k},t)\right]w_{t,k}\bigg\}dt

(Branched SB Problem)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\text{s.t.}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t,k}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t,k},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}_{k}(\bm{X}_{t,k},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0},\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{X}_{T,k}\sim\pi_{T,k}\\
w_{0,k}=\delta_{k=0},\hskip 9.24994ptw_{T,k}=w^{\star}_{T,k}\end{cases}

where the weight of the primary branch is given by
w_{t,0}=1+\int_{0}^{t}g_{0}(\bm{X}_{s,0},s)ds
and the weights of the
K
secondary branches is given by
w_{t,k}=\int_{0}^{t}g_{k}(\bm{X}_{s,k},s)ds
.

When considering a setting where the total mass across all branches is conserved, we can add the constraint
\sum_{k=0}^{K}w_{t,k}=1
for all
t\in[0,T]
which enforces that the the growth rates sum to zero, i.e.,
g_{0}(\bm{X}_{t,0},t)+\sum_{k=1}^{K}g_{k}(\bm{X}_{t,k},t)=0
. In this setting,
all
the mass that is lost from the primary branch, such that growth rate is negative
g_{0}<0
, is redistributed to the secondary branches, such that at least one branch has positive growth
\exists k,\;g_{k}>0
. To formulate a tractable form of the (
Branched SB Problem
), we can reframe the problem as a
conditional stochastic optimal control
(CondSOC) problem that can be tractably solved given a finite set of samples from an empirical initial distribution
\pi_{0}
and a multi-modal terminal distribution
\pi_{T}
.

Proposition 5.16

(Branched Conditional Stochastic Optimal Control (Proposition 2 in
tang2026branchsbm
))
.

Define the endpoint conditioned density of each branch as
p_{t,k}(\bm{X}_{t,k}):=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0,T,k}}[p_{t,k}(\bm{X}_{t,k}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T,k})]
, where
\pi_{0,T,k}
is the joint coupling between the initial distribution
\pi_{0}
and the
k
th mode of the terminal distribution
\pi_{T,k}
. The set of optimal control drifts and growth terms
\{\bm{u}_{k},g_{k}\}_{k=0}^{K}
that solve the Branched SB problem defined in Definition
5.15
can be obtained by minimizing the sum of Unbalanced Conditional Stochastic Optimal Control problems defined as:

\displaystyle\inf_{\{\bm{u}_{k},g_{k}\}_{k=0}^{K}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T,0})\sim\pi_{0,T,0}}\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{p_{t|0,T,0}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}_{0}(\bm{X}_{t,0},t)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}_{t,0},t)\right]w_{t,0}\bigg\}dt

(5.115)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T,k})\sim\pi_{0,T,k}}\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{p_{t|0,T,k}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}_{k}(\bm{X}_{t,k},t)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}_{t,k},t)\right]w_{t,k}\bigg\}dt

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t,k}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t,k},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}_{k}(\bm{X}_{t,k},t))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0},\quad\bm{X}_{T,k}=\bm{x}_{T,k}\\
w_{0,k}=\delta_{k=0},\quad w_{T,k}=w^{\star}_{T,k}\end{cases}

where the weight of the primary branch is given by
w_{t,0}=1+\int_{0}^{t}g_{0}(\bm{X}_{s,0},s)ds
and the weights of the
K
secondary branches is given by
w_{t,k}=\int_{0}^{t}g_{k}(\bm{X}_{s,k},s)ds
.

Proof.
To prove that the branched CondSOC problem solves the branched SB problem in Definition
5.15
, we start by defining each branch
k
as solving its own Unbalanced SB problem, defined as:

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{u}_{k},g_{k}}\int_{0}^{T}\bigg\{\mathbb{E}_{p_{t,k}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}_{k}(\bm{X}_{t,k})\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}_{t,k},t)\right]\left(w_{0,k}+\int_{0}^{t}g_{s,k}(\bm{X}_{s,k})ds\right)\bigg\}dt

(5.116)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\begin{cases}\partial_{t}p_{t,k}=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t,k}(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}_{k}))+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t,k}+g_{k}p_{t,k}\\
p_{0}=\pi_{0},\quad p_{T,k}=\pi_{T,k}\end{cases}

(5.117)

Now, it suffices to show that the sum of unbalanced CondSOC problems satisfies the
global Fokker-Planck equation
of the density over all branches, defined as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})=-\nabla\cdot(p_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}_{t})(\bm{X}_{t},t))+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})

(5.118)

where the probability density
p_{t}
is defined as the weighted sum of the density at each branch at time
t
given by
p_{t}(\bm{X}_{t}):=\sum_{k=0}^{K}w_{t,k}p_{t,k}(\bm{X}_{t})
. To derive the Fokker-Planck equation of
p_{t}
, we start by differentiating with respect to
t
and applying the chain rule to get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\left[\sum_{k=0}^{K}w_{t,k}p_{t,k}\right]=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\bigg[w_{t,k}\underbrace{\left(\partial_{t}p_{t,k}\right)}_{\text{branched FP}}+\underbrace{\left(\partial_{t}w_{t,k}\right)}_{:=g_{k}(\bm{X}_{t})}p_{t,k}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\bigg[w_{t,k}\left(-\nabla\cdot((\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}_{k})p_{t,k})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t,k}\right)+g_{k}p_{t,k}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\bigg[-w_{t,k}\nabla\cdot((\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}_{k})p_{t,k})+w_{t,k}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t,k}+g_{k}p_{t,k}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{k=0}^{K}(-w_{t,k}\nabla\cdot((\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}_{k})p_{t,k}))}_{\text{divergence}}+\underbrace{\sum_{k=0}^{K}w_{t,k}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t,k}}_{\text{diffusion}}+\underbrace{\sum_{k=0}^{K}g_{k}p_{t,k}}_{\text{growth}}

(5.119)

First, we rewrite the divergence term into the form of the full Fokker-Planck equation (
5.118
) by applying the linearity of the divergence operator:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K}\big(-w_{t,k}\nabla\cdot((\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}_{k})p_{t,k})\big)

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\left(\sum_{k=0}^{K}w_{t,k}p_{t,k}(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\bigg(\bm{f}\sum_{k=0}^{K}w_{t,k}p_{t,k}+\sigma_{t}\sum_{k=0}^{K}w_{t,k}\bm{u}_{k}p_{t,k}\bigg)

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\bigg(\bm{f}\underbrace{\frac{1}{p_{t}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}w_{t,k}p_{t,k}}_{=1}+\sigma_{t}\underbrace{\frac{1}{p_{t}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}w_{t,k}\bm{u}_{k}p_{t,k}}_{:=\bm{u}}\bigg)p_{t}

\displaystyle=\boxed{-\nabla\cdot\left((\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{u})p_{t}\right)}

(5.120)

where the final equality follows from multiplying and dividing by
p_{t}
. By defining
\bm{u}:=\frac{1}{p_{t}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}w_{t,k}\bm{u}_{k}p_{t,k}
, we have that the total control drift is the mass-weighted average of the control drift for each branch. This is theoretically grounded, as under the global context, the control drift
\bm{u}_{k}(\bm{X}_{t},t)
of a particle
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
along a single branch
k
should be scaled by its probability of being in branch
k
, given by
p_{t,k}(\bm{X}_{t})
, the weight of the particle itself
w_{t,k}(\bm{X}_{t})
, normalized by the total probability of the particle over all branches
p_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
.

Next, we rewrite the diffusion term as the diffusion of the full branched Fokker-Planck equation by applying the linearity of the Laplacian operator:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K}w_{t,k}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t,k}=\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\underbrace{\left(\sum_{k=0}^{K}w_{t,k}p_{t,k}\right)}_{:=p_{t}}=\boxed{\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}}

(5.121)

Finally, for the growth term in (
5.119
), we observe that it is simply the weighted sum of the growth over each branch
\sum_{k=0}^{K}g_{k}p_{t,k}
and doesn’t alter the direction or motion of the particle along the brnached fields in the global context. Therefore, all three terms in (
5.119
) satisfy the global Fokker-Planck equation, and we finish the proof.
\square

This derivation provides insight into how branched Schrödinger bridges behave under the global dynamics. We observe that even when each branch evolves via its own control and growth field, the overall system evolves as a single stochastic process whose probability density is a weighted superposition of all branches. From the perspective of the global Fokker-Planck constraint, branching does not introduce additional forces or discontinuities in the particle motion. Instead, it induces a mixture of control drifts, where the effective drift at any state is the probability-weighted average of the branch-specific controls.

This solves the challenge of mode collapse because each mode is generated with its own control drift, constrained by a terminal weight to ensure that the correct probability mass and distribution are reconstructed. Furthermore, the potential energy function
c(\bm{x},t)
that governs the system dynamics are minimized at the
optimal branched mass redistribution
, such that the optimal control and growth fields
\{\bm{u}^{\star}_{k},g_{k}\}_{k=0}^{K}
that solve the Branched SB problem in Definition
5.15
yields the branching trajectories and their relative weights such that they minimize the total energy required to reconstruct the terminal distribution.

5.6
Fractional Schrödinger Bridge Problem

Up to this point, we have considered only stochastic differential equations (SDEs) with standard Brownian motion (BM) processes that are Markov, where each increment of the SDE is independent of all previous steps. This formulation is a specific design choice that ensures the tractability of the bridge solution and corresponding drift. While this choice is often a sufficient approximation, it ignores the effects of long-range temporal dependencies inherent in complex real-world systems.

Fractional Brownian motion
(fBM) is a generalization of standard BM to non-memoryless processes, where each increment is
dependent
on previous increments. This dependence is characterized by the
Hurst index
(
H
), which determines the
roughness
or
pathwise regularity
of the dynamics and the
magnitude of long-range dependencies
. A Hurst index of
H=0.5
recovers the standard BM.

Definition 5.17

(Fractional Brownian Motion
(
levy1963random
)
)
.

Given standard
d
-dimensional Brownian motion (BM)
(\bm{B}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d})_{t\in[0,T]}
,
fractional BM (fBM)
is a centered Gaussian process defined by integrating over the history of the BM:

\displaystyle\bm{B}_{t}^{H}:=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(H+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}d\bm{B}_{s},\quad t\geq 0

(5.122)

where
\Gamma
is the Gamma function and
H\in(0,1)
is the Hurst index.

We observe that the kernel
(t-s)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}
injects time-dependent memory to the BM by weighting recent states (
s\approx t
) heavily and weighting distant states lightly. For
H>\frac{1}{2}
, the increments are
positively correlated
, resulting in smoother trajectories where each subsequent increment is more likely to be close to the previous increment. For
H<\frac{1}{2}
, the increments are
negatively correlated
, resulting in rougher trajectories where each subsequent increment aims to
undo itself
or revert back to the mean. When
H=\frac{1}{2}
, the kernel reduces to
(t-s)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}=1
which recovers the standard BM process.

Given the dependence on previous states, fBM is not Markov, resulting in an intractable drift for simulation. To overcome this, Markov approximations of fBM have been introduced
(
harms2019affine
;
daems2023variational
)
, which approximate fBM by a weighted sum of
Ornstein–Uhlenbeck
(OU) processes, which are a class of Markov BM processes with a restoring force pulling it back toward the mean.

Definition 5.18

(Markov Approximation of Fractional Brownian Motion
(
harms2019affine
;
daems2023variational
)
)
.

Given a
d
-dimensional fractional Brownian motion (fBM)
(\bm{B}_{t}^{H})_{t\in[0,T]}
, we define a
Markov-approximate fBM
(MA-fBM)
(\widehat{\bm{B}}_{t}^{H})_{t\in[0,T]}
as:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{B}}_{t}^{H}:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\bm{Y}_{t}^{k},\quad\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{K}\in\mathbb{R}

(5.123)

where
\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}
denote
K
Ornstein–Uhlenbeck (OU) processes of the form:

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}:=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\gamma_{k}(t-s)}d\bm{B}_{s},\quad d\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}=-\gamma_{k}\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}dt+d\bm{B}_{t},\quad k=1,\dots,K

(5.124)

where
\{\gamma_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
are the mean reversion coefficients that determine how strongly the dynamics are pulled back to the mean. To approximate the fBM dynamics,
\gamma_{k}:=r^{k-n}
for
r>1
and
n=\frac{K+1}{2}
, which produces a log-uniformly spread time grid of fast-reverting and slow-reverting OU processes.

Intuitively, each OU process contributes one exponential memory scale, where short memory modes are captured by strong mean-reversion OU processes (large
\gamma_{k}
) and long memory modes are captured by weak mean-reversion OU processes (small
\gamma_{k}
). To determine the
optimal finite set
of coefficients
\{\omega_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
for MA-fBM, one can directly minimize the
expected squared error
between the true fBM and the OU approximation over time:

\displaystyle(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{K})=\underset{\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{K}}{\arg\min}\left\{\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\left(\bm{B}_{t}^{H}-\widehat{\bm{B}}_{t}^{H}\right)^{2}\right]dt\right\}

(5.125)

which yields a
L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
optimal approximation
35
35

35

while this does not yield strong pathwise convergence like in
(
harms2019strong
)
, it is more computationally tractable
.

Since the fBM process is determined by the scaled fBM process, which we denote as
\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}:=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\widehat{\bm{B}}_{t}^{H}
, and the
K
OU processes
\bm{Y}_{t}:=(\bm{Y}_{t}^{1},\dots,\bm{Y}_{t}^{K})
that approximate
\widehat{\bm{B}}_{t}
, we define the full reference process
\mathbb{Q}
as
\bm{Z}:=(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\widehat{\bm{B}}_{t},\bm{Y}_{t})
, which follows the SDE:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}:\quad d\bm{Z}_{t}=\bm{F}\bm{Z}_{t}dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(5.126)

where
\bm{F}\in\mathbb{R}^{d(K+1)\times d(K+1)}
is a block matrix encoding the linear coupling between the state
\bm{X}_{t}
and the auxiliary OU processes
\bm{Y}_{t}:=(\bm{Y}_{t}^{1},\dots,\bm{Y}_{t}^{K})
and
\sigma_{t}
is the diffusion coefficient.

However, we observe that the process
\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}
alone is not Markov, as there is no way to predict the next state
\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t+h}
from
\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}
without knowledge of the OU processes, therefore only the full reference process
\bm{Z}_{t}:=(\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t},\bm{Y}_{t}^{1},\dots,\bm{Y}_{t}^{K})
is Markov. Using
\mathbb{Q}
as the reference Markov process, we can derive the form of the Markov fractional Brownian bridge, where we leverage Doob’s
h
-transfrom described in Section
4.4
to condition
\bm{Z}_{t}
on the endpoint marginals.

Proposition 5.19

(Markov Approximation of Fractional Brownian Bridge
(
nobis2025fractional
)
)
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}
be the reference path measure induced by the Markov augmentation
\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}
of a scaled fractional Brownian motion, with reference dynamics:

\displaystyle d\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}=\bm{F}\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(5.127)

Fixing endpoints
\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}
and
\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}
, then the corresponding bridge measure obtained by conditioning
\mathbb{Q}
on these endpoint constraints is defined by the Doob
h
-transform of
\mathbb{Q}
with
h(\bm{z},t):=\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}=\bm{z})
, and the Markov approximation of the fractional Brownian bridge satisfies the SDE:

\displaystyle d\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}=(\bm{F}\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla_{\bm{z}}\log\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}=\bm{z}))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(5.128)

Proof.
To prove this, we leverage Doob’s
h
-Transform described in Section
4.4
, which defines a endpoint conditioned function
h(\bm{z},t)
. Here, we define
h:\mathbb{R}^{d(K+1)}\times[0,T]\to[0,1]
as the endpoint probabilty of a state
\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}
under the augmented path measure
\mathbb{S}
, given by:

\displaystyle h(\bm{z},t):=\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{Z}_{t}=\bm{z})\equiv\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})

(5.129)

where
\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\cdot|\bm{z})
is the conditional density on
\bm{X}_{T}
given
\bm{z}
.

Now, we aim to show that
\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})
satisfies the properties of the
h
-function defined in Section
4.4
, which allows us to directly write the SDE associated with the fractional Brownian bridge. First, we check that it satisfies the (
Martingale Property
) that defines a valid tilting function:

\displaystyle h(\bm{z},t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t}(\bm{Z}_{t+\Delta t}=\tilde{\bm{z}}|\bm{Z}_{t}=\bm{z})h(\tilde{\bm{z}},t+\Delta t)d\tilde{\bm{z}}

(5.130)

To derive an expression for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t}(\tilde{\bm{z}}|\bm{z})}
, we use Bayes rule to decompose
\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t,T}(\tilde{\bm{z}}|\bm{z},\bm{x}_{T})
and apply the Markov property
\mathbb{S}_{T|t,t+\Delta t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z},\tilde{\bm{z}})=\mathbb{S}_{T|t+\Delta t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\tilde{\bm{z}})
to get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t,T}(\tilde{\bm{z}}|\bm{z},\bm{x}_{T})=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{S}_{T|t,t+\Delta t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z},\tilde{\bm{z}})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t}(\tilde{\bm{z}}|\bm{z})}}{\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{S}_{T|t+\Delta t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\tilde{\bm{z}})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t}(\tilde{\bm{z}}|\bm{z})}}{\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})}

\displaystyle\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t}(\tilde{\bm{z}}|\bm{z})}=\frac{\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t,T}(\tilde{\bm{z}}|\bm{z},\bm{x}_{T})\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})}{\mathbb{S}_{T|t+\Delta t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\tilde{\bm{z}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t,T}(\tilde{\bm{z}}|\bm{z},\bm{x}_{T}){\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}h(\bm{z},t)}}{{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}h(\tilde{\bm{z}},t+\Delta t)}}

(5.131)

where we substitute the definition of
h(\bm{z},t)
and
h(\tilde{\bm{z}},t+\Delta t)
in the final equality. Now, substituting (
5.131
) into (
5.130
), we get:

\displaystyle h(\bm{z},t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t}(\bm{Z}_{t+\Delta t}=\tilde{\bm{z}}|\bm{Z}_{t}=\bm{z})}h(\tilde{\bm{z}},t+\Delta t)d\tilde{\bm{z}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t,T}(\tilde{\bm{z}}|\bm{z},\bm{x}_{T})h(\bm{z},t)}{h(\tilde{\bm{z}},t+\Delta t)}}h(\tilde{\bm{z}},t+\Delta t)d\tilde{\bm{z}}

\displaystyle=h(\bm{z},t)\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t,T}(\tilde{\bm{z}}|\bm{z},\bm{x}_{T})d\tilde{\bm{z}}}_{=1}=\boxed{h(\bm{z},t)}

(5.132)

which means that
h(\bm{z},t):=\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})
is a valid tilting function and by Proposition
4.7
