and the reference SDE (
5.126
), the SDE of the fractional Brownian bridge can be written as:

\displaystyle d\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}=(\bm{F}\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla_{\bm{z}}\log\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}=\bm{z})})dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(5.133)

which concludes the proof of the fractional Brownian bridge.
\square
.

We can derive the explicit form of
\nabla_{\bm{z}}\log\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}=\bm{z})
, which turns out to be Gaussian, with the following Lemma.

Lemma 5.20

(Gaussian Form of Fractional Brownian Bridge)
.

The fractional Brownian bridge has a Gaussian transition density
\mathbb{S}_{t+\Delta t|t}(\cdot|\bm{z})
. Conditioned on the terminal state
\widehat{\bm{X}}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}
, the gradient of the log density takes the form:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{z}}\log\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})=\left[\nabla_{\bm{z}}\log\mathbb{S}^{1}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z}),\dots,\nabla_{\bm{z}}\log\mathbb{S}^{d}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})\right]

(5.134)

where for each
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, we have:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{z}}\log\mathbb{S}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})=[1,\omega_{1}\zeta_{1}(t,T),\dots,\omega_{K}\zeta_{K}(t,T)]^{\top}\frac{\bm{x}^{i}_{T}-\bm{\mu}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{z})}{\sigma^{2}_{T|t}}

(5.135)

where the conditional mean
\bm{\mu}_{T|t}
and covariance
\sigma^{2}_{T|t}
are given by:

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}_{T|t}(\bm{z})

\displaystyle=\bm{x}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\bm{y}_{k}\zeta_{k}(t,T),\hskip 9.24994pt\sigma_{T|t}^{2}(\bm{z})=\varepsilon\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}\frac{\omega_{k}\omega_{\ell}}{\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{\ell}}\left(1-e^{-(1-t)(\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{\ell})}\right)

(5.136)

where
\bm{z}=(\bm{y}_{1},\dots,\bm{y}_{K})
are the states of the OU processes.

Proof.
First, we recall the definition of the Markov-approximated fractional BM process given by the state
\bm{X}_{t}
and the OU random variables
(\bm{Y}_{t}^{1},\dots,\bm{Y}_{t}^{K})
defined as:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}:=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega\bm{Y}^{k}_{t},\quad d\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}=-\gamma_{k}\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}dt+d\bm{B}_{t}

(5.137)

which combines to give the expression for the time evolution
d\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}
as:

\displaystyle d\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}d\bm{Y}^{k}_{t}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(-\gamma_{k}\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}dt+d\bm{B}_{t})}={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\gamma_{k}\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}dt+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}d\bm{B}_{t}}

(5.138)

Now, we want to derive an expression for the next state
\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t+\Delta t}
by taking the integral
\int_{0}^{t+\Delta t}
to get:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t+\Delta t}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{t+\Delta t}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\gamma_{k}\bm{Y}_{r}^{k}dr+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}d\bm{B}_{r}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\gamma_{k}\int_{0}^{t+\Delta t}\bm{Y}_{r}^{k}dr+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\bm{B}_{t+\Delta t}

(5.139)

Splitting the integral into
\int_{0}^{t+\Delta t}=\int_{0}^{t}+\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}
and the Brownian increment
\bm{B}_{t+\Delta t}=\bm{B}_{t}+(\bm{B}_{t+\Delta t}-\bm{B}_{t})
, we have:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t+\Delta t}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\varepsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\gamma_{k}\int_{0}^{t}\bm{Y}_{r}^{k}dr+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\bm{B}_{t}\right)}_{=\widehat{\bm{B}}_{t}^{H}}}-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\gamma_{k}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{Y}_{r}^{k}}dr+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}(\bm{B}_{t+\Delta t}-\bm{B}_{t})

(5.140)

Expanding
\bm{Y}_{r}^{k}
using the definition in (
5.124
), we have:

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{r}^{k}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{r}e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}+\int_{t}^{r}e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-t)}e^{-\gamma_{k}(t-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}+\int_{t}^{r}e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}=e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-t)}\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\gamma_{k}(t-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}}_{=:\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}}+\int_{t}^{r}e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}

\displaystyle=e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-t)}\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}+\int_{t}^{r}e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}

(5.141)

Substituting this back into (
5.140
) and applying the Stochastic Fubini Theorem
(
harms2019affine
)
, we get:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t+\Delta t}=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\widehat{\bm{B}}_{t}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\left[-\gamma_{k}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\left(e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-t)}\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}+\int_{t}^{r}e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}\right)}dr+(\bm{B}_{t+\Delta t}-\bm{B}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sqrt{\varepsilon}\widehat{\bm{B}}_{t}}_{=:\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\bigg[-\gamma_{k}\bigg({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}\underbrace{\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-t)}dr}_{=\int_{0}^{\Delta t}e^{-\gamma_{k}s}ds=\gamma_{k}^{-1}(1-e^{-\gamma_{k}\Delta t})}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\int_{t}^{r}e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}dr}_{\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\int_{t}^{r}(\cdot)d\bm{B}_{s}dr=\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\int_{s}^{t+\Delta t}(\cdot)drd\bm{B}_{s}}}\bigg)+(\bm{B}_{t+\Delta t}-\bm{B}_{t})\bigg]

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-(1-e^{-\gamma_{k}\Delta t})\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}}-\gamma_{k}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\int_{s}^{t+\Delta t}e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-s)}drd\bm{B}_{s}}+\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}d\bm{B}_{s}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(e^{-\gamma_{k}\Delta t}-1)\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}}+\gamma_{k}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\bigg(-\underbrace{\int_{s}^{t+\Delta t}e^{-\gamma_{k}(r-s)}dr}_{=1-e^{-\gamma_{k}(t+\Delta t-s)}}+1\bigg)d\bm{B}_{s}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\bigg[(e^{-\gamma_{k}\Delta t}-1)\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}+\gamma_{k}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}e^{-\gamma_{k}(t+\Delta t-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\sqrt{\varepsilon}(e^{-\gamma_{k}\Delta t}-1)}_{=:\zeta(t,t+\Delta t)}}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\gamma_{k}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}e^{-\gamma_{k}(t+\Delta t-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\zeta(t,t+\Delta t)}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\gamma_{k}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}e^{-\gamma_{k}(t+\Delta t-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}}

(5.142)

To derive the conditional mean
\bm{\mu}_{T|t}(\bm{z})
and covariance
\sigma_{T|t}^{2}
, we set
\Delta t=T-t
in (
5.142
) to get:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{X}}_{T}=\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\bm{Y}_{t}^{k}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\zeta(t,T)}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\gamma_{k}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\gamma_{k}(T-s)}d\bm{B}_{s}}

(5.143)

Defining
\bm{Z}_{t}:=(\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t},\bm{Y}_{t}^{1},\dots,\bm{Y}^{K}_{t})
, the conditional mean given the realization
\bm{z}=(\bm{x},\bm{y}_{1},\dots,\bm{y}_{K})
is given by:

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}_{T|t}(\bm{z})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\bm{X}}_{t}|\bm{Z}_{t}=\bm{z}]=\bm{x}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\bm{y}_{k}\zeta(t,T)

(5.144)

where the stochastic integral vanishes under the conditional expectation. To compute the conditional variance
\sigma_{T|t}^{2}:=\text{Var}(\widehat{\bm{X}}_{T}|\bm{Z}_{t})
, we let
I_{k}=\int_{t}^{T}e^{-\gamma_{k}(T-s)}ds
and expand:

\displaystyle\sigma_{T|t}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\text{Var}(\widehat{\bm{X}}_{T}|\bm{Z}_{t})=\text{Var}\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\gamma_{k}I_{k}\right)=\varepsilon\text{Var}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\gamma_{k}I_{k}\right)=\varepsilon\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\omega_{\ell}\text{Cov}\left(I_{k},I_{\ell}\right)

(5.145)

Using
Itô’s isometry
, which states that the covariance of Itô integrals combines to:

\displaystyle\text{Cov}\left(I_{k},I_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Cov}\left(\int_{t}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}e^{-\gamma_{k}(T-s)}}ds,\int_{t}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}e^{-\gamma_{\ell}(T-s)}}ds\right)=\int_{t}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}e^{-\gamma_{k}(T-s)}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}e^{-\gamma_{\ell}(T-s)}}ds

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}e^{-(\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{\ell})(T-s)}}ds=\int_{0}^{T-t}{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.3789,0.832}e^{-(\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{\ell})r}}dr=\frac{1-e^{-(\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{\ell})(T-t)}}{\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{\ell}}

(5.146)

We can derive in the final expression for the conditional covariance:

\displaystyle\sigma_{T|t}^{2}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}\omega_{k}\omega_{\ell}\frac{1-e^{-(\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{\ell})(T-t)}}{\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{\ell}}=\varepsilon\sum_{k,\ell=1}^{K}\frac{\omega_{k}\omega_{\ell}}{\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{\ell}}\left(1-e^{-(\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{\ell})(T-t)}\right)

(5.147)

Therefore, we have shown that
\widehat{\bm{X}}_{T}|(\bm{Z}_{t}=\bm{z})\sim\mathbb{S}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})=\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{T|t}(\bm{z}),\sigma_{T|t}^{2})
. We compute the gradient for the log density
\nabla_{\bm{z}}\log\mathbb{S}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})
for the
i
th dimension of
\bm{x}_{T}
as:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{z}}\log\mathbb{S}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bm{z}}\log\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}_{T|t}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(\bm{x}^{i}_{T}-\bm{\mu}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{z}))^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}_{T|t}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla_{\bm{z}}}\left(-\frac{(\bm{x}^{i}_{T}-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{\mu}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{z})})^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}_{T|t}}+C\right)=\frac{\bm{x}^{i}_{T}-\bm{\mu}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{z})}{\sigma_{T|t}^{2}}\nabla_{\bm{z}}\bm{\mu}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{z})

(5.148)

where the last equality follows from applying the chain rule since only
\bm{\mu}_{T|t}(\bm{z})
depends on
\bm{z}
. Given
\bm{z}:=(\bm{x},\bm{y}_{1},\dots,\bm{y}_{K})
and our definition for
\bm{\mu}_{T|t}(\bm{z})
in (
5.144
), the gradient expands into:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{z}}\log\mathbb{S}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{\bm{x}^{i}_{T}-\bm{\mu}_{T|t}(\bm{z})}{\sigma_{T|t}^{2}}\nabla_{\bm{z}}\bm{\mu}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{z})=\frac{\bm{x}^{i}_{T}-\bm{\mu}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{z})}{\sigma_{T|t}^{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\left[\frac{\partial\bm{\mu}^{i}_{T|t}}{\partial\bm{x}^{i}},\frac{\partial\bm{\mu}^{i}_{T|t}}{\partial\bm{y}^{i}_{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\bm{\mu}^{i}_{T|t}}{\partial\bm{y}^{i}_{K}}\right]^{\top}}

\displaystyle=\boxed{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\left[1,\omega_{1}\zeta_{1}(t,T),\dots,\omega_{K}\zeta_{K}(t,T)\right]^{\top}}\frac{\bm{x}^{i}_{T}-\bm{\mu}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{z})}{\sigma_{T|t}^{2}}}

(5.149)

which is exactly the form for the log gradient of the
h
-function defined in the Lemma.
\square

Now, we can substitute this into the SDE of the fractional Brownian bridge from (
5.133
) to get its complete form:

\displaystyle d\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}=(\bm{F}\bm{Z}_{t|0,T}+\sigma_{t}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}(\bm{Z}_{t|0,T},t)})dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{cases}\bm{u}(\bm{Z}_{t|0,T},t)=[u_{1}(\bm{Z}_{t|0,T},t),\dots,u_{d}(\bm{Z}_{t|0,T},t)]\\
u_{i}(\bm{Z}_{t|0,T},t)=\nabla_{\bm{z}}\log\mathbb{S}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{z})=\left[1,\omega_{1}\zeta_{1}(t,T),\dots,\omega_{K}\zeta_{K}(t,T)\right]^{\top}\frac{\bm{x}^{i}_{T}-\bm{\mu}^{i}_{T|t}(\bm{z})}{\sigma_{T|t}^{2}}\end{cases}

(5.150)

By leveraging the finite-dimensional Markov lift of the MA-fBM approximation, we have derived the explicit conditional law of
\widehat{\bm{X}}_{T}
given
\bm{Z}_{t}
, which remains Gaussian with affine mean and time-dependent variance and whose
h
-function can be derived in closed form. The non-Markovian nature of the marginal process
\bm{X}_{t}
provides a principled way to incorporate long-range temporal correlations into generative stochastic dynamics through a finite-dimensional Markov approximation of MA-fBM. This framework therefore, extends classical Schrödinger bridge problems beyond memoryless dynamics and offers a mathematically tractable route to learning physically realistic long-horizon dependencies.

5.7
Closing Remarks for Section
5

In this section, we expanded the theory of the dynamic Schrödinger bridge problem to a broader array of constraints and problem settings. We start with the
Gassian SB problem
, which we show admits a closed-form solution that is in the class of Gaussian Markov processes. We then introduced the
generalized SB problem
, which extends the classical formulation to systems with mean-field interactions where the dynamics of individual particles depend on the evolving population distribution.

Next, we explored several important extensions of the SB framework that arise in more complex settings. These include the
multi-marginal SB problem
, which incorporates multiple intermediate marginal constraints; the
unbalanced SB problem
, which allows for the creation or destruction of mass along the transport trajectory; and the
branched SB problem
, which captures scenarios where stochastic trajectories diverge toward multiple terminal modes. Finally, we considered an alternative class of stochastic processes driven by fractional Brownian motion, leading to the formulation of the
fractional SB problem
.

These extensions illustrate the incredible flexibility of the Schrödinger bridge framework in describing complex stochastic systems across a wide range of settings. Having introduced the theoretical foundations of both the static and dynamic formulations of the SB problem, in addition to their extensions to diverse constraints and dynamics, we are now prepared to dive into modern
generative modeling frameworks
that leverage Schrödinger bridge theory to construct scalable algorithms for high-dimensional data.

6
Generative Modeling with Schrödinger Bridges

In this section, we develop the connection between Schrödinger bridge theory and modern generative modeling frameworks, showing how generative modeling can be formulated as the problem of learning controlled stochastic dynamics that interpolate between an initial and a target distribution while minimizing relative entropy with respect to a reference process.

We begin with a brief primer on score-based generative modeling (Section
6.1
), highlighting its formulation in terms of forward and reverse-time stochastic processes. We then extend this perspective by jointly learning forward and backward controlled drifts through likelihood maximization over coupled forward–backward SDEs (Section
6.2
). As an alternative paradigm, we introduce diffusion Schrödinger bridge matching, which constructs generative models via path-space reciprocal and Markov projections with parameterized drifts (Section
6.3
). Finally, we present two simulation-free approaches for learning Schrödinger bridges: score and flow matching (Section
6.4
) and adjoint matching (Section
6.5
).

Throughout this section, we show how Schrödinger bridges provide a unifying framework that connects likelihood-based training, path-space KL minimization, and score and flow matching frameworks into a single coherent theory.

6.1
A Primer on Score-Based Generative Modeling

From Section
4.2
, we have shown that Schrödinger bridges are a generalization of diffusion models where the prior distribution is a simple Gaussian prior. Recall the backward SDE (
Time Reversal Formula
) corresponding to the forward SDE with variance-exploding drift given by:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}:=p_{\text{data}}

(6.1)

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\left[-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log\tilde{p}_{s}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s})\right]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s},\quad

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim\pi_{T}:=p_{\text{prior}}

(6.2)

where
\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})
is the density generated by the diffusion SDE at time
s
and
\nabla\log\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})
is a gradient drift that pushes the density towards areas of high likelihood given the noisy data distribution at time
s
. This process guides the diffusion process to samples from the true data distribution
\tilde{p}_{T}(\bm{x})=p_{0}(\bm{x})
. The expression
\nabla\log\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})
is known as the
score function
.

Score-based generative modeling
aims to train a generative model that samples from the data distribution
\pi_{0}:=p_{\text{data}}
by parameterizing the score function with a neural network with parameters
\theta
known as the
score-based model

\bm{s}_{\theta}(\bm{x},s)\approx\nabla\log\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})
which estimates the score function over the state space
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and time coordinate
s\in[0,T]
.

Given the score-based model
\bm{s}_{\theta}(\bm{x},s)
, we can define the estimated backward SDE as:

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\left[-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\bm{s}_{\theta}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},s)\right]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s},\quad\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim p_{\text{prior}}

(6.3)

The distribution of
clean
samples generated from simulating many samples
\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim p_{\text{prior}}
via the SDE (
6.3
) over time
s\in[0,T]
approximates the data distribution
p_{\text{data}}
.

Figure 14:
Score-Based Generative Models.
Score-based generative models learn the score function
\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})
of a diffusion process that progressively perturbs data with noise through a forward SDE. A neural network
\bm{s}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)
is trained using the score matching loss to approximate this time-dependent score. Once learned, the reverse-time SDE uses the estimated score to iteratively denoise samples, transforming noise into data.

A simple objective that is minimized exactly when the score-based model matches the true score function is the
score matching loss

\mathcal{L}_{\text{SM}}

(
hyvarinen2005estimation
;
song2021maximum
)
defined as the weighted squared difference integrated over time:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{SM}}(\theta):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{p}_{s}}\big[\lambda(t)\|\nabla\log\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})-\bm{s}_{\theta}(\bm{x},s)\|^{2}\big]ds

(Score Maching Loss)

For this objective to be tractable, we require a closed-form expression for
\nabla\log\tilde{p}_{s}(\bm{x})
. For generative modeling of a clean data distribution, the forward stochastic process or
noise injection process
can be defined with the conditional distribution
p_{t}(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{t}|\bm{x}_{0}):=\mathcal{N}(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{t};\bm{x}_{0},\sigma^{2}_{t}\bm{I}_{d})
where
\bm{x}_{0}\sim p_{\text{data}}
, which progressively smoothes the data distribution with larger variance
\sigma_{t}>\sigma_{s}
for
t>s

(
song2019generative
)
. This yields the tractable score function:

\displaystyle\nabla\log q_{t}(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{t}|\bm{x}_{0})=\nabla\log\bigg[\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_{t}^{2}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|\tilde{\bm{x}}_{t}-\bm{x}_{0}\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right)\bigg]=\nabla\bigg(-\frac{\|\tilde{\bm{x}}_{t}-\bm{x}_{0}\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}+C\bigg)=\boxed{\frac{\bm{x}_{0}-\tilde{\bm{x}}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}}

(6.4)

which can be optimized with the score matching objective:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{SM}}(\theta):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}(\tilde{\bm{x}}_{t}|\bm{x}_{0}),p_{\text{data}}(\bm{x}_{0})}\left[\left\|\frac{\bm{x}_{0}-\tilde{\bm{x}}_{t}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}-\bm{s}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)\right\|^{2}\right]dt

(6.5)

While score matching provides a simulation-free way to learn the reverse-time drift through estimation of the score function, it does so under the assumption that the generative process has a simple prior distribution, typically a standard Gaussian like in (
6.4
), and the forward diffusion dynamics are chosen to be linear like in (
6.5
) such that it is analytically tractable without simulation. This design constraint restricts the class of admissible dynamics to perturbations of a simple reference process which transforms between noise and data. To extend this idea to model transport between
structured distributions
with an unknown forward control drift, we introduce a likelihood-based training framework for the forward-backward SDEs that characterize the Schrödinger bridge.

6.2
Likelihood Training of Forward-Backward SDEs

Prerequisite: Section
4.3

Just like how likelihood training provides a theoretically-grounded training objective of estimating the score function in score-based generative modeling, we can derive a lower bound for the log-likelihood of the
forward-backward stochastic differential equations
(SDEs) defined in Section
4.3
, which yields a tractable training objective that aims to maximize the lower bound
(
chen2021likelihood
)
.

Figure 15:
Likelihood Training of Forward and Backward Schrödinger Potentials.
The Schrödinger bridge can be learned by parameterizing the log gradient of the forward and backward potentials
\bm{Z}^{\theta}_{t}=\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
and
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\phi}=\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
, which define the forward and backward SDEs between endpoint distributions.
\theta
and
\phi
are trained by maximizing path likelihoods through forward and backward objective functions, each evaluated under trajectories generated by the corresponding controlled SDE. Together, the two likelihood losses couple the forward and reverse dynamics, enabling the model to learn a consistent Schrödinger bridge between the marginals.

Since the definition of
\bm{Y}_{t}=\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
and
\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}=\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
from Section
4.3
jointly determine the log density of the SB solution
\bm{Y}_{t}+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}=\log p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
, we can write the likelihood under the FBSDEs as the estimated value of
\bm{Y}_{0}+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{0}=\log p_{0}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0})
given a data point
\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}
.

\displaystyle\log p^{\star}_{0}(\bm{x}_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\log p_{0}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0})|\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\bm{Y}_{0}+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{0}\;\big|\;\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}\right]

(6.6)

First, we recall the set of three coupled FBSDEs that define the solution to the non-linear SB problem as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t}=\left(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{Z}_{t}\right)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
d\bm{Y}_{t}=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}_{t}\|^{2}dt+\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
d\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}=\left(\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}-\bm{f})+\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\|^{2}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\right)dt+\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\end{cases}

(6.7)

Since the
\bm{Y}_{t}
and
\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{t}
evolve via
backward SDEs
with boundary constraints
\bm{Y}_{T}+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{T}=\log\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})
, we can write
\bm{Y}_{0}
and
\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{0}
as the terminal condition integrated backward in time via the SDEs defined in (
4.50
) such that:

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{T}-\bm{Y}_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}_{t}\|^{2}dt+\int_{0}^{T}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

(6.8)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{T}-\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left[\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}-\bm{f})+\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\|^{2}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\right]dt+\int_{0}^{T}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

(6.9)

which we rearrange to get:

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{0}

\displaystyle=\bm{Y}_{T}-\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}_{t}\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

(6.10)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{T}-\int_{0}^{T}\left[\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}-\bm{f})+\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\|^{2}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\right]dt-\int_{0}^{T}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

(6.11)

Substituting this into the log-likelihood, we get:

\displaystyle\log p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x}_{0})=\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[\bm{Y}_{0}+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{0}\;\big|\;\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\bigg[\bm{Y}_{T}-\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}_{t}\|^{2}dt-\int_{0}^{T}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{T}-\int_{0}^{T}\left[\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}-\bm{f})+\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\|^{2}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\right]dt-\int_{0}^{T}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\;\bigg|\;\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}\bigg]

(6.12)

Combining similar terms, we get:

\displaystyle\log p^{\star}(\bm{x}_{0},0)=\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[\bm{Y}_{T}+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{T}\big|\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}\right]

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}_{t}\|^{2}+\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}-\bm{f})+\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\|^{2}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\;\bigg|\;\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}\right]dt

\displaystyle-\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}+\int_{0}^{T}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\top}d\bm{B}_{t}\;\bigg|\;\bm{X}_{0}=\bm{x}_{0}\right]}_{=0\text{ (Itô integral has zero-expectation)}}

(6.13)

Since the Itô integral has zero-expectation and
\bm{Y}_{T}+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{T}=\log p_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})
, we are left with:

\displaystyle\log p^{\star}_{0}(\bm{x}_{0})=\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\left[\log p^{\star}_{(}\bm{X}_{T})\right]}_{\text{(i)}}-\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}_{t}\|^{2}}_{\text{(ii)}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\|^{2}}_{\text{(iii)}}+\underbrace{\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}-\bm{f})}_{\text{(iv)}}+\underbrace{\sigma_{t}^{2}\bm{Z}_{t}^{\top}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}}_{\text{(v)}}\bigg]dt

(6.14)

which is the log-likelihood of the data point that we aim to maximize. Each term can be interpreted as follows:

(i)

The terminal distribution matching reward that is maximized with the distribution at time
T
generated by the forward bridge matches
p_{T}
.

(ii)

The energy cost of steering samples via the forward potential drift
\bm{Z}_{t}=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
, which is minimized when the log-likelihood is maximized.

(iii)

The energy cost of steering samples via the backward potential drift
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
, which is minimized when the log-likelihood is maximized.

(iv)

This divergence term ensures that the drift is consistent with the time-evolving density
p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
. We can observe this by expanding the divergence term
\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t})
using the identity
\nabla\cdot(u\bm{v})=(\nabla u)\cdot\bm{v}+u\nabla\cdot\bm{v}
rearranged to
u\nabla\cdot\bm{v}=\nabla\cdot(u\bm{v})-(\nabla u)\cdot\bm{v}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p^{\star}_{t}}\left[\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(\sigma_{t}\nabla\cdot\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\big)p^{\star}_{t}d\bm{X}_{t}=\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}p^{\star}_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t})d\bm{X}_{t}}_{=0}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\cdot\nabla p^{\star}_{t}d\bm{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}\cdot\nabla p^{\star}_{t}d\bm{X}_{t}

(6.15)

(v)

The consistency of the forward-backward potentials. Since we want the drifts to generate exactly symmetric opposite bridges and the dot product is minimized at
-1
when the vectors are exactly opposite, this term is minimized when the log-likelihood is maximized.

Since
\bm{Z}_{t}
and
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}
determine the optimal drift in the forward and backward directions based on the SB potential, we can train a generative SB model by parameterizing them with neural networks
\bm{Z}^{\theta}_{t}\approx\bm{Z}_{t}
and
\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\phi}_{t}\approx\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}
with parameters
\theta
and
\phi
. Defining the SB loss with (
6.14
), we get a theoretically-grounded
maximization objective
that lower bounds the true log-likelihood
\log p_{0}^{\star}(\bm{x}_{0})\geq\mathcal{L}_{\text{SB}}
defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{SB}}(\theta,\phi)=\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[\log p_{T}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{T})\right]-\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}^{\theta}_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\phi}_{t}\|^{2}+\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\phi}_{t}-\bm{f})+\sigma_{t}^{2}\bm{Z}^{\theta\top}_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\phi}_{t}\bigg]dt

(6.16)

Jointly training both
\bm{Z}^{\theta}_{t}
and
\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\phi}_{t}
with this objective is carried out by
(i)
simulating the forward trajectory of the SDE.
\bm{X}_{0:T}
using
\bm{Z}_{t}^{\theta}
and
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\phi}
,
(ii)
computing the maximum likelihood objective
\mathcal{L}_{\text{SB}}
with (
6.16
), and
(iii)
backpropagating through the SDE solver for every time step with respect to both
\theta
and
\phi
, which requires maintaining the full computational graph of the SDE.

While this training scheme works for low-dimensional data, it becomes computationally infeasible for high-dimensional data like images. To overcome this, we can store SDE trajectories in a replay buffer as sequences of static states
\bm{X}_{t}
while discarding the computational graph containing the gradient path. Crucially, this
breaks the dependency of the trajectories with the current model parameters
, but it enables us to reuse the same SDEs over multiple gradient updates. Updating one model, like
\bm{Z}_{t}^{\theta}
, with respect to
\nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}_{\text{SB}}
would change the SDE
\bm{X}_{t}
, so updating
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\phi}
simultaneously with the original SDE would break the symmetry of the optimization problem.

Instead, we can leverage the symmetric property of Schrödinger bridges:

(i)

Given the true
forward
potential drift
\bm{Z}_{t}=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}
, the optimal
backward
potential drift
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}^{\phi}
can be learned to match the forward trajectories.

(ii)

Given the true
backward
potential drift
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}
, the optimal
forward
potential drift
\bm{Z}_{t}^{\theta}
can be learned to match the backward trajectories.

To train the backward potential drift given trajectories generated with the frozen forward model
\bm{Z}^{\bar{\theta}}_{t}
, where
\bar{\theta}:=\texttt{stopgrad}(\theta)
, we can use the same likelihood maximization objective defined in (
6.16
) but dropping all terms that are not dependent on
\phi
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{SB}}(\phi)=-\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{\theta}}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\phi}_{t}\|^{2}+\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\phi}_{t}-\bm{f})+\sigma_{t}^{2}\bm{Z}^{\theta\top}_{t}\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\phi}_{t}\bigg]dt

(6.17)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\mathbb{P}^{\bar{\theta}}:d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{X}_{t}))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}

(6.18)

Given that the SB is symmetric in either direction, the objective for training the forward potential drift given trajectories generated with the frozen backward model
\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\bar{\phi}}_{t}
, where
\bar{\phi}:=\texttt{stopgrad}(\phi)
, is the same as (
6.17
) but flipping
\bm{Z}_{t}^{\theta}
and
\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\phi}_{t}
given by:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\text{SB}}(\theta)=-\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{\phi}}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{Z}^{\theta}_{t}\|^{2}+\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}\bm{Z}^{\theta}_{t}-\bm{f})+\sigma_{t}^{2}\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\phi\top}_{t}\bm{Z}^{\theta}_{t}\bigg]dt

(6.19)

\displaystyle\text{s.t.}\quad\mathbb{P}^{\bar{\phi}}:d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}-\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t}))dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{X}_{T}\sim\pi_{T}

(6.20)

Derivation sketch.
This symmetric objective can be derived by defining a reversed time coordinate
s:=T-t
and defining the prior non-linear process as the reverse-time analog of the forward prior dynamics with SDE
d\bm{X}_{s}=-\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{s},s)ds+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{s}
. Then, redefining the Hopf-Cole linear PDE constraints and forward-backward SDEs with negative drift
-\bm{f}
and following a similar derivation from (
6.8
) to (
6.14
) for the log-likelihood
\log p^{\star}_{0}(\bm{x}_{T})=\mathbb{E}[\bm{Y}_{0}+\widehat{\bm{Y}}_{0}|\bm{X}_{T}=\bm{x}_{T}]
of a sample
\bm{x}_{T}
from the
prior
distribution or
\pi_{T}
.
\square

This section shows that the optimal forward and backward control drifts defined by the Schrödinger potentials can be learned through mazximizing a lower bound on the log-likelihood of reconstructing both of the marginal constraints. These objectives lead to a symmetric alternating optimization procedure that mirrors the structure of Sinkhorn’s algorithm from Section
1.5
. Next, we move on to an alternative perspective, considering the optimization process as performing iterative Markovian and reciprocal projections rather than maximizing likelihoods.

6.3
Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge Matching

Prerequisite: Section
4.5

Building on the Iterative Markovian Fitting (IMF) procedure from Section
4.5
, we now describe the
Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge Matching
(DSBM) algorithm
(
shi2023diffusion
)
. This algorithm unifies ideas from denoising diffusion
(
song2020score
;
ho2020denoising
)
and flow matching
(
lipman2022flow
)
to solve the SB problem with arbitrary marginal distributions by parameterizing the Markov drift optimized to match the Schrödinger bridge drift through Markovian and reciprocal projections.

While the IMF procedure provides a theoretically grounded procedure for constructing the Schrödinger bridge through alternating KL projections in path space, its formulation remains abstract and infinite-dimensional. To make these ideas computationally tractable in high-dimensional settings, we can leverage the explicit SDE representation of the Markovian projection, defined as:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\big(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|t}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t}\right]\big)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(Forward Markovian Projection SDE)

and parameterize the forward-time Markov control drift
\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|t}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t}\right]
with
\bm{u}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)
such that it converges to the optimal drift
\bm{u}^{\star}
through the sequence of IMF iterations.

Although the Markovian projection preserves the bridge measure
\mathbb{M}^{\star}_{t}=\Pi_{t}
in theory (Proposition
4.10
), parameterizing only the forward-time SDE results in errors in practice, where the terminal marginal
p_{T}
generated from simulating (
Forward Markovian Projection SDE
) may not exactly match the true marginal constraint
\pi_{T}
. Therefore, to avoid error accumulation during the IMF sequence, we also parameterize the
reverse-time Markovian projection
, which we define below.

Proposition 6.1

(Forward and Reverse Time Markovian Projections (Proposition 9 in
shi2023diffusion
)
.

Given a mixture of bridges
\Pi=\Pi_{0,T}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}
in the reciprocal class
\Pi\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
of the reference measure
\mathbb{Q}
generated by the SDE
d\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}
, the Markovian projection
\mathbb{M}:=\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)
can be written as both forward and reverse time SDEs defined as:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\big(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|t}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t}\right]\big)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(6.21)

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\big(-\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{s},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0|T-s}}\big[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T-s|0}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}|\tilde{\bm{X}}_{T})|\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}\big]\big)dt+\sigma_{T-s}d\tilde{\bm{B}}_{s}

(6.22)

with initial conditions
\bm{X}_{0}\sim\Pi_{0}
and
\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim\Pi_{T}
, respectively.

Proof.
The proof of this proposition follows directly from the definition of the Markovian projection in (
4.10
) and applying the (
Time Reversal Formula
) described in Section
4.2
.
\square

Parameterizing both the forward-time Markovian projection with drift
\bm{u}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)
and the reverse-time Markovian projection with drift
\bm{u}_{\phi}(\bm{x},t)
, we outline the Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge Matching (DSBM) algorithm
(
shi2023diffusion
)
as follows.

Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge Matching

DSBM generates a sequence of Markov projections
(\mathbb{M}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and reciprocal projections
(\Pi^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
initialized at
\Pi^{0}:=\pi_{0,T}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}
by alternating between the following steps:

(1a)

Solve the forward-time Markovian projection
\mathbb{M}^{2n+1}:=\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi^{2n})
by updating a parameterized drift
\bm{u}_{\theta}
to minimize
\mathcal{L}_{\text{DSBM}}(\theta):=\text{KL}(\Pi^{2n}\|\mathbb{M}^{\theta})
.

(1b)

Define the reciprocal projection as
\Pi^{2n+1}:=\mathbb{M}^{2n+1}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}

(2a)

Solve the backward-time Markovian projection
\mathbb{M}^{2n+2}:=\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi^{2n+1})
by updating a parameterized drift
\bm{u}_{\phi}
to minimize
\mathcal{L}_{\text{DSBM}}(\phi):=\text{KL}(\Pi^{2n+1}\|\mathbb{M}^{\phi})
.

(2b)

Define the reciprocal projection as
\Pi^{2n+2}:=\mathbb{M}^{2n+2}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}

To learn the forward and reverse time Markovian projections in Steps
(1a)
and
(2a)
, we can minimize loss functions defined as the KL divergence
36
36

36

In
shi2023diffusion
, the parameterized control drift is not scaled by the diffusion coefficient, which yields an extra
\sigma_{t}^{2}
in the denominator. Since we define the control as
\sigma_{t}\bm{u}_{\theta}
in the SDEs, the extra
\sigma_{t}^{2}
vanishes.
as derived in Section
2.6
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{DSBM}}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{t,T}}\bigg[\left\|\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})-\bm{u}_{\theta}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right\|^{2}\bigg]dt

(Forward DSBM Loss)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{DSBM}}(\phi)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{t,0}}\bigg[\left\|\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{0})-\bm{u}_{\phi}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right\|^{2}\bigg]dt

(Reverse DSBM Loss)

Given sufficient expressivity of
\theta,\phi
, it is easy to see that optimizing the above losses for all
(\bm{x},t)
exactly yields the control drift of the Markov projection
\text{proj}_{\mathcal{M}}(\Pi)
:

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{\theta^{\star}}(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|t}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{t})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(6.23)

\displaystyle\bm{u}_{\phi^{\star}}(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0|t}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{0})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(6.24)

The corresponding reciprocal projections performed in
(1b)
and
(2b)
are obtained by first simulating trajectories
\bm{X}_{0:T}
either with the forward SDE using
\bm{u}_{\theta}
or in the reverse SDE using
\bm{u}_{\phi}
to obtain samples from the endpoint law
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim\mathbb{M}_{0,T}
, and then sampling from the conditional bridge
\bm{X}_{t}\sim\mathbb{Q}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
of the reference process
\mathbb{Q}
.

Figure 16:
Diffusion Schrödinger Bridge Matching.
Illustration of the diffusion Schrödinger bridge matching (DSBM) procedure with alternating Markovian and reciprocal projections. Starting from an initial reciprocal measure
\Pi^{0}=\pi_{0,T}\mathbb{Q}_{\cdot|0,T}
, the algorithm alternates between
(i)
forward-time Markovian projections
\mathbb{M}^{2n+1}
which updates the parameterized forward drift
\bm{u}_{\theta}
to match
\Pi^{2n}
and
(ii)
reverse-time Markovian projections
\mathbb{M}^{2n+2}
which updates the parameterized reverse drift
\bm{u}_{\phi}
to match
\Pi^{2n+1}
. Each Markovian projection is followed by a reciprocal projection that constructs a mixture of reference bridges conditioned on the updated endpoint laws, denoted
\Pi^{2n+1}
and
\Pi^{2n+2}
. This alternating procedure produces a sequence of path measures that progressively reduce the KL divergence to the optimal bridge, converging to the Schrödinger bridge solution which occurs when the Markov and reciprocal projections reach equilibrium
\mathbb{M}^{\star}=\Pi^{\star}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}
.

Optimizing these losses through Algorithm
6.3
exactly performs the Iterative Markovian Fitting (IMF) procedure from Section
4.5
through parameterized control drifts of SDEs. Therefore, by Theorem
4.17
, we have that the unique fixed point of the diffusion SBM algorithm yields the optimal Schrödinger bridge
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
. Rather than separating the forward and reverse Markovian projection steps,
shi2023diffusion
shows that they can be performed simultaneously, which mimics the true IMF procedure where each projection corresponds to the exact Markov and reciprocal projections.

Joint Training of Forward and Reverse Markovian Projections

Since both objectives can be computed from sampling a pair
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim\Pi_{0,T}
from the joint distribution and sampling the intermediate state
\bm{X}_{t}\sim\mathbb{Q}_{t|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
from the bridge measure, we can optimize both
\theta
and
\phi

jointly
. After each iteration
n
, the updated parameters can be used to define the forward Markov process
\mathbb{M}^{n+1}_{f}
and the backward Markov process
\mathbb{M}^{n+1}_{b}
with the following SDEs:

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}^{n+1}_{f}

\displaystyle:d\bm{X}_{t}=[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}_{\theta}(\bm{X}_{t},t)}]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}

(6.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}^{n+1}_{b}

\displaystyle:d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=[-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{u}_{\phi}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)}]ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\bm{B}_{s}

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim\pi_{T}

(6.26)

At equilibrium,
the forward and backward SDEs should match
, which means reversing
\mathbb{M}_{f}^{n}
yields
\mathbb{M}_{b}^{n}
and reversing
\mathbb{M}_{b}^{n}
yields the
\mathbb{M}^{n}_{f}
. To enforce this during training, we can compute the time-reversal of the backward SDE (
6.26
) using the (
Time Reversal Formula
) to get:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{-\bm{u}_{\phi}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\Pi_{t}^{2n}(\bm{X}_{t})}_{\text{should match }\bm{u}_{\theta}(\bm{X}_{t},t)}})]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}

(6.27)

To ensure the control drifts in (
6.27
) and (
6.25
) are aligned, we leverage a consistency loss defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{cons}}(\theta,\phi)=\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{t}^{2n}}\bigg[\|\bm{u}_{\theta}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\bm{u}_{\phi}(\bm{X}_{t},t)-\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\log\Pi^{2n}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})}\|^{2}\bigg]dt

(Consistency Loss)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{t}^{2n}}\bigg[\left\|\bm{u}_{\theta}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\bm{u}_{\phi}(\bm{X}_{t},t)-\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|t}^{2n}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}|\bm{x}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0|t}^{2n}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}|\bm{x}\right]\right)}\right\|^{2}\bigg]dt

where we rewrite the score function
\nabla\log\Pi^{2n}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
with the known conditional densities
\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{0|t}
using the identity:

\displaystyle\nabla\log\Pi^{2n}_{t}(\bm{x})=\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{T|t}^{2n}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\Pi_{0|t}^{2n}}\left[\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(6.28)

which defines the marginal score at time
t
as the sum of the conditional Gaussian scores at the endpoints. Therefore, the total
joint training loss
can be defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{DSBM}}(\theta,\phi)=\mathcal{L}_{\text{DSBM}}(\theta)+\mathcal{L}_{\text{DSBM}}(\phi)+\lambda\mathcal{L}_{\text{cons}}(\theta,\phi)

(6.29)

where
\lambda>0
is a positive weight that defines the strength of the consistency loss.

This formulation provides a tractable approach for performing path-space projections using parameterized drifts that can be simulated at inference time via an SDE solver. In practice, the Markov projection requires learning both forward and backward drifts to satisfy the marginal constraints, despite the underlying equivalence of the bridge measures. However, this approach remains computationally intensive, as training still relies on simulating full stochastic trajectories due to the absence of a closed-form sampling procedure for the optimal bridge. Motivated by this limitation, we now consider an alternative perspective in which the intermediate states admit tractable expression, enabling simulation-free matching objectives.

6.4
Simulation-Free Score and Flow Matching

Prerequisite: Section
4.1
,
4.2

To overcome the restriction to the Gaussian prior distribution of score-based generative modeling described in Section
6.1
, we highlight the simulation-free score and flow matching ([SF]
2
M;
tong2023simulation
) framework which extends score matching to arbitrary prior distributions. Crucially, we will show that given the
optimal entropic OT coupling
(Section
Static SB Problem
), [SF]
2
M solves the (
Dynamic SB Problem
) through a
endpoint-conditioned objective
with the same gradient as the unconditional objective.

Figure 17:
Conditional Score and Flow Matching.
Conditional score and flow matching learn the dynamics of stochastic bridges by conditioning trajectories on endpoint pairs
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim\pi_{0,T}
. The model jointly estimates the conditional control drift
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
and the conditional score
\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
, which describe how trajectories evolve between the endpoints. By minimizing the combined objective, the learned dynamics produce the mixture of Brownian bridges which is equal to the Schrödinger bridge
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
given the entropic OT coupling
\pi_{0,T}^{\star}
.

This framework considers a data-driven SB problem, where we have empirical samples from both the marginal distributions
\bm{x}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}
and
\bm{x}_{T}\sim\pi_{T}
. In this setting, we can solve the entropic optimal transport (OT) problem between empirical samples to determine the optimal coupling
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
, from which the solution to the dynamic SB problem with Brownian reference process
\mathbb{Q}
is defined simply as a
mixture of Brownian bridges weighted by the optimal static coupling
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
as proven in Proposition
4.3
and Corollary
4.2
.

Recall from Section
4.2
, where we derived the (
4.40
) as:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}d\bm{X}_{t}=\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}\\
d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=-\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)+\sigma_{T-s}^{2}\nabla\log p(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},T-s)ds+\sigma_{T-s}d\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{s}\end{cases}

(6.30)

where we define the reference drift as pure Brownian motion
\bm{f}:=0
. Crucially, the score function
\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
appears in the reverse-time drift as a
correction
term that compensates for the entropy-producing forward diffusion, ensuring that the reversed dynamics reproduce the correct marginal distributions. This decomposition of the control and score function motivates a
combined objective
that learns a parameterized control drift
\bm{u}_{\theta}
, which learns the
reverse control

\bm{u}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)\approx\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
37
37

37

since the control is non-deterministic, we will just consider the reverse control as
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
and omit the negative sign for simplicity.
, and the score function
\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})
.

Definition 6.2

(Unconditional Score and Flow Matching Objective)
.

The
unconditional score and flow matching objective
aims to match a parameterized control field
\bm{u}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and score function
\bm{s}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
to the true velocity and score function defining the solution to (
Dynamic SB Problem
) by minimizing:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{U[SF]}^{2}\text{M}}(\theta):=\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\bigg[\underbrace{\|\bm{u}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)-\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}}_{\text{flow matching loss}}+\underbrace{\lambda(t)^{2}\|\bm{s}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)-\nabla\log p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})\|^{2}}_{\text{score matching loss}}\bigg]dt

(Unconditional Objective)

where
p^{\star}_{t}
is the optimal marginal density of the dynamic SB, and
\lambda(t):[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
is some positive weight.

While this objective is theoretically sound, both
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
and
\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})
are undefined or intractable for general target distributions. In this setting, we assume access to explicit samples from both marginal distributions
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
and define the tractable control drift and score for a Brownian bridge between a predefined coupling
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim\pi_{0,T}
given by:

\displaystyle\bm{u}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})

\displaystyle=\frac{1-2t}{t(1-t)}(\bm{x}-(t\bm{x}_{1}+(1-t)\bm{x}_{0}))+(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{0})

\displaystyle\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})

\displaystyle=\frac{t\bm{x}_{1}+(1-t)\bm{x}_{0}-\bm{x}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}t(1-t)}

(6.31)

Using this definition of the endpoint-conditioned velocity and score function, we can define the conditional velocity and score over the empirical distribution
\pi_{0}
by taking an expectation:

\displaystyle\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0,T}}\left[\frac{\bm{u}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}{p_{t}(\bm{x})}\right]

(6.32)

\displaystyle\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{0,T}}\left[\frac{p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}{p_{t}(\bm{x})}\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right]

(6.33)

These tractable definitions for the
conditional
control drift and score function motivate the definition of the
conditional score and flow matching objective

(
tong2023simulation
)
, which we show yields the same gradients as the unconditional objective.

Proposition 6.3

(Conditional Score and Flow Matching Objective (Theorem 3.2 in
tong2023simulation
))
.

Consider the
conditional score and flow matching objective
which aims to match a parameterized control drift
\bm{v}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and score function
\bm{s}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
to a distribution of velocity and score functions conditioned on the endpoint
\bm{z}\sim\pi_{T}
by minimizing:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{[SF]}^{2}\text{M}}(\theta):=\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t|0,T},\pi_{0,T}}\bigg[\underbrace{\|\bm{v}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)-\bm{u}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\|^{2}}_{\text{conditional flow matching loss}}+\underbrace{\lambda(t)^{2}\|\bm{s}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)-\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\|^{2}}_{\text{conditional score matching loss}}\bigg]dt

(Conditional Objective)

where the expectation is taken over samples from the endpoint law
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim\pi_{0,T}
and samples from the conditional distribution at time
t\in[0,T]
given target endpoints
\bm{x}\sim p_{t|0,T}(\cdot|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
. Then, we have that the gradients of the conditional objective match the gradients of (
Unconditional Objective
) such that
\nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}_{\text{[SF]}^{2}\text{M}}(\theta)=\nabla_{\theta}\mathcal{L}_{\text{U[SF]}^{2}\text{M}}(\theta)
.

Proof.
The goal of this proof is to show the equivalence between the gradients of the conditional and unconditional expectations:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t|0,T},\pi_{0,T}}\left[\|\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)-\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\|^{2}\right]=\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\|\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)-\bm{w}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}\right]

(6.34)

which can be applied for both the conditional flow matching loss with
\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T}):=\bm{u}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
and the conditional score matching loss
\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T}):=\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
. Expanding the squared loss in the conditional objective, we have:

\displaystyle\|\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)-\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\|^{2}=\underbrace{\|\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}}_{\text{independent of }\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-2\langle\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t),\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\rangle}+\underbrace{\|\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\|^{2}}_{\text{independent of }\theta}

(6.35)

where we observe that the first term is independent of the conditional pair
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
, which means that it is clearly equivalent to the unconditional gradient. The last term is independent of
\theta
which has a gradient of zero with respect to
\theta
. Therefore, we can write the difference between the unconditional and conditional expectations as:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t|0,T},\pi_{0,T}}\left[\|\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)-\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\|\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)-\bm{w}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t|0,T},\pi_{0,T}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-2\langle\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t),\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-2\langle\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t),\bm{w}(\bm{x},t)\rangle}\right]

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t|0,T},\pi_{0,T}}\left[\langle\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t),\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\langle\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t),\bm{w}(\bm{x},t)\rangle\right]

(6.36)

Now, we aim to show that the expectations are equivalent. Starting from the

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{p_{t}}\left[\langle\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t),\bm{w}(\bm{x},t)\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t),{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{w}(\bm{x},t)}\rangle p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t),{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}{p_{t}(\bm{x})}\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\pi_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})d\bm{x}_{0}d\bm{x}_{T}}\right\rangle p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}

\displaystyle\overset{(\bigstar)}{=}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t),\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}{p_{t}(\bm{x})}\pi_{T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}p_{t}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}d\bm{x}_{0}d\bm{x}_{T}}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t),\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\pi_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}d\bm{x}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}d\bm{x}_{0}d\bm{x}_{T}}

\displaystyle=\boxed{\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p_{t|0,T},\pi_{0,T}}}\left[\langle\bm{w}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t),{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{w}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}\rangle\right]}

(6.37)

where
(\bigstar)
follows from factoring out the scalar values and applying Fubini’s theorem to change the order of integration, resulting in the equivalence between the marginal and conditional objectives.
\square

This result is well established in flow matching literature
(
lipman2022flow
;
tong2023improving
)
as a way of training parameterized flows that approximate an intractable marginal distribution using empirical samples from the target data distribution. However, this marginal distribution does not yet solve the Schrödinger bridge problem, as the coupled distribution
\pi_{0,T}
from which
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})
is sampled does not necessarily align with the entropic OT coupling. To establish how the conditional score and flow matching objective can be used to solve the (
Dynamic SB Problem
), we establish the following proposition.

Proposition 6.4

(Score and Flow Matching Recovers the Schrödinger Bridge (Proposition 3.4 in
tong2023simulation
))
.

Let
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
denote the path measure that solves the dynamic SB with marginal constraints
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and pure Brownian motion reference process
\sigma\mathbb{B}
. Consider the optimal endpoint law
\pi_{0,T}^{\star}
that solves the (
Entropic OT Problem
) with quadratic transport cost
c(\bm{x},\bm{y}):=\|\bm{x}-\bm{y}\|^{2}
:

\displaystyle\pi^{\star}_{0,T}=\underset{\pi_{0,T}\in\Pi(\pi_{0},\pi_{T})}{\arg\min}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\bm{x}-\bm{y}\|^{2}d\pi_{0,T}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+2\sigma^{2}_{t}\text{KL}(\pi_{0,T}\|\pi_{0}\otimes\pi_{T})\right\}

(6.38)

If the parameterized score function
\bm{s}^{\star}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)
and control drift
\bm{v}^{\star}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)
globally minimize the (
Conditional Objective
) under the coupling
(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim\pi_{0,T}^{\star}
, then the resulting stochastic process is given by the SDE:

\displaystyle d\bm{X}_{t}=\left[\bm{v}_{\theta}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}^{2}\bm{s}_{\theta}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}

(6.39)

and generates the Schrödinger bridge path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
.

Proof Sketch.
The
key idea
is that the (
Conditional Objective
) is minimized pointwise for all
(\bm{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]
when:

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)=\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim p_{0,T|t}}\left[\bm{u}(\bm{x},t|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right],\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{s}_{\theta}(\bm{x},t)=\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\sim p_{0,T|t}}\left[\nabla\log p_{t}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\right]

(6.40)

where
p_{0,T|t}
is the posterior distribution over the endpoint law
\pi_{0,T}
given an intermediate state
\bm{x}
which can be expressed using Bayes’ rule as:

\displaystyle p_{0,T|t}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T}|\bm{x})=\frac{p_{t|0,T}(\bm{x}|\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})\pi_{0,T}(\bm{x}_{0},\bm{x}_{T})}{p_{t}(\bm{x})}

(6.41)

This minimizer is exactly the probability flow drift and score function of the
mixture of Brownian bridges
. From Section
4.1
Corollary
4.2
, we showed if
\pi^{\star}_{0,T}
is chosen to be the
entropic OT plan
with quadratic transport cost, then this bridge mixture is precisely the Schrödinger bridge, so the learned SDE recovers the Schrödinger bridge
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
.
\square

By leveraging a two-stage framework that first determines the optimal static SB coupling and learning the conditional velocity and score functions, score and flow matching provide a scalable, simulation-free framework for learning Schrödinger bridges. However, a key limitation of this approach is that it requires explicit samples from both the source and target distributions to construct the conditional objectives and endpoint couplings. In many practical settings, such as those that are only known up to an unnormalized density or energy function, explicit samples may not be readily available. This motivates our discussion of alternative approaches that do not rely on paired or explicit samples from the target distribution.

6.5
Schrödinger Bridge with Adjoint Matching

Prerequisite: Section
3

In this section, we will explore how the
adjoint matching
framework
(
domingo2024adjoint
)
has been applied to efficiently solve the Schrödinger bridge problem as described in
Adjoint Schrödinger Bridge Sampler

(
liu2025adjoint
)
.
Adjoint matching
(AM;
domingo2024adjoint
) is a generative modeling framework that efficiently solves the stochastic optimal control (SOC) problem, which has been extended to various applications, including fine-tuning
(
domingo2024adjoint
)
and sampling
(
havens2025adjoint
;
park2025functional
)
. The
adjoint variable
in the context of the Schrödinger bridge problem refers to the gradient of the value function
V_{t}(\bm{x})
which defines the optimal control drift:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=\sigma_{t}\nabla\psi_{t}(\bm{x})=-\sigma_{t}\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})=-\sigma_{t}\nabla J^{\star}(\bm{x},t;u)

(6.42)

Standard methods for solving for the adjoint variable by directly differentiating through the (
SOC Objective
)
(
han2016deep
)
or directly match the target
\nabla J^{\star}(\bm{x},t;u^{\star})
with importance weighted matching objective
(
rubinstein2004cross
;
zhang2014applications
;
domingo2024stochastic
)
, however, adjoint matching introduces a computationally favorable and fundamentally different approach.

Definition 6.5

(Adjoint State)
.

The
adjoint state
, denoted
\bm{a}:C([t,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, of a stochastic optimal control (SOC) problem is defined by taking the gradient of the SOC objective defined in (
SOC Objective
) to get:

\displaystyle\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)=\nabla\left(\int_{t}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}_{s},s)\right)ds+\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})\right)

(6.43)

which yields the gradient field of
J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})
in expectation:

\displaystyle\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(6.44)

The adjoint state can be solved backward in time, given the terminal condition
\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},T;\bm{u})=\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})
by integrating:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bm{u})

\displaystyle=-\left[\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bm{u})^{\top}\left(\nabla(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right)+\nabla\left(c(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}\right)\right]

(6.45)

which yields the alternative form:

\displaystyle\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\int_{t}^{T}\bigg(\nabla(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{s},s)^{\top}\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bm{u})+\sigma_{t}\bm{v}(\bm{X}_{s},s))

\displaystyle+\nabla\bigg(c(\bm{X}_{s},s)+\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\|^{2}\bigg)\bigg)ds+\nabla\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})

(6.46)

Rather than directly matching the target adjoint vector field
-\sigma_{t}\nabla J^{\star}(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}^{\star})
, it considers an objective that matches the vector field generated by the
current control

-\sigma_{t}\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})
, which bypasses the need for importance weighting while obtaining a optimizer gradient that is
equal
, in expectation to that of the target adjoint objective.

Proposition 6.6

(Basic Adjoint Matching Yields the Optimal Control (Proposition 2 in
domingo2024adjoint
))
.

Consider
basic adjoint matching objective
defined with the adjoint state
\bm{a}:C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}(\bm{u}):=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\big\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\nabla\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bar{\bm{u}})\big\|^{2}dt\right],\hskip 9.24994pt\bar{\bm{u}}=\texttt{stopgrad}(\bm{u})

(6.47)

where
\bar{\bm{u}}=\texttt{stopgrad}(\bm{u})
is the control drift where the gradient with respect to
\bm{u}
that generates the path are not tracked, i.e., the path
\bm{X}_{t:T}
cannot be differentiated through. Then,
\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}(\bm{u})
has a
unique
minimizer that is equal to the optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}
.

Proof.
To derive the minimizer of the functional objective (
6.47
), we can compute the first variation of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}
by defining a slightly perturbed control drift
(\bm{u}+\epsilon\bm{v})
, where
\bm{v}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an arbitrary vector field, to get:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{d}{d\epsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}(\bm{u}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\epsilon\bm{v}})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{d}{d\epsilon}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\bm{u}+\epsilon\bm{v})}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\nabla\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bar{\bm{u}})\right\|^{2}dt\right]\bigg|_{\epsilon=0}

(6.48)

Applying the property
\frac{d}{d\epsilon}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{y}+\epsilon\bm{z}\|^{2}\big|_{\epsilon=0}=\frac{1}{2}\left[2(\bm{y}+\epsilon\bm{z})\bm{z}\right]|_{\epsilon=0}=\langle\bm{y},\bm{z}\rangle
, we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{d}{d\epsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}(\bm{u}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\epsilon\bm{v}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{d}{d\epsilon}}\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bar{\bm{u}})}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\epsilon\bm{v}(\bm{X}_{t},t)}\right\|^{2}dt\right]\bigg|_{\epsilon=0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\big\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{v}(\bm{X}_{t},t)},{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bar{\bm{u}})}\big\rangle dt\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x}\sim p_{t}^{\bar{u}}}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\big\langle\bm{v}(\bm{x},t),\underbrace{\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bar{\bm{u}})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]}}_{\text{must vanish point-wise at optimality}}\big\rangle dt\bigg]

(6.49)

where we use the law of total expectation given that only
\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bar{\bm{u}})
depends on the path
\bm{X}_{t:T}
. Given that
\bm{v}(\bm{X}_{t},t)
is
arbitrary
, the only solution where the first variation (
6.49
) is zero is one where the expectation evaluates to zero for
all

\bm{v}
, which occurs if and only if for all
(\bm{x},t)
, the following is satisfied:

\displaystyle\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bar{\bm{u}})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]=0

(6.50)

Therefore, we can write the functional derivative of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}(\bm{u})
with respect to
\bm{u}
evaluated pointwise at
(\bm{x},t)
as:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}(\bm{u})(\bm{x},t)=\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bar{\bm{u}})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(6.51)

where the
critical points
satisfy:

\displaystyle\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)=-\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bar{\bm{u}})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]}\overset{(\ref{eq:adjoint-state-2})}{=}-\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})}

(6.52)

To prove that any
\bm{u}
that satisfies (
6.52
) for
all

(\bm{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]
is the optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}
, we establish the following Lemma.

Proposition 6.7

(Fixed Point Solution to Optimal Control (Lemma 6 in
domingo2024adjoint
))
.

Consider a control drift
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
that satisfies
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)=-\sigma_{t}\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})
for all
(\bm{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]
. Then, we have that the function
J(\cdot,\cdot;\bm{u}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation defined in (
2.72
). Since the HJB equation has a
unique solution
, we can conclude that:

\displaystyle\forall(\bm{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T],\quad J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})=V_{t}(\bm{x})\implies\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\equiv\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=-\sigma_{t}\nabla V_{t}(\bm{x})

(6.53)

Proof.
First, we decompose
J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})
using (
Bellman’s Principle of Optimality
) which states that the optimal cost of time
t
is equal to the incremental cost over
[t,t+\Delta t]
and the cost of time
t+\Delta t
to get:

\displaystyle J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\int_{t+\Delta t}^{T}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}_{s},s)\right)ds+\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}_{s},s)\right)ds}\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}J(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t;\bm{u})}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(6.54)

Subtracting
J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})
from both sides, dividing by
\Delta t
, and taking the continuous time limit
\Delta t\to 0
, we get:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\lim_{\Delta t}\left\{\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[J(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t;\bm{u})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})}}{\Delta t}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\|^{2}+c(\bm{X}_{s},s)\right)ds\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]}{\Delta t}\right\}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathcal{A}^{u}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\|^{2}}+c(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathcal{A}^{u}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})\|^{2}}+c(\bm{x},t)

(6.55)

Expanding the controlled generator
\mathcal{A}^{u}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})
with (
Controlled Generator
), we get:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathcal{A}^{u}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})+\langle\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})+\langle\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle+\langle\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}),\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})+\langle\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle+\langle\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}),-\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})}\rangle+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})+\langle\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\|\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})\|^{2}}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})

(6.56)

Substituting this into (
6.55
) and completing the square, we get:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathcal{A}^{u}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})\|^{2}}+c(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})+\langle\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\|\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})\|^{2}}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})\|^{2}}+c(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})+\langle\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})\|^{2}}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})+c(\bm{x},t)

(6.57)

Rearranging terms, we recover the HJB equation from (
Value HJB Equation
):

\displaystyle\partial_{t}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{-\langle\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}),\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\rangle-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})}_{\text{uncontrolled generator}}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})\|^{2}-c(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathcal{A}_{t}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})\|^{2}-c(\bm{x},t)

(6.58)

and since we define
J(\bm{x},T;\bm{u})=\Phi(\bm{x})
, we have shown that
J(\cdot,\cdot;\bm{u})
satisfies the HJB for all
(\bm{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]
given
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)=-\sigma_{t}\nabla J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})
. By uniqueness of the solution to the HJB, we can conclude that
J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})=V_{t}(\bm{x})
and
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)=\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)
is the optimal control.

\square

The basic adjoint matching objective
\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}
provides a theoretical foundation for the adjoint matching method but remains computationally inefficient as it requires differentiation through the cost functional, which depends on the full trajectory. Since we have shown that
\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)
is the
unique minimizer
of (
6.47
), it can be written as the conditional expectation of the regression target:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u^{\star}}}\left[-\sigma_{t}\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bm{u}^{\star})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(6.59)

At optimality, the forward control and adjoint state should be balanced everywhere, so we can multiply both sides of the equation by the Jacobian
\nabla\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
to get:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)}=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u^{\star}}}\left[-\sigma_{t}\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bm{u}^{\star})^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(6.60)

Since the left hand side depends only on
\bm{x}
, we can rearrange to get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t:T}}[\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)}+\sigma_{t}\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bm{u}^{\star})^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]=0

(6.61)

which shows that
at optimality
, both terms that depend on the control
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
from the adjoint derivative (
6.45
) evaluate to zero. Leveraging this,
domingo2024adjoint
introduces the
lean adjoint state
, which drops the
\bm{u}
-dependent terms to obtain a computationally more efficient objective.

Definition 6.8

(Lean Adjoint State
(
domingo2024adjoint
)
)
.

The
lean adjoint state

\tilde{\bm{a}}:C([t,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined by the following differential equation which can be solved backward in time, given the terminal condition
\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X},T)=\nabla\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})
as:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)=-\big[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)^{\top}\nabla\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\nabla c(\bm{X}_{t},t)\big],\quad\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X},T)=\nabla\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})

(6.62)

Unlike the
adjoint state
defined in Definition
6.5
, the lean adjoint state
\tilde{\bm{a}}
does not depend on the control
\bm{u}
and does not require computing the Jacobian
\nabla\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
.

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)=\int_{t}^{T}\left(\nabla\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{s},s)^{\top}\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{s:T},s)+\nabla\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\right)ds+\nabla c(\bm{X}_{T})

(6.63)

Using this lean adjoint state, we can construct an objective that directly matches the optimal control without requiring explicit computation of the value function or its gradients.

Proposition 6.9

(Lean Adjoint Matching Yields the Optimal Control (Proposition 7 in
(
domingo2024adjoint
)
))
.

Consider the
lean adjoint matching objective
defined with the lean adjoint state
\tilde{\bm{a}}:C([t,T],\mathbb{R}^{d})\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\bm{u}):=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)\right\|^{2}dt\right]

(Lean AM Objective)

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{u}}:=\texttt{stopgrad}(\bm{u})

where
\bar{\bm{u}}:=\texttt{stopgrad}(\bm{u})
is the non-gradient tracking control drift. Then,
\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\bm{u})
has a unique minimizer which is exactly the optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}
.

Proof.
To prove this, we first establish the form of
some
critical point
\hat{\bm{u}}
of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\bm{u})
and show that it is also a critical point of the
basic adjoint matching
loss
\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}
. Then, we can apply the result from Proposition
6.7
to conclude that
\hat{\bm{u}}
is unique and is equal to the optimal control
\hat{\bm{u}}=\bm{u}^{\star}
.

Step 1: Derive the Critical Point.

Since the form of the (
Lean AM Objective
) depends on a random variable
\bm{X}_{0:T}
, to take the functional derivative, we need to evaluate it for some deterministic state
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
. Since
\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)
is the only term in
\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}
that contains randomness in
\bm{X}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}
after fixing
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
, we add and subtract the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]
to the expression inside the square in
\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}
to get:

\displaystyle\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)=\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]}+\underbrace{\sigma_{t}\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]}}_{\text{vanishes when evaluated at a point }\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}}

(6.64)

Then, substituting this expression back into the (
Lean AM Objective
), we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\bm{u})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]}+\sigma_{t}\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]}\right\|^{2}dt\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]}\right\|^{2}\right]dt+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\sigma_{t}(\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T})-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}])}\right\|^{2}\right]dt}_{\text{not dependent on }\bm{u}}

Then, computing the functional derivative
\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\bm{u})(\bm{x},t)
evaluated at
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
, we get:
