\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\bm{u})(\bm{x},t)=\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]}

(6.65)

Since the first variation of
\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\hat{\bm{u}})
of critical points
\hat{\bm{u}}
is zero, we get that the critical points of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\hat{\bm{u}})
satisfy:

\displaystyle\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\hat{\bm{u}})(\bm{x},t)=0\implies\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{x},t)=-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]}

(6.66)

which we will show is also a critical point for the
basic adjoint matching
objective
\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}
in (
6.47
).

Step 2: Matching Critical Point to Basic Adjoint Matching Objective.

Using the same observation for obtaining the
lean adjoint state
, we note that at optimalty, the forward control and adjoint state can be balanced which allows us to multiply both sides of (
6.66
) with the Jacobian
\nabla\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{x},t)
to get:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}_{t},t)}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}-\sigma_{t}\nabla\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}_{t},t)^{\top}}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{t}^{T}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\sigma_{s}\nabla\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}_{s},s)^{\top}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{s:T},s)|\bm{X}_{s}]}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\|^{2}\right)}\right)ds\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}\bigg]=0

(6.67)

Then, adding this zero-expectation balancing condition to the conditional expectation of the lean adjoint state
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]
using the definition in (
6.63
), we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\left(\nabla\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{s},s)^{\top}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{s:T},s)|\bm{X}_{s}]+\nabla\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\right)ds+\nabla c(\bm{X}_{T})\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{t}^{T}\left(\sigma_{s}\nabla\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}_{s},s)^{\top}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{s:T},s)|\bm{X}_{s}]+\nabla\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\|^{2}\right)\right)ds\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}\bigg]}_{\text{zero-expectation balancing condition (\ref{eq:zero-expectation-cond})}}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{t}^{T}\left(\nabla(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{s},s)+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{s}\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}_{s},s)})^{\top}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{s:T},s)|\bm{X}_{s}]+\nabla\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\right)ds

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt+\nabla\left(c(\bm{X}_{T})+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\|^{2}}\right)\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}\bigg]

(6.68)

Since the
adjoint state

\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bm{u})
also solves an equivalent integral equation from (
6.46
) with
arbitrary

\bm{v}
defined as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bm{u})|\bm{X}_{t}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{t}^{T}\left(\nabla(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{s},s)+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{s}\bm{v}(\bm{X}_{s},s)})^{\top}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{s:T},s)|\bm{X}_{s}]+\nabla\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\right)ds

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt+\nabla\bigg(c(\bm{X}_{T})+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{v}(\bm{X}_{s},s)\|^{2}}\bigg)\bigg|\bm{X}_{t}\bigg]

(6.69)

Setting
\bm{v}:=\hat{\bm{u}}
in (
6.69
) yields the
unique
38
38

38

The uniqueness of the integral equation follows the expressing the difference between two arbitrary solutions as an integral inequality and observing that the largest squared distance between the two solutions is bounded by the integral of itself. By Grönwall’s inequality, this means their squared difference must equal zero, and thus the solution is unique. For full proof, see Proposition 8 in
(
domingo2024adjoint
)
.
solution at
\mathbb{E}[\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\hat{\bm{u}})|\bm{X}_{t}]=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Substituting this equality into the functional derivative of the basic adjoint matching loss
\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}(\bm{u})
from (
6.51
), we get:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}(\hat{\bm{u}})(\bm{x},t)

\displaystyle=\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}[\bm{a}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t;\bm{u})|\bm{X}_{t}]}=\hat{\bm{u}}(\bm{x},t)+\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:lean-adjoint-proof1})}{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}-\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]}+\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)|\bm{X}_{t}]=0

(6.70)

which implies that all critical points
\hat{\bm{u}}
of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}
are critical points of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{basic-AM}}
, and by Proposition
6.7
, we conclude that
\hat{\bm{u}}
is
unique
and
equal to the optimal control

\hat{\bm{u}}=\bm{u}^{\star}
.
\square

Given this
general form
of the adjoint matching objective, we can further simplify it for the case where the reference process is pure Brownian motion (
\bm{f}:=0
) and the running cost is zero (
c:=0
), which results in the backward time evolution
\frac{d}{dt}\tilde{\bm{a}}
defined in (
6.62
) to
vanish
. Then, the
lean adjoint state
for all
t\in[0,T]
reduces to the gradient of the terminal cost with respect to the current state given by
\tilde{\bm{a}}(\bm{X}_{t:T},t)=\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})
. Therefore, the
simplified adjoint matching
objective becomes:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{simple-AM}}(\bm{u}):=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})\|^{2}dt\right]

(Simplified AM Objective)

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{u}}=\texttt{stopgrad}(\bm{u})

To further simplify the objective,
havens2025adjoint
introduces the
reciprocal adjoint matching
objective, where rather than taking the expectation over full trajectories
\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}
by repeatedly simulating the controlled SDE, we can leverage the
key property
that given
\bm{f}:=0
,
c:=0
, and
\bm{X}_{0}=0
, the (
Simplified AM Objective
) depends only on
(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{X}_{T})
and the joint distribution of
(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{X}_{T})
under the optimal Schrödinger bridge measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
be factorized as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{X}_{T})=\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\mathbb{P}_{t|T}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{T})=\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\mathbb{Q}_{t|T}(\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{T})

(6.71)

since
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is in the reciprocal class
\mathcal{R}(\mathbb{Q})
, which shares the conditional bridge
\mathbb{P}_{t|T}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{T})=\mathbb{Q}_{t|T}(\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{T})
to the reference process. This gives us the objective:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{RAM}}(\bm{u}):=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{t}\sim\mathbb{Q}_{t|T}^{\bar{u}},\bm{X}_{T}\sim p^{\bar{u}}_{T}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})\|^{2}dt\right]

(Reciprocal AM Loss)

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{u}}=\texttt{stopgrad}(\bm{u})

Crucially, the samples
\bm{X}_{t}\sim\mathbb{Q}_{t|T}
are
independent
conditioned on a terminal state
\bm{X}_{T}\sim p^{\bar{u}}_{T}
, enabling training on arbitrarily many intermediate samples given a single terminal state
\bm{X}_{T}
. To minimize this objective,
havens2025adjoint
proposes an efficient
iterative two-step algorithm
called
adjoint sampling
.

Adjoint Sampling Algorithm

The
adjoint sampling
algorithm
(
havens2025adjoint
)
is an iterative two step procedure:

(i)

First, simulate the forward-time controlled SDE using the non-gradient tracking control
\bar{\bm{u}}
to obtain a fixed set of samples
\bm{X}_{T}\sim p^{\bar{u}}_{T}
and evaluate the gradient of their terminal cost
\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})
. Then, store the set of samples in a replay buffer
\mathcal{B}=\{\bm{X}^{i}_{T},\nabla_{\bm{x}^{i}_{T}}g(\bm{X}^{i}_{T})\}_{i=1}^{B}
.

(ii)

Optimize the (
Reciprocal AM Loss
) by repeatedly sampling intermediate states
\bm{X}_{t}
from the posterior
\bm{X}_{t}\sim\mathbb{Q}_{t|T}
conditioned on samples from the replay buffer
\mathcal{B}
.

This process repeats until the optimal
\bm{u}^{\star}
is reached.

Intuitively, optimizing an arbitrary control
\bm{v}
with (
Reciprocal AM Loss
) can be considered a
reciprocal projection
(Proposition
4.13
), where we constrain the endpoint as
p^{\bar{u}}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})

and
a
Markovian projection
(Proposition
4.10
) by projecting on the Markov bridge measure
\mathbb{Q}_{t|T}(\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{T})
, where
\mathbb{Q}
is the Markov reference Brownian motion.

Proposition 6.10

(Convergence of Adjoint Sampling (Theorem 3.2 in
havens2025adjoint
))
.

Consider optimizing the (
Reciprocal AM Loss
)
\mathcal{L}_{\text{RAM}}
given the current control
\bar{\bm{u}}
as
projecting
an arbitrary control
\bm{v}
onto the bridge
\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=p^{\bar{u}}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})
where
p_{T}^{\bar{u}}
is the target distribution:

\displaystyle\text{proj}(\bm{u})=\underset{\bm{v}}{\arg\min}\text{KL}\left(\mathbb{P}^{v}(\bm{X}_{0:T})\|p^{\bar{u}}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})\right)

(6.72)

Then, at each iteration, we obtain the update:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{n+1}=\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n})-\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n}))

(6.73)

where
\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}
is the functional derivative of the adjoint matching objective
\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}
defined in (
6.65
). The
unique
fixed point
\bm{u}=\text{proj}(\bm{u})=\bm{u}--\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\bm{u})
is exactly the optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}=\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{\star})
.

Proof.

For this proof, we leverage the form of the critical point in (
6.66
) and the functional derivative in (
6.65
).

Step 1: Unifying the Reciprocal Adjoint and Lean Adjoint Matching Objectives.

First, we define a more general form of the reciprocal adjoint matching objective, where the matching target is an arbitrary vector field
\bm{v}
, and the current control is
\bm{u}
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\bm{u};\bm{v})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})\|^{2}dt\right]

(6.74)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{RAM}}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{L}(\bm{u};{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\text{proj}(\bar{\bm{u}})}),\quad\mathcal{L}_{\text{simple-AM}}(\bm{u}):=\mathcal{L}(\bm{u};{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bar{\bm{u}}})

(6.75)

which are equivalent definitions of the (
Reciprocal AM Loss
) and the (
Simplified AM Objective
) given
\bar{\bm{u}}=\texttt{stopgrad}(\bm{u})
.

Step 2: Derive Expression for Adjoint Sampling Iteration.

Given the relationship between the lean adjoint and reciprocal adjoint matching losses defined in (
6.75
), we can apply (
6.66
) to write an optimal iteration of minimizing
\mathcal{L}(\bm{u};\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n}))
as satisfying:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{n+1}(\bm{x},t)=-\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n})}}\left[\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})|\bm{X}=\bm{x}\right]

(6.76)

Since the
\mathcal{L}_{\text{RAM}}
is just
\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}
with the target set to
\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n})
, we also apply (
6.65
) to write the functional derivative of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}
evaluated at
\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n})
as:

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n}))

\displaystyle=\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n})+\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n})}}\left[\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})|\bm{X}=\bm{x}\right]

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{-\sigma_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n})}}\left[\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})|\bm{X}=\bm{x}\right]}_{=:\bm{u}^{n+1}}}

\displaystyle=\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n})-\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n}))

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}^{n+1}(\bm{x},t)}

\displaystyle=\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n})-\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{n}))

(6.77)

which concludes the proof of (
6.73
) in the Proposition.

Step 3: Fixed Point of Adjoint Sampling Iteration.

We will now show that
\bm{u}
is a fixed point of adjoint sampling such that
\bm{u}=\text{proj}(\bm{u})
if and only if
\bm{u}
is a critical point of the (
Lean AM Objective
), which implies that
\bm{u}=\bm{u}^{\star}
by Proposition
6.9
.

First, we show
{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}=\text{proj}(\bm{u})\implies\bm{u}=\bm{u}^{\star}}
. Suppose
\bm{u}=\text{proj}(\bm{u})
. By (
6.73
), we have
\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\text{proj}(\bm{u}))=\frac{\delta}{\delta\bm{u}}\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}(\bm{u})=0
. This means that
\bm{u}
is a critical point of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{AM}}
and by Proposition
6.9
, we have that
\bm{u}
is unique and equal to the optimal control
\bm{u}=\bm{u}^{\star}
.

Next, we show
{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}=\bm{u}^{\star}\implies\bm{u}=\text{proj}(\bm{u})}
. Suppose
\bm{u}=\bm{u}^{\star}
. Then, by Proposition
6.9
, we know
\bm{u}=\bm{u}^{\star}
. Since by definition,
\bm{u}^{\star}
generates the optimal target of the projection
\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0:T})
, projecting onto
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
would yield itself, so
\bm{u}^{\star}=\text{proj}(\bm{u}^{\star})
.
\square

Adjoint sampling (Box
6.5
) provides a theoretically-grounded and computationally efficient method of obtaining the optimal control
\bm{u}^{\star}
when the posterior under the reference dynamics can be easily sampled as
\bm{X}_{t}\sim\mathbb{Q}_{t|T}(\cdot|\bm{X}_{T})
, such as the linear Brownian motion case where
\bm{f}:=0
and the initial distribution is a dirac delta at zero
\pi_{0}:=\delta_{0}
. However, its restriction to the dirac delta prior prevents more general settings with
informative priors
, such as Gaussians or task-specific priors.

Additionally, adjoint matching requires the reference process to be
memoryless
, such that the joint distribution of
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})
can be factorized as
\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})=q_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})q_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})
. This is to prevent the
initial value function bias
described in Box
3.1
, where the optimal joint distribution
\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})
derived in Proposition
3.9
is dependent on an intractable initial value function
V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})=\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})+V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}

(6.78)

Figure 18:
Schrödinger Bridge with Adjoint Matching.
Adjoint matching learns the Schrödinger bridge by alternating between optimizing the forward half-bridge through the adjoint matching loss for
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
and optimizing the backward half-bridge through the corrector matching loss for
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})
.

Although leveraging a memoryless reference drift guarantees sampling paths that generate the true target distribution
\pi_{T}
, it prevents the use of more informative prior distributions for sampling complex target distributions and crucially excludes all tasks where the goal is to map between distributions rather than simply sampling from a target distribution. This motivates using the (
SB-SOC Objective
) introduced in Section
3.2
, which leverages the SB potentials
(\varphi,\hat{\varphi})
defined by:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\star}(\bm{x},t)=-\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x}),\hskip 9.24994ptp_{t}^{\star}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}(\bm{x})

(SB Optimality)

\displaystyle\text{where}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{y}|\bm{x})\varphi_{T}(\bm{y})d\bm{y},&\pi_{0}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{0}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{x})\\
\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{y})d\bm{y},&\pi_{T}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})\end{cases}

Using these equations, we can replace the terminal cost in
\mathcal{L}_{\text{simple-AM}}
(
Simplified AM Objective
) with the definition in (
SB Terminal Cost
) given by
\nabla\Phi(\bm{x})=\nabla\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})}{\pi_{T}(\bm{x})}=\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})-\nabla\log\pi_{T}(\bm{x})
to get the
Schrödinger bridge adjoint matching
(SB-AM) loss.

Definition 6.11

(Schrödinger Bridge Adjoint Matching Objective)
.

The
Schrödinger bridge adjoint matching
(SB-AM) loss which solves the (
SB-SOC Objective
) is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{SB-AM}}(\bm{u}):=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\bar{u}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}(\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\log\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})-\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\log\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T}))\right\|^{2}dt\right]

(SB-AM Objective)

where
\bar{\bm{u}}=\texttt{stopgrad}(\bm{u})
is the non-gradient-tracking control drift.

To tractably compute
\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\log\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x}_{T})
, we define the
bridge-matching or corrector matching objective
(CM), which has been applied to both data-driven
(
shi2023diffusion
;
liu20232
)
and sampling problems
(
liu2025adjoint
)
.

Proposition 6.12

(Corrector Matching Objective)
.

The gradient of the log Schrödinger bridge potential
\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})
can be expressed as the minimizer of the
Schrödinger bridge corrector matching
(SB-CM) loss defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{SB-CM}}(\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}):=\mathbb{E}_{p^{\star}_{0,T}}\left[\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})-\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|0}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{0})\|^{2}\right]

(Corrector Matching Objective)

where the minimizer defines the backward gradient of the log potential:

\displaystyle\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})=\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\star}_{T}=\underset{\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}}{\arg\min}\mathbb{E}_{p^{\star}_{0,T}}\left[\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})-\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|0}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{0})\|^{2}\right]

(6.79)

Proof.
Starting with the definition of
\hat{\varphi}_{t}
from (
SB Optimality
), we have:

\displaystyle\nabla\log{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})}=\frac{1}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})}\nabla\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{y})d\bm{y}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})}\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{y})d\bm{y}=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})}}{p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{y})}d\bm{y}

(6.80)

Recalling from (
SB-SOC Joint Density
) that the joint density between any two timepoints
s\leq t
is given by
p_{s,t}^{\star}(\bm{y},\bm{x})=\mathbb{Q}(\bm{X}_{s}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{s}(\bm{y})\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})
, we observe that we can replace the highlighted terms to get:

\displaystyle\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})\frac{1}{p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})}\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{y})\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})}}_{=:p^{\star}_{0,t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})}d\bm{y}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})\underbrace{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}p^{\star}_{0,t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})}}{p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})}}_{=p^{\star}_{0|t}(\bm{y}|\bm{x})}d\bm{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})p^{\star}_{0|t}(\bm{y}|\bm{x})d\bm{y}=\mathbb{E}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}p^{\star}_{0,t}}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{0})}|{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}}\right]

(6.81)

This can be rewritten as a
regression objective
where pairs
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{t})\sim p^{\star}_{0,t}
and we minimize a parameterized function
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}(\bm{x}):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
that minimizes the square loss:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\star}_{t}:=\underset{h_{t}}{\arg\min}\mathbb{E}_{p^{\star}_{0,t}}\left[\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{X}_{t}|\bm{X}_{0})}\|^{2}\right]

(6.82)

where the minimizer aligns with the true log potential
\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})
. For
t=T
, we obtain the expression
\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})
that appears in
\mathcal{L}_{\text{SB-AM}}
(
SB-AM Objective
) as:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\star}_{T}:=\underset{\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}}{\arg\min}\mathbb{E}_{p^{\star}_{0,T}}\left[\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|0}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{0})}\|^{2}\right]

(6.83)

which is our definition for the (
Corrector Matching Objective
).
\square

Although this provides a concrete variational objective for obtaining
\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})
required to optimize (
SB-AM Objective
), it requires sampling
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\sim p^{\star}_{0,T}
. In the case of sampling from an energy-based distribution, we have no explicit access to the target distribution
p_{T}^{\star}
, which is exactly the challenge addressed by the
adjoint Schrödinger bridge sampler

(
liu2025adjoint
)
algorithm. Rather than sampling pairs from the optimal joint distribution
p^{\star}_{0,T}
, we optimize the corrector
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}
using samples from the joint distribution
p^{\bar{u}}_{0,T}
generated with the frozen control
\bar{\bm{u}}
. However, optimizing
\bm{u}
using
\mathcal{L}_{\text{SB-AM}}
(
SB-AM Objective
) also requires computing
\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\log\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})
, which introduces cross-dependencies between the parameterized variables.

This naturally motivates an
alternating optimization scheme
, that switches between training
\bm{u}
with the parameters of
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}
fixed and optimizing
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}
with the parameters of
\bm{u}
fixed. Concretely, we can interpret this as optimizing a pair of
forward and backward SDEs
that are characterized by the control drift
\bm{u}
in the forward time coordinate
t\in[0,T]
and correction term
\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}=\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{T-s}
in the backward time coordinate
s=T-t\in[0,T]
, respectively:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{u}:\quad d\bm{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\right)dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}

(6.84)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\widehat{Z}}:\quad d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\left[-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},s)+\sigma^{2}_{s}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{T-s}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s})\right]ds+\sigma_{s}d\bm{B}_{s},\quad

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim\pi_{T}

(6.85)

where
\hat{\varphi}_{T-s}
is defined in the forward time coordinate via (
SB Optimality
) with the terminal constraint
\nabla_{\bm{x}_{T}}\log\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x}_{T})=\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}(\bm{x}_{T})
. Then, optimizing both (
SB-AM Objective
) and (
Corrector Matching Objective
) reduces to determining the optimal pair
(\bm{u}^{\star},\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{\star}_{T})
, where the path measures generated by both the forward and backward SDEs align with the Schrödinger bridge path
\mathbb{P}^{u^{\star}}=\mathbb{P}^{\widehat{Z}^{\star}}=\mathbb{P}^{\star}
. Indeed, the alternating optimization scheme achieves this goal, which we will show by establishing that optimizing (
SB-AM Objective
) generates the
optimal forward half-bridge
(Proposition
6.13
) and that optimizing (
Corrector Matching Objective
) generates the
optimal backward half-bridge with from the terminal constraint
(Proposition
6.14
).

Proposition 6.13

(Adjoint Matching Solves the Forward Schrödinger Bridge (Theorem 4.1 in
liu2025adjoint
))
.

Consider optimizing the (
SB-AM Objective
) with respect to the control drift
\bm{u}^{(k)}
at iteration
k
, then the path measure generated by the control
\mathbb{P}^{u^{(k)}}
solves the
forward half bridge
defined by the initial marginal constraint
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{u^{(k)}}=\underset{\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\arg\min}\left\{\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{P}^{\widehat{Z}});\mathbb{P}^{u}_{0}=\pi_{0}\right\}

(6.86)

where
\mathbb{P}^{\widehat{Z}}
is the path measure generated
time-reversed SDE
defined in (
6.85
).

Proof.
The goal of this proof is to show that optimizing the control drift
\bm{u}^{(k)}
using the corrector
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}^{(k-1)}
from the previous iteration solves the
forward half-bridge that is closest in KL divergence to the reverse-time dynamics generated from
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}^{(k-1)}
. To do this, we first define the forward-time dynamics corresponding to the reverse-time SDE induced by
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}^{(k-1)}
. Then, we apply Itô calculus to write the variational KL objective as an Itô integral which reduces the forward half-bridge matching objective to the (
SB-AM Objective
). Throughout the proof, we will denote the density induced by
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}^{(k-1)}
as
p^{\widehat{Z}}\equiv p^{\widehat{Z}^{(k-1)}}
and denote
\hat{z}_{T}
as the potential which generates the gradient field
\widehat{\bm{Z}}=\nabla\log\hat{z}_{T}
39
39

39

While the potential
\hat{z}_{T}
is not the value typically learned in practice, it provides a useful notation for defining the terminal constraint that the dynamics induced by the learned gradient field
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}
.

Step 1: Define the Forward SDE from the Backward Dynamics.

Since the corrector
h^{(k-1)}
defines the reverse-time dynamics via (
6.85
) following the time coordinate
s=T-t
, we can define the corresponding forward-time dynamics for
t
using the (
Time Reversal Formula
) to get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\widehat{Z}}:d\bm{X}_{t}=\bigg[\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{-\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}_{t})}_{(\bigstar)}}\bigg]dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{X}_{T}\sim\pi_{T}

(6.87)

The (
Corrector Matching Objective
) aims to match
\mathbb{P}^{\widehat{Z}}
with a path measure induced by some control drift
\bm{u}
with the forward-time SDE:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{u}:\quad d\bm{X}_{t}=(\bm{f}(\bm{X}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)}_{(\diamond)}})dt+\sigma_{t}d\bm{B}_{t},\quad\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}

(6.88)

which minimizes the KL divergence to
\mathbb{P}^{\widehat{Z}}
. Matching
(\diamond)
to
(\bigstar)
can be expanded using the KL divergence derived via Girsanov’s theorem in Section
2.6
as:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{P}^{\widehat{Z}})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\left\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)+\sigma_{t}\left(\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})-\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t}))\right)\right\|^{2}dt\right]

(6.89)

which depends on the integral of gradient terms
\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})
and
\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}_{t})
. Since we know that the Itô integral evaluates to the difference between boundary conditions, we can rewrite the objective using Itô’s calculus.

Step 2: Apply Itô’s Caclulus to Simplify Integral.

To expand the gradient terms, we can apply (
Itô’s Formula
) to the Itô processes defined by
\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})
and
\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})
which contains the desired gradient term. Since the KL divergence (
6.89
) is respect to the
\mathbb{P}^{u}
, the underlying Itô process follows the SDE in (
6.88
), which we denote as
(\bm{X}_{t}^{u})_{t\in[0,T]}
. First, for
\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})
, we have:

\displaystyle d\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})=

\displaystyle\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})}+(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{u})(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)^{\top}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})\right]dt

\displaystyle+\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})d\bm{B}_{t}

(6.90)

To derive the expression for
{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})}
, we recall that it follows a deterministic integral
\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{y})d\bm{y}
with terminal condition
\hat{\varphi}_{T}=\hat{z}_{T}
. From Section
3.2
, we show that any function applied to a Markov process defined by a terminal constraint satisfies the (
Feynman-Kac Formula
). Given the terminal constraint
\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})=\hat{z}_{T}(\bm{x})
, the (
Feynman-Kac Formula
) for
\hat{\varphi}_{t}
is given by:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})=-\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x}))+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x}),\quad\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})=\hat{z}_{T}(\bm{x})

(6.91)

To obtain
\partial_{t}\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}
from (
6.91
), we apply the chain rule and the divergence property
\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}\hat{\varphi}_{t})=(\nabla\cdot\bm{f})\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\bm{f}^{\top}\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}
to get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}_{t}}=\frac{1}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\left[-\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}\hat{\varphi}_{t})+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}\right]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}((\nabla\cdot\bm{f})\hat{\varphi}_{t}-\bm{f}^{\top}\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t})+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\frac{\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}-\bm{f}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\frac{\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}}_{\text{Laplacian trick}}}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}-\bm{f}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}(\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\|\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\|^{2})}

(6.92)

where the final equality is obtained from applying the Laplacian trick
40
40

40

derived as

\displaystyle\Delta\hat{\varphi}_{t}

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}=\nabla\cdot(\hat{\varphi}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})=\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}\cdot\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\hat{\varphi}_{t}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}

\displaystyle=\hat{\varphi}_{t}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\cdot\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}+\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\right)=\hat{\varphi}_{t}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\|\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\|^{2}}+\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\right)

. Finally, substituting the expression for
\partial_{t}\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}
from (
6.92
) into (
6.90
) and canceling like terms, we get:

\displaystyle d\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left[-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}-\bm{f}^{\top}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}+\|\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\|^{2})+({\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{f}}+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}){{}^{\top}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\right]dt+\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left[-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\|^{2}+(\sigma_{t}\bm{u})^{\top}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\right]dt+\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}

(
\log\hat{\varphi}
-SDE)

Next, we apply Itô’s formula to
\partial_{t}\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u})
:

\displaystyle d\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u})=

\displaystyle\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u})}+(\bm{f}+\sigma_{t}\bm{u})(\bm{X}_{t}^{u},t)^{\top}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u})+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u})\right]dt

\displaystyle+\sigma_{t}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u})d\bm{B}_{t}

(6.93)

In this case, since
p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u})
is defined by the forward-time SDE (
6.87
), we can apply the (
Fokker-Planck Equation
) to write the time-evolution of the density
\partial_{t}p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}_{t}^{u})
as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\bigg({\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{(\bm{f}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\sigma_{t}^{2}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}})}_{\text{drift of the SDE (\ref{eq:sb-am-fwd-proof1})}}}p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}\bigg)+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot((\bm{f}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}})-\nabla\cdot(\sigma_{t}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}_{=\nabla p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}})+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot((\bm{f}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}})-\sigma_{t}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\nabla\cdot({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}})}_{\Delta p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}}+\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot((\bm{f}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}})-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}

(6.94)

Then, we can apply the chain rule to express
\partial_{t}\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{x})
from (
6.94
) as:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}=\frac{1}{p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\left[-\nabla\cdot((\bm{f}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}})-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\Delta p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}\right]}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}\left((p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})+(\bm{f}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})^{\top}\nabla p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}\right)-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\frac{\Delta p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}{p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot(\bm{f}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})-(\bm{f}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\nabla p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}{p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\frac{\Delta p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}{p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}}_{\text{Laplacian trick}}}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}-(\bm{f}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t})^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}(\Delta\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}+\|\nabla\log p^{\widehat{Z}}_{t}\|^{2})}

(6.95)

Finally, substituting the expression for
{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}
from (
6.95
) into (
6.93
) and canceling like terms, we obtain:

\displaystyle d\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}=

\displaystyle\bigg[-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}-({\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{f}}-\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}){{}^{\top}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}}({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\Delta\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}+\|\nabla\log p^{\widehat{Z}}_{t}\|^{2})

\displaystyle+({\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{f}}+\sigma_{t}\bm{u}){{}^{\top}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\Delta\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}\bigg]dt+\sigma_{t}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg[-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}^{\top}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\log p^{h}_{t}\|^{2}+(\sigma_{t}\bm{u})^{\top}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}\bigg]dt

\displaystyle+\sigma_{t}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}d\bm{B}_{t}

(
\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}
-SDE)

Observing that (

log

^
φ

-SDE
) and (

log

p
t

^
Z

-SDE
) have several matching terms, we can cancel them by subtracting (

log

p
t

^
Z

-SDE
) from (

log

^
φ

-SDE
) to get:

\displaystyle d\log\hat{\varphi}

\displaystyle-d\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}=\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\|^{2}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}(\sigma_{t}\bm{u})^{\top}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma_{t}^{2}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}\right]dt+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}d\bm{B}_{t}}

\displaystyle-\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\nabla\cdot\bm{f}}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sigma^{2}_{t}\Delta\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}^{\top}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}-\frac{\sigma_{t}^{2}}{2}\|\nabla\log p^{\widehat{Z}}_{t}\|^{2}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}(\sigma_{t}\bm{u})^{\top}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}\bigg]dt+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\sigma_{t}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}d\bm{B}_{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}(\sigma_{t}\bm{u})^{\top}(\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}-\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}})}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}_{t}}{2}\|\nabla\log p^{\widehat{Z}}_{t}\|^{2}+\sigma^{2}_{t}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}^{\top}\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}\right]dt

\displaystyle+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}d\bm{B}_{t}}

(6.96)

where the terms inside the bracket are almost a perfect square of
\bm{u}+\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}-\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}
. Completing the square with
\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}\|^{2}
, we get:

\displaystyle d\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}-d\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left[\frac{1}{2}\big\|\bm{u}+\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}-\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}\big\|^{2}\right]dt}_{\text{matches the KL divergence objective}}-\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}\|^{2}dt+\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}d\bm{B}_{t}

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1}{2}\big\|\bm{u}+\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}-\nabla\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}\big\|^{2}\right]dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}\|^{2}dt+d\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}-d\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}-\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{t}}{p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}}d\bm{B}_{t}

(6.97)

which recovers the integrand from our KL divergence objective.

Step 3: Rewriting the KL Divergence.

Using (
6.97
), we can rewrite the KL divergence objective in (
6.89
) as:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{P}^{{\widehat{Z}}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\|^{2}+d\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})-d\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})\right)dt-\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})}{p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})}d\bm{B}_{t}}_{=0\text{ (Itô integral)}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}d\log\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})}_{\log\hat{\varphi}_{T}-\log\hat{\varphi}_{0}}}-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}d\log p_{t}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t})}_{\log p^{\widehat{Z}}_{T}-\log p^{\widehat{Z}}_{0}}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt+\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}p_{T}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})}}-\underbrace{\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{X}^{u}_{0})}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}p_{0}^{\widehat{Z}}(\bm{X}^{u}_{0})}}}_{\text{constant}}\bigg]

(6.98)

where the term dependent on
\bm{X}^{u}_{0}
is a constant with respect to
\bm{u}
since we fix the initial distribution at
\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}
. Therefore, substituting
\hat{\varphi}_{T}=\hat{z}_{T}^{(k-1)}
into the objective, we have:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{P}^{\widehat{Z}})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{u}(\bm{X}_{t},t)\|^{2}dt+\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\hat{z}^{(k-1)}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}}{\pi_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}+\text{const}\bigg]

(6.99)

which is proportional to the (
SB-AM Objective
) up to an additive constant, and we conclude that the control
\bm{u}^{(k)}
obtained from minimizing (
SB-AM Objective
) solves the forward half-bridge that minimizes
\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{P}^{\widehat{Z}})
.
\square

However, we have already shown in (
3.1
) that solving the forward half-bridge for arbitrary prior distributions and reference drifts results in a mismatch of the target distribution, which motivated the definition of (
Corrector Matching Objective
) to define the terminal cost as
\Phi(\bm{x}):=\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})}{\pi_{T}(\bm{x})}
. While this definition of the terminal cost provably eliminates the initial value bias as shown in Section
3.2
, we can further show that by optimizing (
Corrector Matching Objective
), we obtain the optimal corrector
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}^{(k)}
that induces the backward half-bridge which is closest in KL to the forward dynamics defined by any arbitrary control
\bm{u}
while correcting for the bias created at the terminal distribution
\pi_{T}
.

Proposition 6.14

(Corrector Matching Solves the Backward Schrödinger Bridge (Theorem 4.2 in
liu2025adjoint
))
.

Consider optimizing the (
Corrector Matching Objective
) with the control drift
\bm{u}^{(k)}
from the
k
th iteration, then the path measure generated by the corrector
\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}^{(k)}
solves the backward half bridge defined by the terminal constraint
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\widehat{Z}}=\underset{\mathbb{P}^{\widehat{Z}}}{\arg\min}\left\{\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u^{(k)}}\|\mathbb{P}):p^{\widehat{Z}}_{T}=\pi_{T}\right\}

(6.100)

where
\mathbb{P}^{u^{(k)}}
is the path measure generated from the learned control at iteration
k
defined in (
6.84
).

Proof.
This proof starts by defining the time-reversal of the forward half-bridge generated by
\bm{u}^{(k)}
from the
k
th iteration of the algorithm and showing that optimizing (
Corrector Matching Objective
) yields the optimal reverse-time dynamics that enforce the terminal constraint.

Step 1: Time Reversal of Forward Controlled SDE.

Given the control drift from the current iteration of the optimization algorithm
\bm{u}^{(k)}
, we can define the corresponding backward SDE following the reversed time coordinate
s:=T-t
using the (
Time Reversal Formula
) to get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{u^{(k)}}:\hskip 9.24994ptd\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\left(-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},s)-\sigma_{s}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{u}^{(k)}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},s)}+\sigma_{s}^{2}\nabla\log{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p^{u^{(k)}}_{s}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s})}\right)ds+\sigma_{s}d\bm{B}_{s},\hskip 9.24994pt\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim\pi_{T}

(6.101)

where the highlighted terms are defined by the control
\bm{u}^{(k)}
. Since we have shown that
\bm{u}^{(k)}
solves the forward half-bridge in Proposition
6.13
, it satisfies the SB equations given by:

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{(k)}_{t}(\bm{x},t)=\sigma_{t}\nabla\log\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{cases}\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{T|t}(\bm{y}|\bm{x})\varphi_{T}(\bm{y})d\bm{y},&\pi_{0}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{0}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{x})\\
\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{Q}_{t|0}(\bm{x}|\bm{y})\hat{\varphi}_{0}(\bm{y})d\bm{y},&{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p^{u^{(k)}}_{T}}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{T}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})\end{cases}

(6.102)

which does not necessarily satisfy the terminal constraint
p_{T}^{\star}=\pi_{T}
. The goal of the (
Corrector Matching Objective
) is to generate the backward half-bridge that minimizes the divergence from the forward half-bridge while constraining the terminal marginal to
p^{\widehat{Z}}_{T}=\pi_{T}
.

Step 2: Deriving the Matching Objective.

To do this, we aim to match some arbitrary control
\bm{v}(\bm{X}_{s},s)
to the time-reversal of the forward half-bridge in (
6.101
) where the initial states
\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim\pi_{T}
are sampled from the target marginal
\pi_{T}
. To define the matching loss as a KL divergence, let
\bm{v}(\bm{x},s)
be an arbitrary control drift defining the reverse-time SDE initialized at
\pi_{T}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{v}:

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=(-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},s)+\sigma_{s}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{v}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},s)})ds+\sigma_{s}d\bm{B}_{s},\hskip 9.24994pt\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\pi_{T}}

(6.103)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{u^{(k)}}:

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=\left(-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},s)+\sigma_{s}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(-\sigma_{s}\nabla\log\varphi_{s}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s})+\sigma_{s}\nabla\log p^{u^{(k)}}_{s}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}))}\right)ds+\sigma_{s}d\bm{B}_{s},\hskip 9.24994pt\tilde{\bm{X}}_{0}\sim{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}p^{u^{(k)}}_{T}}

(6.104)

which we will show matches the result obtained from minimizing (
Corrector Matching Objective
) at optimality. Now, expanding the KL divergence
\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u^{(k)}}\|\mathbb{P}^{v})
as shown in Section
2.6
, we have:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u^{(k)}}\|\mathbb{P}^{v})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u^{(k)}}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{2}\big\|{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(-\sigma_{s}\nabla\log\varphi_{s}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s})+\sigma_{s}\nabla\log p^{u^{(k)}}_{s}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}))}-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{v}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},s)}\big\|^{2}ds\right]

(6.105)

Minimizing (
6.105
) yields for all
(\bm{x},s)
, the following expression for
\bm{v}(\bm{x},s)
:

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{\star}(\bm{x},s)=-\sigma_{s}\nabla\log\varphi_{T-s}(\bm{x})+\sigma_{s}\nabla\log p^{u^{(k)}}_{s}(\bm{x})=\sigma_{s}\nabla\log{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\frac{\varphi_{s}(\bm{x})}{p^{u^{(k)}}_{s}(\bm{x})}}_{=\hat{\varphi}_{T-s}(\bm{x})}}=\sigma_{s}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{T-s}(\bm{x})

(6.106)

where we use the (
SB Optimality
) which defines
p^{u^{(k)}}_{t}(\bm{x})=\varphi_{t}(\bm{x})\hat{\varphi}_{t}(\bm{x})
. Substituting this expression into the backward path measure
\mathbb{P}^{v}
in (
6.103
), we have the
optimal half-bridge is generated by the SDE
:

\displaystyle d\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s}=(-\bm{f}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s},s)+\sigma_{s}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{T-s}(\tilde{\bm{X}}_{s})})ds+\sigma_{s}d\bm{B}_{s},\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\hat{\varphi}_{T}}=\frac{p_{T}^{u^{(k)}}}{\varphi_{T}}

(6.107)

which is
fully characterized
by the terminal condition
\hat{\varphi}_{T}=\frac{p_{T}^{u^{(k)}}}{\varphi_{T}}
from which
\hat{\varphi}_{T-s}
can be defined using (
SB Optimality
). From Proposition
6.12
, we show that optimizing (
Corrector Matching Objective
) yields
\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}_{T}=\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{T}(\bm{x})
given the optimal SB density
p^{\star}_{t}
, so applying the same logic, we have:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{Z}}^{(k)}_{T}:=\underset{\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}}{\arg\min}\mathbb{E}_{p^{u^{(k)}}_{0,T}}\left[\left\|\widehat{\bm{Z}}_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})-\nabla\log\mathbb{Q}_{T|0}(\bm{X}_{T}|\bm{X}_{0})\right\|^{2}\right]\overset{(\ref{prop:sb-correction-match})}{=}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\nabla\log\hat{\varphi}_{T}}

(6.108)

which yields the same backward time SDE through the terminal constraint as the optimal drift
\bm{v}^{\star}
, and we have shown that optimizing (
Corrector Matching Objective
) is
equivalent to finding the optimal reverse-time dynamics that correct the SB forward-time SDE
such that it satisfies the terminal constraint.
\square

The results from Proposition
6.13
and Proposition
6.14
indicate that alternating between optimizing (
SB-AM Objective
) and (
Corrector Matching Objective
) is equivalent to alternating between solving the forward Schrödinger half-bridge that satisfies the initial marginal to solving the backward Schrödinger half-bridge that satisfies the target marginal. This alternating scheme is reminiscent of our discussion of
Sinkhorn’s algorithm
from Section
1.5
, but now adapted using an efficient
matching objective
. Just like the adjoint matching algorithm (Box
6.5
), we can optimize (
SB-AM Objective
) and (
Corrector Matching Objective
) by repeatedly optimizing over samples from a replay buffer.

Remark 6.15

(Adjoint and Corrector Matching Doesn’t Require Target Samples)
.

We highlight that optimizing (
SB-AM Objective
) and (
Corrector Matching Objective
) does not require explicit samples from
\bm{X}_{T}\sim\pi_{T}
since the samples used to compute the objective are purely from sampling
(\bm{X}_{0},\bm{X}_{T})\sim p^{u}_{0,T}
from the SDE induced by
\bm{u}
. The dependence on
\pi_{T}
only appears when computing the loss, which can be computed as a
probability under
\pi_{T}
, which can be empirical or a pre-defined potential energy function, as we will discuss further in Section
8.3
.

Throughout this section, we have demonstrated how the adjoint state can be used as an efficient variational framework for solving Schrödinger bridge problems by alternating between two tractable objectives that correspond to the forward and backward Schrödinger half-bridges. Rather than directly optimizing over path measures, the method reduces the problem to learning the forward control drift and the backward correction through cheap matching objectives that can be evaluated using trajectories generated by the current dynamics. This formulation yields several practical advantages:

(i)

It avoids the need for expensive likelihood ratios or full path-space KL computations, replacing them with local drift-matching losses that are straightforward to estimate.

(ii)

The alternating optimization naturally mirrors the structure of Sinkhorn iterations in entropic optimal transport, providing convergence guarantees to the optimal SB control
\bm{u}^{\star}
.

(iii)

The corrector matching objective does not require explicit samples from the target distribution, enabling training on unknown energy-based target densities.

(iv)

Since the objectives do not require backpropagation through SDE trajectories or maintaining the full trajectory in memory, it can easily scale to high-dimensional systems.

These properties make adjoint matching a practical and flexible approach for learning Schrödinger bridges in complex generative modeling settings.

6.6
Closing Remarks for Section
6

In this section, we explored several generative modeling frameworks grounded in Schrödinger bridge (SB) theory. We began with the classical score-based generative modeling paradigm (Section
6.1
), which can be interpreted as a specialized instance of the SB formulation in which the forward process is fixed, and learning focuses on estimating the reverse-time dynamics. Building on this perspective, we then leveraged SB theory to generalize to controlled forward processes, where both the forward and backward control drifts can be learned through likelihood-based training (Section
6.2
).

Next, we introduced an alternative viewpoint through the Iterative Markovian Fitting (IMF) procedure and the diffusion Schrödinger bridge matching algorithm (Section
6.3
), which draws on the Markov and reciprocal projection theory developed in Section
4.5
. In this formulation, solving the Schrödinger bridge problem can be understood as performing iterative projections in path space.

To overcome the limitations in optimizing over full stochastic trajectories in the path space from the previous approaches, we conclude with two approaches for learning Schrödinger bridges with efficient
matching objectives
, including score and flow matching (Section
6.4
) and adjoint matching (Section
6.5
), which locally optimize the control drift to generate trajectories consistent with the optimal Schrödinger bridge dynamics.

Overall, this section builds the intuition behind the core generative modeling frameworks that leverage Schrödinger bridge theory. While it is not intended to be an exhaustive review of algorithmic developments in the field, it should provide the core theoretical foundations needed to understand a broad class of modern generative modeling techniques.

So far, we have restricted our attention to the
continuous state space
, where data is represented as continuous-valued vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The structure of the continuous state space is
required
for many ideas developed throughout this guide, including stochastic differential equations, path measures, and Brownian motion. This naturally raises the question:
How does Schrödinger bridge theory extend to the
discrete state space
, where states belong to a finite or countable set rather than a continuous vector space?
This is precisely the question that we explore in the next section:

(i)

We will introduce the concept of stochastic processes in the discrete state space as
continuous-time Markov chains
(CTMCs), where the control drift in SDEs takes the analogous form of a
transition rate matrix
in discrete state spaces.

(ii)

While the structure of the discrete Schrödinger bridge problem remains the same, we will introduce fundamental differences in the KL divergence in discrete state spaces. This will provide the theoretical grounding for our discussion on solving the discrete Schrödinger bridge problem with generative modeling, which adapts several ideas developed in the previous sections for the discrete state space.

In doing so, we extend the Schrödinger bridge framework from diffusion processes on continuous spaces to jump processes on discrete state spaces, laying the groundwork for an even broader class of generative modeling methods.

7
From Continuous to Discrete State Space

Now that we have built the foundation required to understand and construct the Schrödinger bridge where the states exist as continuous latent vectors in some state space
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we will now take a detour to the
discrete state space
, where states exist as probabilities of existing in a finite set of discrete states. We will see that rather than representing dynamics with velocity fields that transport states via smooth lines, dynamics in the discrete state spcae are represented with
transition rates
that characterize the instantaneous change in the probabilities of existing in each state.

In this section, we introduce discrete state path measures not as SDEs but as
continuous-time Markov chains
(CTMCs) defined by their rate matrices (Section
7.1
). Leveraging the theory of CTMCs, we define the
discrete Schrödinger bridge problem
, and extend the definitions for the Radon-Nikodym derivative (RND) and KL divergence to CTMCs (Section
7.2
). Then, we analyze two methods of solving the discrete SB problem which mirror the continuous state space, starting with the stochastic optimal control formulation (Section
7.3
and
3.2
) and concluding with the discrete analog of Iterative Markovian Fitting using Markovian and reciprocal projections (Sections
7.5
and
7.6
).

7.1
Continuous-Time Markov Chains

The discrete state space can be defined as a finite set of states
\mathcal{X}=\{1,\dots,d\}
and the probability simplex
\Delta^{d-1}
over the
d
discrete states given by:

\displaystyle\Delta^{d-1}=\left\{\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\bigg|x_{i}\in[0,1],\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}=1\right\}

(7.1)

To define a stochastic path measure
\mathbb{P}
that lies on the simplex
\Delta^{d-1}
, we introduce the theory of
continuous-time Markov chains
(CTMCs) in the probability space
(\Omega,\text{Pr})
, where
\Omega\in D([0,T],\mathcal{X})
is the space of left-limited and right-continuous (cádlág)
41
41

41

piecewise continuous paths with jump discontinuities where the right limit is the actual value.
paths over
\mathcal{X}
and
Pr
is the probability measure over events. A CTMC is a stochastic process that evolves over time
\bm{X}_{0:T}
whose probability law is defined by a time-dependent
generator
or
transition rate matrix

(\bm{Q}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}})_{t\in[0,T]}
of the form:

\displaystyle\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\frac{1}{\Delta t}\left(\text{Pr}(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})-\bm{1}_{\bm{x}=\bm{y}}\right)

(7.2)

which defines the instantaneous rate of transitioning from state
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
to state
\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}
at time
t
. Since the state transitions must remain on the probability simplex, the transition rates satisfy the following conditions:

\displaystyle\forall\bm{x}\neq\bm{y},\quad\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\geq 0,\quad\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=0

(7.3)

A generator
\bm{Q}

uniquely
defines a path measure
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega)
, under which we can define the transition probability over a discrete time interval
[t,t+\Delta t]
as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})=\begin{cases}\Delta t\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})&\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}\\
1-\Delta t\sum_{\bm{z}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{z})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})&\bm{y}=\bm{x}\\
\end{cases}

(7.4)

which can be derived from the definition of the generator as the instantaneous jump rate, and multiplying that by the time interval
\Delta t
for all states
\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}
with
\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})
accounting for the error of multiple jumps within the discrete interval. To ensure that the probabilities are normalized and sum to one, i.e.,
\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})=1
, we subtract all probabilities of leaving the state
\bm{x}
as to get the probability of remaining at
\bm{x}
.

Figure 19:
Piecewise Càdlàg Path of Continuous-Time Markov Chains.
Illustration of a càdlàg stochastic trajectory, which is right-continuous with left limits. Between jump times
t_{1},\dots,t_{5}
the process evolves continuously, while discontinuities occur at the marked times. The filled markers indicate the value of the process
\bm{X}_{t}
(the right limit), while the open markers denote the left limit
\bm{X}_{t^{-}}
.

Given that CTMCs are cádlág paths, we denote the left limit of a state
\bm{X}_{t}
as
\bm{X}_{t^{-}}=\lim_{s\uparrow t}\bm{X}_{s}
, where
\bm{X}_{t^{-}}\neq\bm{X}_{t}
at jump times from state
\bm{X}_{t^{-}}
to state
\bm{X}_{t}
. A
key property
of CTMCs is that they satisfy the
Kolmogorov forward equation
which define the time evolution of the path.

Lemma 7.1

(Kolmogorov Forward Equation for CTMCs)
.

The forward-time dynamics of a CTMC
\bm{X}_{0:T}
with probability measure
p_{t}(\cdot):=\text{Pr}(\bm{X}_{t}=\cdot)
and generator
\bm{Q}_{t}
satisfies the
Kolmogorov forward equation
defined as:

\displaystyle\forall\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X},\quad\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})=\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})p_{t}(\bm{y})=\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}\left(\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{y})-\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})p_{t}(\bm{x})\right)

(7.5)

where that the probability measure
p_{t}(\cdot)
is
unique
given a pair of endpoint conditions at
t\in\{0,T\}
and
t\mapsto\bm{Q}_{t}
is continuous over time
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.
This proposition can be shown simply by defining a forward transition probability over the discrete time increment
[t,t+\Delta t]
and taking a limit as
\Delta t\to 0
to get the expression for
\partial_{t}p_{t}
. First, using (
7.4
) we have:

\displaystyle p_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\text{Pr}(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}=\bm{x}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{y})}p_{t}(\bm{y})

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:ctmc-transition-prob})}{=}\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(\bm{1}_{\bm{x}=\bm{y}}+\Delta t\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}))}p_{t}(\bm{y})

\displaystyle=p_{t}(\bm{x})+\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})

(7.6)

Taking the continuous time limit as
\Delta t\to 0
, we have:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left[\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})\right]=\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{y})

\displaystyle=\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{x})+\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{y})=-\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{y})+\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{y})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}\left(\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})p_{t}(\bm{y})-\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})p_{t}(\bm{x})\right)

(7.7)

which is exactly the Kolmogorov forward equation defined in (
7.5
). To prove
uniqueness
, we can write (
7.7
) in vector form as:

\displaystyle\forall t\in[0,T]:\quad-\partial_{t}\bm{p}_{t}=\bm{Q}_{t}\bm{p}_{t},\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\bm{p}_{t}=(p_{t}(\bm{x}):\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X})\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{X}|}

(7.8)

which is a linear ODE in
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{X}|}
. Since
t\mapsto\bm{Q}_{t}
is continuous, linear ODEs have a unique solution.
\square

Having derived the Kolmogorov forward equation, which describes how the state distribution
p_{t}
evolves
forward
under the time-dependent generator
\bm{Q}_{t}
, we now ask how CTMC dynamics evolve
backwards
from a terminal constraint. As we have seen in the continuous state space, the idea of terminal conditioning is the foundation for solving the Schrödinger bridge problem, as it allows us to define the optimal dynamics that generate a target distribution. The idea of evolving CTMC dynamics backward is exactly captured by the
Kolmogorov backward equation
, which we define next.

Figure 20:
Continuous-Time Markov Chains (CTMC).
A CTMC defines a stochastic process on a finite state space
\mathcal{X}=\{1,\dots d\}
that evolves through jumps between discrete states governed by a time-dependent transition rate matrix
\bm{Q}_{t}
. At each jump time
t_{k}
, the system transitions from the current state
\bm{X}_{t_{k}}
to a different state
\bm{y}
according to the rate
\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{y})
. The evolution of the probability distribution
p_{t}(\bm{x})
satisfies the Kolmogorov forward equation, which balances incoming and outgoing probability flows induced by the transition rates.

Lemma 7.2

(Kolmogorov Backward Equation for CTMCs)
.

Consider a CTMC
\bm{X}_{0:T}
under the measure
\mathbb{P}
with generator
\bm{Q}_{t}
and let
\phi_{t}(\bm{x}):=\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{T})|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]
given an arbitrary test function
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then, the reverse-time dynamics of
\phi_{t}
satisfies the
Kolmogorov backward equation
defined as:

\displaystyle\forall\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X},\hskip 9.24994pt-\partial_{t}\phi(\bm{x})=\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\phi_{t}(\bm{y})\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}(\phi(\bm{y})-\phi(\bm{x}))\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y}),\hskip 9.24994pt\phi_{T}(\bm{x})=\phi(\bm{x})

(7.9)

which admits a
unique
solution
\phi
when
t\mapsto\bm{Q}_{t}
is continuous over all
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.
Since
\phi_{t}(\bm{x})
can be considered some cost function to go from
\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}
to the terminal state
\bm{X}_{T}
, we can expand the inner function
\phi(\bm{X}_{T})
using the law of total expectation to write
\phi_{t}(\bm{x})
with respect to a discrete time step
\phi_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})
:

\displaystyle\phi_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\phi(\bm{X}_{T})}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]=\mathbb{E}[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{T})|\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}]}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]=\mathbb{E}[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\phi_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t})}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]

(7.10)

Then, applying the transition probability of a discrete time step defined in (
7.4
), we have:

\displaystyle\phi_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\phi_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t})}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}]=\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\phi(\bm{y}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{t+\Delta t}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\phi_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{y})\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{1}_{\bm{x}=\bm{y}}+\Delta t\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})}\right)

\displaystyle=\phi_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})+\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\phi_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{y})\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})

(7.11)

Now, subtracting
\phi_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})
from both sides, dividing by
\Delta t
, and taking the continuous time limit
\Delta t\to 0
, we get the reverse time dynamics
-\partial_{t}\phi_{t}(\bm{x})
as:

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}\phi_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left[\frac{\phi_{t}(\bm{x})-\phi_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})}{\Delta t}\right]=\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\phi_{t}(\bm{y})\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\phi_{t}(\bm{y})\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\phi_{t}(\bm{x})\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\phi_{t}(\bm{y})\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\phi_{t}(\bm{x})\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})}=\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}(\phi_{t}(\bm{y})-\phi_{t}(\bm{x}))\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})

(7.12)

which is the Kolmogorov backward equation. To prove
uniqueness
, we follow the proof of the forward equation and write (
7.12
) in vector form as
-\partial_{t}\bm{\phi}_{t}=\bm{Q}_{t}\bm{\phi}_{t}
, which is a linear ODE in
\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{X}|}
. Since
t\mapsto\bm{Q}_{t}
is continuous, linear ODEs have a unique solution.
\square

CTMCs can be interpreted as the
discrete analog of stochastic differential equations (SDEs)
in the continuous state space, where the time-dependent generator conditioned on generator
\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\cdot):\mathcal{X}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is analogous to the time-dependent
drift

\bm{u}(\bm{x},t)
of an SDE. Since we have characterized the forward and reverse evolution of path-measure CTMCs, we are ready to formulate the discrete-state-space analog of the Schrödinger bridge problem, which aims to recover a CTMC that satisfies a pair of marginal distributions while remaining close to a reference CTMC.

7.2
Discrete Schrödinger Bridge Problem

In this section, we formulate the
discrete Schrödinger bridge problem
for continuous-time Markov chains (CTMCs) on a finite state space
\mathcal{X}=\{1,\dots,d\}
. Just like the continuous-space Schrödinger bridge problem, the objective is to find a path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
that is closest, in relative entropy or KL divergence, to a given reference process
\mathbb{Q}
, while matching prescribed marginal constraints
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
.

Since the
control drift
in continuous SB theory is denoted
\bm{u}(\bm{x},t):\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we use
\bm{Q}^{u}_{t}(\bm{x},\cdot):\mathcal{X}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
to denote the CTMC generator of the
controlled path measure

\mathbb{P}^{u}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathcal{X}))
, which we aim to optimize to recover the Schrödinger bridge. To denote the generator of the
reference process

\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathcal{X}))
, we use
\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\cdot):\mathcal{X}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.

Definition 7.3

(Discrete Schrödinger Bridge Problem)
.

Consider a reference CTMC measure
\mathbb{Q}
with generator
\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}
and a pair of marginal distributions in the discrete state space
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
. The
discrete Schrödinger bridge problem
aims to determine the optimal CTMC path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
with generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}
where
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
p_{T}=\pi_{T}
that solves the minimization problem:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\star}=\underset{\mathbb{P}^{u}\in\mathcal{P}(C([0,T];\mathcal{X})}{\arg\min}\big\{\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{Q}):p_{0}=\pi_{0},p_{T}=\pi_{T}\big\}

(Discrete SB Problem)

where the KL divergence
\text{KL}(\cdot\|\cdot)
between CTMCs is defined as:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{Q})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\int_{0}^{T}\left[\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left(\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}\log\frac{\bm{Q}^{u}_{t}}{\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}}+\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}-\bm{Q}^{u}_{t}\right)(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})\right]dt

(7.13)

Solving the discrete SB problem yields the CTMC path measure that minimizes the discrepancy from the reference jump process, which penalizes differences in jump intensities, jump times, and transition structure over the time interval
t\in[0,T]
.

To define the form of the KL divergence, we first derive the
Radon-Nikodym derivative between CTMC path measures
, which defines the probability ratio of a CTMC
\bm{X}_{0:T}
under two path measures
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
with
generators

\bm{Q}_{t}
and
\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}
.

Proposition 7.4

(Radon-Nikodym Derivative Between CTMCs)
.

Consider two CTMC path measures
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
with generators
\bm{Q}
and
\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}
and initial distributions
p_{0}=\pi_{0}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{0}=\pi_{0}^{\prime}
. Assume that the
\pi^{\prime}_{0}\ll\pi_{0}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\ll\mathbb{P}
, where the generators satisfy
\bm{Q}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=0\implies\bm{Q}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=0
. Then, the logarithm of the Radon-Nikodym derivative is given by:

\displaystyle\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}}(\bm{X}_{0})+\sum_{t:\bm{X}_{t^{-}}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}},\bm{X}_{t})}{\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}},\bm{X}_{t})}+\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}(\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t})(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})\mathrm{d}t

(7.14)

Proof.
To derive the RND for CTMCs, we first consider the
discrete-time case
, where we break down the time horizon into time steps
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}<\dots<t_{K-1}<t_{K}=T
where the time intervals are separated by
\Delta t
. Then, we can write the log ratio of a discrete path
(\bm{X}_{t_{k}})_{k\in\{0,\dots,K\}}
under the probability measures
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
as:

\displaystyle\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}}(\bm{X}_{0})+\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{X}_{t_{k}})}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{X}_{t_{k}})}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t)

(7.15)

From (
7.4
), we can derive the probability ratio for two distinct cases given an interval
[t_{k},t_{k+1}]
: the case of at least one change in state in
[t_{k},t_{k+1}]
and the case of no change in state in
[t_{k},t_{k+1}]
. First, for the case when at least one jump is made, the log ratio becomes:

\displaystyle\log\frac{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{X}_{t_{k}})}{\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{X}_{t_{k}})}=\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\Delta t\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\Delta t\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})}}=\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}})}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}})}}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})

(7.16)

For the case when no jumps are made, the log ratio becomes:

\displaystyle\log\frac{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{X}_{t_{k}})}{\mathbb{P}(\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{X}_{t_{k}})}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}1-\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t_{k}}}\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}1-\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t_{k}}}\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})}}

\displaystyle=\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t_{k}}}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{y})}-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{y})}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})

(7.17)

Substituting (
7.16
) and (
7.17
) into (
7.15
) and taking the continuous time limit
\Delta t\to 0
and
K\to\infty
, we have:

\displaystyle\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\bigg\{\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}}(\bm{X}_{0})+\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\log\frac{\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}})}{\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}})}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t_{k}}}\left(\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{y})-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{y})}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t)\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}}(\bm{X}_{0})+\sum_{t:\bm{X}_{t^{-}}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}},\bm{X}_{t})}{\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}},\bm{X}_{t})}+\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}(\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t})(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})\mathrm{d}t

(7.18)

which is the exact form of the log RND between
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{P}
.
\square

Using this result, we can easily derive the
KL divergence between CTMC path measures
, which is the foundation of the discrete Schrödinger bridge objective.

Corollary 7.5

(KL Divergence Between CTMCs)
.

The KL divergence between two CTMC path measures
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
with generators
\bm{Q}
and
\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\|\mathbb{P})=\text{KL}(\pi_{0}^{\prime}\|\pi_{0})+\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{T}\left[\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left(\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}\log\frac{\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t}}{\bm{Q}_{t}}+\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}\right)(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})\right]dt

(7.19)

and can be equivalently written as:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\|\mathbb{P})

\displaystyle=\text{KL}(\pi^{\prime}_{0}\|\pi_{0})+\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{T}\left[\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left(\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}}{\bm{Q}_{t}}\right)(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})+(\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t})(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{X}_{t})\right]dt

(7.20)

Proof.
Recalling that the KL divergence
\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\|\mathbb{P})
is simply the expectation of the log RND over paths from the first argument, we have:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\|\mathbb{P})=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}}(\bm{X}_{0})+\sum_{t:\bm{X}_{t^{-}}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}},\bm{X}_{t})}{\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}},\bm{X}_{t})}+\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}(\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t})(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})dt}\right]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}}(\bm{X}_{0})\right]}_{(\bigstar)}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{t:\bm{X}_{t^{-}}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}},\bm{X}_{t})}{\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}},\bm{X}_{t})}\right]}_{(\blacklozenge)}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}(\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t})(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})dt\right]}_{(\diamond)}

(7.21)

The first term (
\bigstar
) can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}}\underbrace{\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}}(\bm{X}_{0})\right]}_{\text{only depends on }\bm{X}_{0}}=\mathbb{E}_{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{X}_{0}\sim\pi^{\prime}_{0}}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\pi_{0}}(\bm{X}_{0})\right]=\text{KL}(\pi^{\prime}_{0}\|\pi_{0})

(
\bigstar
)

To write the second term (
\blacklozenge
) as an integral, we can consider the discrete time case for
0=t_{0}<\dots<t_{k}<\dots<t_{K}=T
, distribute the expectation, and take the continuous time limit as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(\bm{X}_{t_{k}})_{k\in\{0,\dots,K\}}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\bigg[

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{1}[\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}\neq\bm{X}_{t_{k}}]}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}})}{\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}})}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}}),\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}|\bm{X}_{t_{k}})}}\left[\bm{1}[\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}\neq\bm{X}_{t_{k}}]\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}})}{\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}})}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}})}}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t_{k}}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\bm{y}|\bm{X}_{t_{k}})}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{y})}{\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{y})}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}})}}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t_{k}}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\Delta t\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{y})}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}^{\prime}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{y})}{\bm{Q}_{t_{k}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}},\bm{y})}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})}\right]

\displaystyle\underset{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\Delta t\to 0}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left(\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}}{\bm{Q}_{t}}\right)(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})\right]

(
\blacklozenge
)

Plugging these expressions for (
\bigstar
) and (
\blacklozenge
) into (
7.21
), we get:
