\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\|\mathbb{P})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\text{KL}(\pi^{\prime}_{0}\|\pi_{0})}_{(\bigstar)}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}}{\bm{Q}_{t}}}\right)(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})dt\right]}_{(\blacklozenge)}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t}})(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})dt\right]}_{(\diamond)}

\displaystyle=\text{KL}(\pi^{\prime}_{0}\|\pi_{0})+\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}}{\bm{Q}_{t}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t}}\right)(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})\right]dt

\displaystyle=\text{KL}(\pi^{\prime}_{0}\|\pi_{0})+\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}}{\bm{Q}_{t}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t}}\right)(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})\right]dt

(7.22)

which is the simplified KL divergence between
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{P}
. We can also use the equality
\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{x})=1-\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
to write
\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}(\bm{Q}_{t}-\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t})(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})=(\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t})(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{X}_{t})
, which gives us:

\displaystyle\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\|\mathbb{P})

\displaystyle=\text{KL}(\pi^{\prime}_{0}\|\pi_{0})+\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{T}\left[\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{\prime}}{\bm{Q}_{t}}}\right)(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})+\int_{0}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}(\bm{Q}^{\prime}_{t}-\bm{Q}_{t})(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{X}_{t})}\right]dt

(7.23)

which is an equivalent expression for KL divergence between CTMCs using the rate of remaining at a position or the
stay rate
.
\square

Figure 21:
Discrete Schrödinger Bridge Problem.
The discrete SB problem seeks a controlled CTMC path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
that is closest, in KL divergence, to a reference CTMC
\mathbb{Q}
while matching the prescribed marginal distributions
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
.
Top
: CTMC trajectories under the sub-optimal controlled path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
with generator
\bm{Q}^{u}
.
Bottom
: CTMC trajectories under the Schrödinger bridge path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
with generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}
obtained by minimizing the KL divergence with the reference generator
\bm{Q}^{0}
while maintaining the terminal marginals.

This decomposition reveals that the KL divergence between two CTMC path measures is the sum of the discrepancy between their initial marginals and a time-integrated KL divergence of the generator matrices at
jump times
, where the state
\bm{X}_{t}
jumps to a new state
\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}
. This provides an intuitive interpretation for the discrete Schrödinger bridge problem, which selects, among all Markov processes matching the marginals, the one whose jump dynamics deviates minimally in KL divergence from the reference dynamics. While we defined the canonical reference process in the continuous state space as Brownian motion with a reference drift
\bm{f}
, which is often set to
\bm{f}:=0
, we can also define two common forms of the reference generator
\bm{Q}^{0}
below.

Forms of the Reference Generator for CTMCs

The
reference generator

\bm{Q}^{0}
defining the CTMC
\mathbb{Q}
is the baseline discrete stochastic dynamics that is minimally reweighted to match the prescribed marginal constraints. The choice of
\bm{Q}^{0}
fundamentally changes the solution to the (
Discrete SB Problem
). Here, we will highlight the
uniform generator
and the
pre-trained generator
as common choices.

Uniform Generator.
The uniform generator
\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=\begin{cases}\frac{\gamma(t)}{|\mathcal{X}|-1}&\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}\\
-\gamma(t)&\bm{y}=\bm{x}\end{cases}

(Uniform Generator)

which corresponds to a homogeneous jump process such that from any state
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
at time
t
, the state remains unchanged with rate
-\gamma(t)
and jumps uniformly to any other state
\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}
. This means that the optimal generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}
is entirely determined by the marginal constraints. It can be interpreted as the discrete state space analog of pure Brownian motion and is useful for settings where prior dynamics are unknown.

Baseline Transition Generator.
A more structured alternative is to define the reference generator
\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}
with baseline transition rates of the system’s natural dynamics or from a
pretrained model
, where the model is trained to capture the dynamics of the system from data samples. This choice embeds prior knowledge about plausible transitions into the reference process. The Schrödinger bridge then acts as a minimal correction of the pretrained dynamics to match the prescribed marginals.

While we have defined the (
Discrete SB Problem
) objective and provided common forms of the reference generator, it remains unknown how to optimize the generator such that it yields the optimal CTMC while preserving the marginal constraints. In the remainder of Section
7
, we introduce two concrete methods of solving the discrete SB problem, each of which has deep connections to methods in the continuous state space. Specifically, we introduce
stochastic optimal control (SOC) of CTMCs
, which extends the SOC ideas from Section
3
to the discrete state space, and
discrete diffusion Schrödinger bridge matching
, which extends the ideas of Markov and reciprocal projections from Section
4.5
and diffusion Schrödinger bridge matching algorithm from Section
6.3
to the discrete state space.

7.3
Stochastic Optimal Control of CTMCs

Prerequisite: Section
3

Just like in the continuous state space, we can reframe the SB problem as a
stochastic optimal control
(SOC) problem, where the Schrödinger bridge aligns with the lowest cost path from any intermediate state to a state in the target distribution. In this section, we build the theoretical foundations of SOC in the discrete state space, highlighting the deviations from the continuous state-spacee formulation, which will lead us to explicitly defining objectives for tractably solving the discrete SB problem with SOC in Section
7.4
.

First, we define the
cost functional

J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}):\mathcal{X}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
which returns the
cost-to-go
from a intermediate state
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
to the target distribution
\pi_{T}
under the
controlled
path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
:

\displaystyle J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u})

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}^{u}_{s}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}C_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},\bm{y})}ds+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})}\bigg|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

(7.24)

which measures the expected cumulative running cost
C_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
incurred from time
t
until the terminal time
T
and the terminal cost
\Phi(\bm{x}):\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
. The running cost is defined as the instantaneous KL divergence between the controlled generator
\bm{Q}^{u}_{t}
and the reference generator
\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}
given by:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}C_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})}

\displaystyle:=\text{KL}(p^{u}_{t}\|q_{t})=\left(\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}}{\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}+\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}\right)(\bm{x},\bm{y})

(7.25)

The objective of the SOC problem is to determine the
optimal control
generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}:=\bm{Q}^{u^{\star}}
that minimizes the cost-to-go functional:

\displaystyle J^{\star}(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}^{\star}):=\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}J(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}),\quad\forall(\bm{x},t)\in\mathcal{X}\times[0,T]

(7.26)

which generates the
optimal CTMC path measure

\mathbb{P}^{\star}:=\mathbb{P}^{u^{\star}}
with generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}:=\bm{Q}^{u^{\star}}_{t}
. Analogous to the continuous setting, the optimal cost-to-go satisfies (
Bellman’s Principle of Optimality
). For a small time increment
\Delta t
, the cost decomposes into the cost accumulated over
[t,t+\Delta t]
and the optimal cost from
t+\Delta t
onward:

\displaystyle J^{\star}(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}^{\star})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\int_{t}^{t+\Delta t}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\int_{t+\Delta t}^{T}}\right)\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}^{u}_{s}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}C_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{s},\bm{y})}ds+\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})\bigg|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg\{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}C_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})}_{\text{optimal cost of jumps over }t\to t+\Delta t}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[J^{\star}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t},t+\Delta t)|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]}_{\text{optimal cost of all jumps over }t+\Delta t\to T}}\bigg\}

(7.27)

The optimally controlled measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
can be obtained by defining the
value function

V_{t}
as the
optimal cost-to-go

V_{t}(\bm{x}):=J^{\star}(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}^{\star})
, which yields the
dynamic programming relation
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}V_{t}(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg\{\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}C_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})+\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t})}|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]\bigg\}

(7.28)

Since no more running cost
C_{t}
can be incurred at time
t=T
, the terminal value function is equal to the terminal cost
V_{T}(\bm{x})=\Phi(\bm{x})
.

To obtain an explicit characterization of the optimal CTMC dynamics solving the stochastic optimal control problem in (
7.27
), we derive the structure of the optimal process in a sequence of steps. The key goal is to connect the dynamic programming formulation of the SOC problem with the change-of-measure perspective underlying Schrödinger bridges. Concretely, we first determine the form of the optimal controlled generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}
, then characterize the value function through the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation, and finally use these results to recover the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
and its likelihood ratio with respect to the reference process.
The derivation proceeds as follows:

(i)

We derive the form of the optimal generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}
that defines the law of paths under the path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
that solves the SOC problem in (
7.27
) (Proposition
7.6
).

(ii)

We show that the value function in (
7.28
) satisfies the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation (Corollary
7.8
).

(iv)

We derive the form of the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
that solves the SOC problem (Proposition
7.9
).

(iii)

Finally, we derive the Radon-Nikodym derivative (RND) between the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
, and the reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
(Proposition
7.10
).

Together, these theoretical ideas will form the basis for solving the discrete SB using SOC. We start with the derivation of the optimal generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}
with respect to the value function
V_{t}
(
7.28
) which will naturally lead to the proof that the value function satisfies the HJB equation.

Proposition 7.6

(Optimal Generator)
.

Given the generator
\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}
of the reference process
\mathbb{Q}
, the
optimal generator

\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}
of the process
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
that solves the SOC problem in (
7.27
) takes the form:

\displaystyle\bm{Q}_{t}^{\star}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})},\quad\forall\bm{x},\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}

(Optimal Generator)

where
V_{t}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is the value function defined in (
7.28
).

Proof.
First, we expand the second term of the value function in (
7.28
), which defines the Bellman recursion, to get:

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\;

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t})\big|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]=\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{y}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\mathbb{P}^{u}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t}=\bm{y}|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x})}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:ctmc-transition-prob})}{=}\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\sum_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{y}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\left(\bm{1}_{\bm{x}=y}+\Delta t\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})(\bm{1}_{\bm{x}=y}+\Delta t\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}(\bm{x},\bm{x}))}_{\text{Case: }\bm{y}=\bm{x}}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{y})\Delta t\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}(\bm{x},\bm{y})}_{\text{Case: }\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}))\bigg]

(7.29)

For any CTMC generator, the sum of transition rates from a given state
\bm{x}
to all states
\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}
must sum to zero, we have
\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{x})=-\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
, which we can substitute into (
7.29
) to get:

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\;

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\big[V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t+\Delta t})\big|\bm{X}^{u}_{t}=\bm{x}\big]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})\left(\bm{1}_{\bm{x}=y}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-}\Delta t{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}^{u}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})}\right)+\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{y})\Delta t\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}))\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\Delta t\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}(\bm{x},\bm{y})}+\Delta t\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{y})\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})\right]

\displaystyle=V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})+\Delta t\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}(\bm{x},\bm{y}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}(V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{y})-V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x}))}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})

(7.30)

Substituting this back into (
7.28
), we have:

\displaystyle V_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg\{\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}C_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}+V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})+\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}(\bm{x},\bm{y})(V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{y})-V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x}))+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})}\bigg\}

\displaystyle V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg\{\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}C_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}(\bm{x},\bm{y})(V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{y})-V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x}))\bigg\}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})

\displaystyle V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg\{\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}C_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\Delta t\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}(\bm{x},\bm{y})(V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{y})-V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x}))\bigg\}+\mathcal{O}(\Delta t^{2})

(7.31)

where we rearrange the Bellman recursion to express the forward difference
V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{x})
, which introduces a minus sign before the infimum. This expression is used to get the time derivative
\partial_{t}V_{t}
, by dividing both sides by
\Delta t
and take the limit
\Delta t\to 0
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})=\lim_{\Delta t\to 0}\left\{\frac{V_{t+\Delta t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{x})}{\Delta t}\right\}

\displaystyle=-\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg\{\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\bigg(C_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}(\bm{x},\bm{y})(V_{t}(\bm{y})-V_{t}(\bm{x}))\bigg)}_{f(\bm{Q}^{u})}}\bigg\}

(7.32)

Defining
f(\bm{Q}_{t}^{u})
as the function inside the infimum for all
\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}
and expanding
C_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})
as the KL divergence in (
7.27
), we can take the derivative
f^{\prime}(\bm{Q}_{t}^{u})
with respect to
\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}
to get:

\displaystyle f(\bm{Q}^{u}_{t})

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{Q}^{u}_{t}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}}{\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}}\right)(\bm{x},\bm{y})-\bm{Q}^{u}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+\bm{Q}^{u}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})(V_{t}(\bm{y})-V_{t}(\bm{x}))

(7.33)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\bm{Q}^{u}_{t})

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}}{\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}}(\bm{x},\bm{y})+V_{t}(\bm{y})-V_{t}(\bm{x})

(7.34)

Setting
f^{\prime}(\bm{Q}^{u}_{t})=0
to obtain the minimizer
\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}
:

\displaystyle\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{\star}}{\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})\implies\boxed{\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}}

(7.35)

which is the form of the optimal generator.
\square

From the result (
7.35
), we can also observe that by rewriting the exponential as a fraction, we recover a form analogous the
Doob’s
h
-transform
described in Section
4.4
for CTMCs.

Remark 7.7

(Doob’s
h
-Transform of CTMCs)
.

The optimal generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}
is the Doob
h
-transform of the reference generator
\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}
where the
h
function is defined as the exponentiated value function
h(\bm{x},t):=e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}
:

\displaystyle\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\frac{e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})}}{e^{V_{t}(\bm{y})}}=\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\frac{e^{-V_{t}(\bm{y})}}{e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}}=:\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\frac{h(\bm{y},t)}{h(\bm{x},t)}

(7.36)

which can be interpreted as tilting the generator toward states
\bm{y}
that minimize the optimal cost-to-go defined by
V_{t}(\bm{x}):=J^{\star}(\bm{x},t;\bm{u}^{\star})
.

Now we will show that the value function satisfies the HJB equation, which is analagous to our derivation in Section
2.7
in continuous state spaces. Crucially, this defines the discrete SOC problem with a non-linear PDE which can be transformed to a linear equation via exponentiation, which acts as a
discrete analog of the Hopf-Cole transform
discussed in Section
2.8
.

Corollary 7.8

(Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman Equation)
.

The value function in (
7.28
) satisfies the
Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equations
, defined as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})=\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\left(e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}-1\right)\iff\partial_{t}e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}=\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\left(e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}-e^{-V_{t}(\bm{y})}\right)

Proof.
To prove this, we substitute the final form of the optimal generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}=\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}
defined in (
Optimal Generator
) into the equation for
\partial_{t}V_{t}
in (
7.32
) to get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})=-\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}}\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}}}{\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}}-\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}+\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}}(V_{t}(\bm{y})-V_{t}(\bm{x}))\bigg]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}}\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}}}{\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}}-\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}+\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}}(V_{t}(\bm{y})-V_{t}(\bm{x}))\bigg]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}-\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}(V_{t}(\bm{y})-V_{t}(\bm{x}))}-\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}e^{V_{t}(\bm{y})-V_{t}(\bm{x})}+\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}+{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}(V_{t}(\bm{y})-V_{t}(\bm{x}))}\bigg]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}\left(1-e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}\right)=\boxed{\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}\left(e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}-1\right)}

(7.37)

which gives us the first HJB equation in the Corollary. To get the second expression, we can differentiate
e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}
and apply the chain rule to get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}=e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{eq:ctmc-partial-t-value})}{=}e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\left(e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}-1\right)}

\displaystyle=\boxed{\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{x}}\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\left(e^{-V_{t}(\bm{y})}-e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}\right)}

(7.38)

which is the second HJB equation in the Corollary.
\square

Using this Corollary, we can derive the
optimal path measure
, which can be defined in terms of the value function
V_{t}(\bm{x})
.

Proposition 7.9

(Optimal Path Measure)
.

The optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
given the value function
V_{t}(\bm{x})
can be expressed as:

\displaystyle p_{t}^{\star}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{Z_{t}}q_{t}(\bm{x})e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})},\quad Z_{t}:=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x}\sim q_{t}}\left[e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}\right]

(7.39)

Proof.
From Remark
7.7
, we can consider the probability of a state
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
under the optimal path measure as its probability under the reference measure tilted by the
h
-function defined as
h(\bm{x},t):=e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}
which yields
\xi_{t}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{Z}q_{t}(\bm{x})e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}
, where
Z
is the normalization factor. To show that
\xi(\bm{x})
is indeed the optimal path measure, we must check that it satisfies
Kolmogorov’s forward equation
defined in Proposition
7.1
for the optimal generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}
. First, taking the partial derivative, we get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\xi_{t}(\bm{x})=\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{Z}q_{t}(\bm{x})e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}\right)=\frac{1}{Z}\left(e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}q_{t}(\bm{x})}+q_{t}(\bm{x}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\partial_{t}e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}}\right)

(7.40)

Applying the Kolgomorov forward equation from Lemma
7.1
to the reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
and the HJB equations from Corollary
7.8
, we have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}p_{t}^{0}(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{lemma:ctmc-kolmogorov-fwd})}{=}\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}\left(\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})q_{t}(\bm{y})-\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}(\bm{x},\bm{y})q_{t}(\bm{x})\right)

(7.41)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\partial_{t}e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}}

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{corollary:ctmc-hjb})}{=}\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\left(e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}-e^{-V_{t}(\bm{y})}\right)

(7.42)

and substituting this back into (
7.40
), we get:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\xi_{t}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{Z}\left[e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}\left(\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})q_{t}(\bm{y})-\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}(\bm{x},\bm{y})q_{t}(\bm{x})\right)}+q_{t}(\bm{x}){\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\left(e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}-e^{-V_{t}(\bm{y})}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}\bigg[\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})\frac{1}{Z}q_{t}(\bm{y}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}}\underbrace{-\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\frac{1}{Z}q_{t}(\bm{x})e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}+\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\frac{1}{Z}q_{t}(\bm{x})e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}}_{=0}-\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\frac{1}{Z}q_{t}(\bm{x}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}e^{-V_{t}(\bm{y})}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}\bigg[\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\frac{1}{Z}q_{t}(\bm{y})e^{-V_{t}(\bm{y})}}_{=:\xi_{t}(\bm{y)}}}e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}-\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y}){\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\underbrace{\frac{1}{Z}q_{t}(\bm{x})e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}}_{=:\xi_{t}(\bm{x})}}e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}\bigg[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}}_{=:\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})}}\xi_{t}(\bm{y})-{\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\underbrace{\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}}_{=:\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})}}\xi_{t}(\bm{x})\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{x}\neq\bm{y}}\left(\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}(\bm{y},\bm{x})\xi_{t}(\bm{y})-\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\xi_{t}(\bm{x})\right)

(7.43)

which is exactly the Kolmogorov forward equation for the generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}
that defines the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
. Since we prove in Lemma
7.1
that the solution to the Kolmogorov forward equation is
unique
, we have shown that
p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{Z}q_{t}(\bm{x})e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}
. We derive
Z_{t}
such that the probability distribution is normalized, i.e.
\sum_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=1
, as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}Z_{t}}}\sum_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}q_{t}(\bm{x})e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}=1\implies{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}Z_{t}}=\sum_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}q_{t}(\bm{x})e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x}\sim q_{t}}\left[e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}\right]

(7.44)

which concludes our proof of the optimal path measure.
\square

Using this form of the optimal path measure
p^{\star}_{t}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{Z}q_{t}(\bm{x})e^{-V_{t}(\bm{x})}
, we can now derive the RND between the optimal and reference path measures.

Proposition 7.10

(Radon-Nikodym Derivative of Optimal and Reference Path Measure)
.

The
Radon-Nikodym Derivative
(RND) of the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
with generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t}
and the reference path measure
\mathbb{Q}
with generator
\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}
is given by:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\frac{1}{Z}e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})},\quad Z:=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{x}\sim q_{T}}\left[e^{-\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})}\right]

(Optimal RND)

where
\Phi(\bm{x}):\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is the terminal cost function.

Proof.
Starting from the definition of RND between two CTMC path measures in Proposition
7.4
, we can write:

\displaystyle\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\log\frac{p_{0}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{0})}{q_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}+\sum_{t:\bm{X}_{t^{-}}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{\star}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}},\bm{X}_{t})}{\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}},\bm{X}_{t})}+\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}(\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}-\bm{Q}^{\star}_{t})(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})\mathrm{d}t

(7.45)

Now, we can substitute the expression for the optimal path probability
p^{\star}_{0}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{Z}q_{0}(\bm{x})e^{-V_{0}(\bm{x})}
from Proposition
7.9
and the optimal generator
\bm{Q}^{\star}(\bm{x},\bm{y})=\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y})e^{V_{t}(\bm{x})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}
from Proposition
7.6
to get:

\displaystyle\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})

\displaystyle=\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\frac{1}{Z_{0}}q_{0}e^{-V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}}}{q_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})}+\sum_{t:\bm{X}_{t^{-}}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\log\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}},\bm{X}_{t})e^{V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}})-V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})}}}{\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}},\bm{X}_{t})}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left(\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})e^{V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}}\right)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})-\log

\displaystyle Z_{0}+\sum_{t:\bm{X}_{t^{-}}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left(V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}})-V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})\right)+\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})\left(1-e^{V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}\right)\mathrm{d}t

(7.46)

Since a CTMC is a piecewise càdlàg function, we can define jump times
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}<\dots<t_{K-1}<t_{K}=T
. Then, we have that in the time interval
[t_{k},t_{k+1}]
the state
\bm{X}_{t_{k}}
stays fixed until the left limit
\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}^{-}}
where it jumps to state
\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}
. Therefore, we can define the value difference over all time steps as the sum of changes in value at state
\bm{X}_{t_{k}}
over the time interval
[t_{k},t_{k+1}]
and the value change over the jump between states
\bm{X}_{t_{k}}
and
\bm{X}_{t_{k+1}}
, given by:

\displaystyle V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})-V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\left({\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}V_{t_{k+1}}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}})-{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}V_{t_{k}}}(\bm{X}_{t_{k}})\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}\left(V_{t_{k}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{X}_{t_{k}}})-V_{t_{k}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5898,0.6484}\bm{X}_{t_{k-1}}})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{K-1}\int_{t_{k}}^{t_{k+1}}\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})dt+\sum_{t:\bm{X}_{t^{-}}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left(V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})-V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}})\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})dt+\sum_{t:\bm{X}_{t^{-}}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left(V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})-V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}})\right)

(7.47)

Isolating
V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})
and substituting the HJB equation from Corollary
7.8
, we get the expression:

\displaystyle-V_{0}(\bm{X}_{0})

\displaystyle=-V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})+\int_{0}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\partial_{t}V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})}dt+\sum_{t:\bm{X}_{t^{-}}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left(V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})-V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}})\right)

\displaystyle=-V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})+\int_{0}^{T}{\color[rgb]{0.2891,0.5664,0.8867}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})\left(e^{V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})-V_{t}(\bm{y})}-1\right)}dt+\sum_{t:\bm{X}_{t^{-}}\neq\bm{X}_{t}}\left(V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t})-V_{t}(\bm{X}_{t^{-}})\right)

(7.48)

Finally, substituting (
7.48
) back into (
7.46
) and cancelling terms, we get:

\displaystyle\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=-V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})-\log Z_{0}\implies\boxed{\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{X}_{0:T})=\frac{1}{Z_{0}}e^{-V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})}}

(7.49)

which yields the form of the RND between the CTMC that solves the SOC problem and the reference path measure. Substituting
V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})=\Phi(\bm{X}_{T})
yields the final result.
\square

Having derived the stochastic optimal control formulation for CTMCs, we have shown that the optimal dynamics arise from an exponential tilting of the reference generator and that the resulting optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
admits a simple Radon–Nikodym derivative with respect to the reference CTMC
\mathbb{Q}
. In particular, the likelihood ratio depends only on the terminal value function
V_{T}(\bm{X}_{T})
, revealing that the SOC solution can be interpreted as an entropy-regularized change of measure on path space.

From this perspective, solving the SOC problem is equivalent to computing a KL projection of path measures, where the optimal controlled process is the closest process to the reference dynamics that satisfies the desired terminal value constraint. This observation provides a direct connection to the Schrödinger bridge problem. In the next section, we explicitly formulate discrete Schrödinger bridges using the SOC framework, allowing the objectives developed in Section
3.3
to be adapted to discrete state spaces and enabling practical algorithms for learning optimal CTMC dynamics.

7.4
Discrete Schrödinger Bridges with Stochastic Optimal Control

Prerequisite: Section
3.3

Given our construction of SOC for CTMCs in Section
7.3
, we can now explicitly write the (
Discrete SB Problem
) in the form of a stochastic optimal control functional, which contains a running cost that corresponds to the KL divergence between the controlled bridge measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and the reference measure
\mathbb{Q}
and a terminal cost that ensures the optimal process satisfies the terminal constraint
\bm{X}_{T}\sim\pi_{T}
.

Definition 7.11

(Discrete Schrödinger Bridge Problem with SOC)
.

Consider the discrete SB problem where
\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}
is the generator of the reference CTMC
\mathbb{Q}
and
\pi_{0},\pi_{T}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
are the initial and terminal constraints on the finite state space
\mathcal{X}
. The
discrete Schrödinger bridge problem
can be formulated as a
stochastic optimal control
(SOC) problem which seeks a controlled generator
\bm{Q}^{u}_{t}
of a controlled CTMC
\mathbb{P}^{u}
that minimizes:

\displaystyle\inf_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}^{u}_{t}}C_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},\bm{y})dt+\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})\right],\hskip 9.24994pt\bm{X}^{u}_{0}\sim\pi_{0}

(Discrete SB-SOC Objective)

where the running cost
C_{t}(\bm{x},\bm{y}):\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
and terminal cost
\Phi(\bm{x}):\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
are defined as:

\displaystyle C_{t}(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},\bm{y})

\displaystyle:=\text{KL}(p^{u}_{t}\|q_{t})=\left(\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}\log\frac{\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}}{\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}+\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}\right)(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},\bm{y}),\hskip 9.24994pt\Phi(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})=\log\frac{\hat{\varphi}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})}{\pi_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})}

(7.50)

The optimal controlled process
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
defines the Schrödinger bridge between
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{T}
relative to the reference dynamics
\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}
.

Now that we have established that the SOC problem can be solved in the discrete state space, we can easily adapt the objectives defined in Section
3.3
to solve the SB-SOC problem for discrete variables
(
nusken2021solving
;
zhu2025mdns
)
.

Definition 7.12

(Discrete Relative Entropy (RE) Loss)
.

The
relative entropy
(RE) loss between the controlled path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
and the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is defined as the KL divergence:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}(\mathbb{P}^{u},\mathbb{P}^{\star}):=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{u}\|\mathbb{P}^{\star})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{u}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}-\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}\right]

(Discrete RE Objective)

which can be written in terms of the controlled and reference generator
\bm{Q}^{u}
and
\bm{Q}^{0}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{RE}}(\bm{Q}^{u}):=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}^{u}_{0:T}\sim\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg[\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}^{u}_{t}}\left(\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}\log\frac{\bm{Q}^{u}_{t}}{\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}}+\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}\right)(\bm{X}^{u}_{t},\bm{y})}_{\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}}+\underbrace{V_{T}(\bm{X}^{u}_{T})+\log Z_{0}}_{-\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}}\bigg]

(Discrete RE Loss)

As discussed in Section
3.3
, the (
Discrete RE Objective
) requires backpropagating through the full stochastic trajectory simulations, so we can use (
RERF Loss
) as a practical surrogate loss for (
Discrete RE Objective
).

Next, we will adapt the (
CE Loss
) to the discrete state space. Similarly to Definition
3.15
, the expectation over the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is generally intractable during training, so we define the expectation over an arbitrary path measure
\mathbb{P}^{v}
and
reweighting
the log RND by the RND between
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
and
\mathbb{P}^{v}
.

Definition 7.13

(Cross-Entropy Objective)
.

The
cross-entropy
(CE) loss that optimizes a controlled path measure
\mathbb{P}^{u}
to match the optimal path measure
\mathbb{P}^{\star}
is defined as the reverse KL divergence:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}(\mathbb{P}^{u},\mathbb{P}^{\star})

\displaystyle:=\text{KL}(\mathbb{P}^{\star}\|\mathbb{P}^{u})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\star}}\left[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{v}}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg[\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg(\underbrace{\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}}_{\text{constant w.r.t. }\mathbb{P}^{u}}+\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg)\bigg]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg[\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{v}}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{u}}\bigg]+C

(7.51)

where
\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\star}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}
vanishes in the gradient as it is an additive constant with respect to
\mathbb{P}^{u}
. The CE objective can be written in terms of the controlled and reference generator
\bm{Q}^{u}
and
\bm{Q}^{0}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{CE}}(\bm{Q}^{u})=\frac{1}{Z}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v}\sim\mathbb{P}^{v}}\bigg[e^{W(\bm{X}_{0:T}^{v})}\bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{\bm{y}\neq\bm{X}^{v}_{t}}\left(\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}\log\frac{\bm{Q}^{0}_{t}}{\bm{Q}^{u}_{t}}+\bm{Q}_{t}^{u}-\bm{Q}_{t}^{0}\right)(\bm{X}_{t},\bm{y})\bigg)\bigg]+C

(7.52)
